Endorsements

“Franziska Frank makes a compelling case that in leadership, humility is a source of strength, not a sign of weakness. She offers useful guidance for identifying your weaknesses, increasing your self-awareness, improving your relationships, and accelerating your learning.”

Adam Grant, #1 New York Times bestselling author of THINK AGAIN and host of the TED podcast WorkLife

“This is a great work. In some parts I would even compare it to Peter Senge. It is profound, has warmth, humor and absolute practical relevance. The book shows us how after defeat comes learning in engaged, reassuring humility to draw from it strength for renewal. Humility eases, awakens, increases receptivity, and, moreover, turns you into a more agreeable person. There is a Danish proverb: ‘He who carries his nose high is always in bad company.’ Humility as elaborated by Dr. Frank helps us to simply wear our nose straight. This is just as it should be.”

Peter Baumgärtel, Director Talent Development Germany, Eastern Europe, Nordics, Sitel Group

“We are living in a time when we can hardly plan for the long term. That means we have to become much more flexible in our thinking and simply get used to not having everything under control and being able to know everything. For this, we need humility. We also need it to stick to our own development, to reflect and not fall into rigidity and comfort. That is why I appreciate Ms. Frank's book very much. It not only puts a finger on the open wounds, but also shows practical ways into a future in which employees and managers get their ego under control and take responsibility for the bigger picture. Living leadership in humility, so to speak.”

Claudia Zeimes, HR Manager Bayerngas GmbH & Leadership Coach

“As CEO, my maxim is that any manager’s success ultimately is simply the sum of the employees’ successes. You need to build on the strengths of each individual and value their commitment, creativity and hard work. It is my job to get behind them instead of putting myself above them.

The gratifying insight for me from Ms. Frank's compelling book is that it clearly proves the effectiveness of humble leadership. I will continue being alert – a great culture is ruined in no time!”

Axel Ritzberger, CEO Utz Gruppe

“Humility is a big topic that has occupied me since I was a child. In our company, we've been trying to live it for decades, too. For example, with as much appreciation as possible: There are no extra offices, no extra toilets and no extra meals for us executives. Instead, we strive to work not only at the same level but also with maximum transparency. I have now found the scientific evidence that we are on the right track in Ms. Frank's book. In addition to data and enriching stories, I also received a bevy of tips that will help us as we move forward. With humility it is like with many other things: you have to work on it, develop yourself further. Standing still means taking a step backwards here, too!”

Artur Peplinski, CEO igus International

“I'm pleased that research is finally confirming what I've been trying to practice for a long time: managers, especially high-level ones, need to engage more with all employees, listen more, direct less and let employees take responsibility. Even if this is difficult for some employees at first, it is the only way to get the commitment that is suitable for our times. What excites me about the concept of humility, as Ms. Frank describes and teaches it, is that it provides evidence of exactly how effective these behaviors are. In addition, humility places a strong focus on the benefits of making mistakes. After all, if it is said that you won't make a mistake at 50, then you also won't learn anything from the age of 50 onwards. And that is not only be lacking in humility but also in responsibility for the company!”

Hartmut U. Müller, Vice President IT Technology & Cross Functions at Mercedes-Benz Group AG

===== FILE 3/23: xhtml%2fhalftitle.xhtml =====

The Power of Humility in Leadership

To many people, the words “leader” and “humble” are not natural bedfellows. Yet once they have grasped the definition, most employees desire a humble leader, while a majority of managers believe they already are one. What appears deceptively simple is trickier than expected. Narcissism, lack of perception, and fixed mindsets, neuronal default settings are only a few of the stumbling blocks on the path to humility.

What exactly is this sought-after humility? Humility consists of four key elements: 1) Seeing one’s own strength and weaknesses and revealing them where needed for the bigger picture; 2) Appreciating others for what they are, do now and can do; 3) Being open and willing to learn; 4) Understanding that we are all only a small part of a larger picture, easily replaceable and favored by luck and circumstance. Therefore, humility has nothing to do with being weak or hiding the light under the bushel. Instead, it is about clarity, taking a step back from one’s ego and thus being able to serve the greater picture.

The author’s own research with more than 3,500 managers contributes to the canon of positive effects of humility that have been measured by dozens of researchers during the last decade. Humility benefits employees (ranging from better performance, more innovation, stronger resilience to better client relations, and stronger morals), the organization (ranging from better ambidextrous strategies, a better culture to fewer sunk costs) and the managers themselves (ranging from more seen leadership potential to less stress and better relationships with employees). Dozens of case studies, quotes from more than 170 interviews with top managers, lively storytelling of real-life examples, and solid research with actionable takeaways, plus personal assessments, make this an eminently readable and practical book for managers worldwide.

About the Author

Dr. Franziska Frank (©Uwe Klössing | werdewelt berlin)

Dr. Franziska Frank works as a freelance trainer, keynote speaker and author in three languages: English, German and Russian. She is also Visiting Lecturer and Affiliate Program Director at ESMT Berlin, Germany’s Nr.1 business school.

Her core theme is the art of influencing in all its facets:

Influencing by indirect interventions – living mindfully as a humble leader.
Influencing by direct interventions such as the right structures for decisions, the correct approach to negotiations and the precise phrasing in intercultural communication.

At the age of 15 she left her hometown Munich to attend an English boarding school where she completed O- and A-Levels. She read history at Peterhouse, Cambridge (B.A. and M.A.), followed by two honors degrees in law in Germany. During her studies, she worked as a freelance journalist for the Süddeutsche Zeitung and other newspapers. After her doctoral thesis, she worked as a management consultant at the Boston Consulting Group for six years, followed by ten years as a full-time program director at ESMT Berlin. Since 2016 she works freelance and has published numerous books in English and German.

===== FILE 8/23: xhtml%2fpreface3.xhtml =====

Introduction

Yesterday I was clever, so I wanted to change the world. Today I am wise, so I am changing myself.

Rumi (Persian Poet 1207–1273)

Summary

Humility is about courage – the courage to really see yourself as you are; the courage to appreciate others for what they do; the courage to always be open and willing to learn; and the courage to see the bigger picture. Especially in our day and age, this fundamental virtue of humility is vital. Whoever embraces the contents of this book will not only understand what positive impact humility has to offer for employees and the organization but also grow to reap the benefits of humility for themselves.

Are you courageous? If not, you can stop reading right now. Because humility is about courage. About a lot of courage.

You need the courage to see yourself as you really are. Not as you would like to be. The courage to see your weaknesses and mistakes, bear them, keep them in check, learn to conquer them or at least to own up to and at times apologize for them.

You also need the courage to see your strengths and use them to their full. The courage not to hide or don a comfortable cloak of modesty, but to really use your prowess where it is needed. Not to avoid conflicts, but engage with them where this is vital for the role that you have taken on. This also entails moving into the limelight, where this is necessary for the cause. At first glance, this may sound surprising in a book on humility. You will understand!

You need the courage to acknowledge others for what they are doing right now and what they can do in the future. To respect and appreciate them. Not to ignore their shortcomings, but to focus benevolently on their strengths. This is strenuous and must be done with awareness.

You need the courage to always and relentlessly stay open-minded. Not to rest comfortably in your existing knowledge or extensive experience, but to always scan the horizon for what is new. What is out there for the team, the company, for yourself and others? What new knowledge, what new skill, what new behavior should be mastered? And then master it.

This is the courage that George Bernhard Shaw refers to when he wrote: “The only man who behaved sensibly was my tailor: he took my measure anew every time he saw me, whilst all the rest went on with their old measurements and expected them to fit me.”1 You need the courage to take new measurements of yourself, others, situations. At every moment. Always. And be brave enough to forget your old assessment and assumptions.

And last but not least, you need the courage to see that every one of us is simply a small and finite creature, that is easily replaceable. That we are all part of a much bigger picture. A picture in which chance, luck and circumstances play a huge role. A bigger picture that goes beyond the individual at this moment in time to encompass not only your individual life as a whole but – depending on the situation – the team, the company, the world, its fundamental values and (even) spirituality.

Once you see all this, being humble no longer rings of cowering, being overmodest or servile as it does for some, but like a prerequisite for anyone who wants to take on the good fight for the world he, she and we live in.

Instead of being a sign of weakness, humility then represents a fundamental virtue that every manager should internalize and foster in order to successfully lead themselves and others.

Sadly, this insight, as well as the necessary courage to follow up on it, is not always present. In fact, all too often it is bitterly lacking.

Let me give you a tiny example upfront: 80 percent of managers admit to taking out the easy way at least once a year. How? By taking defensive decisions which they know full well are not the right ones. 80 percent!

Why would they do this? In most cases, simply to protect themselves, their ego and their position. A full 20 percent even admit to going down this safe path for half of their decisions.2

Here it is staring us balefully in the eye: The danger of the ego, the peril of focusing only on yourself.

Humility on the other hand goes beyond the self, sees the role the individual has taken on, sees others as well as the larger picture, is willing to learn and fight, does not cling to its own ego and can make others shine.

So, let us cut to the chase: What type of leaders do we need in this day and age?

Narcissists who think they are the center of the world?
Servile martyrs, who sacrifice themselves and prefer to whine and whimper rather than act and set those boundaries which their role requires?
Egoists who cannot bear others performing well, or egocentrics who do not even notice that others exist?
Narrow-minded people who think they have seen everything, know everything that needs to be known and sardonically insist on neither them nor the world being able to change?
Insular thinkers who never see the bigger picture, where the dots are, how they connect, but stick to the small because it is far easier to control?

And if we now realize that we do not want any of these leaders, then let us – courageously – look at what is happening right here and now in the 21st century. What sort of managers do we have?

A powerful hypothesis, of course, would be that the current situation is catastrophic, indeed: CATASTROPHIC:

That the typical CEO, the typical founder, the typical manager is narcissistic, arrogant, unwilling to learn, narrow-minded and about to lead the economy down the drain.
That their hubris, their lack of humility has skyrocketed in the last decades.
That only a return to the long-forgotten virtue of humility will pull the world back from the brink of economical and societal abyss.

The hope then would be that everyone feels shocked by this real-life depiction, eagerly picks up this book and while reading it ripens along with its lucid propositions, the neat research, the logical conclusions. Freed from tiresome hubris and elephantine ego, the next day they rush into work, elated and ready for a bright future. There, employees cheer their newborn, iridescently humble superior and immediately transform into equal, responsible partners. The result and the happy end: Together they drive the company to undreamt-of heights of success and interstellar growth.

Of course, such strong hypotheses are beautiful. They shake you up, create an emotional reaction of joyful approval or angry rejection that brings decent attention to the book. But such theories simply have no place in a book dedicated to humility. Why? One of the key elements of humility would thus be disregarded: the benevolent recognition of strengths, the non-judgmental perception of what complexity there is.

Simply put: The world of management is not a catastrophe. Many managers, whether they manage 4, 40, 400, 4,000, 40,000 or 400,000 employees, simply want the best for their organization, their department, their employees, and they are fighting a good fight for it.

Equally, many managers on the executive floor or lower down the corporate ladder are aware of the fact that they are not superheroes, that they have strengths AND weaknesses and that others would also perform well in their place. They see themselves as replaceable, favored by luck and circumstances. Many managers deploy their employees wisely and strive to give them the appreciation they deserve. They work at establishing an open atmosphere for learning, and open culture for dealing with errors and even realize that they are part of a larger context as individuals – be it in the company, in society, in the global economy or in a universal bond.

If this is the case, why this book? Why this persistent emphasis on the courage needed to be humble?

Because we are stuck in everyday life, which puts enormous pressure on managers to deliver results – how then can the individual manager avoid hogging the limelight?
Because there is fear: The fear of the manager as to what will happen if others find out that he/she does not know everything and repeatedly makes mistakes – how then can a manager openly own up to weaknesses or genuinely praise others?
Because there is the storyline: The storyline, which loves to link successes and failures to the individual manager – and how then can one stop feeling like Atlas who has to master all – alone – and who rightfully stands at the center of attention?
Because there is a lack of knowledge: A lack of knowledge about what exactly humility and how great its impact is – and how then can you apply it with the courage that it requires?
And finally, because the word is exactly that: “humility.” A word that rings of saints, being demure or servile – so how then should it be desirable at all?

This book not only shows what humility is and why it has a vital place in business life and also politics. It also shows by using dozens of examples how quickly humility goes missing even in the best-intentioned individuals and organizations. It shows what damage this lack of humility causes and how the conscious practice of humility has a positive effect on the whole organization, the employees and the managers themselves.

As each morning we look in the mirror and tidy our exteriors, in the same vein we should take a daily look at ourselves, at how we lead ourselves and others. This book will make it easier to not only steal surreptitious glances at yourself but will also give you the energy to freshen up internally and strengthen yourself. Why? Because, typically it is only small behaviors, little elements of your mindset, aberrant thoughts in yourself, in others, in the company, that need to be noticed in order to react properly. Consciously. Humbly.

What will aid you during this journey that the book proposes to take you on? Being open-minded. Every so often putting the book down and really reflecting on how things are in yourself and in your company. Avoiding the quick conclusion that this piece of research or this story does not apply to you. Steering clear of (presumptuous) assumptions that this behavior will not work with your people. Keeping away from thoughts, that this or that person will always be like that and you will never be able to effect a change in them.

Our brains love the efficiency of the status quo. Our ego defends itself with the best rhetoric against perceived attacks. Our attention bounds towards the obvious. You have to consciously use energy to recognize for yourself what is really going well and what is worth changing. This also takes a lot of courage, since you, like all managers, really have enough other things to do!

Will it all be worth your while? In the end, only you can be the judge of that. However, I know that this book contains facts, analyses, ideas and stories that will improve every manager who internalizes them.

That does not sound one iota humble, does it? Yet it is. Because humility is also about strengths and I know the strengths of this book.

At the same time, I also admit its weaknesses. It has become quite long because there are so many important stories, ideas, facts and analyses. It is also based on hundreds of pieces of research. And research usually means small samples, just a few hundred instead of thousands. Actually, to be precise, the research that is covered in this book has been run with approximately 35,000 managers and employees all over the world. That is a big number and a tiny one at the same time. Much of the book is also based on stories and interviews. And each story is unique and will never fit your circumstances exactly.

Yet I would like to follow Paolo Coelho in this: “Let us be absolutely clear about one thing: we must not confuse humility with false modesty or servility”3 and therefore I now simply submit the book to you as it is.

What is the structure of the book? It follows a red thread from understanding what humility is to its concrete implementation. The whole has 14 steps that build logically on each other.

A first introductory look at humility today. Do investors want it? Do the press or employees? Is it a genuine trend?
What exactly is humility – not in the world of theology or philosophy, but in everyday management, in this our very own digital age? How can humility be measured?
What is the current situation of humility in companies? How big is the percentage of humble leaders?
What is the impact of practiced humility? What benefits are there for employees, the company or the managers themselves?
How to put humility into practice? How can you really see yourself in all your strengths and weaknesses?
How can you truly appreciate others? Are you actually able to see enough of what they do?
How can you always be open? Is there a limit to what you can learn?
How can you perceive and keep the bigger picture in mind? During your everyday work?
Are there limits to humility? Times or situations where humility is out of place? What can go wrong if I use humility at the wrong time or with the wrong people?
Are there relevant differences in humility when it comes to nationality, gender or age?
Is there a difference in humility when it comes to political leaders?
What examples of companies and leaders are there to inspire?
What are the concrete steps that I can take to implement humility in myself, my employees and my organization?
A section of FAQs to answer concrete questions that often crop up during the teaching of humility.

Next to the scientific research, I will share dozens of examples from managers, history as well as military successes and failures. What will these examples be about? About seeing where something humble has happened or – and this is even more important – where, instead, there was a failure, a humility disaster. For many of these disasters, we first have to train our perception. Initially, the response or behavior will look quite ordinary and quite correct to you. Only at second or even a third glance will the debacle reveal itself with all the damage it causes.

Furthermore, four pieces of my own research will be included: In Chapter 3, I go into detail on two quantitative ones that I ran with more than 2,500 participants. The learnings from more than 170 interviews that I ran with Top Executives will be interspersed throughout the whole book.

In Chapter 10, I will dive in more deeply into my research regarding gender, age and nationalities. In Chapter 11, I will refer to a piece of research with more than 200 participants on politics and humility.

What will you be able to do after reading this book?

You will be better at perceiving yourself. In your strength and weaknesses. And understand how and when to show them.
You will recognize where you may not have seen others properly and where you may have failed in appreciation.
You will see the point of being open to learning on more leadership dimensions than you may believe possible right now.
You will determine what your personal bigger picture is and how you can keep that alive during the stress of your daily life.
You will understand what this virtue of humility can and will provide for your employees, your organization and yourself.
Finally, you will know, how to bring forth humility in your area of responsibility and beyond.

What does it take? And here I repeat myself deliberately!

It takes that you do not merely read, but that you reflect again and again about your own experiences, your own situation, your own employees.

That you do not judge the people in the stories, but ask yourself, where have I – perhaps less poignantly – thought, said, done something similar?

And that you already now pat yourself lovingly on the shoulder and praise yourself for having the courage to embrace such an uncool-sounding word as humility. And the courage not only to embrace it verbally but also cognitively, emotionally and with spirit!

Notes
Shaw, B. (1922). Man and Superman: A Comedy and a Philosophy. Brentano’s, First Act.
Artinger, F. M., Artinger, S., & Gigerenzer, G. (2019). CYA: Frequency and causes of defensive decisions in public administration. Business Research, 12(1), 9–25.
   https://twitter.com/paulocoelho/status/871010118867013632?lang=en. Retrieved 12.11.2022.

===== FILE 9/23: xhtml%2fc1.xhtml =====

Chapter 1 The Image of Humility Today

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-1

Chickens don’t praise their own soup.

Saying from Martinique

Summary

Humility has negative connotations for some and is not immediately appreciated by all. Professional investors as well as the press need a second glance to treasure the virtue. However, employees as well as managers are yearning for it – seeing it as a prerequisite for dealing with today’s upheavals, crises and opportunities.

In my more than 30 years of experience, I have not often encountered managers who possess the characteristic of humility. Humility is actually the opposite of what we tend to see: Managers become managers precisely because they are not humble.

(Interview No. 8, VP People and Development, 15,000 employees)

It was after one of my classes that a manager connected with me and wanted to share a story. He was a successful HR leader and had been passionate in supporting his board member in organizing this session. The aim had been to showcase humility, the research, how it could be learnt and applied within everyday management. He thanked me and said: “That was a fine discussion. I am delighted that humility was taken on board so enthusiastically. I was not sure, if that was actually possible.” I looked at him in surprise. This was a cooperative insurance, which was no stranger to the idea of humility being something positive. They even had it written in the company’s values. He continued:

I actually have a T-Shirt with the word “humility” printed on it in huge letters. And you know what? Whenever I wear it, I get negative comments on the street. People call me “weirdo,” diss and sneer at me. I bet that would not be the case if the T-Shirt said “Arrogance” or “Narcissism”.

This story made it clear to me that the word “humility” still has some uphill battles to fight before being positively seen. So far “humility” or “humble” and “humble” are not words to open everyone’s heart. Instead, they are often seen as unappealing and can – in the public mind – be linked to being self-effacing, subservient and even sycophantic. When students were asked to describe what the word “humble” meant to them, more than 50 percent answered that someone who is humble feels badly about themselves, is ashamed, sad, lonely or shy.1 About 10 percent of older adults, when prompted to talk about humility, embarked on a story in which they found themselves exposed and humiliated. And while, in general, humility was perceived as a positive virtue, it was not seen as a particularly useful strength in leaders (nor entertainers, which many of us would think can be quite similar…). In addition, the more narcissistic the participants were, the more negatively humility was seen – closer to shame, humiliation and low self-esteem.2 What effect this attitude has on political leaders, we will see in more detail in Chapter 11.

And what about us? Are we actually able to appreciate someone who acts with humility?

Let us begin with a small test: Whom would you rather have for your boss?

The thoughtful manager who struggles with their own “not knowing,” regularly consults the team, emphasizes its achievements, presents luck and chance as the driver for their own successes and their own mistakes as the cause for failures.

Or?

The manager who offers security? The one who moves forward – albeit somewhat noisily – with a clear plan and concrete instructions. The one who pulls us along and neither shares doubts (if there are any) nor failures or weaknesses (even if he or she is aware of them).

Cross your heart.

The first one seems a little cumbersome, less sexy, less alpha, less tangible. And is it not wonderfully convenient to believe in someone who has all the answers? How pleasant to allow oneself to trundle along mindlessly? Submit to the luxury of following without having to do much thinking. Enjoy the comfort of not having to expose one’s mistakes nor be guilty of errors? How could one ever err, if the path was clearly staked out by the wise forces from above?

There are indeed some initial indications that the second type of manager is overly treasured by investors, the press and employees. Let us look at the research.

At First Glance, Professional Investors Do Not Like Humble Executives

We all know that professional investors are experts, so we should be able to rely on their assessment. Correct? Can we really? Doubts have already been sown by those studies that show that professional investors assess and trade companies differently on sunny as compared to cloudy days.3 Or by those studies that reveal how investors prefer to trade companies with names that come early in the alphabet4, companies that have cool tickers such as GEEK for Internet America, GRRR for Lion Country Safari or MOO for United Stockyards5 or generally prettier names6.

These doubts about the rationality of investors are given credence by a study from 2019 that took a look at companies from the Standard & Poor’s 500 Index to see how humble CEOs were faring in the assessment of “expert” investors.7

Firstly, the researchers spent hours searching for video footage from CEOs of these 500 largest listed U.S. companies. For any of the companies where this material was available, they dug into the performance data. The result: 122 CEOs with data of more than 880 years of company performance.

In the next step, the researchers trained external observers to watch the anonymized videos and assess the CEOs’ level of humility according to defined criteria. A kind of anti-ego value.

At the same time, the researchers analyzed the publicly stated expectations of the analysts, the subsequent results published by the companies and, of course, the overall performance of the company on the stock market. What they saw was profoundly human! The analysts had measurably lower expectations of those companies that were led by “humble” CEOs. In the spirit of: “She only ever talks about her team that means she will not be the type of agentic leader we are looking for.” Or “He’s so calm, he admits to errors, no way will he be tough enough to turn the company round.”

Since market performance is strongly driven by whether a company underperforms or overperforms when pitched against expectations, analysts were subsequently thrilled when those companies that they had (foolishly) underestimated performed better than expected. “Wow, this guy is doing really well,” “What a successful lady.” Now something strange happened. The lack of expectation regarding the earnings per share turned into a meaty valuation bonus. Ergo: companies led by humble CEOs eventually delivered a better market performance than those led by unhumble CEOs.

Since this is complex, let us think this through again: Step 1: The analysts underestimate humble CEOs. Step 2: They are positively surprised by the company’s results. Step 3: They give a substantial valuation bonus.

So, in the long run, it is clear that humility does not harm – and we will later see more data on how it actually delivers a more positive bottom-line result.

By the way, analysts actually do not learn to correct this expectation discount in subsequent years! Just like in “Groundhog Day,” the analyst's brain automatically keeps on reacting less positively towards CEOs who are not brimming with ego. As a consequence, they regularly have lower expectations and are regularly astonished to find that “Wow, these CEOs are great,” which, once they realize, they and the entire market consequently reward.

As readers of a book on humility you must therefore sadly face the fact, that at this very moment in time, professional analysts and investors are drivers for egocentric, narcissistic CEOs being more appreciated than the humbler versions. Initially. We as humans tend to be at first more impressed - almost animalistically - when we are confronted with a chest pounding narcissist than with a prudent sage who places focus on his or her team and acknowledges errors. It is only, once we tune into our rationality that this changes. And the more that rationality is bolstered by data, the easier the correction will become.

So, we can expect this automatism to change once humility becomes an “official” value criterion for CEOs. That which happens now only at a second glance will become first and primary.

Take-Away: Gloom turns to light – Professional investors, while surprisingly critical of humble CEOs, ultimately award a premium for their good results.

The Business Press Loves Simple Explanations

What about the business press? Are expert journalists prepared to recognize and appreciate humility in organizations?

In one way, of course, they are. The number of articles on humility and leadership has skyrocketed in recent years. Typical titles are “Do You Have Enough Humility to Lead?,” “How to Build Humility, the Magic Ingredient in Leaders’ Success,” “Why You Need Humility to Lead through Change” or “The Importance of Leader Humility in a Downturn.”

Board members also regularly speak publicly of their own humility as regards the tasks, responsibility and honors they face or receive.

Yet if you look at the regular run-of-the-mill company portraits, there is still little space for humility. Instead, most business journalists tend to religiously adhere to the story of the great executive hero that drives a company up to new heights or down into old pits. The performance of the company and even the industry is more or less exclusively judged by the CEOs,8 regardless of how often they may try to communicate to the outside world that it was the employees who were instrumental in creating the success, or that these positive results were largely due to the dynamics of the industry or that they were simply lucky.

Why is it so difficult for business journalists to follow a different tack? Because it is easier for the human brain to make a connection between only two points of reference: CEO and company performance. A straightforward story that is much easier to explore than all the thousand other reasons for success and failure. This neuronal default setting results in articles with sentences like the following:

Welch has delivered extraordinary growth, increasing the market value of GE from just $12 billion in 1981 to about $280 billion today. No one, not Microsoft’s William H. Gates III or Intel’s Andrew S. Grove, not Walt Disney’s Michael D. Eisner or Berkshire Hathaway’s Warren E. Buffett, not even the late Coca-Cola chieftain Roberto C. Goizueta or the late Wal-Mart founder Sam Walton has created more shareholder value than Jack Welch.9

Or somewhat more up-to-date:

More assertive, more intelligent, more structured - top managers have to be above average in many fields. Many of them are. But only one of them becomes Manager of the Year. This year it is Oliver Bäte. Since 2015 he has been Chairman of the Board of Management of Allianz S.E., one of the largest insurance groups in the world. And since then, Bäte has been delivering: Allianz posted a record profit in 2018 and has been shining for years with an above-average dividend yield.10

This halo awarded to existing CEOs of successful companies leads to newcomers being regarded hugely critically. Look at what happened to Tim Cook of Apple. A 2011 editorial of the Huffington Post predicted that Apple would never recover from Jobs’ death:

His management, even his vision is replaceable. But that brilliant sense of taste, to which Apple owes their success, will not be matched by the next regime.

In other words, the external observer cares little about what dozens of other reasons might have contributed to the success of the company. They find it hard to look beyond the public facade into the detailed workings of a company. They focus more on the Steve Jobs of this world than on chief designers like Jony Ive who shaped the design of Apple products far more than Jobs did. They overlook that Tim Cook was the one who had already been running the operations of Apple for many years and who had also ensured that design was a top priority.

In short, they tend to overlook the sum of people, structures, processes, culture and market environments that determines any company’s success. Of course, this is difficult to see and evaluate from the outside, making it so much easier to look to the CEO for explanations of why something is happening. And it has a wonderfully reassuring effect to work with this simple linearity. “A did X, that is why Y happened.”

By the way, if asked about themselves, external observers tend to reject any simplistic arguments of cause and effect. Any journalist worth their salt would argue: “What we write and how we perform as a paper is primarily our doing and not that of our editor.”

So, what will happen if a humble CEO tries to place employees into the spotlight, the company culture, the market environment and the bigger picture? The eyes of the press and with it the eyes of the readers will stay fixed squarely and unfairly on the activities of the CEO. UNTIL! Until both readers and writers begin to look beyond simple models of causality and take time to let complexity sink in.

Which is not to deny that a CEO or a manager can have a powerful impact. (We will revisit this in more detail later.) Yet what we all must learn to see is that many concrete business results are also due to other factors.

Take-Away: Causality defeats humility – Many journalists see causality in the person alone, even in someone who acts with humility - that is, the individual ego and individual achievements are seen as the all-justifying yardstick - not, on the other hand, the colleagues, the employees or the situation.

Only Employees Already Know that They Want Humility

The final question then is: What do employees want from their CEOs? Well, they are actually clever and differentiate. On the one hand they see the person at the helm as the source of much that is good or evil. 45 percent of 1,700 managers surveyed in 2015 emphasized that the company's reputation mainly depends on its CEO.11 And 50 percent believe this to be true not only today but also for the future. Why: They claim that CEOs who are positively perceived bring in more investors (87 percent) as well as the right type of media attention (83 percent). Which, as we have seen, is actually a very true representation of the facts.

Employees, though, also have a second perspective – the internal one. And here humility is right at the top of the list. Employee surveys show that humble behavior on the part of their leaders is desired.12 And my research shows (in Figure 1.1) that a full 97 percent of employees see humility for managers as important or very important. When it comes to looking at their direct manager, they are slightly less needy with 89 percent. For board members, it drops down to still a high 85 percent – presumably honoring the need of the media to see all powerful leader.

Figure 1.1 How important is humility according to the definition?

And even for themselves, they see humility according to the definition (which we will talk about in the next chapter in detail) as relevant with a full 88 percent.

The managers themselves agree as you can see in Figure 1.2. Humility scores high for all – most of all for themselves.

Figure 1.2 How important is humility: Answers from CEOs and top managers.

Take-Away: Employees see clearly – Employees are aware of the power attributed to CEOs in the outside world. At the same time, they generally appreciate a humble leader.

Now that we have seen what employees and managers themselves think it is not surprising that humility is a big trend. In early 2022, the term “humble leadership” had more than 67 million Google hits. Journals, magazines and dozens of researchers have also taken up the topic – that means the time must be ripe, correct?

Let us see why today is the right time for humility.

Because we all still remember what happened during the 2010 BP oil spill. CEO Tony Hayward made the mistake of focusing on himself and not the bigger picture and the pain others were experiencing when he said: “I want my life back.” The public was relentless in punishing this conspicuous lack of humility. More recently, think about how egocentric leaders such as Adam Neumann from WeWork or Uber Travis Kalanick lost all public backing. Grandiose narcissism is more and more being seen critically.
Because business leaders are allowed to admit mistakes today without being seen as weak. Just remember how Elon Musk admitted that he had put “too much new technology into the Model 3 all at once.”13 There were no long-term negative repercussions. Nor were there when Zoom CEO Eric Yuan apologized in April 2020 for not having been on top of security measures. The share price showed that this oversight was forgotten in less than two weeks.
Because companies themselves have started to ensure that new positions are filled with humble executives. Head hunters report that they are asked to fill more and more positions with leaders who show this virtue.14 A clear evidence that companies themselves have put the issue on the agenda.
Because digital transformation needs a different kind of leadership. Both research and press agree: “What is needed is leadership that relies less on hierarchy and ego and more on collaboration in a community.”15 Many of my interviews confirm this. Let us take just one quote: “In my view, digitization requires humility. It simply fits better to the way people have to work together.” (Interview No. 11, Head Learning & Development, 300,000 employees)
The rapid changes, the obligation to cooperate more with customers and even with competitors, requires a different mental attitude. In the age of New Work with fewer status symbols to go around, with the clear need to share and distribute leadership, there is no room left for individual hubris and excessive ego.
And finally, because the downside of ego-focused leaders is just too great! A full 70 percent of employee motivation depends on the manager!16 From motivation stems productivity. And if the manager is only focused on himself or herself, not on the employees or the bigger picture, the motivation in the department or company will plummet.

Before you whole-heartedly now embrace the concept of humility and become part of the cultural shift, let us look first in the next chapter whether you buy the definition of humility as research proposes it.

===== FILE 10/23: xhtml%2fc2.xhtml =====

Chapter 2 Defining and Measuring Humility for Everyday Business

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-2

Humility is not thinking less of yourself; it is thinking of yourself less.

Rick Warren (American author born 1954)

Summary

Humility has long been regarded as consisting of moderation, prudence and self-knowledge. In recent centuries, it acquired negative connotations of being connected to weakness, servility and naivety. Modern research has succeeded in shining a brighter light on the term by focusing on its four core elements. Humility is not seen as a leadership style, but as a fundamental virtue that underlies any leadership behavior.

The Stoics are best at humility; they do not take themselves so seriously.

(Interview No. 109, Managing Partner, 1,600 employees)

It was one of my first evenings as a fifteen-year-old German girl at an English all-girls boarding school. One of the six other girls in my dormitory was fed up with me. She shouted: “That’s not the way we pack, that’s not the way we wash, that’s not the way we speak in England.”

My reaction? I squabbled back, thought the girl stupid and shifted into a satisfying mode of hostility for the next few years.

Only later did I understand that what I would have needed there and then was a view from the balcony. An understanding of who I was, an understanding of where these other girls had come from culturally, a willingness to learn about them and see that there was a bigger issue at stake here: I was in their culture; it and I were hugely different. I should have prepared myself for the difference. Instead, I had been quite human and quite stupid at the same time: I had simply jumped into school life, acting on autopilot as I had always done, observing little and listening even less.

My four years there ended up being a glorious experience. But a bit of humility as defined today would have made it yet greater.

Looking at Humility in the Historical Perspective

Historically, humility is truly an old hat. Look at the ancient Chinese, for whom humility encompassed moderation and wisdom and was the key to good leadership.

600 years before Christ, the philosopher Lao Tzu wrote politically astute:

Why is the sea king of a hundred streams? Because it lies below them. Therefore, it is the king of a hundred streams.

If the sage would guide the people, he must serve with humility. If he would lead them, he must follow behind.

In this way when the sage rules, the people will not feel oppressed; When he stands before them, they will not be harmed.

The whole world will support him and will not tire of him. Because he does not compete, He does not meet competition.1

Some 300 years later, the philosopher Xunzi stressed that a successful leader should be “strong but not brutal; humble but not inferior.”

Clearly, humility already then had nothing to do with being of “lesser” value. Hiding one's light under a bushel, being overmodest or weak is not part of the historical definition.

The Greeks followed along the same path. They took for granted that everyone should see themselves in their weaknesses and strengths. Admittedly, the famous phrase “know thyself,” written on the pillars of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi around 450 BC, was first intended to focus us on our limitations. Yet, with Plato around 330 BC, the emphasis shifted. We as humans can develop and build on what we already have. The consequence: in any given moment our focus should not only be on our faults but also on what it is that we are good at. How to focus on both at the same time? Using “wise perception,” a tool that was advocated by the Greeks and seen as trainable.

Plato’s contemporary Xenophon went one step further and defined humility as THE core virtue which is instrumental in allowing our other virtues to shine. He used the example of a war horse whose strength and power only unfolds when both are under the control of humility.2 Men are seen as being no different to horses: Their powers only truly shine once controlled by humility.

Aristotle expanded this Greek understanding of humility with his principle of moderation. Wisdom lies in avoiding extremes and taking a middle position.3 This means no excessive self-confidence but also no feelings of inferiority; an ego that is not overly strong but also not too weak. At the heart of every human should lie balance.

The Stoics found this balance in removing overpowering and draining emotions from reality. They also focused on the bigger picture. Emperor Marcus Aurelius perceived it as follows in his “Meditations”:

You have the power to strip away many superfluous troubles located wholly in your judgment, and to possess a large room for yourself embracing in thought the whole cosmos, to consider everlasting time, to think of the rapid change in the parts of each thing, of how short it is from birth until dissolution, and how the void before birth and that after dissolution are equally infinite.4

How do religions look at humility? Buddhism sees it as a positive and necessary condition: First I must recognize this very self of mine, the extent and the demands of my own ego, before I can consciously let it go.

It was the monotheistic religions that struck a blow at the concept. Humility was instrumentalized for a hierarchy, in which individuals find themselves below the authority of God and the church.5 Mankind must accept this low and inferior position and under no circumstances rebel against either.

This view fits well with the roots of the German term for “humility” which is “Demut.” The original term “diemuotī” comes from Old High German and consists of the two words “serve” (dienen) (dionōn) and “courage” (Mut) (muot). While today many tend to look at the word courage as the key element of humility, the Church for centuries looked to the serving and obeying part. Independent thinking and courage were not emphasized. To be brave against God, to be an advocate of mankind with its strengths, was not a focus.

Of course, there were also thinkers such as Thomas Aquinas, who cleverly posed humility in relation to the size of the soul – the greater the humility, the larger the soul. Only he who was humble could think big, feel big, be big-hearted. Yet, in general, the focus in an authoritarian church was directed towards obedience.

It was this emphasis on service and submission that led the philosopher Nietzsche to revile humility as the morality of a slave – turning man into a “writhing worm.”6 The Übermensch, once he had finally developed, would no longer be in need of humility. Instead, Nietzsche saw humility as “one of those dangerous, slanderous ideals behind which cowardice and weakness, and therefore submission to God, lie hidden.”7

Charles Dickens put up a monument to this negative view of humility in David Copperfield. The character of Uriah Heep professes to be “umble, very umble” and is merely pushing sham humility into the face of others while sneakily pursuing his own evil plans.

Humility Today

In recent decades, the image of humility has changed considerably. For most of today’s philosophers and theologians, humility is powerful and has to do with a sense of proportion, self-knowledge and, of course, self-esteem. The etymology of the word “humility” is used to bolster this view. Just as the word “human,” “humility” also stems from the Latin word “humus” which has to do with earth and being grounded. When are you grounded? When you do not believe yourself to be greater than you really are. But you also do not need to turn yourself into mud!

A few examples for this change in perception:

The British Rabbi Jonathan Sacks sees humility as an appreciation of oneself, one's talents, abilities and virtues as well as an appreciation of others coupled with an openness to the world.8
The philosopher Christian Halbig focuses on the value we assign to ourselves: “Humility without self-respect becomes sheer despair at the demands of the moral laws.”9
The American professor of philosophy, Robert Solomon, compares humility to a speech given at a film award ceremony. Arrogance and false pride should be avoided. But self-mortification is also wrong. “Humility need not be pathetic; it is often no more than a realistic assessment of one's own contribution and the recognition of the contribution of others.”10

In consequence, humility is not about being modest or misjudging oneself. It is merely about having a different focus. A focus towards others. Thinking less about yourself and more about others as well as the bigger picture.

Management thinker Jim Collins already made this clear in his 2001 classic From Good to Great:

Level 5 leaders embody a paradoxical mix of personal humility and professional will. Level 5 leaders look out the window to attribute success to factors other than themselves. When things go poorly, however, they look in the mirror and blame themselves, taking full responsibility.11

At the same time, humility is also about excellence. Humble leaders are seen to have outstanding competence. In recent years, this has even become a prerequisite. In other words, only those who are excellent can be humble.12

Leadership research since 2012 has exploded on the topic. Its core findings?

Firstly, it has established that humility is not an additional leadership style (more details on this in the FAQs) but a foundational virtue. This virtue alongside the values that come with it allows you to employ all leadership styles that are needed.

Secondly, it has identified four elements of humility and tested the concept with samples of more than 35,000 employees and managers to ensure that this is not old wine in new skins, but a valuable stand-alone concept which delivers measurable results.

Humility is shown by those who demonstrate the following:13

A clear willingness to assess themselves accurately in their weaknesses but also in their strengths (and be willing to show them where it makes sense for the bigger picture).
A displayed appreciation of the strengths and contributions of others.
An ability to continuously learn and be open.
A cultivated understanding that the world is bigger than oneself. This is the idea of “self-transcendence” and even “spirituality.”14

Some researchers explicitly name this fourth element: Only those can have humility who recognize that they are a small part of a bigger whole, are finite, easily replaceable and that many of their successes are due to chance, luck and circumstances; who can put themselves in perspective with their demands, their wishes as well as with their mistakes and failures. For those who do not name this self-transcendence explicitly, it is seen as an integral part of the other three points: If leaders assess themselves correctly, they see that they only play a small role in a larger context. If leaders appreciate others, the focus shifts from a mono-causal ego to the bigger world. If leaders are open and willing to learn, their understanding grows as to how much bigger the world is and how little each of us can ever know and influence.

A quick look at each of the four core elements before we go into more detail in the later chapters.

Assessing Yourself Correctly

A lady went up to Picasso’s table in a restaurant. She asked him to scribble a picture on her napkin. Whatever it turned out to be, she would be willing to pay any price. Picasso did as she asked, then gave her the napkin and announced: “$10,000.” The lady was stunned: “But that only took you 30 seconds.” And Picasso replied: “No, it took 40 years.”15

This is humility in the modern sense of the word. Picasso did not hide his light under a bushel – he correctly assessed himself, his decades of striving for perfection and quoted a price that reflected that.

Should – as is most likely – this beautiful story be apocryphal, a similar one concerning the American painter James Whistler (1834–1903) is vouched for. In 1878 he was called up before court to defend his painting “Nocturne in Black and Gold.” The accusation was: “It has only taken you two days to complete the painting, yet you charge 200 Guineas (today about $27,000). This is usury.” Whistler replied: “No, I ask it for the knowledge of a lifetime.”16

Humility is about knowing yourself in an objective, not ego-blind manner with all your weaknesses but also with your strengths. Hence: If I can do something optimally and it helps the bigger picture, I may and must say so.

Where then, you may ask, lies the difference between humility and arrogance? The answer is simple: the humble person does not revel or luxuriate in what they are great at, they do not spend much time thinking or talking about it. They look at it like their individual height. You have X centimeters to your name, but waste no time thinking about it, nor do you praise yourself for it or spend energy to bring your height to the fore of other peoples’ minds.17

As to weaknesses: if there is something that I am not good at, I should at least know it. Then I can do something about it. Or employ people who are better at it than me. And where needed, I should also show my weaknesses and errors.

Sounds easy? Well, there are considerable obstacles on the path to clear self-knowledge. Which ones and how to overcome them, we will see in Chapter 5.

Appreciating Others

Panasonic founder Matsushita was eating in a fine restaurant. During the main course, he asked the cook to come to his table. The chef rushed from the kitchen, anxious as to what might have gone wrong with such an important customer. Matsushita smiled at the nervous man and said that he wanted to apologize. He had not been able to finish the steak, which he had enjoyed very much. It was vital for him to say this face to face. Otherwise, Matsushita was fearful that the cook would feel bad seeing a half-eaten steak brought back into the kitchen.18

Humility is about appreciating others. It is an interpersonal virtue that thrives on valuing others for what they do and what their contribution is. While this sounds easy as pie, it is often surprisingly difficult. Depending on the piece of research, between 25 percent19 and 59 percent20 of employees complain that they are not appreciated at work. Which lack of appreciation has a considerable and measurable impact on their motivation and performance.

A typical example of an accidental failure in appreciation is the following situation:

In the annual feedback meeting that will also determine the bonus, the manager asks how the employee evaluates the success of an event that he had organized with some colleagues. The employee proudly and enthusiastically describes what had been done, how much the clients enjoyed the event, how fine the outcome was…. only to be stopped short by the manager saying: “That is not what I heard. I heard from the other team members that you had a tough time as a team. Arguing and fighting!”

At this moment, the meeting might as well be stopped – there is little that can be done to repair the damage. The employee will not feel appreciated and as a consequence will not be willing to listen. By not having said something positive about the event first, the manager has ruined her chances of discussing how the team might perform more harmoniously together next time. Is the manager an evil person? Most likely not! Only a person mindlessly focusing on the one point she had jotted down about the event. Instead of first listening, appreciating and then leading the employee to gain an understanding of where things might be improved in the future. Which then could have led to an interesting discussion about team development and forming, storming, norming and performing – a discussion which now will never take place.

On the other hand, even a little appreciation can quickly break down barriers, as seen in this example:

There was one participant in a management development program whom everyone found challenging. He was an elderly gentleman, just before retirement, white hair. It was late in a multi-modular seminar that the topic of how to hold team meetings came up. At the end of the discussion, the gentleman suddenly spoke up: he had been working in the company for years and had recently been assigned to a new manager. He knew that he had been passed on as a problematic case, simply for the manager to sit out. The manager had then established team meetings and he, the employee, was asked to give an update on his work. “This manager was the first person in all of 35 years to ask what it is that I do.” This was touching and impressive: on the one hand, the humility of the employee, who was willing to get involved in the process, on the other hand, the humility of the manager, who had not given up on this employee but instead had asked and listened. The other participants had respect for the journey that this participant had made. After all, he was just a person who had never been properly appreciated.

(Interview No. 12, VP Development Change, 100,000 employees)

So while appreciation sounds so easy and most of us believe ourselves to be good at it, we often stumble. How to appreciate correctly, we will see in Chapter 6.

Openness and Willingness to Learn

In my late thirties, I talked to a 70-year-old professor from America about e-books and said that – as an owner of more than “real” 5,000 books – I would probably never read books this way. The venerable-looking gentleman smiled at me and said: “We have now put all the materials for the MBA students on an e-reader. I personally like using my reader for textbooks and for novels when travelling.”

I was caught up short and realized that, while being only half his age, I clearly had to work on my openness towards the new.

Humility is based on the inner conviction that both personal growth and behavioral change are possible. For many, this feels like a liberation: “I don't have to keep on being like this or acting like this if I don't want to.” This mindset of being both a grounded person and capable of transformation is a great catalyst for change. It prevents people from constructing excuses for self-stagnation, as so many tend to do.21

When it comes to learning, there is one statistic that really stands out: 60 percent of managers complain that they have never received a single day of training on how to lead.22 This is a shocking piece of information. Is it not? But please pause here. It is definitely sad that companies make no concerted effort to arm their managers with necessary leadership skills. Yet, most likely we also have a real lack of humility in the managers themselves.

This is the first example where it becomes clear that a lack of humility is often apparent only at second glance. Initially, we are all out to chide the companies. But at second glance, we ask ourselves: Who of these managers looked for a training course by themselves? Who sought a conversation with their leadership or with their HR? Who spent some of their own money to invest in learning how to fulfill the new role that they had CHOSEN to accept? Clearly none of the 60 percent. After all, it is so wonderfully practical to blame the organization for one's own ignorance and incompetence.

This lack of humility as seen in an unwillingness to learn and embrace the new also explains the following: In the year 2014, only 34 percent of all employees actually aspired to become managers. For men, this was 40 percent, for women a sad 29 percent. And only 7 percent of all employees wanted to become board members.23 By 2020 this had even become worse: only 9 percent of all employees wanted to become any form of manager.24 This is an indication that in many of us there is – at one level – a fundamental unwillingness to learn and develop new skills.

In Chapter 7, we will see what all can be learnt and how to be truly open.

The Bigger Picture

Abraham Lincoln was asked why he had taken three of his closest rivals into government. Every single one of them had fiercely attacked him during the previous election campaign. There was no reason to assume that they would become meeker now and cause him less trouble. Lincoln countered: “I had looked the party over and concluded that these were the very strongest men. Then I had no right to deprive the country of their services.”25

A good manager should see the bigger picture. Not only where he or she stands in the corporation but also how the members of their team are faring, what the company needs and what role they and the company play in the world. Yet sadly many seem to struggle with this. Why? One reason is that on a simple biological level we perceive through our own eyes, ears and through our own thinking. It is no wonder then that we initially pay the greatest attention to ourselves as individual beings. At the same time, we are also aware that we live in societies and need these to survive. This actually pushes us to seeing the bigger picture.

Since societies are different, it is no wonder that at first glance the way to perceive the bigger picture differs all over the world. Researchers, for example, have shown that the Japanese tend to focus more on the whole, whereas Americans focus on the more prominent and the individual.26

Does this national result always holds true? Is one nation condemned to be more aware of the bigger picture and the other more of seeing the largest fish? No. It turns out that the way we perceive is strongly related to social mobility. The less people feel they are able to look for new relationships, the more they depend on the existing society, the more they train their brain to perceive the bigger picture.27 This applies to a society like Japan just as much as it does to a small village in the Cotswolds or the Rocky Mountains in the times of Corona.

In other words, we humans can train ourselves for what we need to see. And in the case of a manager who wants to be a good leader, we need not only to see how the team is doing, where it stands within the department, how the company is faring, but also what is happening in society and how humans are connected with each other and even with the planet.

We will see in Chapter 8 how you can identify your bigger picture and defend it in everyday life.

What Managers and Employees Say about Humility

Let us now look at what managers and employees say about the term and the concept of “humility.” A few quotes from my interviews.

Humility has to do with inner stature, knowing who you are and who you are not.

(Interview No. 22, Head HR, 21,000 employees)

Humility is about respect for everybody else – regardless of where you stand in society. It is the realization that you cannot be successful on your own. Much has to be right: Timing, culture, country, people, conditions. Humility is valuable when you have a reason to be arrogant but you are not. Humble is not submissive – it is not about thinking low or less about yourself. Instead, it is about respecting yourself.

(Interview No. 29, Group Head Customer Experience and CSR, 27,000 employees)

Humility has nothing to do with being a wuss. It’s about appreciative leadership, real interest, listening properly and admitting when one is wrong.

(Interview No. 13, CFO, 3,900 employees)

For me humility is also about gratitude. Becoming aware again and again that I am in a very privileged position.

(Interview No. 102, CEO, 1,800 employees)

I follow the oracle of Delphi – do nothing to excess.

(Interview No. 42, CTO, 7,000 employees)

98 percent of those interviewed agreed with the definition of humility as proposed by research. The remaining 2 percent struggled with being a “small” part of the bigger picture. They feared that the focus on smallness could lead to lethargy.

When asked to talk about humility in their own words, there was mention of being “down-to-earth,” “working together as equals,” “appreciating everyone regardless of whether doorman or CEO” as well as about “asking for feedback with an open heart,” the courage to “involve people across hierarchies,” “asking for help,” “admitting when you are wrong,” “allowing others to convince you” and finally “willingness to put the interests of the group above your own.”

Two interviewees would have preferred not to use the term “humility” and resort to “gratitude” or “vulnerability” instead, but the overwhelming majority liked the definition and the concept.

One interviewee summed it up very nicely:

The other day someone said about an actor: He takes the audience seriously and sees himself as not especially important. For the humble manager that means: They take employees, competitors and customers very seriously and do not see themselves as especially important.

(Interview No. 8, VP People and Development, 15,000 employees)

Quantitative research with hundreds of employees confirmed this view of humility. The word cloud (Figure 2.1) shows that most see humility in a positive light. Being concerned with the bigger picture, accepting things, being grateful and able to admit to mistakes. Only a few negative elements come up, such as being servile or restraining strengths.

Figure 2.1 What is seen as humility.

So, in general, if you ask employees and managers to reflect about the topic of humility, the understanding is very close to the modern, non-authoritarian version of researchers.

What about you? How does the word “humility” sound to you?

A few words about the use of the word “humility” in this book. I have to accept that for some the words “humility” and “humble” still carry elements of weakness, servility, being overmodest or of being humiliated. I hope that the meaning of the word will slowly change for you during the course of the book and morph into something strong and powerful.

Bob Stahl, a wonderful meditation teacher from the US told me the following: “Once you realize that the opposite of humility – narcissism and egocentricity – causes so much suffering, then you understand how humility becomes a greater pathway for deep listening and mutual respect that fosters understanding, connection and interconnection.”

My hope is also that those of you that are skeptical regarding the word give the concept behind it a chance. The following acronym may help in shifting the focus to the concept behind humility.

Humility in a nutshell:

Humble are leaders who
Unveil themselves to themselves as well as others
Measure others positively
Believe in openness and learning
Look to the bigger picture
End the rule of their ego
Measuring Humility

How can we measure humility correctly? You can imagine that it is not very helpful to ask someone: “Excuse me, is your direct supervisor humble? On a scale of 1 to 10?”

This sort of question inevitably leads to mis-reporting on several levels. Many have no clear concept of what exactly the term “humility” refers to. It may be that for some the word is primarily about being modest, submissive, holding back. For others it may have something to do with religion and adhering to authorities. Whatever – it is quite clear that you will elicit many wrong answers.

If you answer the question about yourself, other dangers arise: Someone who full well knows their strengths and weaknesses and tries to lead humbly may consider themselves far less humble than their narcissistic colleague. Why? Because they will have a clear idea of where there is still room for improvement compared to someone who is radiating hubris.

For this reason, only some of the more than 22 different questionnaires developed in recent years to measure humility are suitable for this book. Namely those that a) are not (only) completed by the manager themselves and b) ask about the key elements of humility without using the term “humility.”

My preferred scale is the Owens' Expressed Humility Scale from 2013.28 It typically has, what is important in research circles, a high internal consistency – the so-called Cronbach Alpha – of 0.92 to 0.96, which is excellent. I have supplemented this scale with three questions on the subject of the bigger picture from Ou's questionnaire, also highly valued in research circles.

If you would like to see where you stand, I suggest following a three-step process.

First answer these 12 questions for yourself (see Table 2.1). Be honest with yourself. Only you will see the results.

Most managers will now have two different results. For the vast majority of respondents, the answers to the first questionnaire will be more positive than those to the second. Once people focus their mind on which of their intentions are outwardly visible grades plummet. Why? Now we realize that we may only be appreciating three of our ten employees regularly, or that we are mainly asking for feedback from a small circle of trusted peers.

For some managers, it is the other way round – that the second questionnaire shows better results than the first. This could be an indication of the so-called Imposter Syndrome, which you will hear about later. If your scores are considerably less in the first questionnaire, you may actually be underselling yourself to yourself.

It would be ideal if – as a third step – you distribute this questionnaire to your team and, once completed, have it aggregated. Whether this is done anonymously or openly depends on your company culture. You can decide whether to ask for “This person” or “My manager.” That is only a matter of individual taste (Table 2.3).

Once you have the results, you can see where you stand in comparison to thousands of other managers, as we will see in the next chapter.

All questionnaires are available for download at www.humility-book.com. You simply have to enter the code Humility-24/7. You can also connect with me if you are interested in comparing your results with others.

What is a good score? One in which all three versions of the questionnaire result in an average score across all 12 questions of more than 4.0. Then you can count yourself among the humble leaders and expect to reap the benefits of humility as described in the following chapters.

Chapter 3 The Status of Humility in Companies in Our Day and Age

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-3

A humble man is not afraid of failure. In fact, he is not afraid of anything, even of himself.

Thomas Merton (US-American Author 1915–1968)

Summary

Only 35 percent of managers score as humble if assessed by their employees. When asked about themselves, people hugely overestimate their own humility. The size of the company has only little relevant impact on the humility of the leader. Regarding the gender of the leader, younger female leaders are somewhat humbler than elder ones. With male leaders, there is no measurable age difference. While spending a long time in a position does not have an impact on humility, the level of leadership in the company has a small effect. Diverse and smaller teams make humble behavior for employees easier. The pandemic situation around Covid 19 seem to have led to a slight increase of humility.

It had been a long day – the board members relished the fact that the official part was over. Dinner had been pleasant and now they were sitting in the glorious medieval hall, graced by a burning fire, wall paintings, deep armchairs, a humidor and full glasses. The hotel had its own sommelier. Between one of the board members, Alex, and the sommelier a conversation started about one of the red wines on offer. After some happy banter, the sommelier opened the bottle that had been decided on and took a sniff. He smiled happily and poured the wine. Alex took a first sip. And frowned. And took a second sip. Decisively he put the glass down and said: “This wine is corked.” The sommelier took up the bottle again, sniffed and said: “I cannot smell it.” Alex insisted: “It is corked.” The sommelier brought a glass for himself, poured and took a sip. “No, sorry, I don’t taste it.” The situation escalated while the onlookers offered different ways of solving the stalemate. The sommelier insisted on the wine being perfect and refused to replace it. This riled Alex so much, that after a few more minutes fighting, he stormed out of the hall and shouted.

I will make sure that this hotel goes down the drain. I will make it known how bad the service is – and if anyone should ask in a year’s time why this hotel is doing so badly, you can tell them, it is thanks to the board member from room 145.

This experience made a big impression on me. Not only was I surprised at the stubbornness of the sommelier. The wine was not cheap at $80 the bottle, but in such a fine hotel a solution should have been found. But even more, I was surprised by the fact that Alex was clearly keen to use his position in order to sanction a merely private irritation.

Yes, surely this was an annoying situation. But was it worthwhile becoming so angry? Driving his status home, while behaving like a child. All day, in the interactions at the workshop, Alex had been hypersensitive. Everyone had to be careful not to tread on his ego, make sure he did not feel attacked or even worse, not taken seriously. It truly astounded me that someone who was so much focused on himself, would be in a position to run a whole company with thousands of employees.

This story stuck, so when I had the chance to do research on humility, I jumped at it. The key question I had was: What is the percentage of non-humble versus humble managers? How many managers act like Alex and are self-focused, status-driven and obviously not in control of their feelings? Are they a small minority? Who is statistically humbler? CEOs? Managers lower down the corporate ladder? Female or male managers? Younger or older managers?

Simply put: What is the status quo of humility in our companies in this day and age?

First, let me confuse you initially with two different research results. A study from 2015 suggests that only one out of four CEOs is seen as humble. These 25 percent are hugely valued: they are perceived as outstanding corporate leaders.1

While the 2016 HR@Moore Survey, when it surveyed 126 CHRO about the narcissistic and humble behaviors of their CEOs came up with a whopping 60 percent seen as humble, and a mere 5 percent as being strong narcissists.2

So at the outset, we have a huge gap between 25 and 60 percent of humble leaders. Who is right? Well, let us look at this in more detail.

Since 2020 I have been conducting various pieces of research on humility with more than 3,500 participants.

The study consisted of three separate parts – the second part of which is still ongoing to generate a global database in which every manager and employee can match themselves.

400 global Executives (50 percent C Level) recruited from my network and via LinkedIn from all over the world assessed their direct reports.
Nearly 2,500 Employees (of whom more than 50 percent were also managers) from all over the world answered questions regarding their direct managers. This happened in three waves. One set of 350 was recruited early 2020 via the online research portal Prolific, another 350 from the employees of the 400 global Executives above, via contacts I have via my executive education programs and via LinkedIn. Another set of 1,800 was recruited in 2021 via Prolific and Upwork and since then via LinkedIn. Out of this whole group of 2,500 approximately 900 also answered a question about narcissism.
Qualitative interviews with more than 170 Top Managers recruited via LinkedIn and other contacts from Executive Education. 55 percent of the interviewees were C-Level. They came from 20 countries, all company sizes and represented most industries. 75 percent were males, 25 percent females

In the quantitative research, a 5-point Likert-Scale was used with answers ranging from “Strongly disagree” via “Disagree”, “Neither nor”, “Agree” to “Strongly agree”. As a result, answers equal and above “Agree” – i.e., 4 out of 5 points – are evaluated as positive. Why? Would you call someone humble if their employee answers to the question: “My manager seeks feedback even when it is critical” with a ‘neither nor”? I guess not.

So anyone described as humble in the upcoming pages, will have got a score of 4 or more out of 5.

Most of the questions did not use the word ‘humility’. Instead, they employed the expanded Expressed Humility Score, which I described in chapter 2, for an evaluation of the 12 behaviors that are seen to demonstrate humility.

Only afterwards, when I shared the extended definition of humility and a short one about narcissism, then and only then were participants asked to rate their leaders and themselves as regards to these definitions.

Other questions such as precedents of humility, personality types and mindsets were also investigated. To these I shall revert later during the course of the book.

In Figures 3.1, 3.2 and 3.3 you can see the descriptive statistics for the three parts of the study.

Figure 3.1 400 Top managers assess their employees.

Figure 3.2 2,500 Employees look at their direct manager.

Figure 3.3 More than 170 interview partners.

The result? There are some good and quite a lot of bad news!

Not Enough Managers Show Humility

There are different ways of looking at humility in managers – some with negative, some with a more positive result.

The first option is to look at how many managers scored in the humble zone for all the 12 behaviors. I.e., how many managers exist, where employees said “Agree” or “Agree strongly” to every single of the 12 sub-elements of humility.

That is a sad number of merely 35 percent of truly or somewhat humble managers. With most managers being a wishy washy neutral or worse (Figure 3.4).

Figure 3.4 Percentage of humble managers according to employee assessment of behaviors.

Some may say that one needs not get a high score on all 12 sub-elements, and that it is even more important to see what employees say of their managers once they have heard the definition of humility.
