This is a more positive result (see Figure 3.5). Then 23 percent of leaders are seen as truly humble and 44 percent are seen as somewhat humble by their employees.

Figure 3.5 Percentage of humble managers according to employees after reading the definition.

That is not a small number and needs to be appreciated.

YET. Yet, even if you choose to follow this second form of assessment, this 67 percent still means that more a third of managers are obviously not able to give their employees the feeling that they act beyond their own ego, appreciate them, are willing to learn and see the bigger picture.

Where do they fail most?

Figure 3.6 shows the answers. These are now not tied to an individual but are the percentage of leaders for each separate leadership behavior. So, in an individual leader’s case she might get a 5 for praising but only a 2 for asking for feedback. In the graph below you see the summary for all the 2,500 managers assessed and how the breakdown is for each sub-element.

Figure 3.6 Sub-elements of humility that managers are strongly struggling with.

We can see some positive things first. It seems that leaders in general are not too bad when it comes to noticing the strength of others (78 percent), appreciating their contribution (73 percent) and being open to the ideas of others (75 percent). There are some middling scores in the sixties and then some truly bad scores when it comes to seeking feedback (a mere 52 percent), and generally the topic of seeing and being part of the bigger picture (between 45 and 51 percent).

We will discuss later when we come to the stumbling blocks for humility, what to do with these results. Merely let it be noted now, that there is definitely room for improvement!

And remember that this way of looking at it is already seen with a positive slant, since looking at the different sub-elements separately shifts our perception away from the fact, that only 35 percent of managers were seen as humble on all 12 dimensions.

How do you personally evaluate the situation? Please reflect a moment. How high would you say is the percentage of humble leaders around you?

People Overestimate Their Own Humility

Before we dive into different analyses about age, size of company, gender etc. let us look at how the employees (of whom more than 50 percent are managers themselves) who assessed their direct manager see their own personal contributions to humility.

And here (in Figure 3.7) we see the huge danger of assessing your own humility. The overwhelming majority sees themselves as paragons of humility! The lowest score is 68 percent for “trying to make the world a better place”. That seems quite self-critical until we remember that they gave their direct superiors 22 percent less, namely a miserly 45 percent.

Figure 3.7 People see themselves as grandiosely humble.

In general, we are clearly dealing with saints:

99 percent are “willing to learn from others.”
96 percent both “acknowledge when others have more strengths and skills” and “take notice of other’s strengths.”
95 percent are “open to ideas of others.”
93 percent “admit when they do not know how to do something.”

This points gently yet firmly into the direction of overconfidence and wishful thinking. If you look at the difference in average scoring between the managers and their employees the difference becomes yet more striking. For every single behavior, employees score themselves as far better than their leaders – most of the behaviors for all the participants reaching into the golden zone of “Agree” with all of the humble behaviors (see Figure 3.8).

Figure 3.8 There is a strong tendency to overestimate oneself.

And remember how there were only 35 percent of direct managers, who scored a 4 or above on all 12 sub-elements of humility? Yet 70 percent of employees and managers award themselves this halo!

But who knows, possibly this is not hyperbolic but simply correct? Maybe people in their role as employees truly do excel at humble behaviors?

Well, one indication that this is not the case lies in the way direct managers assess their employees. The 400 top managers made clear, that they see considerable gaps to be closed with their employees.

Again, the good news is, that depending on the behavior, they see between 61 and 76 percent of their workforce as getting it right. These figures are quite close to what the employees saw in their leaders – when they looked at the individual sub-elements of humility.

But – as you can see in Figure 3.9 still at least a third of employees appears not able to do something as simple as seeking feedback! And nearly 40 percent fail to compliment others on their strengths – while the employees had believed themselves to be nearly perfect with approx. 80 percent.

Figure 3.9 What top managers see in their employees.

This discrepancy has serious consequences: if I already think of myself as a humble employee or a humble manager (remember about 50 percent of the employees were also managers) why should I look at my behavior more closely? Why try to see myself and the situation more clearly if I am already such a glorious being?

It is vital to be aware of this (over-)confidence: the misperception that you and your team are already doing perfectly fine. In most cases, there is considerable room for improvement and you would be a wise and humble person to recognize that.

Size of Company Only Selectively Relevant

Many interviewees expected that the size of the company would be very relevant. If you look at this word cloud in Figure 3.10 this will become clear. Employees were asked, who in their view shows humility.

Figure 3.10 Who shows humility in business.

Please pause briefly and let the following results resonate:

The largest number of participants responded to the question “Who do you consider to be acting with humility in the business world?” with “No one”!
After “No one” comes “few”, SMEs, NGOs and finally at last two managers Nadella and Gates.

Why would people expect a difference when it comes to SMEs? The assumption or the stereotype is that smaller companies sport a warmer culture, have an all-caring boss, who often is the owner and therefore acts with the best interests of all at heart.

Sorry to have to disappoint you here. Small companies are in no way better than larger ones.

There is no statistical difference in any of the 12 sub-elements of humility as regards the average or the percentage of direct humble managers.

Only on two fronts do smaller companies fare better. Namely, when it comes to managers beyond one’s direct superior. Here – as you can see in Figure 3.11 – mid-size and larger companies do far worse, even though all the scores are generally depressing!

Figure 3.11 Some differences according to the size of the company.

These somewhat better results for smaller companies could have something to do with an internal cohesiveness. The larger the company the fewer details yet the more rumors you will hear about other departments. In addition, with more departments in larger companies, there may be more collisions between the different responsibilities. Due to lack of knowledge or due to regular clashes with other departments it will be easy to assume that other managers are actually far worse. In smaller companies, however, it is easier to see what the other departments are doing, there are also less likely to be overlaps in responsibility.

As regards top management the argument could be that in small companies, I have a chance to actually see, meet and hear the top managers and let them become human to me. In companies with more than 250 or 1,000 or 10,000 employees this is much harder and would need the concerted efforts of Satya Nadella at Microsoft in order for a top manager to make a positive impression on all the employees.

This piece of research therefore should be an incentive for all but in particular for larger companies to look at their internal communication and see how they can succeed in generating a visible culture of humility for the top management.

All in all, however, it must be said, that the differences between the companies by size are not there for the direct manager. Regarding the 12 leadership behaviors all companies do equally well or equally poorly – regardless of whether they have fewer than 251, fewer than 1,000, fewer than 10,000 or more than 10,000 employees.

The Interesting Issue of Men and Women

When arrogance, narcissism and hubris are discussed, there is more mention of men than women. When it comes to humility, instead, many interviewees bring female executives into play. Out of the 170 interviews more than half said, that women are humbler both as employees as well as managers.

This verbal assessment is initially confirmed by data when we look at what superiors say about their direct reports. As you can see in Figure 3.12 the 400 Top managers see their women reports as showing far more humble behavior than their male reports. Between 17 percent (Believes that everything is under control) and 44 percent more (Admits when does not know how to do something) – which is truly a large difference.

Figure 3.12 Top managers see female employees mostly as more humble than men.

On the other hand, you still have more than half of respondents, seeing absolutely no difference in their employees, with some actually crediting men with more humility – going up to 10 percent for complimenting others on their strengths.

Nevertheless, the result for women as employees could be taken as an indication that employees then should also perceive women managers as humbler. Why? If 400 Top managers, say of their direct reports most of whom are leaders themselves (remember more than 72 percent of the participants were C-Level and C-1, so their direct reports by definition will mostly also be leaders) that women are humbler, then we would expect employees to also see a measurable difference.

Here we are in for a number of surprises:

At first glance, if you look at the percentage of men and women that manage to score a 4 or more on all 12 subsets of humility, they come out quite close. Yet already with an interesting twist. Remember on average 35 percent of managers manage to reach that score. For men it is 36 percent and for women 32 percent as you can see in Figure 3.13.

Figure 3.13 Female managers seen as less humble than male managers by their employees.

While that is not yet statistically relevant it gives us an indication that not all may be as expected.

The second indication is, that there is no statistically relevant difference on the average score female and male leaders receive from their employees. As you can see in Figure 3.14 both sexes are seen as equally bad or equally good. Remember that a 1 is a simple agreement of the employees with the relevant statement, while a “strongly agree” which would be a 2, is nowhere in sight.

Figure 3.14 At first glance no statistical difference between men and women.

If you look at the percentages in detail, there is a small, yet significant difference on two fronts which you can see in the Figure 3.15. While 68 percent of male managers are seen as acknowledging when others know more as compared to 62 percent of female managers, female managers on the whole seem to be better at understanding that not all is under control, with 60 percent rated as humble versus only 52 percent amongst male leaders.

Figure 3.15 Some differences between the genders.

Small fish, you might say.

Yet when you dive more deeply into the different age groups, something surprising happens.

On the one hand, unlike many of my interviewees expected there is hardly a relevant difference regarding humility and age in men. The younger generation is not filled with humility, nor do the older managers reap a huge amount of the expected benefits of wisdom.

With two exceptions. The young generation of men seems to be more able to ask for feedback even when it is critical. 59 percent of the male leaders below the age of 40 are assessed by their employees as doing this, versus only 48 percent of those over 50 years of age.

Still a small number – yet matching what we know about younger generations and their wish for regular feedback.

On the other hand, there is a higher percentage of older manager that take notice of other people’s strengths. 79 percent of the managers over 40 versus only 71 percent of those under fifty. As if awareness of others was indeed growing over time.

In general, however as you can see in Figure 3.16, there is no big difference across the ages. (Only where the percentage is shown is there a statistical difference.)

Figure 3.16 Percentage of male managers seen as humble by their employees.

The same holds true for the average score across all 12 sub-elements. It is around 36 percent, with the drop to 34 percent for older men not being statistically significant (Figure 3.17).

Figure 3.17 Average humility for male managers.

For female managers, the story appears to be a different one. It is here that we find an age gap. Female leaders above the age of 50 are seen as significantly less humble than the female leaders below the age of 50 on a number of dimensions.

Even though the difference is not everywhere statistically different, it gives us quite some large gaps between younger and elder women of between 9 and 11 percentage points. This is quite a stunning result, that you can see in Figure 3.18.

Figure 3.18 Percentage of female managers seen as humble by their employees.

This result is supported when you look at the drop in average humility for the elder age group (Figure 3.19).

Figure 3.19 Average humility for female managers.

Before we look at explanations for this, let us also look in more detail at how the different age groups compare across gender. In the group of leaders below 40 (Figure 3.20) female leaders exhibit some more humble behaviors than men. Apart from when it comes to asking for feedback even if it is critical.

Figure 3.20 Comparing female and male leaders <40.

Again, remember: Only the points where the percentage is recorded are statistically relevant. There is a significant difference, when it comes to such “officially” female skills such as complimenting or showing appreciation. Yet, at the same time, considering how much difference was expected between men and women regarding humility even in this age group, women are not in general humbler.

Even this small difference disappears in the age group between 40 and 50 (Figure 3.21). Female leaders are not seen as humbler. And, indeed the opposite holds true for the dimension ‘Sees as a small part of a larger whole’ where men are ahead.

Figure 3.21 Comparing female and male leaders <50.

And for the age group above 50 female managers are actually less humble compared to men (Figure 3.22).

Figure 3.22 Comparing female and male leaders >50.

And while there are no sub-elements where there is a direct relevant difference, all the little gaps add up so that only 24 percent of female leaders are seen as humble versus 34 percent in the same age group of men (Figure 3.23).

Figure 3.23 Percentage of humble managers across gender and age.

In the light of this analysis, it is then no longer surprising that there is no average difference between the genders, since the humbler younger female managers make up for the less humble older ones.

But how to explain that female managers seem to become less humble the older they get?

There may be several explanations, all of which require further research to stand the test. On the one hand, it may be that the generation of women over 50 has decided (or felt compelled) to show less humility in the corporate context in order to be seen as strong and promotable. This is the whole issue of women managers putting on leadership behaviors that are judged to be male in order to fight their way up the career ladder. Much anecdotal evidence for this comes from the interviews and discussions with my executive education participants.

On the other hand, it may also be the case that female managers are judged more critically by their employees than men in the same position. Research shows clearly that women are often perceived more negatively than men. For example, women leaders are seen as more at fault, when they have committed an error as compared to their male peers.3 When a male manager has made a mistake, his rating as a leader drop by 10 percent, while the female manager was immediately perceived 30 percent worse!

Want more examples? Female professors receive worse grades than their male counterparts – not for the way of teaching but for the identical online content that they have distributed!4 In addition, female leaders are penalized when they lead with emotions as well as when they employ too little emotion.

So, the fact that somewhat older female leaders score worse, may have to do with them being judged more harshly.

If a man is arrogant that is not perceived as arrogant but as self-confident.

(Interview No. 38, Director, 390,000 employees)

Thirdly, it may be that companies actually (tended to?) prefer promoting non-humble female managers, believing them to have more chance for success.

The discussion remains open and research will continue. It will be interesting to see if the younger generation of women will also become less humble with age, or if they will keep their slight advantage. Also, whether as so many interviewees predicted and hoped for themselves, elder leaders of both sexes will learn to find it easier to be and lead humbly.

I will return to this topic of gender and age together with a glance at nationalities in Chapter 10.

Only Some Difference in Humility According to the Level in the Company

Employees see more or less an equal number of humble leaders among each level of leader. The only differences as you can see in Figure 3.24 is that there are significantly more humble managers in the band of C-1.

Figure 3.24 Percentage of managers seen as humble by level.

That comes as a surprise on two fronts. On the one hand, a number of interviewees mentioned how much easier it was for them to be humble right at the top of the company. Now they could hire the best people to compensate for their weaknesses and no longer needed to actually pretend not to have weaknesses. Now they gave themselves the permission to act more freely and set the tone, instead of being in a chain of command where someone else defined the culture.

On the other hand, numerous employees mentioned how they expected humility to decrease right at the top, since there would be less honest feedback given. They saw the danger that Top Management would actually living in a bubble.

Both arguments are not borne out by the quantitative research. Apart from CEO – 1 there is an average score of around 35 percent with no difference between the CEO and CEO – 2 and lower. Only the CEO – 1 level is doing better at 43 percent. This may have to do with the bigger picture that managers at that level already observe while there may still be more room than right at the top for receiving feedback.

In addition, there are some other smaller differences to observe.

Firstly, when it comes to making the world a better place: here around 51 percent of the upper echelons (C and C-1) are seen as humble leaders versus a mere 39 percent at the lower levels. Figure 3.25 shows clearly, how our grasp of the bigger picture grows once our role demands of us that we have to answer questions such as “why are we here as a company” or “what does our company want to contribute to society”?

Figure 3.25 The higher the level the more purpose.

Secondly, as you can see in Figure 3.26, the C-Level falls prey to the belief that things can be controlled, the illusion of being master of fates. We will dive more deeply into this topic later on, yet here it is interesting to see that while 61 percent of leaders lower down the rank believe that not everything is under their control, only 49 percent at C-Level are seen by their employees to have the same understanding.

Figure 3.26 The higher the more belief of being in control.

The Length of Time Spent in a Position Has No Impact on Humility

Humility and routine are bad bedfellows.

(Interview No. 35, CDO, 4,700 employees)

I experience with our managers who have been long in one position that their own ego prevails. They find it harder to appreciate others, see their own strengths and weaknesses.

(Interview No. 43, Head HR, 15,000 employees)

Even though research with a smaller number of data points seemed to suggest that there is a decrease in humility after more than 10 years in a position, the newer analysis for this book with more than 2,500 participants did not yield those results.

As seen in the quotes above, many interviewees expected there to be a decrease in humility the longer a manager is in their position. But this is not borne out by the data.

So, while job rotation may be good for the culture of innovation and cooperation, it does not seem to be necessary for fostering humility.

Diversity Is Better for Humility

One third of the top managers surveyed have direct reports with over 70 percent being men. In this group, by their own assessment, various elements of humility drop between 4 and 10 percentage points compared to more diverse groups (Figure 3.27).

Figure 3.27 Lack of diversity has costs.

While this research only looked at gender as an element of diversity, we know from other experts that we can expect similar positive results from a mix in nationalities, education and personality type.

All in all, yet another indication that diversity truly pays off.

Number of Direct Reports Matter

Many leaders lead more than 5 direct reports. In the current research 85 percent of managers do this. And they pay a price for this.

Which one? The top managers have themselves reported humility costs on numerous dimensions in Figure 3.28. In particular, most of the learning from others seems to get lost. Which is not surprising, since in larger groups it may not clear whom one can learn from. In addition, there will be fewer meetings for all to connect and discuss once the direct reports exceed a certain number.

Figure 3.28 Downsides of larger teams.

This result is also interesting in that it points to the fact that it is once more the situation that is decisive on how much humility there is. Just as being in a primarily male teams seem to have an impact, in just such a way is the organizational impact of team sizes also visible.

Since this is the case, every manager needs to make sure that larger teams have other benefits to compensate for losses on the humility front. Or spend more time ensuring that bigger teams also get the chance to learn from each other.

Covid-19 Changes a Little

We see more gratitude towards employees, partners, customers.

(Interview No. 4, Corporate Vice President Digital Marketing, 50,000 employees)

Yes – there is almost a forced humility: you must let people do their own thing. And then many people have realized that it can be done.

(Interview No. 11, Head Learning & Development, 300,000 Employees)

There was the chance to learn what people are capable of. In general, you can say that many employees are hugely underestimated.

(Interview No. 12, VP Development Change, 100,000 employees)

I have the impression that some managers have simply stopped leading.

(Interview No. 129, CEO, 600 employees)

It used to be enough to be charismatic and drop in on people ever so often. Now in Covid times, even charismatic leaders have to find a new way to lead.

(Interview No. 15, Head of L&D Europe, 1,500 employees)

The interviewees agree – something has happened in terms of humility during the Covid-19 crisis. In both directions! On the one hand, the crisis has shown that there is much to acknowledge and that one cannot and should not have everything under control. Many things have become more humane. People talk more with and know more about each other.

At the same time, there is an opposite trend: the decisive crisis manager, who likes to decide everything on his own, has been given a wide rein. Also, a number of managers have simply stopped leading.

The feeling is therefore: “The crisis only reinforces what is there” and does not necessarily trigger a fundamental change towards more humility.

The figures confirm these ambivalent statements: On the one hand 36 percent of those surveyed see more humility among managers in this Covid-19 period and 33 percent expect this effect to continue. On the other hand, this is only just a third. For two thirds, everything seems to have remained the same and they expect nothing else.

Four Activities Strengthen One's Own Humility

There are activities that have a discernible correlation with humility – and can also be assumed to be causal.

For example, as you can see in Figure 3.29, significantly more executives are seen as humble if they have a coach, have studied abroad, read a lot or listen to a lot of podcasts, or do volunteer work. And this is true for both the behaviors observed and the humility employees see in their leaders after seeing the definition of humility.

Figure 3.29 Activities that correlate with more humility.

This already gives us a preview of the fact that humility can be learned!

 

Takeaway: A long way ahead – This research suggests that while there are some interesting differences in gender, age, size etc. there is quite a lot of work ahead for most managers and companies to develop humility. How is your company faring? How are you doing yourself on the different dimensions?

In the next chapters, we will see the large benefits that humble leaders convey and also dive more deeply into why so many people (around 90 percent) think of themselves as being humble, while not actually being so! You by now know the number 35 percent as being the overall percentage of humble leaders. A sad result!

Notes
Gaines-Ross, L. (2015). What executives value in their CEOs. Harvard Business Review, 5.
   https://www.hrmagazine.co.uk/content/features/the-prevalence-of-narcissist-ceos. Retrieved 12.11.2022
Brescoll, V. L., Dawson, E., & Uhlmann, E. L. (2010). Hard won and easily lost the fragile status of leaders in gender-stereotype-incongruent occupations. Psychological Science, 21(11), 1640–1642. https://hbr.org/2016/04/research-we-are-way-harder-on-female-leaders-who-make-bad-calls. Retrieved 12.11.2022
Mitchell, K. M., & Martin, J. (2018). Gender bias in student evaluations. PS: Political Science & Politics, 51(3), 648–652.

===== FILE 12/23: xhtml%2fc4.xhtml =====

Chapter 4 The Benefits of Humility

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-4

It is amazing what you can accomplish if you do not care who gets the credit.

Harry S. Truman (American President 1884–1972)

Summary

Over the past ten years, research in hundreds of projects has identified measurable, predominantly positive, results of humility. These can be divided into three groups: Effects of a humble leader on the employees (e.g., in terms of performance or creativity), results for the entire organization (e.g., in terms of strategy or culture) and positive consequences for the leader himself or herself (e.g., in terms of performance or stress).

While I am standing here before you, the top team is away on a two-day retreat, discussing the future of our strategy. If they had needed me last year or this year, they would have asked. As it is I will join the discussion in a few days’ time.

The person saying this is not a junior member of the company but Wilf Blackburn, regional CEO of Prudential Asia. An energetic, alert Englishman, who has worked abroad for decades and who clearly manages to keep his ego in check. No need for him to be present at the yearly retreat. Let the team brainstorm freely, without any self-censorship in front of the boss.

“In many cases I am not actually needed,” he continues. There was a crisis in 2018 when the company had accidentally deducted too many premiums from their customers. He went to his team and said: “I'm sure you can handle this!” The result?

I realized precisely how much they wanted me to leave the room so they could work on the problem in peace. The Chief Technology Officer actually brought pizza – and the great thing was not the pizza, but that he was not needed.

Once the problem was solved, Wilf apologized publicly to all the customers – an unusual step and one that was appreciated by customers and employees alike. Sheela Parakkal, Chief Human Resource Officer cites a positive internal impact:

Before the crisis, a fairly junior member of the team had just handed in her resignation. After we had dealt with the crisis for ten days, she withdrew her notice since, in her words, “we have shown how we are as one in the face of crisis.”

Sheela emphasizes that when Wilf joined the company, it was ready for someone like him. “We had already started on a journey of defining purpose and living values. But Wilf was the jet fuel. He had the courage, the charisma and the vision.” She continues:

At the same time, he respects differences in opinion and gets people to realize options and choices elsewhere. Many people have been actually exited for the bigger picture, but he spent a lot of time mentoring and coaching them – with a huge respect for each individual.

Wilf tells a story which illustrates this point.

I am not big on hierarchy; I have always found it a hindrance. We have no status symbols – no one has their own desk; we do not show people’s rank on business cards. I try to push decisions downwards and stop them from coming upwards. Of course, that sort of culture takes time to instill. For example, I thought it vital, that we stop working behind closed doors. So, I picked an empty desk in the Walk-In Customer Service Centre and worked from there until the whole office had been converted to open space. Before this was done, I asked all my managers “Is there anyone who still needs their own private office?” A number of people raised their hand. I said: “Thank you, I will help you find jobs in other companies.” And I did. Every single one of them has moved to a leadership culture better suited to them. I would never sacrifice an employee, but always try to find a new role for them with us or outside of us.

He looks at the bigger picture and tries to balance what employees require with what the company needs:

We want to attract people who seek experience and plan on staying about 5–6 years. That way we keep on getting fresh ideas. Of course, we do not fire anyone who wants to stay but our focus is on those employees who are keen to continue learning and many therefore wish to move on. We also want to have a great alumni net – just like the consultants and lawyers. I just recently heard an impressive example from an Indian company where I am on the supervisory board. They wrote to every alumnus of theirs: “If you lost your job during Covid, please let us know – we are hiring.” What an amazing sense of community.

Wilf talks openly about his personal development:

Last month, I was at a Swiss business school – many managers and employees think that my talking about the training and my “need” for it is a sign of weakness. But we all need to keep on learning and refreshing ourselves.

When it comes to his assessment of his own persona, he is very clear:

You know, as a CEO I am simply playing a role. Nothing more. I was once at Disney World in Paris and saw someone wearing a Minnie Mouse costume. Just as that person in the Minnie Mouse costume should not get their self-esteem from their costume, in the same way I should not get my self-esteem from the CEO costume.

How does he see his impact:

20 years ago, I was thought of as a Maverick – now, what I do is no longer seen as crazy. And it will be continued: My successor has been picked by the company to continue what I started – to bring his own stamp, of course – but not to fundamentally change direction.

I found Wilf inspiring – and wondered how he had become the leader he was. He explained it with a twist:

You see, I was never so wildly excited by the content of the work – so I had no wish to lead by mere mastery. Instead, I wanted to focus on the people and how I can influence them without playing the hierarchy card. My Executive MBA really helped there – all those great case studies.

When I embarked on this book, Wilf came to mind as a proverbial humble leader. Someone who knows his weaknesses (“I am not so big on details; that drives my line manager nuts”), knows his “strengths (“I focus on doing the things that only a CEO can do”), appreciates others by empowering them (“you can do this”), is willing to openly learn (“the three most powerful words are: I don’t know”) and sees the bigger picture (“it is vital that I help these 20 CEOs become the best they possibly can become”).

The question that baffled me following the conversations with him and Sheela, was the following. “Hearing these great stories, how can we know that they really have a measurable impact on the employees, the organization and the leader him or herself.”

Wilf gave only some indications that his behavior at the helm may have been effective. There was mention of less fear, more engagement, good customer relations, a new type of employee coming to join the firm – but he strongly pointed away from himself instead to the whole company culture, the changing times and, and, and….

Sheela as Head of HR provided more concrete data points, namely that the company used to rank quite low as number 124 for employer of choice. Over the years of working with Wilf, it had moved up to 42 and then to number 11. In 2021 it was actually named one of the top 15 employers in the region – the only insurance company in the group.

Yet the question for me still was, whether there was strong evidence from other firms, quantitative proof that humility in a leader was actually working? Could I find data that would help convince those managers who were worried about being seen as weak? Those that feared that their employees would begin taking the easy way out if not firmly taken at hand? Those that were apprehensive of what their bosses would think of a humble me? Those that saw stress on the horizon if they tried something new?

250 academic articles, dozens of books, numerous conversations with other academics later, it was clear that I could! In my hands, I had dozens of measurably positive effects that a humble leader generates. Hundreds of studies that showed in beautiful graphs, in long tables what exactly the impact is on employees, the organization and the leader him – or herself of knowing/showing strength and weaknesses, appreciating others, being open to learning and seeing the bigger picture.

And I will share them in the following pages in order to award you the proof that humble leadership is not some wishy-washy PR, some new HR trend but a timeless impactful virtue that brings not only joy but also success!

From these lofty heights now to some lowly and humbler comments to put what will follow into perspective:

By its very nature, research is always about statistical results. This means that in general humility causes X for the employees, for the organization and for the manager.

This concept of statistical probability is usually obvious to everyone. Yet, when teaching about the effects of humility, I tend to encounter the hope that research actually gives a sort of guarantee. A guarantee, that if I do X, the outcome will be Y. Sadly, this I cannot offer. Full stop. There is no assurance that in every or even any situation where you apply humility you will achieve exactly the effects described. Why not?

Firstly, numerous conditions in your organization, in you or your staff will play a role, so in your case the effects may occur fully, little or not at all.

Secondly, no results are “one hundred percent” results. In other words, research does not suggest that you have a result of 0 percent without humility and of 100 percent with full humility. Instead, averages are formed over larger groups and these averages show a tendency. This tendency is statistically relevant, which means that humble leaders in total have this or that effect. The precise size of the effect is not an issue. We do not get to see if a 0.5-point higher score on humility makes employees 20 percent more creative. The only thing we know is that in the game of large numbers more humility in leaders leads to more creativity.

Also, in each individual situation, the outcome may be different. With one group of employees, it may have an obvious and great impact; in others, it may only have a small or negligible effect. Nevertheless, if you regularly act humbly, you increase the probability that those effects, which researchers have uncovered, will also appear in your case.

The Fundamental Effects of Humility

What does research say in general? That humility gives you the flexibility to go through your daily management routine far more consciously and far less burdened by your ego. It is humility that enables you to behave in a way that is appropriate to each and every situation.1 What humility offers is a sort of view from the balcony. A balcony that shows how things really are to a non-judgmental eye and allows the manager to ask the question: What exactly does this situation require of me as a leader?

The consequence of this view from the balcony is that it enables the manager to choose flexibly between all different modes of reactions as well as between different leadership behaviors.

Let us think of a humble naval officer. During the planning of a mission, she not only involves her staff but also her superior, be it with a relationship- or task-oriented leadership style. While the mission is running and there is danger on the horizon, she switches to an authoritarian style and gives precise instructions that must be obeyed. After the mission, she focuses on discussing errors and potential improvements, while paying homage to the achievements of the team. This is what humility is about – seeing herself in the greater picture and picking the tools, that are required to align the team with the task.

Leadership is a task, a role that has nothing to do with my ego. It is about what exactly is needed right now, and what I need in order to make the river flow as it needs to flow.

(Interview No. 145, Consultant to CEOs)

This may be done by narrowing the river path, blocking certain outflows or allowing for stretches of calm and shallow water.

If, in the example above, instead the officer only uses authoritarian styles from beginning to end, she will most surely fail and definitely receive far less information and fewer ideas from her staff.

If you do not insist on your rank, position or seniority, you can learn a lot as a manager. Informal channels open up. The manager learns things he or she would not learn if employees had the feeling that the manager was judging everything.

(Interview No. 12, VP Development Change, 100,000 employees)

The manager faces the same challenges. Firstly, he must know his own strengths and weaknesses. That will allow him to bring other voices to the table when planning a strategy, a project, a meeting. In crisis mode, he may have to act in an authoritarian manner, taking on full responsibility and allowing no dissent. After the crisis, there will be time for analysis as well as acknowledgment of the individual contributions. The view from the balcony enables this vital change in behavior.

Ergo: Humility frees you from having to follow any one set path and allows you to truly bring yourself and others on board without your ego getting in the way.

Most of this book will be dedicated to business research. Yet, there are some inspirations that we can draw from other fields.

A study from 2020 demonstrates that patients of humble doctors are more satisfied and more trusting. This is despite the fact that humble doctors clearly communicate their doubts and errors to patients. What a good lesson for leaders. What else? Patients treated by a humble doctor also feel healthier!2 You may object: Feeling healthy is all very well but surely health is objective. And the test should be if they are actually healthier? Well, we do know that subjective experience is one element of how we feel – a sort of subjective objectivity. And we also know that the famous placebo effect triggers physical and measurable improvements in patients. This effect can be weaker or stronger. Take a look at this example: if patients with Parkinson are told that their medication (a placebo) costs $1,500 per dose, they subsequently have better motor functions than those that are given a (placebo) pill for $100 per dose.3 It therefore makes sense that the humility of the doctor is as effective as a placebo pill – leading not only to more satisfaction and trust but also to genuine better health.
A therapist's humility creates a stronger and more supportive relationship with the patient. Why? The humble therapist listens more to the patient, takes on their perspective and judges less. This has the positive consequence of patients being far more willing to work on their key issues. Which leads to faster change and the feeling of quickly getting better.4 Add to this cultural humility and therapy across different cultures gets to be far more successful.5 How would this result differ in a culturally humble business manager? I expect not at all.
Those who are humble are better able to resist substance abuse. Why? Humility fosters self-control.6 Which insight those managers struggling with alcohol or drugs – approximately 10 percent7 – could use well.
If you have been primed towards humility, you become less aggressive and revengeful. How do researchers uncover this? Well, a typical approach is to see how much people shout, swear or honk their horns when they are provoked. This is far less when people have been nudged into a state of humility. Another sort of wonderfully weird approach is to give people the opportunity to pour chili sauce on the food of a person who has criticized them… More humility, less spice revenge!8
Last but not least, humility has a positive impact on interpersonal relations. Researchers analyzed how committed and satisfied each partner is to the relationship. The result is crystal-clear: those relationships in which the partners have an attitude of humility beat the others by a long way.9 Why? Humility measurably keeps the individual ego under control. As a consequence, partners are not so easily offended by each other. Instead, humility makes me realize that I may not be seeing the situation correctly, that I might also be getting things wrong, that the other party might not be all weird and that there might actually be good points in his or her strangely different perspective. The result: more understanding, coupled with the willingness to be more forgiving and to work on the relationship in tough times.

In the following pages, the core focus will be on research about managers leading with humility. Yet, I will continue to include interesting and enriching research from other areas where it fosters the overall understanding. Or the dramatic effect! Since this book is to serve the concept of humility and you, I will employ anything that will help remember things better. And great stories, drama and emotions help our brains to take in and store the new.

Benefits of Humility for Employees

Jim worked at a large conglomerate with more than 300,000 employees. In recent years, he began to feel that the narcissistic CEO was taking credit for all and generating a culture of pushiness – just the opposite from the leadership culture he wanted to live in. Having reached board minus one, not liking the atmosphere any better the higher he had got, he thought to himself: “Time to leave.” A smaller company with 2,000 employees asked him to become CEO once the current one, let’s call him Mark, had retired two months later. Jim enthusiastically joined the company and was appalled. Mark had no habit of talking or listening to his people. In meetings, when Mark peremptorily asked for questions or comments, even his board members fell silent, while lower levels scarcely dared look up. When Jim spent his first two weeks simply asking and listening to employees, Mark sneered: “Why the heck are you doing this? What on earth are you expecting from this wasteful use of your time.” When I spoke to Jim, he was still surprised: “He obviously thought me esoteric and weird. Simply because I had decided to go to the employees, ask and listen.”

Once Mark retired, some elements of the culture changed immediately: “People began asking questions, they commented and they objected. It is wonderful to see how engaged people have become.” Jim is cautiously optimistic: “Of course, we still have a long way to go, but I am hopeful that the first building block is in place: engaged employees.”

It is when you hear stories such as this, that the first set of research results which in Figure 4.1 show measurable benefits for the employees of a humble leader will not come as a surprise: A far greater percentage of those working for a humble leader are better able to see their own contribution as well as their purpose. Many more of them are motivated, satisfied, feel fairly treated and are less likely to want to change their job than compared to those working for a non-humble leader.

Figure 4.1 Some positive effects of humility.

Since purpose and motivation are some of the key drivers for performance, it is not surprising that one of the key findings in humility research is that it improves the performance of the employees as well as other elements.

In the following, I will look at the evidence that research has generated and show you seven areas in which humble leaders generate a measurable difference in their employees.

Better Individual Performance

In the long term, humility will lead to an increase in performance, higher motivation, more productivity and better quality.

(Interview No. 7; VP Training, 20,000 employees)

People like humble leaders and work more for them.

(Interview No. 50, Global HR leader 24,000 employees)

There are five reasons why employees of a humble leader perform measurably better.

Employees of a humble leader feel – by their own assessment – more energy and less exhaustion. Energy translates into activity and the willingness to put effort into what needs to be done. Interestingly this effect is stronger, the more the manager is perceived as “powerful.”10 This makes sense: if I work for a manager who is respected internally, can get things done, then their humble behavior will not only give me a boost but also the feeling that what I work on is valuable to the department and the company as a whole. (We will see later that a humble manager is a) typically believed to be more powerful, and b) because he/she sees what the role requires will fight typically the good fight to get as much power as is needed to get things done.) Yet even for a not-so-powerful manager, the effect on individual performance is still measurable and positive. Employees “feel empowered in their work, more inspired by their managers and feel that their company is the right place to work.”11

Employees of a humble leader also feel more authentic and less vulnerable – which again gives them more energy and motivation.12 Let us look in more detail at this piece of research.

Participants were divided into two groups and asked to imagine themselves as a head server for a busy restaurant. At a retreat with numerous restaurants, their direct manager Alex accepted an award from the CEO of the company and responded in one of two ways.

The first Alex said: “Thank you for this award! I’m honored to accept it. I’ve worked really hard since I took over as manager to make this restaurant one of the top performing in the country. I’m really proud of what I’ve accomplished and it’s great to be recognized for that success here. As a manager, I often receive feedback and advice about how to run the restaurant but I truly believe that I’ve been successful because I’ve listened to my gut and made the important decisions on my own. I hope to continue this success into the future. Thank you again!”

The second Alex said: “Thank you for this award! We’re honored to accept it. We’ve worked really hard as a team to make this restaurant one of the top performing in the country. We are really proud of what we have accomplished and it’s great to be recognized for that success here. As a manager, I often receive feedback and advice about how to run the restaurant and I truly believe we’ve been successful because of all the valuable input I get from everyone working in the restaurant. We hope to continue this success into the future. Thank you again.”

Not surprisingly participants were much happier with the second manager – but only, if they felt the speech was authentic and not put on (we shall see later that faking humility simply does not work). As a consequence, the feeling of vulnerability in the employees decreased fundamentally, as you can see in Figure 4.2.

And imagine an atmosphere where employees do not feel vulnerable – then they will be far more willing to think on their feet, take on responsibility and therefore perform better.

The humble manager fosters a culture that sees her or him as easily replaceable, as not always needed. The result: the leader takes a step back and employees are empowered. They take more responsibility, are more courageous to solve issues by themselves, which allows the manager time for the more important tasks.

Figure 4.2 Interaction of humility with follower vulnerability.

If I take myself too seriously and take on the role: “I know everything, I can do everything” well then, I have to know and decide everything. If I take myself less seriously, then I give more room for others to take over tasks. As a result, I make myself less important. For me personally, this leads to a situation in which, if I don't have to deal with all the “crap”, I have more time for the really important things.

(Interview No. 2, Head of Talent, 10,000 employees)

  In a humble culture, employees also find it easier to be part of a team. They feel that they belong to each other, which makes them willing to work together with extraordinary intensity, show professional excellence in all activities and adopt the open attitude which is needed for “humble enquiry.” What exactly is this humble enquiry? It signifies the ability to really ask, investigate and listen. As a result, difficult situations and behaviors are not covered up but communicated. Equally, no idea will be excluded from the outset, but instead all assumptions can be brought out into the open, questioned and new paths explored.13

Picture a company in which the manager accepts input from everyone. From the department heads as well as from the engineers, the designers and of course from the employees who work directly at the machines or with the client. As a consequence, a problem in the production process, an issue with a customer, a drop in productivity or in quality will be solved much faster through bottom-up information and effort, than in a team where the manager conveys that he or she knows everything.

Look at this lovely, if apocryphal, story of a new CEO. In his factory he sees a young man leaning against the wall and visibly doing nothing. The new CEO wants to show some powerful leadership and resolutely steps towards the man. “How much do you earn.” Somewhat surprised the man looks up: “400 dollars a week.” The CEO says: “Wait,” goes to his office, comes back after two minutes, hands the man 1,600 dollars and says: “Here is the salary for 4 weeks. Now get out and don't you dare come back.” Satisfied, he turns to his people and asks: “Won't anyone explain to me what this madman has been doing here?” And from one corner of the room comes the voice: “That was the pizza delivery service.”

This is what happens when you don't listen, don't ask, don't invite people to be active.

The third reason for greater performance is that humble leaders actually engage in “affirmative coaching.”14 The positive consequences of affirmation have been known for a long time. One of the most famous studies concerns new cadets on their training ship. Those who were told at the moment of recruitment that they were unlikely to get seasick, and even if they did, that this would not in any way affect their performance, spent far less time feeding the fishes. Plus, their superiors rated them as more capable than the control group. (Of course, as is usual in these studies, the supervisors did not know that an experiment had taken place.)15

Want an example for affirmative coaching? Look back at Wilf Blackburn: “I am sure you can handle this” is exactly one of these sentences, that if said with the trust a humble leader has in his/her people will work nicely.

Performance also grows due to employees experiencing a sense of greater self-efficacy.16 “Self-efficacy” denotes the belief held by a person that they can overcome difficult situations through their own efforts. That they are not victims of the situation but can play an active role and achieve relevant impact. To attain this self-efficacy, the person must clearly have the opportunity to be effective, that is to master something by themselves. The more often he or she succeeds in doing this, the more likely it is that this feeling of self-efficacy will help the person succeed at new and harder tasks.

Let us take negotiators as an example. Those that have developed a feeling of self-efficacy through experience and practice do not perform worse if they feel anxious before their negotiation. Unlike those without this experience. For them a feeling of anxiety spells death to ambition: they bring home significantly worse results.17

Self-efficacy is the counterpart of learned helplessness. If I cannot see that my actions have any effect, human creativity fades away, as does the willingness to make an effort. Why should I make an effort if the manager never listens to my suggestions? Why even bother thinking? Just as some people refuse to load the dishwasher if they are always criticized by their partner for how they do it.

If you are self-effective, you can set higher goals. And we have long known that higher goals have a positive effect on people's performance. Two beautiful examples from Harvard Professor Ellen Langer:18 She divides participants into two groups. One group is asked to do 100 jumping jacks. The second group has to jump 200 times. Both groups are asked to flag when they get tired. The first group gets tired at 65 to 70 jumps, the second at 130 to 140. Regardless of the goal, after about two-thirds of the task performance decreases.

The same happens when participants have to type one or two pages of text. No matter the size of the planned task, most of the errors occur after two-thirds have been completed.

What can we learn from this? If I set myself a higher target, my good performance will extend for longer. If I know that I managed X the last time, then I can take on X plus Y and will probably succeed in it.

Look at the hotel chain Ritz Carlton and how they set employees on the road to self-efficacy.

The Ritz-Carlton has given itself the motto “We are Ladies and Gentlemen serving Ladies and Gentlemen.” This immediately conveys a high level of appreciation for the employees. On top of this, each employee has a budget of $2,000 at their disposal. What for? To provide a customer with a WOW moment. No matter how. For example, one employee boarded a plane to pick up a forgotten computer. In another case, a hotel engineer built a wooden path into the sea so that a handicapped boy could go swimming. And in one instance following a water damage, there was not only a letter of apology including the direct extension to the hotel plumber, but also a big spanner made of chocolate.19

The $2,000 is available for each customer. That is generous. And looking at the average life time, customer value of $250,000 it is also a sensible investment.

This simple organizational decision to award employees with funds allows them to develop self-efficacy. I not only have my personal resources at my fingertips, but also monetary resources. From this and experience grows self-efficacy.

The fifth reason for greater performance lies in the fact that employees of a humble leader are more resilient in times of crisis. They are more inclined to accept uncertainties and willingly take on board new information and facts.20 After all, their manager has never claimed to know hundred percent where the journey was going; never said that this was all the information there was and never gave the employees the feeling that it all would be easy. In this way, the employees are not frightened or resistant, but remain flexible and become noticeably more efficient.

They also learn to get up again faster when a crisis or error has occurred.21 With a humble leader who shares information, employees have understood – as had the Stoics – that there are some things that can and others that cannot be influenced. What they can influence they now begin to influence via their actions; that which they cannot influence they do not take personally. The research is very clear on this: Those who have this perspective face the demands of difficult situations better – are more resilient and as a consequence perform better.22

Take-Away: Humility brings performance – Five solid reasons why employees perform better when they have a humble leader: Employees have more energy, are empowered, are strengthened by affirmative coaching, experience more self-efficacy, and are more resiliant.

Now that’s something!

Please take a moment to reflect, when you in the past were driven to great performance. Was there anything that your manager did to inspire you? Be it with confidence, with freedom, with praise, with a willingness to allow mistakes.

And how are you doing with your own employees right now? Any room for improvement?

Better Team Performance

In our day and age there is less and less work done simply by one person, and more done in teams. In particular in changing teams, working within agile concepts and structures.

As we know, the cost of dysfunctional teams is huge. Where colleagues avoid each other, compete for ideas and resources, hide information and generally refuse to collaborate effectively, companies lose out on potential. Full stop. How many teams have you seen, where negative behaviors have led to knowledge not being shared, creativity not being fostered and innovations not being generated? How many teams have you seen, where one or two “rotten” apples seriously damage not only the culture but also the performance of the team?

I bet numerous. Just as one of my interview partners see them in his company.

I see many teams failing with innovation. Not because there is no creativity, but because people are in competition mode. They prefer to defend their own area of power instead of collaborating.

(Interview No. 55, Director Global Learning, 34,000 employees)

A humble leader measurably has an effect on the work and the performance of the team.23 How? By being a behavioral model, showing own strength and weaknesses, openly appreciating team members and modeling teachability. In this way, the humble leader encourages positive and frequent interactions among team members. He or she is what can be termed “social oil,” ensuring smoothness in the team process.

Humble leaders actually use their influence to promote positive interactions while at the same time reducing negative intragroup connections. The result: A supportive environment that advances overall team effectiveness.

Since humble leaders are not frightened of losing personal power, they are more willing to share power and support egalitarianism. This is contagious and leads to followers being more willing to work constructively with their peers. It also leads to team members becoming more open and patient with others. The fine outcome: others are no longer seen as hindrances for getting the work done, but as useful resources whose weaknesses are outweighed by their strengths.

Finally, there is a sense of forgiveness in teams led by a humble leader – and as we all know, being allowed to make mistakes in the pursuit of innovation is not detrimental but actually vital to the performance of teams.

It is therefore not surprising that one of the most successful authors on leadership Stephen Denning postulates: “Agile, in short, requires humility from leaders. We don’t mean a false humility but rather the sort that accelerates learning and bolsters the confidence of every team member.”24

Take-Away: Teams need humilty – A humble leader not only nudges individuals towards higher performance but also teams.

More Creativity and Innovation

Humility promotes initiative and has valuable effects for innovation.

(Interview No. 10, Deputy Head IT, 1,000 employees)

Being humble is a prerequisite to implementing an open innovation approach.

(Interview No. 26, CIO, 68,000 employees)

As I have just described, something happens to the attitude of employees when the manager acts with humility.

We have seen that humble managers empower their employees. As a result, they have more energy, feel self-efficacy and resilience. At the same time a humble leader also awards a sense of psychological safety. “Psychological safety” is a deep-rooted feeling of security at work. Once in place, it gives people the freedom of being able to speak up and act without fearing for their self-image, status or their career. The employee feels a sense of belonging, secure in the process of learning new things, contributing and questioning the status quo without being embarrassed, marginalized or punished.

This feeling is lacking for those who are shouted at, denied loyalty, regularly deprived of or bluntly assigned tasks and who live with the basic underlying feeling that they could be dismissed at any moment.

The importance of psychological safety was first seen in 2012 when Google embarked on a journey to uncover what makes effective teams.25 This endeavor was called “Project Aristotle” and analyzed different groups and teams at Google, assessing them on numerous dimensions.26

The project looked at team composition (e.g., personality traits, emotional intelligence, skills, demographics) as well as team dynamics (e.g., what it was like to work with teammates). They conducted hundreds of double-blind interviews with leaders to get a sense of what they thought drove team effectiveness.

What they found shook up existing wisdom on team compositions and behavior: The single best predictor of team effectiveness was not intelligence, emotional or otherwise. It was not the leader’s vision or anything to do with the way the team gave itself rules. No, the best predictor of a well-functioning and successful team was simply psychological safety. The perception of each team member that it is okay for me to take risks in this group, that I can show weaknesses or gaps of knowledge. That I will not be punished for offering ideas, asking questions, admitting errors, challenging others.

The last point is vital, since it means, that psychological safety is also lacking when I feel unable to challenge others, when there is too much avoidance of conflict in the company.

There’s still a lot to be done when it comes to psychological security. At the moment it is still like this: we are like a family; you do not step on each other's toes. But it is part of the process that issues are actually brought to the table.

(Interview No. 6, Head HR, 300,000 Employees)

A feeling of psychological safety allows for conflicts to be aired and resolved without negative consequences for the team or the individual.

Research shows that a manager who knows his or her own strengths and weaknesses recognizes others for what they do and is still open to new things is a key factor in creating a sense of safety among employees. She will not be an angry or choleric manager at the helm; he will not be somebody who does not care about the well-being of their employees, but instead both will take on the role of a partner on an equal footing. A kind of partner, who at times may be wise, who at times may be not, but who will fill the required role with respect for the team. The result: employees feel safe.27

Reflect on your last meetings with people higher up the hierarchy. How safe did it feel to speak up? Did you, if only in the smallest sense, censor the content of what you were saying in order not to endanger yourself?

How big exactly is the impact of a leader’s humility on psychological safety? Let us look at one study.28 85 teams were asked first to how assess the humility of their leaders and then how psychologically safe they feel. The correlation could not be clearer – and could hardly be stronger if you look at Figure 4.3.

Figure 4.3 Psychological safety and humility.

The more humility, the more psychological safety. Full stop!

What direct results does this psychological safety have? It increases the motivation of employees, their willingness to commit themselves to the job and to put in much positive effort.29

People are then more willing to get involved and go the extra mile.

(Interview No. 6, Head HR, 300,000 employees)

Humility makes more people want to get involved.

(Interview No. 2, Head Talent Management, 10,000 employees)

With a safe environment comes employee creativity. Who is capable of being creative or willing to invest lots of mental energy, when they constantly have to battle to secure their position? Or if they are not appreciated for what they have created? Who can be original when there is a risk of the boss happily appropriating any of their good ideas? Or when errors are a catastrophe and the “guilty” punished?

There is an additional element that has to do with taking perspective. The more perspectives someone is able to see, the more creative they become. Imagine building chairs. If you only take the perspective of men, who are two meters tall and wear long trousers, your ideas will never veer towards chairs that can be lowered for shorter people or materials that need to be chosen in order to protect legs clad with nylon tights.

Humility of the leader engenders this perspective taking, which in turn leads to more creativity in teams.30

Employees of humble leaders are also more active in taking the initiative to craft and optimize their jobs to solve the creative task more effectively.31

They also raise their voice, both in a positive and critical sense. Since the manager is willing to listen and since he or she asks many questions, the employee finds it hard to consistently remain passive.32 And this is vital for creativity. Creativity does not exclude conflict, but instead requires a constant and productive struggle for the best ideas. This means that the willingness to express oneself openly and, if necessary, critically is crucial for the process. Only then are the best ideas identified and brought to realization.

It is a healthy company, when people dare to go into conflict. When sparks fly and people shout at each other, I can go home in peace. When people say ‘Good idea’ and afterwards give you the finger, this is a company without humility.

(Interview No. 57, Director L&D, 70,000 employees)

In summary, humble managers have an indirect effect on the innovative ability of the company through fostering psychological safety.

This effect also occurs since the manager visibly acts for the good of the organization, not for him or herself. In other words, they demonstrate a kind of altruism which ensures that employees are more willing to experiment, work openly and constructively with colleagues in other departments, something which would not have occurred to them without this role model. It also creates more opportunities for organizational learning. Both are important elements for successful innovation.33

And even employees that had withdrawn can be snapped open with humility.

Ronny Grossjohann and Robert Harms had been given the task of building a new production facility at Siemens to manufacture burners for power station turbines. They were unable to achieve any substantial progress within the usual planning process and decided to take a radical change of direction. The workers themselves were to build the factory. “We'll let the people organize themselves! We just provide a room and let go.”

The result:

Everywhere where we let go, things happened that we would never have managed by ourselves! We suddenly experienced at first hand the energy and self-confidence of the staff, and quickly understood: The core of the knowledge can be found within the base. The more we let go, the more ground-breaking the ideas became. Employees who had previously worked in silence, standing at “their” machine for 20 years suddenly joined in the lively discussion and stood up for their ideas. In the debate they took responsibility for the design of the production process - and for six-figure budgets!

The happy end: “After half a year of fruitless standstill, all of a sudden, our only task was to create the space and bring together the knowledge of the employees!”34

One short digression on the topic of innovativeness. Is it correctly hailed as being vital for good business results? Does greater innovativeness necessarily lead to a higher return of assets? Does truly every company fare better if it drives innovations?

Researchers looked at 47 mid-size companies that were driving process innovations.35 They found that innovativeness only leads to a substantial return on assets in those companies that sported psychological safety. The higher the psychological safety, the higher the return on assets and the greater the achievements of the set innovation goal (Figure 4.4).

Figure 4.4 Psychological safety/process innovation and return on assets.

Why? Because innovations only work when they are implemented – and this long and tedious process requires stamina, motivation and clear acceptance of responsibility for one’s own part in the bigger picture. Which again is enabled and fostered by the humble leader.

By the way, creativity is measurably greater in companies in which the exchange of knowledge is strongly encouraged. Here too, managers with humility are predestined to ensure this exchange in and across departments. After all, they create an atmosphere for learning and experimental thinking.

Humble managers also give the employees a feeling of belonging. And no matter where you are in the world, this feeling increases innovation by a good 40 percent.36 What an impressive value.

Take-Away: Humility makes employees creative and innovative – The safety that employees feel with a humble leader promotes creativity and the open and critical thinking that is necessary for innovation. If creativity and innovation are important in your company, then humility is an almost mandatory requirement!

More Emotional Labor and “Deep Acting”

Some phrases need explanation before we can dive into their connection with humility. This section contains three such phrases. The first is: “Emotional labor.”

Work life often demands specific emotional reactions from us. There are frequent moments, when it is not appropriate to show one’s true feelings: Imagine you are working at an airline counter that has just announced a flight delay of four hours. This will make you miss a friend’s birthday dinner. Can you show your irritation and sadness? No, you still have to be friendly to furious customers and push your personal emotions into the background.

The same is true in many other cases: If you have to withdraw a discount that has been granted to a customer; if you are sitting in the IT department and the server is down again; if you have to communicate bad news to your team – in all these situations, it can easily happen that your inner feelings do not match that which the outside world needs from you. Your role requires ever so often, that you should not share your very own feelings such as irritation about the internal discount policy, anger at the technology or furious incomprehension of the strategic decisions of your own company.

Instead, the situation demands that you express other emotions, be it calm, confidence or friendliness – everything that may help the other side deal with the situation.

Remember Tony Hayward from BP, who during the oil spill left his role, focused on his personal irritation and obviously did not manage to look beyond himself.

In other words, there is often discrepancy between what you are feeling at the moment and what you are allowed or encouraged to show. In order to achieve a positive expression, a smile, a friendly voice, a confident charisma, emotional labor needs to be done.

Now the other two concepts come into play: “surface acting” and “deep acting.” Surface acting is the process of clenching my teeth: I smile even when I don't feel like it; I stay friendly even when I would rather slam down the receiver on the rude customer.

We can all do this surface acting. And everyone knows that it takes an enormous amount of energy to show a different face to the outside world than you have on the inside. It is therefore not surprising that surface acting leads to emotional exhaustion, more resignations in companies and generally poorer performance.37

Things are different with the so-called “deep acting.” Here employees make an emotional effort in such a way that they actually end up feeling that which they need to feel. Who does this? For example, employees with a strong customer orientation. They find it easier since they genuinely empathize with the customer. They manage to put themselves into their shoes and get an inkling of what it is that the customer is actually in need of right now.

Others go down a different route in order to generate the genuine feeling. They remind themselves of how they have decided to label themselves. “I would never shout at a customer,” or “I do not lose my calm, come what may.” (This self-labeling is a very powerful tool about which I have written more in another book.)

“Deep acting” leads to the person actually feeling and experiencing the required feeling. This is measurably good for the customers, since they typically see through superficial acting and do not feel valued. Deep acting, on the other hand, is perceived positively. Consequently, customers often manage to switch out of their own self-focus, end up being calmer and giving more positive social feedback – perhaps they thank sincerely, or they manage to smile back despite the crisis.38 This again has an effect on the employee and his or her growing positive feeling.

Deep acting not surprisingly leads to employees feeling less exhausted, sleeping considerably better and quitting less!39

Now that the phrases “emotional labor,” “surface” and “deep acting” are clear, let us look at the humble leader. He or she inspires employees towards deep acting, which in consequence they find easier.40 This effect of the humble leader is all the greater in companies where customer centricity is lower. Why? If customer focus is engrained in the culture, then an individual manager has little additional impact, humble or not. If, however there are no clear rules, no explicit culture, then the humble leader has a large and measurable impact.

How exactly does a humble leader inspire employees towards more deep acting? One reason is that they are more appreciative of the achievements of others, so they are more likely to praise employees for coping or having coped well with difficult situations. This experience from past difficult situations ends up creating genuinely positive feelings for the new difficult situation.

The second reason is that the humble manager with their willingness to see the bigger picture projects a clear picture of someone who has the best interests of the customers and the employees at heart. As a consequence, such managers will in most cases succeed in keeping their own emotions tuned to the needs of the situation. The employee then not only knows the manager to be loyal and supportive but also has their calmness and the view of the bigger picture as an inspiration. If my manager never yells at an employee or a customer, even in the most stressful situations, then I will also learn to do this less. Deep acting will come automatically.

Take-Away: Humility brings positive emotions – Research shows that employees of humble leaders succeed more easily in adjusting their emotions to the necessity of the situation. This is a very pleasing result of humility, which can only be acknowledged with a genuinely felt smile.

Stronger Morals and Ethics

It may come as little surprise that the humility of a leader, in which he or she puts himself or herself in perspective and is willing to learn, is positively linked to the moral and ethical attitude of the employees. Indeed, research shows exactly that.41

In general, it is difficult to have a direct influence on the moral attitude of your employees. If you preach morality, there is a risk that they will be irritated and prefer to engage (frequently covert) in resistance rather than follow your words. This is even more the case if what the manager preaches does not correspond with what the employees can observe in the manager himself.

A typical example is the following anonymized real story: The CEO as well as the divisional managers, one of which is called James, attach great importance to the value of loyalty. They regularly talk about it and emphasize how important loyalty is for the company. How much it is valued and how important it is that everyone is loyal to the company, each other and customers.

One day, the moment of truth arrives: one of James' employees – Olga – resigns. It is known that she had been unlucky in having had an accident and nevertheless having to embark on numerous journeys for James. She also had many different projects with a number of other people. One of these was Jack. In the exit interview with James and the head of HR, the employee Olga was asked why she had quit. Next to many other issues, including the broken leg, she also mentioned a complicated project that she had run together with Jack. And like a hawk, James now pounces on Jack. In the year-end meeting, he accuses him of failing to work well with employees, of being too harsh, of not being able to lead others. No attempt to question or clear up the facts, no willingness to listen and no loyalty. Instead, James brings the ultimate killer argument. When Jack dares to question whether one single comment by Olga should really be taken so seriously, James retorts: “Where there's smoke, there's fire.” Over the next few years, other employees are regularly asked about Jack, specifically probed for bad management behavior. Only negative comments are passed on to Jack. Is it any wonder that whenever the official value of “loyalty” is mentioned, ironic winks or sarcastic grunts are the normal reaction?

Between moral preaching on the one hand and a solid silence when it comes to values or ethics lies the path of humility. What does this path look like? A willingness by the managers to honestly scrutinize themselves and their own moral competence. A willingness to appreciate the moral struggles and behavior of others coupled with a demonstrated willingness to learn.
