How exactly does the manager achieve this? By being willing to employ the same openness as when looking into one's strengths and weaknesses. By being willing not only to define one’s values but also to observe one's own evil.

“Evil” is a really big word and unusual in a management book. Many of us like to think that in peacetime we are generally on the side of the good guys. Yet, if you look more closely, there is enough evil that can emerge in mundane day-to-day business. Let me give you a short list: Consciously nagging at others; consciously allowing obvious errors to run their course because it is too exhausting to do anything about them; letting a bad egg in your team ruin the atmosphere while you look away; going for what you know are wrong decisions, since they are easier to communicate and less dangerous to your position; letting yourself go and yell at someone; or, as in the example above with James and Jack, shoving people into mental drawers and doing everything possible to keep them there.

The evil here, of course, is a lesser evil, but I expect that everyone – after a short and possibly painful examination – has to admit that they are not safe from it. This does not mean that you have to chastise yourself. As always, the first thing that needs to be done is to simply recognize what there is within each of us of laziness, anger, sensitivities, carelessness and stubbornness. And then make yourself aware whether you want this behavior in yourself and in others.

The direct consequence of such introspection: You have to be sure of your own values. What is important to you in life, in interaction, in relationships?

That costs energy and time – because you have to be on your toes so as not to be taken by surprise in everyday life.

Matt Mulford, an UK-based professor of negotiation, tells of an expat manager who invites his business partners to a big dinner. When he comes to the restaurant, the restaurant owner stands at the door saying: “Unfortunately, your business partners had to cancel, but here is the bill of €8,000 for the cooked meal, which unfortunately we had to throw away.”

A classic kickback. If you have not thought through beforehand what you are prepared to do for a business deal and what not, it is possible that you will sign the invoice merely because you were not prepared and are bewildered. Which means that accidentally you will have bribed someone and realize this only later on when you “wake up” and are horrified at what you have done.

In times of corporate governance, this example is perhaps less common – but do you for example know your own values towards suppliers?

Sergei Petrov's goal was to make his company, the Rolf Group, the largest car importer in Russia. This meant serving international customs. When his CFO suggested for the second time not transferring an outstanding sum to the Austrian suppliers, but rather wait and accept the penalty, Petrov said (in a rather Russian manner): “Listen, man, I love you very much, and I’m ready to go dancing or fishing with you, but I’m going to hand over the finances to someone else—someone whose mentality corresponds to that of our foreign partners.” That turned out to be Matt Donnelly, the Irishman I recently made CEO of the business.42
Or look at an international technology company that has decided to pay all its suppliers only after 60 days. Imagine the impact this has on a local tradesman. Or on a trainer leading a seminar. While the motive is understandable, the result is clearly a negative reputation amongst all suppliers which the CFO most likely did not want.

If you do not align your actions with values, it may happen that you end up equally accidentally ruining your reputation. It is also possible that as in the example of James you unwittingly create a negative atmosphere in your department by not even noticing that you are demanding loyalty from your employees without awarding it to them.

Let us remind ourselves that James in accusing Jack of making Olga leave is not wanting to be “evil” or without values. Indeed, James has some big values in mind: i.e., protecting the team and generating a positive atmosphere in which employees want to stay. The only problem is that he did not really think through how to reach this goal. He did not reflect that using one piece of hearsay would weaken the culture of loyalty not strengthen it.

How can the manager share moral awareness? Openly disclosing in meetings where moral positions are at stake and putting them up for discussion. Making their own personal struggle explicit. Pointing out that in many cases there is no clear black or white and trying to penetrate the grey area together with the employees. Admitting mistakes, while at the same time honoring the moral behavior of employees. All of which contribute to making moral behavior a general benchmark.

How do researchers measure that employees become more moral if they work for a humble manager? This is done by the employees first assessing the moral humility of the manager with numerous questions from scales such as one shown in Chapter 2. To remind you: These questions measure humble behaviors and do not use the word “humility.”

Next, the readiness to behave unethically is evaluated. This is done by the employees themselves as well as by the manager. What is unethical behavior? Examples are the “slightly incorrect” filing of travel expense reports (the word “slightly” is just one of those self-protective judgments that people like to use when violating rules) and the nicking of company property (“nicking” is used as the friendly face of stealing).

The result? The more humility employees perceive in the manager, the less questionable moral behavior is found among them as a whole group. Are all influenced equally? No. When researchers dug more deeply into the numbers, a relevant distinction came up: There is a greater “contagion effect” of a humble manager to those employees that have internalized the topic of humility. There will always be a lesser impact of a humble leader on hardened, egocentric employees than on those who are practicing the virtue of humility themselves.43

One final point as to how humble leaders support their employees in living their own as well as the company values. By providing a calm atmosphere and reducing stress. Take this story as an inspiration.

In a seminar for priests to be, the young men are asked to give a lecture on the parable of the Good Samaritan.44 You remember it? Being the only one to help a man, nearly beaten to death by robbers. The aspiring candidates were given days in advance to prepare their material. On the day of the lecture, however, far earlier than expected, each speaker is called up: “Quick, get over there. The audience is already waiting. Hurry up!”

The agitated individual hurries from the room through a long connecting corridor to the lecture hall. In this situation, not a single person, NOT a single one, helps the elderly man, who is lying ill on the floor. All of a sudden, compassion and helpfulness among these future priests has evaporated. Why? Because they are under pressure and – whoosh – their values fly out the window.

Things are different for the second group. No “supervisor” puts pressure on them. No hectic, no panic. Leisurely they stroll towards the lecture theatre and immediately the good Samaritan appears in every single candidate priest. In emotional calm, there is ample room for compassion and helpfulness.

Think back to situations where your manager put pressure on you? How did you manage to make sure that you kept your values intact? Research shows, that 50–88 percent of employees misreport if their superior tells them to.45 What dangerous power to have, and what danger of wielding it without noticing.

How can you make sure that you do not accidentally put pressure on your employees that causes them to forego their own values? Learn to be humble.

Take-Away: Humility strengthens ethical behavior – By exemplifying one's own values and composure, the leader’s humilitas achieves greater employee adherence to moral and ethical principles.

More Acceptance of Differentiating Behavior

Not every employee needs the same approach from their manager, the same type of task, the same type of control, the same type of support or feedback. Instead, there are considerable differences that every manager easily recognizes. Yet, if he or she then decides to act in a differentiating manner, a sad consequence ensues. Often employees perceive this adaptive behavior in a highly critical manner. “Why does X get feedback again and I only get to see the boss every six months?” “How come Y gets to work with a coach and I don't?” How easy then for employees to become demotivated or for the manager to desperately try to treat all employees equally. Clearly neither is healthy for the company.

Research shows the solution: since the humble manager is obviously aware of his or her own strengths and weaknesses and at the same time pays honest and open tribute to others, it is accepted that the manager can see exactly what each employee needs. Instead of alienating the followers, the differentiating behavior of a humble manager actually strengthens the identification with him or her. You can see this in Figure 4.5.

Figure 4.5 Identification with a humble leader.

Thus, differentiating behavior is no longer a question of alleged injustice, but represents instead a valuable individualized measure of attention for each employee.

Consequently, actions that follow this differentiating perception are perceived positively. In other words, it is no longer unfair or selfish if the manager spends more time with one employee. It is simply okay – if one team member is helped indirectly, we are all better off. Attention then is not regarded negatively as unfair treatment.46

This also has something to do with pragmatic leadership. Leadership is not generally about lofty goals and visions, but often merely about each employee wanting to be seen in his or her individual situation. This applies to working hours, the way of working, the opportunities for further development as well as feedback to individual ideas.

Many managers shy away from differentiating treatment in this respect, because they fear both a loss of control and that they will be perceived as unfair. But it is clearly “unfair” if, for example, only extroverts speak at meetings. No matter how many times the manager may ask, the introverts will not necessarily learn to speak up. But outside the meeting, it is then every manager's duty to follow up. In such a case, it would be a misjudgment yet a real danger that extrovert employees see this follow-up as an unfair favoring of their colleagues. With a humble manager this does not happens.

In this way, research can dispel the fear that this type of differentiating treatment is seen as an undifferentiated catastrophe. As long as the process for differential treatment is fair, dealing with each person individually leads to greater satisfaction, better performance and even more willingness to help others within the department or company.47 So once again, humility can be seen as a stroke of luck for the overall company climate.

Take-Away: Humility awards freedom – Humble manager are allowed to behave in a differentiating manner. They are therefore free to respond to their employees in the way that is best for both sides. They do not have to worry that this differentiated treatment will affect the climate in the team negatively.

Better Top Management Teams

There is a basic amount of power, and if it belongs to only one, the others have nothing but powerlessness. Therefore, they do not become involved in action. I cannot increase the amount of power - at least in my understanding. But I can distribute it differently. In this way I empower others, who are also more strongly called upon to act. Encourage and challenge.

(Interview No. 2, Head HR, 10,000 employees)

Decisions taken by several people who act as equals are measurably better.

(Interview No. 10, Deputy Head IT, 1,000 employees)

Not surprisingly, humility also has a positive effect at the highest level. A humble CEO succeeds in getting the top management team to work better and more effectively. The proof?

An analysis of 63 CEOs and 328 members of top management teams: Humble CEOs saw more strengths than weaknesses in their top managers. Their mindset of appreciating others helps them see the good in everyone and be aware of where they themselves may need support. As a result of this positive assessment, they were far happier to work with them, empower them and take decisions together in comparison to those leaders that always pick on others overlooking the beam in their own eye.48 The consequence is a greater integration of the top team, which in turn has a measurable effect on the middle management. Middle management then has more confidence in the culture, becomes more engaged, feels more emotionally connected and performs better.

This contagion starts from the top: If the CEO behaves humbly, this has a positive effect on the top team. The cascade then goes down to the middle level, which in turn motivates the next level.

Humility has a second effect on top teams. In general, humble managers do not keep the leadership solely in their hands. Instead, they follow the concept of shared leadership, where everyone in the team, depending on the situation and the skills needed, can step up to be the driving and guiding force. Not surprisingly, this type of leadership is the recommended method to lead in agile work. Being prepared to let go of your own leadership role is just as important in this structure as taking on the role of a leader.

Sam Rayburn, former White House spokesman, recognized this years ago. “You cannot be a leader, and ask other people to follow you, unless you know how to follow, too.”

The humility of the leader is the key here for this to happen: Since the leader is willing a) to lead where it makes sense, b) to admit weaknesses where this is important and c) to be open to the suggestions of others, team members have the courage to take the lead in those situations where their own strengths are of use.49 And the manager will acknowledge this and not insist on his or her own status, but instead award the necessary trust to the individual board members. The same works on lower levels – in this case the manager will allow employees to take over the rein, where it is for the best of the department or company, without trying to keep power as a protective measure for the ego.

Trust, of course, plays a large role here.

Humility is definitely a source of trust among employees. Especially the aspect of recognizing one's own shortcomings. It is helpful for the employee if managers can really see themselves. There is then a high probability that employee participation will increase.

(Interview No. 5, Head Corporate Academy, 300,000 employees)

This is the secret recipe for humility: It is not merely about cognitive-based trust, but also about the emotional trust, that is generated by the humble manager sharing power, putting him- or herself on an equal footing, admitting to weaknesses.50 It works for normal teams, but is even more impactful for the whole company.

Take-Away Humility strengthens top management – Employees not only look to their direct manager, but also to the top team. This is strengthened by a humble leader and consequently works more effectively.

Summary: Seven Measurable Benefits of Humility for Employees

The humility of the manager has seven positive effects on employees: they perform better as individuals and in teams, can better manage their own emotions, are more creative and act more morally. Empowerment, psychological security and self-efficacy play a major role here. In addition, employees are willing to accept differentiating behavior, which in turn gives the manager more freedom in how to act. And with a humble CEO, the top management team becomes better, which is reflected in the entire corporate culture.

Benefits of Humility for the Organization

In my former company we found a link between emotional intelligence, humility and the number of accidents happening. The humbler the manager the fewer accidents. Why? It had something to do with situational awareness as well as the willingness to receive feedback.

(Interview No. 69, Global Director L&D, 86,000 employees)

It was a market already long divided between large competitors, when in 1989 30-year-old Indian born Sunny George Verghese was tasked by his company to set up an export company from Nigeria. In order to grow and be successful, a special culture was needed. Verghese puts it like this: “We did not have the capacity to take on big risks, so instead took up a multiplicity of small risks. In such an environment, the leaders had to own up to their mistakes, and these become learnings for the organization as a whole.”

Meetings at the company therefore always start with a discussion of what went wrong and Verghese was each time the first to do so: “As a leader, I had to set the tone and pace of the meeting, so I always started with the wrong calls, I had made.” One of the employees added: “If you make a mistake and learn from it you can double, triple or even quadruple your performance. This encourages risk-taking in the organization, and leads to high quality results.”51

The result? 23 years later, Olam International is a company with more than $24 bn revenue and more than 40,000 employees worldwide. The CEO receives accolades on employment sites such as glassdoor – 88 percent of 521 employees give him a highly positive evaluation.52 Everyone who knows how bad glassdoor ratings typically are, will appreciate this.

Let us look what humility can bring to organizations. The famous English historian Edward Gibbon wrote about Rome: “But the decline of Rome was the natural and inevitable effect of immoderate greatness.”53 “Immoderate greatness,” what a great phrase, which many feel can be aptly applied to a number of companies. Some have publicly fallen heavily from power. WeWork, Theranos and Enron to name but a few.

So, the time has come to see if humility will not only save kingdoms, but also companies from ruin.

I want to mention one key benefit upfront. Firstly, because it is vital one, and secondly because the paper about it is unluckily still in the publication process and I cannot go into as much detail as I would wish. Yet, I can quote Professor Amy Ou directly. She is one of the leading researchers in the field of humble leadership worldwide and has found:

The most exciting advancement in my humility research is that we can now measure CEO humility from archival data and find that CEO humility in US public companies indeed has a positive effect on return on assets. Humility clearly seems to add to the “business case” for companies.

This is, of course, a good indication for a positive effect of humility directly on bottom line results. Once the paper is published and others along these lines, I will share them via the different channels. Till then, there are enough other positive results to look at.

More Successful Strategy

It is not at first glance intuitive that humility should lead to better strategies. However, research shows this for three elements: The type of strategy, the communication of strategy and, last but not least, the implementation of strategy.

The Type of Strategy

Looking at strategy, there is consensus that our age requires more from companies. That they are able to “milk” existing businesses for revenue while at the same time investing into new products, processes and business models. This is the well-known balancing act between “exploiting” and “exploring.” Companies must succeed in optimizing the old while at the same time initiating the new.

Is this always accepted easily within a company? We all know that a sales department does not rejoice when an online unit is set up; nor are employees in a factory delighted when a platform is founded to sell not only their own but also their competitors’ products. There are also few that are enthusiastic when a new unit is set up for AI or machine learning and is given better premises and more favorable terms than the existing ones.

The solution to this dilemma is the so-called ambidextrous organization. This means that the management focuses equally on the old as well as the new business. The metaphor is one of using both hands equally. For this job the CEO is predestined, while needing the top management team for the execution and support.

Top management teams, however, are typically organized along responsibilities based on the existing lines: old versus new. This can lead to considerable rivalry, a willingness to fight for one’s own area only and to miss out on seeing the bigger picture.

What does the humble leader bring to this situation? Measurably greater success in terms of an effective ambidextrous organization.54

That sounds good, but the question is, how do humble leaders do it?

As seen above, humble behavior helps achieve integration of the top management team. The CEOs neither dictate nor compete with their top managers. Instead, he or she generates a feeling of working in unison with all pursuing a common goal. Equally, the humble CEO shares leadership and the responsibility that comes with it. And if as a member of the Top Team you are suddenly jointly responsible for the bigger picture, the world begins to look very different. Why would a new field of business then be regarded as a self-cannibalization that must be challenged? Instead, the new unit becomes a desirable necessity to ensure the future of the whole company. In such an atmosphere, also any new business unit learns to appreciate and support the old more easily.

What else does the humble CEO do, statistically speaking? Watch out for the money. Not in the sense of saving it, but rather looking out to where it goes: In companies run by humble leaders, there is a measurably smaller difference between the salary of the CEO and that of the other board members. Why? A humble manager who does not see the need to feed his ego will not need the status of a greatly differing salary.

This goes hand in hand with a generally reduced focus on status symbols.

Humility also means approaching the subject of status with a cheerful serenity. Show a greater suppleness, a light-hearted calm when dealing with conventional status symbols – especially in the office context. Who has what parking space, what car, what mobile phone? Just because I could do it, I do not have to do it. Rather I embed it in the context and think about the effect my status symbols create.

(Interview No. 2, Head of Talent Management, 10,000 employees)

The smaller gap in salary strengthens the feeling in the board members or directors of being at the top of the company together, of being truly one amongst equals.

This is another nudge towards thinking in larger dimensions.

Interestingly, there is also solid evidence that humble CEOs also invest more in R&D. Maybe knowing that they do not know everything, they try to make sure that nothing big is missed. They also focus more on the bigger picture, in this case on corporate social performance55, such as donations to the community, recycling within the company or special efforts in terms of safety. Unsurprisingly, companies with humble CEOs have more measurable success in these areas and are far more active in the wider stakeholder community. They also focus more on diversity, employee relations, environmental impact and product safety/quality.

Research confirms that these efforts do not lead to lower corporate results. This seems to imply that focusing on the bigger picture does no harm, but is simply an additional goal. And as we have seen above: the higher the aims, the better the performance.

Ergo: Don't fear CSR – it doesn’t replace profitability, but complements it.

Last but not least, humility helps managers to not overestimate their own strategy and underestimate competitors. In his classic book on innovation, researcher Clayton M. Christensen warned against all those leaders who tend to overestimate their own perspectives “they overlook fundamental market movements and rush towards the abyss.”56 Like the Roman empire. That egotistical blindness happens far less often to companies led by humble leaders.

Communication of Strategy

There is a second level at which a humble CEO helps the strategy to succeed. And that has something to do with communication. First of all, we must make the following very clear: Research shows that a humble CEO is in no way wishy-washy, overmodest or overcautious. Instead, they are self-confident and have a firm belief in their vision, in themselves and their people.

Professor Bradley Owens, one of the founders of humility research makes this clear: “Indeed, we have found that the humble leader bases his or her trust on the importance of the vision and on their ability to guide the collective in realizing that vision.”57

Initially I trust everyone.

(Interview No. 56, CEO, 700 employees)

Since the humble leader does not assume that he or she can handle everything on their own, they try to communicate the vision so clearly that employees and colleagues feel safe in it and are carried along. And we all know what happens within ourselves when we understand what exactly the goal is and how our own efforts contribute to it. The buy-in is much stronger, the willingness to take unusual paths grows, so that the whole organization is much more clearly moving in one direction together.

In addition, the humble manager can deal with it if the communication does not initially meet with approval.

There was a difficult situation in which it was necessary to shift staff to a new structure. Emotions were running high, people were ranting and raving about me as a manager. It was important for me to let this happen without being upset at the people. I had to allow their emotions to run through me without complaining. And also, to take responsibility. It is not the board of directors, which is far away, that has decided, but it is my decision. At the end of the day, it turned out well because we stayed “in touch”. It is all a part of understanding others, accepting them, going to the hotspots and not weighing every word on the gold scale.

(Interview No. 11, Head L&D, 300,000 employees)

Why does this kind of clear and brave communication happen so rarely? Because many managers are trapped in their own perspective and therefore underestimate how much clarity and repetition, how much time, how much venting of anger people need in order to really be on board.

In addition, they give little room and time for employees to really think through and take responsibility for understanding what the strategy or the change initiative means for themselves. Instead, many managers are easily annoyed when they have the feeling that they are once again not understood. They quickly see this as sabotage or unwillingness – while it is they that are at fault. The important thing to remember is that only the receiver can actually judge how effective the communication has been. And for the sender to understand how much the receiver has understood, there is the amazingly simple tool of asking, what has been understood.

We all know, that most managers are not ill-meaning when they communicate too little or unclearly. As we will see later, making sure, that what we say really reaches the receiver, is not as simple as it seems – but requires focus and concerted effort.

Humble managers are more easily aware of this potential pitfall and keep on communicating, communicating, communicating. Or at least hiring heads of communication. The right ones!

There are two options: You can pick a communications director who tells you that you're great, or you can pick one who says: “My impression is that the company needs the following…. I know you have everything clear in your mind's eye. But not everyone understands it. And my suggestion would be ...” The latter constellation is the most successful. As in many other relationships, it fits well if it is complementary and each has a good eye for the other's strengths and weaknesses. If the constellation is different, if the crucial advisory functions are set up like-for-like, the consequence is almost always narcissistic isolation or group thinking. That is when humility is missing.

(Interview No. 5, Head Corporate Academy, 300,000 employees)

This brings us already to the last reason why humble CEOs have a more successful strategy.

The Implementation

Humble leaders also ensure better implementation of strategies. Why?

Change requires the commitment of the entire organization and the willingness to change old processes and behavioral patterns. If the impulse for this only comes from the CEO, this typically leads to reluctance and a failure to implement changes. But if I am an employee of a humble leader, this has a statistically relevant effect on my willingness as an employee to not only initiate and support changes and improvements but also to take charge of getting them implemented in my area.58

By seeing themselves as part of a whole, employees are more likely to understand their role in this bigger picture, have a feeling for what their part might be and what influence each of them has. This room for influence, however small, is very important.

(Interview No. 7, VP Training, 22,000 employees)

The reasons for this can be found in the fact that a humble leader inspires an increased sense of being part of something bigger, as well as of self-esteem in employees in relation to their organization. They feel more called upon and more empowered to take things into their own hands.

As we know, self-initiative is worth its weight in gold. And it is not hard for a humble manager to deal with the fact that yet another idea has come not from him or her but actually from the team. Precisely, this is what humble managers relish: that others take action and they can learn from their ideas and strengths. To illustrate further, how humility empowers strategy implementation, I want to share an example from military history.

Humility at the military – the example of Moltke

Little humility is expected in the military. Not amongst soldiers, and even less amongst the higher echelons. Was Napoleon humble? No, he thought of himself as a genius and was sure that after his great successes in Europe any other conquest would be a piece of cake. Not aware of Russia’s strength, not aware of the weaknesses of his military planning, he embarked on his invasion of Russia. Worse still, he actually planned almost every action of his troops in advance, forgetting that on the battle field after the first shot is fired there is hardly a detailed plan or concise strategy that can be executed. The result: a defeated and sorely decimated army limping back to France, with the Russians celebrating their victory and commemorating it forever in Tchaikovsky’s rousing overture 1812.

Helmuth Karl Bernhard von Moltke, born in Parchim in 1800 on the other hand was an epitome of humble leadership. He recognized a fundamental weakness in the wartime planning of his age. He put it succinctly: “No operational plan reaches beyond the first encounter with the enemy main force with any degree of certainty.”

From this he deduced for himself and for the Prussian army the new principle of mission command. In other words, he recognized the weaknesses of plans while appreciating others for being able to make good decisions in any situation. Of course, this only held true when they were clear about what the precise objective was and they had learned to use their own brains

What distinguishes mission command from the former way of waging war? In the past, as with Napoleon, there had been concrete orders. “Conquer this hill there by taking position here, break out towards the forest here, take the bridge on the north side...” This order was to be obeyed. No matter what happened. Irrespective of the fact that on the Big Day the envisaged position was occupied by enemy troops or that the bridge on the north side was suddenly the most heavily guarded. Circumstances were not relevant. Full stop. The officer as well as the common soldier simply had to obey. They did not need to know about or truly understand the bigger picture. They needed to follow orders, not have freedom of choice in how to act. The brilliant commander had surely foreseen everything.

With von Moltke, something new, called “thinking obedience” entered the scene. There were no longer orders, but, instead, missions and assignments. For each mission, the larger goal was communicated “You must conquer this hill. If you fail, there is the danger that our whole left front will crumble.”

As a result, every soldier had his mission and knew his place on the larger map. Exactly how he carried out his mission was left to each officer and soldier to decide.

Von Moltke was also a strong advocate of options, none of which in themselves were better than the other – but all possible and therefore all needed to be dealt with. He thought through countless scenarios and tried to protect against the worst. At the same time, he was always ready to spontaneously exploit positive situations.

Others, he awarded room to decide for themselves, while gently pointing to the danger of the ego. Thus, he wrote to Major General Leonhard von Blumenthal:

You will be a better judge there on the spot than I am able to judge from here; I only wish to warn you not to let yourself be dragged away to a fight under any circumstances. It is, of course, much easier to advise resistance at all costs than to dodge, however necessary.

He himself remained calm and untouched throughout his wars. Since he never expected these plans to work out, he was never disappointed or dismayed.

The time he took to reflect and not judge caused him to be nicknamed “the great silent one, the only officer who could be silent in seven languages.”

No wonder that many a CEO has adopted this way of thinking: Giving a view of the big picture, handing out missions and fostering independent thinking within agreed parameters. The most famous of von Moltke’s adherents is probably Jack Welch, who held him in such high esteem that he quoted him in several speeches and dedicated himself to the concept: “Not expect a plan of operations to survive beyond the first contact with the enemy. Set only the broadest of objectives and emphasize seizing unforeseen opportunities as they arise.”59

This awareness of moving targets, this realization that something like thinking obedience within a larger framework can exist, helps not only the managers but also the employees to successfully implement strategies.

One last point as to why humble leaders have better implementation rates: they involve more of their employees already in the initial, in the idea-finding process without relinquishing their power to make decisions. That is, they get more buy-in for any change by involving more upfront.

A manager came into a new department and had a pile of instructions to change things. He took almost a year to listen, ask questions and analyse. At this point the employees were actually beginning to be dissatisfied because they wanted strong leadership in a predefined direction. Finally, in several workshops together with these already impatient employees, a new direction was shaped. Since by now, everyone felt so well integrated, they were ready to support all major changes.

Of course, the manager could have implemented the change differently, but without this positive effect. He was clearly aware that he could learn from his colleagues and his employees. If the people had not been involved and had not been part in the reorganization, there would have been massive pain and protests. Through the involvement, through this very appreciative process, the people involved were now ready to be taken on board.

(Interview No. 10, Deputy Head IT, 1,000 employees)

Viewed from a purely statistical perspective, such behavior increases the probability of good implementation. Everyone who has read that up to 90 percent of all implementations fail should take this to heart. Even if you are less sceptical as regards the implementation rate – humility as a condition for successful implementation will always come considerably cheaper than management consultants entering the scene after a failed attempt of implementing!

Take-Away: Humility leads to a more successful strategy – In times of ambidexterity, a humble leader can better exploit opportunities together with the top team. The humble leader communicates their vision more clearly, gets more employee buy-in, and achieves better strategy execution.

Better Culture

That which is exemplified by the leader is given back: what goes around comes around. Humility brings solidarity and cooperation.

(Interview No. 4, VP Digital Marketing, 50,000 employees)

Where egos predominate, a positive culture is hard to maintain. Insults, vanities, rivalries, flattery, sensitivities will be rampant.

Consider instead, the advantages of a culture in which managers and consequently many of the employees adopt an attitude of humility. This will have a huge impact on two elements of culture. On how to cooperate and how to deal with errors.

Let us look at the culture of cooperation first: A survey of 105 American companies showed that CEOs who bring humility to the company manage to create a strong culture of cooperation.60 Why? The CEO enables employees to see the bigger picture – as a consequence most realize that they have to look beyond their own noses. The practical result of this is a measurable increase of willingness to help others.61

In addition, if I know what it is that I can and in particular all that I cannot do, this will make me gentler and more understanding towards the inabilities or weaknesses of others. Gone the judgment of “Oh, my God, why does he still not get it?” or “Of course, she is late again with the presentation”. Instead: “I wonder how I may help” and “That is quite a difficult project to deal with.”

The other result of humility is that employees are more willing to share their knowledge.

In many companies, knowledge is held back, either consciously to have more power or unconsciously because there is little interest in or concern for the needs of others. What is often missing is the open and generous culture that encourages employees to proactively approach peers or employees to share their knowledge or ask what may be needed.

In companies where there are fewer fights between employees or within the group of managers; in companies where from the top down ego plays less of a role; in companies where employees speak openly about their own errors and those of the managers without losing respect; in a company with a humble culture: there is measurably more knowledge shared.62 These companies sport a culture where seekers who are willing to learn and who realize that they do not know everything, meet those who know, and who feel appreciated for their knowledge.

This positive contagion most easily starts from the top.

In my career I had a Mexican boss called Antonio. He led a team session about humility and explained how by the age of 58 he had truly understood that a team can only be successful if everyone shows humility, asks for help and is at the same time looking out for where their help is needed. Now 15 years have passed and I am known in the company as someone who always looks out for anyone who may be struggling – and then I help and assist even far outside my official role. This is better for the company and the teams. I have learnt that I am not here for myself but for our greater good and our greater success.

(Interview No.101, VP HR, 1,000 employees)

This shows once again, how important it is for leaders to bring their assumptions, their beliefs, their core values out into the open. Only then can you be sure that the whole team is on board.

Janina Kugel, former board member of Siemens, sums up her beliefs in a similar vein:

It is nonsense to make this claim at all (of knowing everything). Changes happen so fast today, there is an incredible amount of information. It is utopian to assume that one individual can collect and evaluate all the factors relevant to decision-making. So, you have to involve in advance all people whose knowledge and experience can contribute to the decision-making process. And if a factor is still not taken into account, adjustments are made afterwards. In agile contexts, it is perfectly normal to say: “We'll try it out now, and if it doesn't fit, we'll go back a bit. This works for smaller and larger decisions. As long as you communicate clearly about it, it's perfectly ok.”63

This quote is the perfect switch to the second huge impact humility has on a company culture which has to do with dealing with errors, failures and weaknesses.

I went to our Chinese factory. They asked if I would do anything different. I mentioned one thing, that did not seem to work as well as I knew it could. By the next morning it was implemented.

(Interview No.137, former Board Member, 4,500 employees)

We have all heard dozens of times that failing fast is good. Unless you run nuclear power stations, of course. But even in nuclear power stations it is vital, that errors are not hidden but brought into the open and that employees truly and honestly communicate their mistakes.

This seems to be a difficult thing: research shows that errors are only shared reluctantly and at most in small groups behind closed doors.64

What can a manager do to change this? Clearly setting an example by admitting errors, weaknesses and misjudgments. If done regularly this behavior slowly but surely becomes contagious as we saw with Olam.

Research confirms this: A study of 161 teams shows that problems and misjudgments are more admitted to and discussed if the manager is acting as a role model of humility.65 Why? The humility of the leader results in every side having “a voice.” The manager’s humility inspires greater team humility, which leads to a stronger focus on the common goal and measurably better team performance. In addition, this results in employees gaining the courage to address deeply unpleasant issues such as mistakes.66

Remember psychological safety? And the fact that employees in a humble culture identify themselves more with the larger goals of the organization? Both effects of humility come together to encourage employees to be more outspoken about their own errors as well as what errors they see in the organization at large.

One of the key figures in this area of research is Amy Edmondson, the Harvard professor who developed the concept of psychological safety.

To her one of the core problems has nothing to do with intentional deviations from established rules. Instead, she points her finger at the key hindrance for a good culture when it comes to all those errors that are made as a result of lack of clarity, pure inattention, overwork, bad structure etc.:

When I ask executives to […] estimate how many of the failures in their organizations are truly blameworthy, their answers are usually in single digits - perhaps 2% to 5%. But when I ask how many are treated as blameworthy, they say (after a pause or a laugh) 70% to 90%.67

These 70 to 90 percent are bound to contain many errors and situations that would be useful to discuss. Errors, that should be considered and not condemned since they could point a way forward. Not discussing such errors and not drawing conclusions from them is a time bomb for the company.

Humble managers end up doing something about these time bombs. A number of interview partners reported on their version of “Fuck Up Nights.”

I have run three now in my department. And there have been these amazing moments that nearly moved me to tears when I see with what the seriousness, profundity and honesty people present their mistakes.

(Interview No. 122, Board Member, 2,000 employees)

We have adopted from Facebook the idea of the so-called “Courageous Penguin Awards”. This means that an executive frankly tells where something didn't work out at all in his or her career and gives an insight what may have been the reason. We often use the original charts from back then, which still radiate a lot of enthusiasm. We present this with a certain amount of humor and self-criticism in order to show what we do differently and hopefully better today.

(Interview No. 4, VP Digital Marketing, 50,000 employees)

Soon this open sharing becomes a normal habit.

When Alan Mulally, in 2006, moved from Boeing to Ford as CEO, there was a fear of admitting those problems for which there was no immediate solution. Alan gave a color code for his top managers to report on: Green meant everything was OK, yellow designated new problems and red were problems with no apparent solution. For weeks on end all reports were a delightful green, which Mulally commented on with astonishment: The loss of $17 billion had to come from somewhere. Then one day it happened: Mark Fields, Ford’s president of the Americas had a problem with the new Ford Edge. The tailgate drive simply was not working. He halted all production and put a red flag into his presentation. All eyes went to the floor, fear went through the room. Then Mulally started clapping. And step by step, over the coming weeks, the other executives opened up, and were willing to talk about those problems, for which they had no easy solution. As a result of these and other changes, Ford managed to get back into black figure from 2009 onwards.68

Initially it may not be intuitive that the more errors are being reported, the safer and the better the organization is. Instead, one suspects that where more errors are talked about, there simply will be more errors happening than elsewhere.

Yet, here again psychological safety comes into play. When I feel safe in a meeting, when I believe that my head will not be ripped off, then and only then will I report errors. Others may then learn from them, and the number of total errors will sink. On the other hand, if I am afraid, there will be fewer official errors, less that can be learned from and as a consequence the number of errors will remain high.

One interview partner described the amazing consequence of such an oppressive culture:

In one of our markets we discovered why there never were any accidents recorded. The reason was, that we had a communicated aim of a zero-accident statistic – so if an accident happened, the person was fired and the report remained unblemished.

(Interview No. 101, VP HR, 1,000 employees)

So, in humble organizations there exists a better handling of errors which is both ritualized as well as spontaneous.

There are two other cultural elements to mention: The emotional climate in teams led by humble leaders is better. Studies show that people who are not humble tend to overreact in conflicts, withdraw and plan revenge.69 They are also less generous and more concerned with their own advantage.

This is not the case in companies with a culture of humility: here emotions do not boil over in such a way that employees are offended. Instead, the whole atmosphere becomes warmer and more generous as regards handing out resources, attention as well as knowledge.

In addition, a culture that looks at the bigger picture and pays tribute to everyone in the company gives rise to a stronger sense of gratitude among employees. And on gratitude, research is manifold and very clear.70 People who feel grateful are healthier, see themselves less as a victim and most importantly are more resilient than those who do not reflect on what one can be grateful for.

Take-Away: Humility creates a better culture – The humble leader creates a culture in which mistakes can be talked about openly. The emotional climate is better, and the connection to the big picture creates a sense of gratitude, which in turn leads to increased cooperation.

Greater Willingness to Take on Responsibility

Some of the managers like to delegate all their thinking about leadership to the Learning and Development department. “Tell us how we can lead in this hybrid world.” Of course, we are willing to help. But at the same time, it is as if they are holding back their own intelligence and not really taking responsibility for their role.

(Interview No. 88, Head of EMEA Learning, 100,000 employees)

It is like the spiderman saying “with great power comes great responsibility.” Yet not all our employees or managers are really taking on full responsibility for their role and their activities.

(Interview No. 55, Director Global Learning, 34,000 employees)

In the more than 170 interviews I held, I not only asked about humility but also about what the biggest delta was in the company culture right now, compared to what it should be like ideally.

Unprompted 20 percent of the CEOs and top managers stated that there was a lack of people taking real responsibility for what they are doing. Instead, they saw employees and managers alike, leaning back comfortably and passing the buck.

I would like to share a personal story on this, where I myself was guilty of such an evasion of responsibility.

At the management consultancy I was working at after my studies, the project leader or the partner would always look over the slides for the steering committee. Initially, I was quite happy to take the easy way out. Which was not really thinking through my slides as if I personally would need to present them to the board of the client the next morning. Instead, I stopped short and mentally as well as in real life shifted the burden for clarity and useability of the slides to the people higher up the line.

It took some time until I adjusted and learned to put myself into the shoes of the person actually presenting.

Typical examples of not taking full responsibility are the following:

Observing bad processes – and not doing anything about them, since it is out of the official scope of one’s job description.
Running a project – and not alerting others to the fact that resources, budget, manpower is missing.
Failing to influence – not thinking through what stakeholders need to be on board, not investing energy in influencing them and then blaming these stakeholders for the failure of the project.
Hogging resources – and not giving them up for the good of the company.
And, what I had done: preparing a presentation – yet not as if one had to hold it oneself thereby mentally delegating the final responsibility for the content to the presenter.

How can you nudge people into taking more responsibility and not wait for the coin to drop?

Trainer and author Jack Malcom retells the following story.71 When Henry Kissinger became President Nixon’s National Security Adviser in 1969, he brought a reputation as a demanding taskmaster. So, when a staffer was given an assignment to write a position paper for his new boss, he understandably put in extra hours and took great pains to produce top-notch work. A day after he had turned it in, Kissinger called him into his office and asked him, “Is this the best you can do?”

The staffer promised he could improve it, and went back to his desk. He canceled his other appointments, called his wife to tell her he would be home very late and worked feverishly on the report: fine-tuning it, adding information, clarifying where necessary etc. At the end of the week, he turned it in. On Monday morning, Kissinger passed by and contemptuously dropped the report on his desk. In a loud voice, he again asked, “Is this the best you can do?”

Stunned and not a little frustrated, the staffer vowed to do even better, and redoubled his efforts from the previous week, putting even longer hours and finding even better ways to express and support his position. Two days later, convinced that it was as close to perfect as he could get it, he turned it in again. The next day, Kissinger again asked, “Is this really the best you can do?” The young man had had enough, so he looked at Kissinger squarely and said, “Yes, that is the best I can do.”

Kissinger replied, “Good. Now I will read it.”

Often employees need to be nudged into actually giving their best. And this does not mean that you have to ignore the 80:20 rule. But more often than not, the 80 percent ends up being a sad 50 percent.

Is a nudge as the one above a humble one? I would say yes. All I am doing as a leader is to get people to take the role that they have chosen seriously.

Equally, many managers shy away from their responsibility of leading people. Instead of following the image of an eagle who primarily soars and only rarely swoops, many managers love to spend large swathes of time down on the ground doing work that others in their team could to far better.

Why? Because for many this is easier than having to do something less immediately obvious. What could this be? Leading your people, removing stumbling blocks for them, advertising your department’s achievements internally, acquiring resources or buy-in from other stakeholders etc.

Interestingly, it is not too difficult to get managers to realize the value of leadership if they ask themselves the following question: If regularly, instead of being with clients or digging into a report I will spend time with my people listening to them, coaching them or removing obstacles out of their way, how much better will they become? The answer usually is somewhere around 5–10 percent. Which will have its impact in measurable business results.

I remember the head of a large corporate university using this type of argument to get buy-in from the board for his extensive program plans. He asked: “How much better will your managers become if they spend two weeks a year with the world’s best business school professors?” The board became enthusiastic and talked about 10, 15 or even 20 percent. Successfully having shifted the burden of proof, he took a modest approach: “Let us be very conservative and say only one percent. How much is that at our bottom line?” And since that was far more than the cost of the executive education programs he was allowed to go ahead just as he had wished.

Back from how managers can prove the value of leadership as to why humility in leaders increases the willingness of people to take on more responsibility, to be more an owner of their tasks.

On the one hand, as we have seen, employees of a humble leaders are more motivated and see more purpose in what they do. So, they will expend more effort on what they are doing.

On the other hand, the humble manager is also more aware of his or her duties. My research in Figure 4.6 shows that those managers that employees identified as humble get a much higher score of 1.53 – well in the golden zone of agree – for seeing their responsibility for their employees, compared to only 0.68 for unhumble managers. Expressed differently, a full 97 percent of managers seen as humble are truly aware of the responsibility towards their employees, versus a much lesser 72 percent of unhumble leaders.

Figure 4.6 Responsibility in humble managers.

The more I am aware of my responsibility, the more I will spend time doing what I have to do as a leader – the soaring instead of the swooping.

This finding was strengthened by a piece of research from 2022, when I specifically asked more than 200 participants worldwide to assess their leaders on both humility and responsibility. And those that were identified as humble took on far more responsibility than the unhumble ones. 83 percent of 100 in comparison to a miserly 62 percent (Figure 4.7).

Figure 4.7 Average level of responsibility taken.

And even within the humble zone, there was an increase in humility the humbler the manager. Remember that you can score up to an average of 5 across the 12 questions. Well, as you can see in Figure 4.8, a growth in humility significantly increases the taking on of responsibility.

Figure 4.8 Responsibility taken grows with humility.

The humble leader is also a role model in showing how not to give up easily, how to focus on those things that only he or she can do and that the bigger picture often clearly demands other things from us than can be found in our detailed job descriptions.

This last point is yet another reason why in a company with humble leaders and employees there will be more accountability. The look at the bigger picture makes clear that we are not victims but that it is up to us to choose how we can respond to different situations.

That means people will not look for someone to blame but instead take control of what we can control.

The authors of the fine book: The Oz Principle72 tell a great story along these lines about an employee who only needed a small nudge to become humble and take control.

In a case that recently came to our attention, a worker was assigned to the packaging department of a manufacturing plant. Not too long after arriving she detected serious problems with products coming from the production line. When she approached the manufacturing line supervisor to complain that “you guys are sending us too many defective products,” the line supervisor replied, “On this line there is no ‘you guys.’ You are as much a part of this line as we are.” From that point on she never said the words “you guys” again. Even more important, she started looking for solutions and stopped pointing fingers because she now understood that she would be accountable for not just her function but for the manufacturing plant’s final result.

Take-Away: From humility flows responsibility – In summary, if your company needs more accountability, humility is one of the tools with which to achieve it!

More Diversity

There is little need to sing the praises of diversity: We all know the research that proves that beyond being simply a matter of fairness diversity also has numerous positive effects.

To name a few:

Mixing employees with different educational backgrounds makes companies more innovative.73
Firms with higher female board representation exhibit higher overall performance, less “dodgy” earnings management, and less excessive risk taking.74
Corporations that embrace gender diversity on their executive teams were more competitive and 21 percent more likely to experience above-average profitability.75
Companies in the top quartile for gender or racial and ethnic diversity are 35 percent more likely to have financial returns above their national industry medians.
Teams with more cognitive diversity generate better results.76

On the other hand, there are also studies that show that age diversity leads to disagreements among teams and workforces, which results in less innovation77, or that there are more conflicts in diverse teams that lead to worse results.78

The key point is that diversity is strenuous. Humans are primed to prefer people that are similar to them. Even 11-month-old babies choose a cuddly toy wearing the same color clothes as they are wearing over those with a different color.79 Biologically, we are initially hard-wired to join groups that we feel similar to us and avoid or even reject those that seem strange. This goes down to the neuronal level: Even our brain perceives in-group members far more quickly than out-group members.80

So, we all need to spend time learning that different can be good and that it is rewarding to look beyond one’s own group. This is uncomfortable and strenuous.

One piece of research illustrates this well: Groups joined by outsiders felt less sure about the decisions they had made and were far more stressed than their peers that worked in homogenous groups. Yet! Yet these groups had actually made far better decisions than the others.81

Once we accept that diversity is strenuous, it becomes simply another task to work on. And this is the reason for why humble leaders and humble employees are good at fostering and reaping the benefits of diversity.

In addition, humble people hold fewer prejudices. Fewer prejudices towards different ethnicities, backgrounds, genders and mindset.82

Why? Because they know themselves better, see how small they are in the larger picture and consequently are more open towards others. They are also aware of when they may be approaching people with a bias in mind and are able to put this bias aside.

If you are not sure how you go about opening up to diversity, here is a suggestion. First you need to face what biases you have. For this you can use one or all of the Harvard Implicit Association tests. They measure how biased we are against gender, color, religion, weight, age, sexuality etc.

Typically, we all are biased towards our in-group and biased against any outgroup. But once you have done the test, seen the depressing results, it becomes quite a simple matter of training yourself to react differently.

This requires you to reflect on positive examples for the group that you may feel as an outgroup. If your gut feeling reacts strongly to lawyers in your company and you would rather eat a moldy apple than go and talk to the legal department about the contract you want to sign tomorrow (as a lawyer by training, I hope I am allowed to use this example…), then a humble person would recognize this initial aversion, quickly conjure up a positive example of a lawyer-manager interaction from the past, reflect on how their chosen role requires a solid and good contract and then go to the lawyer with an open mind. Does this take time? A little. A blink of an eye or the think of a neuron? Yes. Is it strenuous? Of course.

But a humble person does not shy away from something merely because it costs some neuronal activity.

It is therefore not surprising that research shows that humble people are much more willing to hear arguments from what may be termed “opponents” and are less judgmental afterwards.

You can call this intellectual or cultural humility – both result in companies that have more humble leaders and employees to not only be able to more strongly embrace diversity but also get it to work.

Getting it to work requires focus, structures as well as fostering an open mindedness in the team. This goes beyond recruiting and announcing diversity as a value. Actually, it takes a considerable effort to instill structures that allow us in everyday life to perceive others as a valuable resource and not as a threat to our ego. Regardless of their gender, ethnicity or educational background.

One great example for getting people to look beyond their own ingroup comes from a large British multinational.

They instituted an internal policy of “search and spin”. Everyone had the task to share their ideas/learnings/processes with others and was rewarded, if another team of department adopted one. What was even more powerful however was that the reward was considerably higher for any team that adopted something from another team!

(Interview No. 157, Chief Human Resources Officer, 600 employees)

What a great way to wake up our brain to stop seeing others as competition and more as a resource. It also helps to calm our ego from being attacked by others having had great ideas: if I benefit considerably by this then it is of value to my ego to do so.

Take-Away: Humility serves diversity – Humble leaders who are less biased themselves and help develop good practices for diversity in action are key if you want your organization to be more impactful when it comes to diversity.

Less Turnover

One of the costliest events in the day-to-day running of a company is losing existing employees and having to train new ones. The cost is not merely about direct recruitment costs, onboarding and training, but also about the costs of the previous termination, the effect of terminations on the other employees, as well as the increased probability of errors or unseen opportunities by the new, still inexperienced employee. Costs can easily rise to $30,000 per employee.83 Other researchers calculate the costs in terms of salary and estimate them at between 90 and 200 percent of the individual annual salary.84 It is therefore not surprising that some managers see retaining employees as one of their key tasks.

As we have already seen in the research results in Chapter 3, the turnover intention is less if I work for a humble leader. A study from 2020 found that this even holds true for overqualified staff.85 The reason: Humility in the manager leads to more appreciation, more encouragement, greater chances to contribute and less emotional exhaustion. There are therefore fewer redundancies in overqualified staff, in middle management86 and in “normal” employees.87 In a humble department as well as in a company, all groups can identify more with the whole, feel more secure and motivated. One of these studies even showed this effect of a higher retention rate in a very competitive market, where in general employees were hard to hold. Keeping even a few of them translated directly into competitive advantage.

Take-Away: Humility saves costs – Since fewer employees quit when a manager is humble, there are fewer costs for recruiting and training new employees. Existing customer relationships and processes are also less at risk.

Less Investment in Loss-Making Projects

One of the key elements of success for any company is that projects that are going well are completed effectively and that projects which do not show promise are stopped as soon as possible.

This sounds so simple that one sometimes wonders how this can be an issue for any sensible manager. Experience, however, shows that many projects that lose money are continued far longer than they rationally should be. Can you not think of at least one, right now at the top of your head?

For example, keeping offices open which have been loss-making for years, merely because the pain of closing them down appears too great. Or not admitting that product B has failed to deliver what it had promised and pushing away the decision of scrapping it.

Why is this so hard? Well, when is a manager ever thanked for declaring projects as failed? When are canceled projects celebrated or linked to bonuses?

This is our very human aversion to losses. Anyone who even only once has heard the theory of Nobel Prize winner Kahneman will agree: losses hurt far more than profits delight. The reason? A purely biological one. Gains are processed in the pre-frontal cortex, a sort of less emotional CEO of the brain, while losses are experienced through the amygdala. In evolutionary terms this is a far older part of the brain which triggers far stronger emotions.88

Especially managers with an uncontrolled ego find it difficult to consciously, cognitively act against these powerful emotions. This is because their ego is strongly linked to the project, especially if they themselves have initiated it. It is almost impossible for them to stop the project merely because it garners financial losses. Instead, they use (often unconsciously) all their brain power to continue standing up for their project.89 This can even lead to an escalation of public commitment for projects that are clearly doomed.

This is different for the humble leader. Why? He or she has their ego as well as their emotions under control. And the humble manager is aware of the bigger picture which may suffer from a bad project that is allowed to continue running. Due to this humility, writing off a project does not cause the manager to suffer emotionally, since neither their self-image nor their self-confidence is tied to any project. Instead, it is far easier for them to accept sunk costs and not continue investing in disastrous projects. Management thinker Adam Grant has numerous examples in his book Give and Take which show that the key danger for sunk costs and cutting your losses is a felt ego threat.90 Remove this via humility and it is considerably cheaper for the company!

By the way, Andrew Hafenbrack from Insead discovered in 2014 that if you mediate for 15 minutes, sunk costs also lose much of their pain point.91 The mediation focuses the mind on the here and now, away from past investments, and allows easier access to the bigger picture and the future. So adding meditation to humility will make your company even safer.

Take-Away: Humility avoids sunk costs – The humble leader's willingness to see the bigger picture as well as put his or her own ego into perspective avoids corporate disasters from projects that otherwise - without humility - are often continued despite high losses.

More Customer Loyalty

Humility also has a positive effect on the customers of the organization.

A fine study from 2019 shows this clearly.92 The research subject was a bank with more than 300,000 customers. The question was, who of their clients held their credit cards longer and which of these generated more revenue?

The clear finding was that those customers were far more loyal and spent more, when the bank – before contracting – had clearly pointed out the strengths AND weaknesses of their individual offers. While they did not call them strengths and weaknesses, they were very explicit calling them “benefits” and “trade-offs.” In the case of the trade-offs, it was stated that the option X cost $A more than this option Y; or that X carried a higher interest rate than Y.

The result: 20 percent fewer terminations with 10 percent more revenue when compared to those customers who had only received strength-related marketing. The outcomes were even better among those customers who already had several years of experience with credit cards. They terminated a full 33 percent less and made almost 20 percent more revenue.

A nice and lucrative example for showing how useful it is to shows strengths and weaknesses even to the customer.

Another example comes from Favi, a French company which produces die-cast copper alloys and among other things, gearboxes for car manufacturers. The company is regarded as one of the great role models for a self-managed organization. In these a culture of appreciation and humility prevails. There is no hierarchy, only organizational circles in which employees are free to take decisions themselves. The basic assumption is that everyone is good and motivated to do the best for the company and the customers. Errors are discussed openly and not hidden. Which again has a positive effect for the customer. Let us take just one example for this exemplary customer relationship.93

An employee at Favi discovered that a product he was making for Volkswagen had a problem of quality. He immediately stopped the machine and, together with a team, went through all the products already prepared for shipment. They found nothing, but were still worried.

So, without having to ask anyone, they took a company car and drove eight hours to Volkswagen in Germany to check whether any of the products that had already be delivered showed any defects. The quality manager at Volkswagen was astounded: Usually, the process of detecting and eliminating faults was paper-heavy, long and drawn-out. And here were two Favi employees working their way through the pallets all by themselves.

Such behavior naturally leads to good customer relations. The willingness to admit errors has a strengthening effect as experienced by Wilf Blackburn, when he apologized to the clients.

Since we have seen above that the humble leader encourages independent action among employees (remember the example from the Ritz), it is not surprising that humility leads to a better, more open and long-term customer relationship.

Take-Away: Humility binds customers – Customers appreciate not only the increased transparency that humility typically brings, but also the initiative that employees can take in such an atmosphere.

Summary

All in all, humility engenders seven positive effects for the company: a more successful ambidextrous implemented strategy, a greater culture for cooperation and dealing with errors, greater willingness of employees to take on responsibility, more diversity, less turn-over, less investment in loss-making projects and more customer loyalty.

Benefits of Humility for the Leader Themselves

I had been working at the management consultancy by now for four years. I enjoyed the work, while trying to keep my outside interests alive. As an avid reader, I always had a book with me and read the whole train ride, sometimes only emerging from the book world as I entered the lobby. One day, I chatted to a colleague about his habits when going into work. He said: “I use the train rides to think through my day, think through the meetings, what the client needs, what I need to clarify internally.” That shocked me to the core – here I had someone who truly took responsibility for his performance and who invested extra energy on ensuring that he could fulfill the role that he had chosen to take on to the best. Did this humble attitude make him successful? Well, not only was he made partner at the consulting firm but at the age of 44 he became board member of a company with 1,000 employees, a few years later he changed in the same role to one of 14,000.

Does it have a positive effect for the leader if he or she is humble? The story above provides anecdotal evidence. Let us look at the quantitative research.

Better Performance

Humility arms you against the danger of no longer perceiving challenges. Instead, you develop a better sense which situations require decisions or communication. To put it bluntly: if you are humble, it is possible to remain more human. Which humanity many people lose over the years.

(Interview No. 6, Head HR, 300,000 employees)

Humble leaders are more effective and bring in better results, even if you take out the element of luck.

(Interview No. 98, GM, 13,000 employees)

If you are humble, you are more charismatic and all stakeholders will like you. That will make it easier to influence others and be successful.

(Interview No. 158, Head – L&OD and Corporate HR, 70,000 employees)

If we let the ego go and go for real impact, we would be able to achieve so much!

(Interview No. 105, Director Global Knowledge, 196,000 employees)

Why should a humble manager perform better?

I suppose you can think of some arguments straight away: A manager who is open to feedback, who is willing to listen to others, cannot help but be more successful than someone who primarily sees what they want to see and is unwilling to accept input from the outside.

Research confirms this gut feeling with the concrete challenge of technical and adaptive change.

A brief foray into this subject: technical change is the “normal” change that every manager regularly encounters in their organization and which requires minor or major adjustments, that are often strenuous but which can be mastered with the existing know-how and within established procedures. This may be the construction of a new factory or extensive product modifications. Though needing good planning as well as resources, the change is easily mastered, albeit with effort, with the existing expertise in the company.

Adaptive change is altogether a different kettle of fish. This change is about problems or changed circumstances which require that habits, behavior patterns, structures and mindsets are adapted.

It is already clear from the definition that it is vitally important to recognize the difference between the two types of change. For one thing, if I get it wrong, I may be shooting at sparrows with cannons. Imagine, developing an enormous structural and behavioral change for a normal process such as the digitalization of customer data. However, I must also not underestimate the importance of a fundamental change, say in the business model. For this I should be fully aware of the fact that I may need a new culture or a new structure for agile working.

Research shows that a humble leader is far more able to a) see the distinction between the two types as well as b) master each of the changes.94 Why?

Much has to do with being open for new things and being able to take the view from the balcony.95 Let us look at one example from the early days of memory chips.

Memory chips had made Intel great. Yet over the years, Japanese competitors had managed not only to offer the same quality but also a much lower price.

Months went by in which Intel tried everything possible in the field of technological change: they built a new factory where production was cheaper; they tried to find a niche market; they aspired to develop a chip that was fundamentally better than their competitor’s. Nothing worked.

Then finally, in 1985, Andy Grove, the President, went “out onto the balcony.” He turned to his colleague CEO Gordon Moore (exactly, the Moore of Moore's law stipulating that the performance of computers and other technical equipment doubles about every 18 months) – he asked Moore the following question: “If we were kicked out and the board brought in a new CEO, what do you think he would do?”

Moore replied without hesitation: “He would get us out of the memory chips.”

To which Grove retorted, “Then why shouldn't you and I walk out the door and do the same?” And they did – they stopped the production of memory chips and shifted all their force to producing micro-processors.

Intel had the clear insight that they had reached their limits as regards technological change. Humbly they shifted into adaptive change.

What else speaks for a higher performance of the humble? Three additional studies:

One actually showed that those with lower mental abilities can make up for much of this lack of “hardware” through humility.96 Why? Because they are open to feedback, willing to learn and so strongly aware of their own weaknesses that they know they have to do something about them. This is a lovely consolation for those days when you may feel that you are not the smartest person on the planet – humility will definitely be a good pill to take.

Another piece of research shows the consequences of the opposite of humility. The more arrogant someone is, the lower their scores for mental capacity. Again, the question is why? Because arrogant people actually often have low self-esteem which they cover up with arrogance. They will also do anything and everything to avoid asking for help.97

Thirdly, humble leaders also become more innovative themselves. The state of humility creates a “quiet brain.” The ego is given less room and so the humble leader will be less offended by blind alleys, first misses or critical feedback. In a humble brain, new associations are easier and more likely. Consequently, research shows that those tasks that require broad and flexible thinking are better solved by people with a humble mindset.98

Humble leaders know that creative thinking and the resulting innovations are a combination of fearlessness, openness, plenty of observation, stubborn grit and ample sharing. And who is better suited for this than they, who bring the best people on board because they are not afraid of being outdone, who create networks even outside the company, who can consciously take time to think without immediately knowing an answer.

So, in a nutshell – a humble manager will perform better than an unhumble one.

Take-Away: Humility makes the leader more effective – The ego free view from the balcony allows the leader to learn more, act more effectively and be more creative.

More Perceived Leadership Potential and More Promotions
