Humility is also good for the reputation of the manager, because many people then want to join them.

(Interview No. 12, VP Change, 100,000 employees)

As we all know, it is often not possible to precisely measure the performance of a manager. Too seldom is clear data available on what can actually be attributed to the individual person of the manager and to his or her behavior.

What then is the key element of getting you promoted? Even if your company evaluates everyone according to some fixed leader competencies and does this by asking employees, peers, customers and bosses, a key factor in getting promoted will still be how the level above you perceives you.

Does this higher level see the potential in you that you could successfully fill a position above your present one?

The biggest fear that I encounter in discussion with executives about humility has exactly to do with this. Can I be humble and not appear weak? Will my bosses appreciate humble behavior in me or will they not prefer the more narcissistic Alpha colleague of mine? Remember the introduction in which we saw how many of us: investors and the press were quite keen on the narcissistic manager. Are bosses the same?

See at least one clear answer to this question in the following piece of research:99

The situation is as follows: An officer (a lieutenant = low officer rank) is in a military training exercise. The exercise is time-critical and the young officer is making a bundle of mistakes. Some of these are inevitable due to the situation, others could have been avoided. Among his people is an angry subordinate called Jones who repeatedly points out the mistakes and possible solutions. To be honest, he is less pointing them out, but rather cursing at the young officer. This person has the necessary knowledge and experience to help the young officer succeed and save the exercise. Now you see two different versions for a response.

Officer Number 1 says:

Sergeant Jones, there was nothing wrong with my plan. Anyone else would have made exactly the same decision as I did. I don't want your advice on how to command my platoon. Bring your soldiers here, let them bring the equipment or tools they need to repair, and do it now! I don't want to hear from you again until the problem is solved.

Officer Number 2 says:

Sergeant Jones, you are right. I am responsible and I made a bad decision. You have more experience in situations like this and I should have listened to you. Now I have the Commander breathing down my neck and you are the only one who can get us all out of this mess. I am at a loss at this point. What do you think we need to do to solve the problem? What can I do to help you and your soldiers get it done?

How do you decide? I bet that you think the second officer is more capable.

As does nearly everyone to whom I put this question. Until I ask: Whom of these two officers do you believe the American Army leaders would want to promote? Then all negative (typically unjustified) preconceptions about the army bubble up in the executives. They burst out, that, of course, everyone apart from the army would go for officer No. 2. And then they are stunned by the following result: 180 senior officers who read this case rated officer number 2 as having considerably higher leadership potential than the ego-protecting officer number 1.

In the subsequent discussion, the question also often arises if Officer 2 may not have gone a bit too far. “Maybe Jones didn't need to be told how much more he knows... and how little the Officer knows.”

But it is this extreme openness which makes it valuable. Even in such an extreme situation, where Officer 2 honestly and extensively admits that he knows little and really needs help, his leadership potential is seen as much higher.

This research should help dispel the second fear, that employees or other managers think humble leaders as being weak. If you are still not convinced, then look at my research in Figure 4.9. Participants were asked to assess how they and others in the company saw their manager. And the humble manager scores higher on all fronts!

Figure 4.9 How humble managers are seen.

Think about this. Employees think their humble managers more competent and also see the supervisors of their leaders doing the same!

And there is yet another clear point to be made: Humble leaders are seen as more charismatic than non-humble leaders.100 Does that come as a surprise to you? Would you have expected narcissists to be more charismatic? Indeed, narcissists can be good at enthusing employees about their vision, yet it typically becomes clear that he or she is primarily focused on themselves.

A humble leader on the other hand not only treats employees more on an equal level, is other-oriented and willing to invest much energy into communication but also sees the bigger picture which he or she is able to convey.

Unsurprisingly then, there was considerable charisma that I experienced in many of the interviews with the top managers that had found the concept of humility so matching their philosophy that they took the time to talk to me. (And I know that some of you will be thinking, that someone who is interested in humility does not need to be so in real life…. And you are right. Yet for quite a number of interviewees, I had the chance to talk to their employees who corroborated that humility was not merely a publicity stunt catchphrase for their bosses.)

It is therefore clear that if you act humbly – and I stress once again, this does not mean that you are incapable, overmodest or insecure, but that you perceive yourself and others clearly and accept feedback – it is precisely that others will see even more potential in you.

Also it is not for nothing that the American Army has the following requirement of a capable leader in 6.14–6.15 of the Army Leadership Manual:

As a critical element of adaptability, self-awareness enables leaders to recognize their strengths and weaknesses across a range of conditions and progressively employ strengths to correct weaknesses.
To be self-aware, leaders must be able to formulate accurate self-perceptions, gather feedback from others, and change their self-concept as appropriate.
Self-aware leaders are open to feedback from others and actively seek it.101

In most companies the same will apply: Humility, as we understand it, makes promotion more likely. Remember Wilf Blackburn? How his company had so started to treasure humility, that they chose the new CEO to also be one who believes in humility.

In companies where a culture of ostentation and showmanship prevails, it will naturally be more difficult. Sometimes even the humble person has to bite the bullet and go somewhere else where he/she is more appreciated.

Take-Away: Humility signals potential – Contrary to what is feared, humble behavior is not seen as a weakness but as a virtue that a leader requires in order to be able to take on new, unfamiliar tasks. They are therefore viewed more positively for future responsibilities than an arrogant, narcissistic manager.

Less Exhaustion

Leading is exhausting. Every day there are dozens of interactions, moments when decisions have to be made, difficult customers, trade-offs with other departments and clarifications across hierarchical levels.

Not surprisingly, around 37 percent of managers regularly suffer from stress. 30 percent even consider themselves to be at risk for burnout.102 In addition, many leaders are not in good health. US Data puts 82 percent of CEOs as being overweight, with 69 percent in a “hopelessly poor physical condition.” Almost 60 percent are unable to even touch their toes.103

Since we lack data for every country, you will have to monitor yourself and your colleagues to see how strong your relationship is with your toes!

When it comes to further research, there has been a gap for many years with academics focusing more on the effect that managers have on the health and well-being of their employees and less on the health and well-being of the managers themselves.

However, the employee perspective already gives some useful indication: If their manager is a constructive leader, employees feel significantly better and healthier.104

Recently good news also came in for you as a manager. There is one large study from 2020 that scrutinized more than 12,000 executives. The result of which should satisfy all those who strive to lead positively.

The key result? Those managers who lead destructively feel less well overall.105 What sort of leadership is meant here? For example, approaching their employees aggressively, tyrannically or negatively, being overly critical as well as shouting at them. The research shows that both suffer: the employee who is shouted at and left behind full of anger AND the manager who presumably had hoped for some positive results from his own behavior and now cannot discover it.

This is a little reminiscent of the research on catharsis. Contrary to what you might think, it is not at all beneficial for a relationship if you let go of yourself and get the person who angered you into trouble or let rip at them.106

Why? Most people do not wish to be the kind of person who yells at others. Most people actually want to like themselves. This leads to the following vein of argument in the aggressor: “I am a fair person. I usually never freak out. Only when someone is particularly incapable. My freaking out right now, means that the person I just yelled at is particularly incompetent.”

Ergo, the victim is to blame for the uncontrolled outburst by the other partner. The screamer needs this (erroneous) insight to get rid of his or her cognitive dissonance: “I am nice, but have done something unpleasant – the reason for this cannot be within me, I have been forced....”

First conclusion: negative leadership is costly for the leader in terms of general well-being.

What happens with constructive leadership behavior? The opposite is measured: Constructive leadership styles – even while demanding more consciously employed energy – lead to considerably more satisfaction and well-being amongst the managers expressing them.

This finding is even stronger for those managers who lead with humility.107 The key reason is that these suffer measurably less from emotional exhaustion.

A study of 110 managers and their employees showed that those who acted with humility felt in better shape regarding both physical and mental health. They were also far less interested in changing employers, with all the anxiety and uncertainty that entails.

Now the question is, of course, why such proactive people who consciously appreciate others, consciously look at their own weaknesses and strengths, are consciously open and receptive, consciously perceive the bigger picture, why they suffer less from emotional exhaustion? Instead, it looks as if they have far more to do than others that only focus on themselves.

Research points to two main reasons:

Firstly, the humble leader does not have to expend energy to maintain their self-image in front of their employees. After all, he or she is allowed not to be perfect.

One of the founders of humility research, Bradley Owens, describes it as follows: “Our interviews show that humility is liberating for leaders because it frees them from the tyranny of trying to be an omniscient or perfect personality.”108

Secondly, humble leaders have better relationships with their employees. They are awarded with more trust, have fewer brutal conflicts and experience less negative energy and less suppressed anger.

This is also what you hear in the interviews with the managers.

If you act with humility, you can be more yourself and drop these protective measures. With humility you do not feel the strain of having to be the expert in everything. That takes the pressure off.109

This result fits well with the long-established insight that people who assess themselves realistically feel more comfortable with themselves and their own lives.110

Looking at the dark side, numerous studies show that people who have an erroneous self-image – whether they fundamentally overestimate or underestimate themselves – are more likely to get in their own way, perform worse and feel less comfortable overall.111

As a consequence, we have the result that humble managers feel less exhausted.

Take-Away: Humility avoids exhaustion – Humble leaders are better able to assess themselves, do not have to fight to maintain an exaggerated image in front of their employees, and are less likely to face aggression.

Better Dealing with Stress

Humble managers feel good. When I feel good, I stay healthy. This also has an effect on others, who also become positive and healthier. A positive cycle.

(Interview No. 12 VP Change, 100,000 employees)

A study in 2016 finally confirmed that which had already been suspected: Humility not only brings the individual a greater sense of well-being but also greater resilience in stressful situations.

More than 3,000 Americans were interviewed for the study.112 The clear and impressive result: stress, conflicts and difficult life events had fewer negative effects on those who were categorized as humble.

There are five reasons for this:

Firstly, humble people think and ruminate less about themselves. Why? Because they are aware of the fact that they are like all other human beings. They are not perfect and will make numerous errors in all walks of life. As a consequence, they tend not to be ashamed of the situations they find themselves in and are willing to communicate with others and receive support and ideas from them.

In other words, if I am humble and have just made a bad deal, it is easier for me to ask someone for advice on how to get out of my deal than if my self-image depended on my actions. As a result, people who have a healthy (i.e., humble) self-confidence are less easily offended – that is, they also experience less stress because they do not take other people's actions personally.113 Narcissists, on the other hand, refer everything to themselves in a hurtful way, as do people with self-doubt, self-hatred and inferiority complexes (i.e., people with a faulty and unhealthy perception).

Knowing this, it is probably no wonder that the North Korean dictator Kim Jong-Un had his defense minister executed with flak fire in 2014, because he had dared to fall asleep in a conference chaired by the dictator.114

Look at this pleasant example from a less ego-driven person.

Theatre manager Matthias Lilienthal describes a potentially insulting situation:

What was funny was that during the guest performance in St. Petersburg, the director of the theatre came running to me. He was very agitated and apologized profusely for the “crap” that was on the stage. “It would disappear immediately.” That crap was our stage set.115

Secondly, a humble leader appreciates positive things. Even in difficult and stressful times, they manage to perceive that which is positive. Their larger view of the whole helps to put things into perspective.

While I could despair because our biggest customer has quit and moved to our competitor, at the same time, I a) know that this fact will probably not be etched onto my tombstone and b) can perceive gladly how the whole team is already thinking actively about how to compensate for the huge loss. Of course, this does not mean that I fall into laissez-faire and a nonchalant: “C’est la vie… that’s just the way it is.” But the awareness that my life does not end right here and now is useful for a change in perspective. As is the understanding that typically something positive comes out of most things. If only because our brain is so wonderfully good at creating sense-making stories. Just google “Prison is the best thing that ever happened to me” and you will be proud of our neuronal skill set.

Thirdly, humble leaders do not look for someone else to blame. They recognize their own contribution to the difficult situation and therefore, in crisis situations, behave in a much more socially acceptable manner compared to narcissists. These find it impossible to accept that they might have been also at fault, if even partly. In other words, humble leaders do not exacerbate the critical situation by angrily shouting at others or punishing them with contempt. This non-reaction is vital to creating an atmosphere for creative thinking and cooperation. In addition, it keeps all information channels open, which would snap shut if I had managed to offend my employees.

Fourthly, humble managers are far more aware of themselves. They see themselves neither as great heroes nor as great failures. Instead, because they know their strengths and weaknesses and also consider themselves changeable, they feel secure in their identity. As a result, they do not break apart mentally in times of crisis but are more interested in continuing to grow.

And finally, humble managers experience less stress, because they are more likely to forgive themselves. Why? A study from 2020 showed that humble people are less defensive and condemn themselves less.116 They know that all people have faults and do stupid, sometimes even evil things. The word “evil” brings us back to our earlier discussion about evil even in peaceful times. And add to that an understanding of how often each of us may have let someone take the brunt for something, or we may have let our emotions get the best of us or we may have happily blamed someone else because it seemed easier than to realize our own part in the matter. You may wish to name these actions or thoughts evil or merely mindless – only you can decide. Sadly, my experience with myself and others shows that we can be very evil at many a time!

These five reasons lead to humble managers feeling less stressed while leading others, the company as well as themselves.

Take-Away: Humility fosters good stress management – The ability to take responsibility for one's own actions on the one hand, and to forgive oneself on the other, means that difficult situations do not lead to increased stress for a humble leader.

Better Relationships

In my company leaders are not visible. They only go to the shop floor to complain or look for errors. There is no chance for a good relationship.

(Interview No. 59, Head of L&D, 33,000 employees

Those without humility are careless of the company culture – they don’t provide directions and do not stop the “monsters” as they ought to be stopped.

(Interview No. 146, CEO, 11,000 employees)

Many managers fear that if they share their weaknesses, spend much time appreciating others, are always eager to learn that then they may see their authority undermined and will also lose the ability to influence.

It will not come as a surprise to you, that the data shows the opposite. You saw above that employees generally think more highly of a humble leader. Further research shows that the willingness to point out one's own errors and even to apologize for them actually increases employees' trust and loyalty. (We will discuss the limits of this later in Chapter 10).

Leaders who apologize are also more likely to be perceived as transformational leaders by their employees than those who do not.117

Let us briefly reflect on why an apology works. It signals one key fact: I, the manager, am not overly concerned with my ego, with my greatness, with my façade. Instead, an apology which is honestly meant shows that I am all about reducing the impact of my own errors on employees, customers or the organization. The employees reward this behavior with more trust, which in turn bolsters relationships. Further analysis shows that if a manager appreciates others for what they are and do social relations with the employees prosper. This is actually a no-brainer. If I see what my employees do; if I appreciate them for what they do, then clearly, we will have a better relationship. Simply taking time to interact and not just storming in with tasks to hand around will strengthen our relationship. Also, having been appreciated, it is considerably easier for employees to forgive mistakes or ill-considered actions and still approach their leaders openly and with interest.118

One interviewee tells the following story.

I am new to this position and needed to restructure. Since it was not yet clear who would head what departments, I decided on my own, what the yearly raise in salary would be. Due to thousands of other things that I needed to do, I did not communicate this decision. When my first line managers heard this, they complained, that they should have been consulted. I realized, that I could have done this differently – and I apologized.

(Interview No. 148, Head Customer Innovation, 4,000 employees)

Do we think her the weaker for it? No. Do her employees? Well, we do not know for sure. Yet this leader was one of the 10 names that were included in answers to my questionnaire as an excellent and humble leader that I should definitely interview. N = one – but a strong one.

Looking to the military, humble leaders are also more popular with their soldiers than their arrogant counterparts. They are in particular valued for the psychological sense of safety they provide to their subordinates.119

Finally, let me also remind you that humility is helpful for relationships of the manager outside of work. As we saw in the opening chapter, relationships with humble people last longer due to more gratitude and openness. Even the start of a relationship is positively influenced – people are more willing to start a romantic relationship with a “humble” person.120

Take-Away: Humility creates better relationships – Since leaders with humility are able to admit mistakes, they are more human to their employees. This is how better relationships get built.

Better Understanding of Employees

A few years ago, three key supervisors suddenly left my firm, took away some of our key customers and established their own firm. A very painful experience and lesson! I thought I had been very nice to them: I tried my best to offer them a good salary, so I assumed I had made them happy. Later I learned what they wished was to buy a small amount of the company’s share so that they could have a different identity – as an owner of the firm.121

If humble leaders have better relationships, are they also better at understanding what drives their employees? We can affirm this on two levels, on the one hand, in terms of “wanting” and on the other hand, in terms of “feeling.”

The Wanting of Employees

The reason that humble leaders are better in this field has something to do with the humble inquiry. This is the ability to ask questions respectfully, openly and without judgment. Humble managers do this all the time in order to get a clear picture, not only of a situation, but also of their employees.

To explain why this questioning is so important, we need to immerse ourselves in the research.

Prima facie, we could assume that every experienced manager knows quite well what their employees want. Surely, with a bit of energy they will be able to put themselves into their shoes.

Let us test this assumption in the case of call center employees. Is it not the case, that any of us could adopt the perspective of a call center employee, think about what they require as motivation for work and – bingo – we know it!

Unfortunately, research shows something different.122 Experienced managers were asked to imagine what it is like to work as a customer advisor in a call center. What motivates these customer advisors and what motivates themselves?

For themselves, they quickly arranged the order within a provided list. Clearly their motivation came from direct motives such as learning, skills, feeling good about themselves and that the work is worthwhile. This was their exact order.

Learning
Skills
Feel good about yourself
Pay
Worthwhile
Praise
Benefits
Security

When answering the question about the customer advisors, there was a clear assumption that these would be very different. Surely, they would be far more motivated by indirect motives such as: Pay, security, benefits and praise. Learning? Skills? Purpose? Not needed!

Briefly stop here. What would you have thought? Which from the motives cited above are your key ones and which are the ones for the call center employees?

The managers in this piece of research were TOTALLY wrong. When the call center employees were asked directly what motivated them, the top three were: Skills, Worthwhile, Learning. Pay only cropped up in 7th place.

In other words, even an experienced manager is not able to recognize what employees truly want.

I can hear managers grumble: this is only one piece of research. I am sure that in general I get it right. The bad news follows on foot: when it comes to understanding what someone wants, we simply cannot know what it is.

FULL STOP.

In 2018, Harvard professors evaluated the impressive number of 25 studies with almost 1,500 participants to finally lay the topic to rest.123 The sad result: we humans have no idea what the other person want. Yet, once we have spent some energy to put ourselves into the shoes of others, we are very, very certain that we know exactly what they want and can hardly be shaken out of this supposed understanding.

This also happened to me – this time, I was the “victim.” As a trained lawyer, I was repeatedly told during my employment at the business school that I was surely interested in working on topics that had something to do with lawyers.

But I was a historian and management consultant at the same time, and much more interested in topics such as influencing, decision making or neuroplasticity. I can understand the assumption behind this: if, as an external person, I had put myself into my own shoes, I would also have thought that I should and would have a burning interest in law (which I obviously liked and had done well in). But I simply was not interested. Asking and listening instead of supposing would have solved this intricate problem!

Apparently, the attempted change of perspective does not have the desired effect of increasing understanding. Instead, our brain seems to follow our respective stereotypes about the person we are trying to understand.

Remember the story at the beginning? The CEO who was irritated that his people left. He had thought that in their position they should be happy with the money he offered. Yet they had wanted ownership – and his brain had not been able to come up with this, since they were managers in his mind and not owners.

That means, we focus on that which we automatically associate with this sort of position or this type of person. Want another example?

If I put myself in the role of a professor, I objectively score better on tests than if I put myself in the position of a cheerleader.124 If I put myself in the shoes of an older person, I become less competitive than if I put myself in the shoes of a younger person. This is the whole topic of priming.

This is what happened when the managers tried to take on the perspective of the call center employees. In their mind's eye, they saw the picture of a stereotypical call-center employee. This person would surely only be doing the job because they had no better education and no better options. As a consequence, their key interest would be money, security and a bit of praise. Developing skills or purpose does not play a role in such a stereotype.

But this is not the reality. Everyone – even the proverbial dustman – has an interest in making sense of their job. It is just hard for those of us who have chosen different professions to realize this. And this is why the revolutionary simplicity of what the humble executive does provides an automatic solution: Ask. Very simply, asking what the other person needs, thinks and wants. In an open, respectful, non-judgmental manner.

Open questions meet free speaking, meet open mind and result in understanding. This is the one and only way that in my role as a manager I can find out what my employees’ needs are and then, and only then, can I begin to think about whether and how this need can be satisfied.

The other solution is to listen carefully. Even when not asked, people often reveal much about their interests. Then this should be taken on board and followed wherever possible. Otherwise, you end up where unfortunately most people end up in their gift-giving: They are sure that a personalized gift is much better received than if they bought something from the wish list. And are terribly wrong! In all of the studies, the gift-givers were sure that their gift (which was not on the list), would be as highly appreciated. How could it not be? They had really invested their little grey cells into finding something that would be appreciated. But the recipient stubbornly appreciated these gifts far less than the ones they had actually asked for.125

So, the clue is not only to ask but then also to remember what people said and act on this where possible.

Getting back to the beginning of the chapter: Would the business owner have kept his staff if he had asked them, understood their need for ownership and sold them a small share? Most likely….

The Feeling of Employees

The question now is whether the result is any different when it is not a matter of wanting but of feeling. Does it work if I put myself emotionally into someone else's shoes? And here the answer is just as clear: Of course, it helps. Research shows this in many different studies, and finds the humble leader particularly strong here.

The powerful feeling of empathy or compassion does not guarantee a hundred per cent match, but gets quite close. Imagine how you would feel, if you were being nagged at for a piece of work that you spent five days on? Or imagine how you would feel, if someone does not look at you for even one second as you speak? Or imagine, you have an online meeting and everyone has their camera turned off? Or imagine, you are being shouted at for something that to you is no big thing? Immediately we get a pretty good idea of those feelings that arise from someone else's perspective.

Humble leaders are particularly successful in using empathy.126 They understand what people feel while at the same time being aware of their own response. They realize when they need to set limits, so as not to be overwhelmed by what they hear or see. And they are in charge of their own emotions and able to show only those feelings that are appropriate at the moment.

So, a humble leader will have a good idea what it is that their employees feel in any given situation.

For some researchers this raises some concerns. They are fearful that too much empathy from the humble leader, especially in a crisis, will prevent him or her from acting rationally. If, for example, the crisis demands layoffs, the worry is that the empathic leader simply will not be able to go through with it. In addition, the humble leader would more easily fall prey to false alarms by listening and favoring the group that is empathically closest to them.127

This is certainly an interesting point, but to my mind applies to any virtue that is exaggerated. Thus, one can listen too much and act too little; act too much and listen too little; pay too much attention to culture and too little to strategy; pay too much attention to strategy and too little to culture.

The whole point of humility is, that the humble manager does not lose sight of the bigger picture as well as of their own emotional reactions. If the bigger picture needs layoffs for the good of the company, then this will be done. In as appreciative a way as possible. This is called “necessary evils” and everyone can learn to master them.

Humble leaders therefore will rarely lose themselves in empathy or get stuck in one type of leadership behavior only but instead adapt to the needs of the situation.

One interview partner shows that he had learned to see the bigger picture very clearly.

It took humility to realize that my being too relational with followers was in an effort to fulfil my own needs, not theirs. It was not serving them or the business well. I saw that I needed to adjust my leadership style back toward the firmer, command-and control approach.128

If I am only led by empathy, I will not fulfil my leadership tasks as I should. And in some cases, for some time, even an authoritarian style may be the right one!

The key point therefore remains: Humble leaders understand their employees better.

Take-Away: Humility brings deeper understanding of what employees want and feel – Humble leaders know that they know very little and use open and non-judgmental questions to better understand their employees. Increased empathy also allows them to understandwhat emotions and issues are at play.

Summary

Humble leaders win for themselves on six fronts: they perform better and others see more potential in them. They are less emotionally exhausted and can cope better with stress. They have better relationships and a better understanding of other people around them since they have learned to ask and empathize.

The Upside of Humility for Start-ups

No VC wants to work with an entrepreneur who does not have the humility to accept and improve upon what is not good.

(Shubhankar Bhattacharya, VC Investor)129

Each start-up feels unique, with its ideas, its founders, its successes and its failures. Since 90 percent of all start-ups fail, it is of particular interest if humility is a useful or detrimental virtue for them.

In particular, since there is often a focus on the person of the CEO and public perception is that the glitzier such a CEO, the more narcissistically brilliant, the more successful the start-up will rake in money and be subsequently successful. Also in a smaller company, as start-ups tend to be, the impact of the person on the helm is more impactful on the culture of the company than in a large enterprise.

Let us look first at what research says about narcissism and start-ups.

Initially it shows some upsides: narcissistic CEOs have a higher entrepreneurial intention – they seem to be are even hungrier for entrepreneurial success than others.130

Yet there is a clear correlation between narcissism and overconfidence, leading narcissists to more easily branch out into the unknown – with all its dangers. Look at the example of the serial entrepreneur Badr Jafar, who needed to be shocked out of his overconfidence through numerous failures.

My first own venture in that space, which was funded through a small business loan in the UK, turned out to be a success. This gave me an almost unjustified confidence boost and made me think I could turn my hand to anything. Not surprisingly, that turned out to be far from the truth, and after some further successes – but mostly failures – I learnt some extremely valuable lessons, which prepared me well for the trials and tribulations of a diverse and often complex world of business when I joined the family business a few years later.131

In addition, narcissists are measurably less successful in crowd-funding. Why? Because narcissistic entrepreneurs tend to focus on ego-defensive behavior and therefore set less ambitious goals. Investors also “smell” the narcissism and are less willing to support their campaigns.132

Further research shows that narcissistic entrepreneurs are less able to establish good long-term relationships with their investors. The impressive front is revealed over the course of time as being just that – a front.

The same ego-defensive behavior as above also leads founders to not being able to learn from errors and failures.133 A mind that is not open to objective assessments, but feels it has to defend itself will not be able to objectively look at past mistakes and consciously learn from them. Instead, the focus will be on blaming the situation as well as others. In a narcissistic founder there is simply less motivation to learn!

So, is there then a measurable upside of humility?

You will not be surprised to discover that research has uncovered a number of them – specific to start-ups.

The first has to do with top management teams. The humility of a CEO measurably reduces conflict within the team, which in turn leads to a generally better performance of the company.134 It also increases trust in the top management team and results in a better long-term sustainable climate.135

The second one has to do with investors. Research confirms that investors see humble entrepreneurs as having higher constructive qualities and as a consequence they evaluate the venture more positively bolstered with a larger amount of funding.136 This is not surprising if you see the following quote by a partner in a VC firm.

The first question that I ask myself when I meet an entrepreneur is whether he is capable of listening and following advice. If not, I know I cannot work with him whatever the project or his other qualities.137

No room for narcissists, much room for humble founders.

VC Investor Shubhankar Bhattacharya tells the following story which also supports the quantitative research.138

The entrepreneur I was scheduled to meet (at a cafe), called me as he entered the cafe so that we could locate each other. As soon as we met, he apologized. Since he was on time, I could think of no reason for him to be sorry. When I asked him about it, he said that he should have looked me up on social media before the meeting, so that he would not have had to call just to locate me. (Since then, I have been dreaming of the day an Uber/Ola driver will apologize for calling me about the pickup location)

The second entrepreneur welcomed me into his office by saying that he enjoyed my articles and looked forward to discussing them with me (At that time, I had written only a few). During the meeting, he brought out specific examples and anecdotes I had written about and wanted to know about my upcoming posts. I could not have been more flattered.

To appreciate why I was so taken aback by these two displays of politeness and humility, here is the complete perspective:

Both these entrepreneurs had sold their respective companies for over $100 Million in deals that remain highly celebrated. Their reputation therefore, is widespread and well-established (They have nothing to prove to me, or anyone else).

They had nothing to gain from meeting me (At least nothing directly or in the short-term). Neither of these meetings was a pitch. In fact, it was I who reached out to them, requesting an audience.

I am convinced now that their extraordinary success did not happen in spite of their humility, but because of it. In fact, over time, I have observed that humility is the essential hallmark of the best entrepreneurs — The ones who are the most likely to appreciate the value of feedback, recognize the uncertainty of future challenges, and who are capable of building a work-environment that values respect and empathy.

Thirdly, there is evidence that humility and social entrepreneurship go hand in hand.139 This is not surprising when you remember that humility is other-focused and has a view of the larger picture.

And finally, research is clear on the fact that narcissism in a founder hampers innovation140 while humility strengthens it as we have seen via psychological safety and empowerment.

If you recall, that more than 50 percent of start-ups fail because they offer products that the market does not need – in order to avoid that you not only need the innovation to adapt the products to the needs of the customers but also the brave voice of employees to raise the problem. That will only be possible in a humble culture!

So once again humility is better than narcissism or simple mindlessness!

Take-Away: The gift of humility for startups – Although at first one might think that narcissists could be particularly suitable for the start-up phase of a company, this is wrong. Research clearly shows that the founders’ humility helps to raise money, build good contacts with investors and set up the company culturally in such a way that it is more likely to learn from mistakes and become more successful.

A Short Pause

In the previous section, we have seen that humility delivers valuable results at all three levels – for the employees, the organization and the manager themselves.

Now, of course, the question is how you go about achieving this humility?

What exactly does it mean to recognize and show your own strengths and weaknesses? Is that not standard for most adults, for most managers?
What exactly does it mean to appreciate the strengths of others? Should I praise everyone in the IT department for the fact that the website didn't crash today?
And am I not already recognizably open and capable of learning - otherwise I would not have this book in my hand or up on my screen?
This bigger picture – which one is mine? And is it not somewhat unlikely to be able to see it all the time?

We will answer all these questions later down the line. Before a short question to you. Which of the four elements of humility do you think people find hardest to get right?

In my qualitative interviews, I asked my interviewees where they saw the greatest difficulty. In Figure 4.10 you can see that 41 percent say that their colleagues and they themselves struggle most with understanding themselves and showing their weaknesses. The bigger picture came second with 25 percent. 17 percent said that it is at least two out of the four – many even sighed and said: “Of course, it is all four.” Appreciation came up for 11 percent. The six percent who mentioned “learning” pointed out that this deficit was typically only recognized at second glance. Everyone is “officially” keen to learn but then ends up not going to seminars or applying what they learnt in everyday life.

Figure 4.10 Which element of humility is hardest to achieve?

In the next four chapters, we will look at each element of humility in far more detail, see what they entail and what the stumbling blocks are on the way.

Gino, F., & Flynn, F. J. (2011). Give them what they want: The benefits of explicitness in gift exchange. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 47(5), 915–922.

   https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/why-humility-unfair-advantage-shubhankar-bhattacharya/. Retrieved 12.11.2022
Mathieu, C., & St-Jean, É. (2013). Entrepreneurial personality: The role of narcissism. Personality Individual Difference, 55, 527–531.
   https://www.arabianbusiness.com/the-many-faces-of-entrepreneur-q-a-with-badr-jafar-568300.html. Retrieved 12.11.2022

Chapter 5 Willingness to Recognize One’s Own Strengths and Weaknesses (and to Show Them Where It Makes Sense)

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-5

It takes courage to want to show yourself as you really are.

Søren Kierkegaard (Danish philosopher 1813–1855)

Summary

In order to recognize our own strengths and weaknesses, we must avoid numerous stumbling blocks: these range from self-perception to interacting with others. It is as much about narcissism and excessive self-confidence as it is about underestimating one's own abilities. It has to do with thinking yourself too obvious to others as well as believing in too great an importance. Tools are given to analyze your own strengths and weaknesses for your current and any future job.

Carl runs the country operations for a large multinational. He is in charge of the 6,600 employees out of the global 44,000. What really impressed me was his openness. While many of my interviewees chose to speak in general about strength and weaknesses, Carl quickly focused on his own. A tall, big-framed, bald-headed 57-year-old with an air of concentration and focus about him, he held nothing back.

In my former job with a smaller company I rose very quickly through the ranks to board level. And I became reckless and arrogant. I loved the show, was vain and keen on my status symbols. I also thought lying was part of the course, overreached and actually ended up in front of court. That taught me a lot. I found a coach, whom I was willing to listen to – a psychologist who was also an actor – and discovered to my amazement that neither lying nor showing off was essential to success. So, I stopped. And the global board of my new company thought that this kind attitude was sorely needed. They call it: “Being more than seeming to be.” When they hired me, they did not want to hear about any detailed plans I had for the company, they just wanted to see if I matched their values.

What is my task here as CEO? Well, whatever one of my employees can do better, they do. What is left for me is to go where it hurts. Understand what happened, how to help, how to prevent problems in the future.

Here was an impressive CEO who was good at identifying his weaknesses as well as his strengths and was happy sharing both.

Yet for many this is a big hurdle, so it is worthwhile looking more deeply into this.

Many managers wrongly think they are the cleverest in the room!

(Interview No. 135, Manager, 50,000 employees)

Football trainer Jupp Heynckes is a great example. I found it very impressive how he actively and openly addressed his “weakness”: his advanced age. “I cannot guarantee that I will play the coach for 12 hours every day, with 120 percent commitment.”

(Interview No. 1, Board Member, 22,000 employees)

When it comes to recognizing and showing your own strengths and weaknesses, the first question is: Are we talking about absolute or relative strengths and weaknesses here? A short anecdote to better understand what I mean.

At my English girls' boarding school, I was the best at orienteering. Why? You had to read maps and I could do that very well in comparison to the others. It did not at that time matter that I wasn't a good runner – my precision more than made up for the lack of speed. Things changed when I got into Cambridge. On a sunny autumn day, I arrived at the bus an hour after the last runner. 100th place out of 100. Everyone there could easily read maps and, unlike me, the other participants were genuine runners dying to dive into the forest at top speed.

The key lesson: at boarding school, I had no relevant weakness as regards orienteering. Indeed, I could easily cope with what was required of me. At Cambridge I had a huge competition, my strength disappeared and I had a nearly unsurmountable weakness in being light years away from the other participants when it came to running.

In other words, our strengths and weaknesses are relative and must be considered for each situation anew. And since this is about you as a manager, only those strengths and weaknesses will be relevant that you need in this or your desired next position.

You will be aware that we are talking about quite a large number of skills and knowledge that you need. Moreover, the requirements are often hugely different for each position and each organization. Even within one position, there might be considerable adaptation necessary, as the situation, the employees, the superiors, the customers and society change.

One fine example for the impact that a changing situation has on your strengths is former CEO of BMW Harald Krüger. In his first years as CEO, he was very much lauded for his cooperative, consensus-building leadership style.1 Then when the years passed and the pressure from companies such as Tesla could no longer be ignored, his cooperative style began to be seen as dithering as well as lacking in clarity. Employees and investors were yearning for a clear vision and decisive communication. Krueger was not able to deliver. Instead, being CEO appeared to have become an unpleasant chore to him. He was seen as gloomy, distracted and above all unable to be at the helm of a BMW that wanted to shine and have a clear position in the market to aspire to. In 2019 he was replaced by Oliver Zipse who seems to be able to give clear directions as well as restore confidence in employees and investors alike.2

What worked well in the beginning for Krüger ended up in a changed situation becoming a liability for him. Sadly, he seemed unwilling to adapt to the changing big picture and embark on new leadership behaviors such as charismatic storytelling or being the visionary helmsman.

Now that we have seen the relativity of strengths and weaknesses, we have to answer three questions: First, what are the obstacles on the way to identifying your strengths and weaknesses? Secondly, how best to find these strengths and weaknesses in you? Thirdly, how and when to best show them?

Stumbling Blocks When Looking at Strengths and Weaknesses

It is difficult to be humble if you are not self-aware. I find many people cover up their deficiencies, because they do not want to be seen as week. But it is important to also be vulnerable.

(Interview No. 15, Head of L&D, 1,500 employees)

Humility comes from the fact that you understand yourself. If I know and accept myself and know that I am work in progress, I have a different perspective. If I see myself as a completed work of art that needs not be improved, then there will be a high chance that I will be less humble. The majority of people are not able to look inside and understand themselves. It is a very difficult thing to see how ugly you are.

(Interview No. 18, Global Head, 110,000 employees)

People who are really strong are not afraid to show their weaknesses.

(Interview No. 83, CMO, 6,500 employees)

People have difficulties on two levels: Seeing themselves correctly as individuals and perceiving themselves in the interaction with others.

We will start with the four stumbling blocks when it comes to perceiving ourselves.

Narcissism.
Overconfidence.
Imposter syndrome.
Limits of biology.
Self-Perception: Danger of Narcissism

As CEO I often had difficulties with the fact that one is courted so much. There is always the danger of confusing yourself with the office. You think that you have earned it hard, but that is not the case.

(René Obermann, former Chairman of the Management Board of Deutsche Telekom3)

You have to learn to keep yourself in perspective. When I breathe in deeply, people around me stop talking because they think I will say something.

(Interview No. 108, CEO, 3,200 employees)

In my last job I had a CEO who claimed to know within 30 seconds if an employee was a good fit. He was often wrong. But never noticed.

(Interview No. 73, VP HR, 4,100 employees, formerly Head Group HR, 150,000)

Narcissists may be more successful in the short run, pushing the numbers, but the humble ones are more successful in the longer term.

(Interview No. 156, Head HR, 1,300 employees)

The first danger is narcissism as we already saw with the start-ups in the last chapter.

First let us differentiate: There is a healthy narcissism as well as an unhealthy one. Healthy narcissism is essential. It protects the individual from attacks of others as well as from self-hatred. Healthy narcissism equips you with self-esteem and self-confidence. As academics put it: A certain dose of narcissism is necessary to function effectively.4

A useful image for this is to imagine a cat looking in the mirror. If it was an unhealthy narcissist, it would see a lion in midst of either feeble or furiously threatening animals. The healthy narcissist on the other hand would see a fit, alert and generally capable cat surrounded by all types of creatures, typically minding their own business.

Unhealthy narcissism goes to the extremes with self-love and comes in two shapes: The vulnerable and the grandiose one. Grandiose narcissism is characterized by high self-esteem, interpersonal dominance and a tendency to overestimate one’s capabilities. Vulnerable narcissism is defensive, avoidant and has a hypersensitive attitude in interpersonal relations.5 The latter person is easily wounded, cannot deal with negative feedback and needs praise at all times. The former thinks very highly of themselves and does everything to protect that image. Both types feel entitled to special treatment.

This differentiates them from “humble” managers. These know fully well what they can do and appreciate the value of their skills. But they do not expect anything from others.6 Researchers call this attitude of not seeking anything special snappily “hypo-egoic non-entitlement!” Managers who are humble and endowed with a healthy narcissism know where and how they are capable. Yet, they do not expect any special treatment in return or feel entitled to anything special.

A humble CEO, if necessary, would xerox what is needed without taking this as an attack on his or her status. He or she would also not NEED a seat in business class to feel great as the other passengers stumble past enviously into the dark back. However, it may be that he or she needs the business class seat to sleep well and be fit the next day for the big presentation.

A humble manager would also take feedback on board, when both a grandiose or vulnerable narcissist would balk at this. For the narcissists a situation like the following would be unbearable.

This manager endured that an outsider pointed out weaknesses in his field of responsibility and also questioned his expert and leadership role. He calmly accepted the results and then subsequently took countermeasures.

(Interview No. 9, Head internal Insurance, 17,000 employees)

As a result, being an unhealthy narcissist definitely harbors dangers for your own performance. If I cannot stand the idea of an external expert identifying weaknesses, I am unlikely to ever seek genuine feedback from the in or the outside.

So, what are the numbers of unhealthy narcissists out there? If you look at the recent meta-studies, you will see that what is defined as a narcissistic personality disorder is prevalent in between 0.43 and 6 percent of the population worldwide. This is a small number that allows the subsequent question whether this percentage is higher amongst leaders.

Well, there are two pieces of research to look at. Firstly, one amongst 126 CHROs: it found that these only saw 5 percent of their CEOs as truly narcissistic.7 With all the negative connotations of less trust, less cooperation – in a far worse atmosphere. Five percent is a fair number, yet not huge.

Secondly, a piece of my research with 900 employees worldwide. According to this, employees see a huge number of between 25 and 36 percent of their leaders as narcissists. Far more than five percent! Why the big gap between 25 and 36? That seems to be a matter of culture, which I will look at more deeply in Chapter 10. Let it suffice here to say that Indian and Swiss employees see narcissism “only” in 25 percent of the cases, the English, the Dutch and the American see it around 30 percent, while the Singaporeans, the French and the Germans are least happy with their leader with 43, 40 and 37 percent, respectively.

What about the other leaders? Again, it depends on the culture, India sees 65 percent of leaders as humble, the UK, US and the Dutch stand around 60 percent, with the Singaporeans, the French and the Germans with a sadder 50 percent.

The quick thinkers amongst you will have realized that this does not add up to hundred. This is the consequence of there having been a third option, namely “other.” This category hides careless leading. These are leaders that, according to their employees, either do not lead, are mindless or vacillate between different styles. A sort of muddling through leadership. Which is an interesting leadership style!

To understand if what these employees see as narcissism really is narcissism, let us look in more detail at the definitions.

The definition that leadership research uses is as follows: A narcissist is someone who is egotistical, self-focused and vain. Sounds easy to grasp. The question however is, if that is already the psychological personality disorder as defined by DSM 5? The one that comes to results of at most six percent of sufferers?

Take a moment to decide for yourself if the leaders that you would call narcissistic actually behave as defined by the fifth edition of the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders Compendiums, which is the worldwide standard reference for mental disorders.

A pervasive pattern of grandiosity (in fantasy or behavior), need for admiration and lack of empathy, beginning by early adulthood and present in a variety of contexts, as indicated by five (or more) of the following:

Has a grandiose sense of self-importance (e.g., exaggerates achievements and talents, expects to be recognized as superior without commensurate achievements).
Is preoccupied with fantasies of unlimited success, power, brilliance, beauty, or ideal love.
Believes that he or she is “special” and unique and can only be understood by, or should associate with, other special or high-status people (or institutions).
Requires excessive admiration.
Has a sense of entitlement (i.e., unreasonable expectations of especially favorable treatment or automatic compliance with his or her expectations).
Is interpersonally exploitative (i.e., takes advantage of others to achieve his or her own ends).
Lacks empathy: is unwilling to recognize or identify with the feelings and needs of others.
Is often envious of others or believes that others are envious of him or her.
Shows arrogant, haughty behaviors or attitudes.

If now you reflect on the leaders in your company that you know well – are there indeed so many that suffer from this pathological narcissism? Or are the narcissistic traits not strongly dependent on the culture and the system of the company and less tied to a quasi-eternal truth about the individual? This would indeed be my take: When we talk about narcissism in leaders, we do not mean the mental disorder but a self-focused behavior that is either fostered or hindered in the company at hand.

Before we look at this in more detail, let us pause to reflect, whether narcissists are actually all bad.

On the positive side, there is evidence that narcissistic leaders are seen as more visionary, initiate more mergers and expand more internationally.8 On the downside however, there is yet more research which shows that narcissists are fickle in their strategy,9 act unethically,10 more often involve their companies in costly litigation,11 lead to poor performance and higher burnout among employees (especially those with low self-esteem)12 and are far from being as successful as they claim to be.

More details on this latter point: a study from the year 2016 looked at the subjective and objective performance of 968 top managers.13 And came to the conclusion that while the more narcissistic amongst them were enormously impressed by their own performance and consequently by that of their company in terms of sales, turnover and innovation, this performance did not stand up to scrutiny – i.e. these companies were objectively simply not very good at sales, did not have high turnover compared to similar companies and were not particularly innovative. A sad result.

On top of all this, the more narcissistic a CEO, the more they are willing to fudge the company's figures in order to have them show what he or she would like to see.14 This is cleverly called earnings management.

Hearing all this, you may be worried, wish to drop this book and search the internet for a test on how far you are on the unhealthy and extreme side of narcissism. My appeal to you is: Don’t!

Firstly, because you can do the test right here and now. Either you go through the nine elements of the DSM and assess yourself. Or you decide to go for the less “disturbed” version of narcissism from leadership research. Here studies show that you do not have to take lengthy tests with 40-plus questions to find out whether you are a narcissist.15 All you have to do is assign a score to the following question. “To what extent do you agree with this statement: ‘I am a narcissist’?” (Note: The word “narcissist” means egotistical, self-focused and vain. 1 = not true at all, 7 = completely true).

And secondly, even if you should self-diagnose yourself as a DSM subject or as a 7, this is not a lethal diagnosis. Much can be learned by willing narcissists!

For example, while narcissists are seen as less empathetic than other leaders, using their employees for their own purpose without bearing their needs in mind, it takes only a slight nudge, a slight reminder of the existence of other people, to enable someone who is too narcissistic to learn how to empathize with others.16 Equally, coaches report on how narcissists can be coached out of their self-focus and become “normal” leaders.17

Clearly then, managerial narcissism is not something that is unchangeable from the cradle, but something that is strongly influenced by the situation you find yourself in.

Let me give you an example from history: CEOs who started their work careers in times of crisis ended up being less narcissistic than those who started in boom times.18 Why? In times of economic unrest, narcissism is simply not accepted by others around you. Also, if it is harder to find or keep a job, it becomes obvious even to yourself that you may not be the salt of the earth and are possibly not so important after all.

As the opening interview quotes show, unhealthy narcissism gets easily fostered by the environment, by people ascribing much to you and you beginning to believe this. If you earn 20, 40 or even 300 times more than an ordinary worker, it may be hard not to drop over to the unhealthy side of narcissism. If you work at what is considered “the” company or “the” place, this will also have an impact. That is why there are four times as many diagnosed narcissists in fiercely competitive New York than in relaxed Iowa.19

This argument of how the environment influences each and every one of us is also used to explain why researchers have found rising narcissism in young people – the economic boom and the demographic development in many countries have allowed younger people to focus on themselves and misjudge their importance in the grander scheme of things.

On the other hand, it is no wonder then that many people have observed a decrease in narcissism during the Covid-19 crisis. While the health systems are strained and millions all over the world are clearly suffering, narcissism becomes less accepted socially and is as a result less practiced.

For those who cannot imagine such a powerful influence of outside situations on their individual behavior, I recommend looking at an interesting piece of research done with Vietnam veterans.

In the 1970s, the American government was very concerned by the fact that nearly 80 percent of soldiers in Vietnam were regularly using drugs, including heroin. Yet, three years after their return to home, only 12 percent were still addicted. Why? Drugs, even if they were just as readily available in the US, simply were not a good match for everyday life. They did not fit in with the family, with the community, at work. So, most of the addicts simply stopped.20

This fine allegory when applied to unhealthy narcissism says: If it no longer fits, it will stop or at least become less!

The other way around is also true – if I am not careful, I can become more narcissistic in a flash. Look at this: Hungry people feel more entitled and are more focused on themselves than those that have eaten. In consequence, when your stomach is rumbling, you are much more likely to agree with sentences such as: “I deserve more than others,” “If I were on the Titanic, I would be entitled to be in one of the first lifeboats” or “I demand the best because I'm worth it.”21 Hunger subconsciously directs our focus away from other people towards our own needs.

So, it is vital to be aware of how situations shape our narcissism and try to engineer them thus, that they simply fail to foster narcissism. What can this look like? For example, when you are given a task that you believe only you can do, reflect on who would take on your role if you suddenly were forced to go on a three-week top-secret company project without time to connect with anyone back at the office. Immediately, we begin to see that we are not so important after all.

There is also considerable learning that happens over the course of people’s lives. A study from 2019 showed that people are less vain and feel far less entitled at age 41 than at 18.22 As if the oyster from “the world is your oyster” is still beautiful yet harder to crack. And you may have grown in understanding that other oysters also contain pearls.

Some say, that a decrease in narcissism even happened to Steve Jobs. Years after he was fired from Apple as CEO he reflected calmly: “It was a bitter medicine that the patient needed.”23 After this experience, he was said to have become more aware of his own narcissism and, if – from his point of view – the situation and the well-being of the company demanded it, he could occasionally drop it.

Everyone who has read his biography by Walter Isaacson24 knows that he never became a lamb and often enough remained a stinker, but it appeared that later on he was better able to see the bigger picture and sacrifice his narcissism on its altar.

Consequently, there are some researchers that actually postulate the following: As an underlying and trainable virtue, humility can actually go hand in hand with narcissism.25

Let us look at this apparent contradiction: “You may be able to be both humble and narcissistic.”

To some this may sound as if I am postulating that tall is tiny and bright is dark at the same time.

Yet for many people these are not irreconcilable differences. There are those who find the concept of two souls in one body quite easy to internalize. These are the people that are generally aware of a duality in themselves. They observe that they are shy in one group and extroverted in another, that they are lazy on one subject and diligent on another, that they treat most employees fairly and a few unfairly. This means that many people can see and even reluctantly accept the lazy, mean and evil inside themselves as well as the open, caring and altruistic. Because we have known for a long time that both can be found in everyone. Just remember the conclusion Alexander Solzhenitsyn came to: “The line separating good and evil passes right through every human heart.”

In addition, national culture influences how we deal with these inner contradictions. Research shows that, for example, East Asians are less in need of a positive self-image, than Westerners.26 They are both more aware as well as more accepting of the fact that there are many paradoxes and changes in life and that there is no one unified self. This means that these would also find it easier to bear a juxtaposition of a narcissistic and humble side.

In discussions, I find that this apparent contradiction is harder to accept for others than for ourselves. We find it far easier to classify someone in total as arrogant, humble or narcissistic instead of saying: “Today Peter had his humble day; today Susan put on her cloak of arrogance.” For ourselves, however, we understand that, we only had a bad day and will be different and better tomorrow.

If we accept the tenet, that humility can be learned (as we will see later) then in the learning process there will a) always be hick-ups and b) it may be that whatever we learn does not cover all areas of our life, meaning that we can stay partly narcissist even while being primarily humble.

Is there an advantage to this duality? Well, it seems to work well for some. Jack Ma, for example, the founder of Alibaba, appears to be able to do both: take a step back, allow a customer to ring the bell at the start of the company’s IPO and at the same time be on narcissistic display at the company party performing as Michael Jackson.27

Being both at the same time may actually have some advantages for the company. A survey of more than 200 CEOs showed that those who could live both narcissism and humility pushed their companies to be more innovative than those who did not exhibit both characteristics.28 The reason? Narcissism impels us towards the great goal for ourselves, humility towards the great goal primarily for others; both impel us to seek the new. Humility drives us to appreciate others, narcissism to try inspire others and be a shining torch to them.

If you seek an image for this duality between focus on self and focus on others, between narcissism and humility just look at the well-known story of the Indian who awards his grandson (and hundreds of blogs) with the following wisdom.

“There is a battle raging inside me,” the Cherokee grandfather said to his grandson. “It is a terrible fight and it is between two wolves. One is evil – he is anger, envy, sorrow, regret, greed, arrogance, self-pity, guilt, resentment, inferiority, lies, false pride, superiority and ego. The other wolf is good – he is joy, peace, love, hope, serenity, humility, kindness, goodwill, empathy, generosity, truth, compassion and faith. The same struggle takes place in you – and in every other human being too.”

Then the grandson asks, “And which one wins?”

The grandfather replies, “The one I feed.”

This is where most blogs end. They use the story to inspire us to focus on the good in us and that we have a duty to nourish it. Not a bad lesson. Yet, there is an even better one to come, since the original story goes on:

But if you feed them right, they both win. Because if I just decide to feed the white wolf, the black one will hide behind every corner, waiting for me to get distracted or weak and jump to get the attention he craves.

But when I acknowledge him, he is happy and the white wolf is happy and we all win. Because the black wolf has many qualities – tenacity, courage, fearlessness, a strong will and great strategic thinking – which I sometimes need. These are exactly the things the white wolf is missing. But the white wolf has compassion, care, strength and the ability to recognize what is in the best interest of all.

You see, my grandson, the white wolf needs the black wolf at his side.29

How heart-warming and fitting to the fact that we as humans often have both: The selfish with its strong will and the generous with its ample giving.

So, it seems that concentrating on only the good part of yourself may actually be unhealthy and contradict the view of humility as held in this book.

Instead, with a view from the balcony allowing both to be is what defines humility. If our narcissistic elements have some uses, then it is best to be aware of that, use them consciously and bolster ourselves with moments of narcissism if that leads to us better fulfilling the role that we have taken on.

It clearly comes down to being aware. Aware of where you are being overly narcissistic, where you may feel too entitled, where you forget others and think too much of yourself. Once you see the dangers, you will be able to temper them, and allow yourself small forays into narcissism that – if done well – will actually complement your humility.

Take-Away: Keeping narcissism in check – In addition to healthy narcissism, there is also unhealthy narcissism - which should be wisely contained by those who really want to know themselves.

Self-Perception: The Danger of Overconfidence

I do not employ a manager who cannot reflect and relate when something in life did not go as it was planned to. You need to have failed in order to start reflecting.

(Interview No. 32, former Board Member, 3,500 employees)

It is killer criterion in a candidate if there is no form of humility. If the person only talks about what he or she has done, without seeing what others have done or how the system has helped.

(Interview No. 41, CEO, 1,800 employees)

Hybris kills. My biggest fear is to fall for it, because people listen to me.

(Interview No. 145, Consultant to CEOs)

Everyone who understands the Dunning-Kruger effect will be humble.

(Interview No. 140, People Officer, 450,000 employees)

Is there anyone amongst you who does not think that you are a better driver than the average? Exactly. About 90 percent of us think they are better behind the wheel than those erratic people in the other cars. That this cannot be mathematically correct must be obvious to all.

Managers easily fall prey to the same bias of overconfidence. Would you like some data?

A study from the year 2020 shows that 80 percent of managers believe that they have succeeded in ensuring commitment and motivation amongst their employees. That is a great number! Unfortunately, less than half of the employees agree.30

Managers are not alone in their failure in assessing themselves correctly: if employees are asked how much their boss supports them, their answer is a depressingly low 17 percent. Given some time to reflect on what the manager is actually doing directly and what behind the scenes, the answer goes up to 33 percent.31 This is still low – and may reveal to the manager, that they often overestimate as to how useful they are seen by their employees.

Yet, at the same time, it reveals a general key point: We find it hard to correctly assess anything – ourselves or others.

By the way, groups are in no way smarter. All in all, participants set their contribution to a total group result in such a way that the result exceeds 140 percent. If they are given time to think again, to reflect on each situation and each contribution, everyone corrects downwards. To a whopping 123 percent, that is still widely off of the mark.32

Then there is the Dunning-Kruger effect, quoted above, that shows humans to have a tendency to overestimate the extent of their knowledge in most areas. In particular, those that know little seem to believe they are perfectly well equipped. The trick here is to challenge people to really explain what they believe they understand. When asked, for example, if we know how a toilet works, most of us would say yes. Once tasked to explain the mechanics we discover how very shallow our knowledge is.

And now, please reflect. How often have you read the results of a study such as: “X percent of decisions are made incorrectly” or “X percent of employees do not feel appreciated” to think immediately: “Well, that obviously does not apply to me.”

There you may have overconfidence sneaking in through the back door. Of course, there may be things that we really are sure about and can rightly be so. If you have yearly employee satisfaction surveys with well thought out questions and the employees keep on stating, that they feel appreciated and supported, then you can take this at face value and be justly proud. But for the vast majority of things, we merely assume that we know how great or useful we are to others.

This general overconfidence gets exacerbated, the longer someone has been working in the same position. Why? Because they do not get nor listen to regular feedback or spend too little time truly reflecting on themselves. Whether this reflection is possible depends very much on your respective corporate culture – yet in general there is the tendency that people higher up in the hierarchy are less likely to seek or receive open feedback.

There is also a fascinating piece of research that shows that CEOs who are in their first CEO role not only stay longer but also perform better than seasoned CEOs.33 Why? They do not believe that they already know it all – but keep on working on their mental models.

How does overconfidence express itself? Look at the quotes from these CEOs. Very little critical self-perception!

“A great scholar with high morality,” an “ambassador with grand mission and continuous contribution.” These descriptions by others about me concisely summarized me

(Member of the board)34

I help others find a coach but have never found the right one for me. But I get good feed-back from my children.

(Interview No. 36, CEO, 2,000 employees)

Interestingly, excessive self-confidence is even greater among board members of companies which they themselves have founded than among board members who join companies owned by someone else.35 The argument runs as follows: “If, against all odds, I have built something myself, then I must truly be better than others!” Whereas those board members who have joined an existing company realize that something decent has already been built without them and as a consequence manage to put their own contribution into perspective.

This means that you as a reader should look at yourself honestly and consider how you can make sure that you really know what it is that you can do and where you stand in comparison to others.

In one organization that I had the privilege of advising, the head of the department always stressed that his team was not very good and that it needed him to have the best ideas. He emphasized how hard he had to pummel and push his team towards success. When talking to the team members, they had a fundamentally different view: the head of department hardly ever listened, so none of the team members could present their ideas, and in the few cases where they did, the manager happily snatched up these new ideas for his own.

What else should you know about overconfidence to be armed against your own?

Women in management positions are statistically less overconfident than men. Full stop. A few examples: Only 43 percent of women say that they are the best in a group of four, compared to 75 percent of men.36 This is still overconfident, since the real number should of course be 25 percent but not as far off as the men.

Female top managers also take more cautious decisions and borrow less for their companies. They seem to have a longer-term vision, and try to have more financing options at hand when business opportunities arise.37 By contrast, more male managers believe that the volatility of the company's cash flow is less than it actually is. They therefore underestimate the probability of bankruptcy as well as the costs of emergencies and defaults. Those managers also have too much confidence in their own superior skills and are too optimistic about future growth opportunities and their ability to resolve any negative eventualities. As a result, they opt for less flexible capital structures with a higher debt-equity ratio and a higher share of long-term debt than women.

However, not all men tick the same way. There are differences that even seem to have some biological roots. A piece of research from 201838 revealed the following surprising finding: Men who were exposed to less testosterone in the womb had a tendency to become overconfident later in life!

First of all, you need to let this sink in.

And in the end, you have to ask yourself what exactly this means and how the researchers believe they can know how men had fared as embryos.

Researchers realized a long time ago that the length of the fingers gives an indication of how much testosterone the embryo was exposed to in the womb. If the ring finger is longer than the index finger, hundreds of studies have shown that this is an indication that the baby has been exposed to more testosterone.

With this knowledge in hand, a search for correlations started. How does this unit of testosterone, for a fetus, relate to different topics ranging from life successes, personality, diseases as well as overconfidence?

Interestingly, men with longer ring fingers seem to suffer statistically less from overconfidence and sport greater achievements than those who have shorter ring fingers. A similar result is seen in athletes where those who with more testosterone in the womb were measurably more successful.
