According to one theory, this biological starting point gives rise to a kind of “defensive pessimism.” This means that those who were bathed in testosterone expect less of themselves set lower expectations, so that they consequently can manage their own fears and perform better. This defensive pessimism is said to bring with it a feeling of alertness and attention, which then results in better performance.

If you should begin now to stare at your finger and be itchy to go onto the net to measure where your finger ratio puts you in these pieces of research best to heed one piece of advice: Don't do it!

First of all, none of the many experiments show a 100 percent correlation between finger ratio and outcome. Instead, what researchers find is a mere tendency. And you have to remember that statistics is the game of large numbers and is not relevant for each and every individual.

Secondly, and more importantly, a tendency means just that – not a must, but a can. And as we will see later, when a weakness is recognized, almost everything can be learned.

I suggest that you take this research only as an additional wake-up call so that you will be prepared to look at your own strengths and weaknesses with fierce clarity as well as generous kindness.

What is the downside if you don’t? As we saw in the introductory interview remarks – people may no longer hire you for a job if all you exude is overconfidence.

Take-Away: Defeat overconfidence – To think of oneself as better without foundation obscures the view of one’s own self. Only those who know about the human tendency to place themselves above others can correctly perceive themselves.

Self-Perception: The Imposter Syndrome

I know quite a lot of managers who build up distance because they do not have a solid self-confidence. And you can do that so wonderfully with 6 doors and an assistant between you and your employees.

(Interview No. 96, Board Member, 220,000 employees)

To love others, you need to love yourself.

(Interview No. 61, COO, 5,000 employees)

The opposite of overconfidence is underestimating yourself. This is the so-called “imposter syndrome”, named thus by researchers in the late 1970s. This syndrome describes a feeling which many successful people harbor that they will not be able to meet other people's expectations. Whatever they have accomplished in terms of grades, whatever they are praised for in terms of skills, they see this entirely as the result of chance. If something goes wrong, however, they see this as a personal failure which reveals deep and unchangeable truths about themselves that cannot be corrected with practice.

Researchers assume that 70 percent of all people feel this syndrome once in their life.39 Some admit to it openly such as Sheryl Sandberg, COO of Facebook: “There are still days when I wake up feeling like a fraud.”40 Howard Schultz, CEO of Starbucks, reveals his uncertainty equally if less directly: “Very few people, whether you’ve been in that job before or not, get into the seat and believe today that they are now qualified to be the CEO. They’re not going to tell you that, but it’s true.”41

In a 2014 survey with 116 CEOs and Top Managers, the Executives said that their biggest fear was being found out as incompetent!42

Although the imposter syndrome is reported somewhat more frequently by women, it also occurs in men, who according to studies are actually more stressed by it than women.43

Other researchers have called people who suffer from this feeling an “insecure overachiever”.44 If you describe this to an audience, in particular, of lawyers and management consultants, many of them nod. First reluctantly, and then when they see the large number of heads moving in the same direction, they do so more vigorously. Why is this feeling so strong in these groups? Both jobs appear to attract people who like to strive and strive and never feel satisfied with the result – or even with themselves.

In all of these cases, the result indicates that people end up completely misjudging themselves. The consequence is that they become hyper-perfectionist, afraid of feedback and do not dare undertake tasks that anyone in their environment would consider unproblematic for them.

We would not need to worry about leaders having impostor syndrome if it meant that they also became perfectionists at leading. Yet research shows that people who underestimate themselves often find it difficult to concentrate on their leadership tasks. Leading needs a focus away from yourself and your own fears, it also entails the willingness to let go – which insecure overachievers find harder. Instead, insecure leaders are trapped in unhealthy self-reflections about themselves and are fearful of being exposed. They consequently tend to cling to those elements of the job that they can control and do not progress on their road to becoming a leader who can rise above things and see the bigger picture.

This is often coupled with them feeling tormented by the fact that, if they were to cross their hearts and look into themselves deeply, they actually do not like themselves very much. For some, this even goes so far that they end up being fundamentally critical of their own being. Interestingly, many people do not even notice this dislike of themselves but have to be prodded into noticing it by coaches.

What is an indication for you not really liking yourself? Take a good quick test by looking at your inner dialogue. If you have too many thoughts along the lines of: “Okay, I've messed up again!” or “What a dumbo, I am!” or “Bet, I'm wrong once again. No way, will this work!” – then this may be an indication that you do not really accept or approve of yourself. Or to use the really big word: that you do not really love yourself.

In many of my interviews, executives pointed out that they knew of numerous people in their teams, that seem to undervalue themselves, that do not seem to really be a peace with themselves. The result is a lack of self-esteem, which in turn prevents them from weighing themselves objectively and with professional, loving distance. Research shows that people who do not value themselves are also more needy. They require more praise from others, overrate negative feedback and therefore see their strengths too little and their weaknesses too much.45 This means that such people do not act with humility simply because they cannot perceive themselves properly. Moreover, wanting to be liked by others impairs their freedom of thought and action.

Many people who do not like themselves also end up not liking others. They are unwilling to take feedback on board, since why bother repairing that which is broken and without value. They are tough on others because they cannot generate a warmth for others that they lack themselves.

The result: these then will actually be worse leaders than your happy go lucky narcissist, from whom at least some crumbs of self-love may drop onto others.

To sum up, research tells clearly that people are better off if they are friendly to themselves and appreciate what they have achieved and the struggles they have had. If they do this, they feel better mentally and emotionally.46 And emotionally stable and happy managers are far more effective.

The task therefore is not to be too skeptical of yourself – but instead see what it is that you do well. How to start? Try and see yourself through the eyes of a good friend. What would he or she say about you? And then believe that they know you and others well enough for you to be able to believe their assessment.

Take-Away: Not hiding one’s light under a bushel – For many, a lack of self-love leads to seeing one's weaknesses as too great and one's strengths as too small. This prevents true humility.

Self-Perception: Limits of Biology

Finally, biology sets limits to the correct perception of your real self. Some of these limits are very obvious. For example, in many organizations it is considered admirable and good form to get by with little sleep.

An American survey of 35,000 executives shows that only 28 percent sleep more than 7 hours per night.47 German managers on average sleep six hours and ten minutes, an hour less than the average German.48

It would be hubris as well as overconfidence to think that with little sleep you can work and lead just as well. Research is damningly clear: Tired leaders are less effective and less inspiring.49 Why? Because they are not in control of their emotions, they cannot see the big picture, nor can they intelligently steer themselves and others, since a vital part of themselves is somewhere dozing off.

It is, therefore, a sign of understanding one’s own limits if the manager realizes and act on this. Luckily, a few have started being outspoken about this.

Jeff Bezos from Amazon admits openly that he needs eight hours of sleep, in order to make good decisions.50 Carlsberg board member Cees' t Hart sees his performance suffers when he sleeps less than 7 hours.51

Slowly this knowledge is gaining traction. The above-mentioned survey shows that the higher the position, the more sleep the manager gets. As a group, most board members end up sleeping between seven and eight hours. Why? Perhaps the visibility in the organization and to the public makes a weak performance so much more dangerous that it becomes a corporate safety measure to reach for the light switch in the evening. Would it not even be the key task of the risk officers to ensure sufficient sleep in the company leaders?

Or is it the other way round: the executive has only reached the top position, since he or she has realized the impact of sleep on their own performance and has consequently, merely rationally, decided to sleep more!

You have to check honestly – how much sleep do you need in order to perform at your best? Nothing wrong with a few short nights, but if your way of working regularly causes you to sleep too little, you actually lack humility. Humility before the task you have taken on and humility before the bigger picture. Remember how King Canute demonstrated that there are bigger forces at play when he tried to command the water to stop rising and was washed away. In the same way there are bigger forces for us humans to contend with – one of them is our basic need for sleep.

The second one is nutrition. Does nutrition have an impact on you? Well, sure – everyone in today’s age knows about healthy eating. Yet, what is less well known is that perception and behavior also change, if you get your nutrition wrong. As trivial as it sounds: If you do not pay attention to your diet, you will not be the master of your emotions.

How can you tell?

Dozens of studies show that people with too little glucose in their blood make less considered decisions.52 Bad for a leader. They also end up being in a worse mood.53 Catastrophic for a leader who wants to be well-balanced.

An experiment from 2014 demonstrates this problem of emotional control in a delightfully absurd and memorable way.54 107 couples were asked to measure their blood sugar levels in the morning and evening. They were then given a voodoo doll with the following instruction:

This doll represents your spouse. At the end of each day, for 21 consecutive days, you put between 0 and 51 needles into the doll, depending on how angry you are at your spouse. You will do this alone, without your spouse being present.

Now that' s an experiment!

The result? The lower the blood sugar level, the more needles end up in the soft doll body.

What about everyday working life? It has also been shown that experts were less positive and less ready for change when they were hungry than when they were fed. With full stomachs, they were far more open to new things.55 This indicates that our brains need to be well nourished in order to work properly.

Now the question is, of course, whether you as a rational person would not notice that you are being irrational, simply need to eat something and need not be angry at your spouse or your employees.

Unfortunately, we typically fail to get to the root cause of the problem. After all, our brain economically prefers to pick the one thing that comes to its mind most easily. And until we train our neuronal pathways enough, it appears far more probable to them that our spouse has done something silly or the employee has messed up than that we have eaten too little. It is usually only in retrospect after lunch we notice others as being quite intelligent and pleasant after all.

Not all, however, is lost. As already indicated, we can teach ourselves to notice when we are drifting off into the primary needs of our body, behaving irrationally or embarking on making bad decisions, simply because we lack sleep or food. We can learn to notice when we lose the mental grip on ourselves. We can also learn to stop and reflect on how we are reacting, and then prime ourselves into imagining how we might react differently, if this was the first day at work after a long and relaxing vacation.

Or simply go out to lunch or home to sleep!

Take-Away: Accepting biological limits – There is a danger of disregarding the signals from the body and the brain. As a result strengths cannot be played out and weaknesses become stronger. Overall: less performance.

Having now identified the stumbling blocks in our own perception, let us look at what we need to be aware of in the interaction with others. Two key areas: Firstly, thinking that we are too obvious and clear in what we do. Secondly, overestimating our own significance.

In the Interaction: Considering Yourself as Being Too Obvious

Many do not have the courage to really give open feedback and then the employee is in for an unpleasant surprise after the trial period.

(Interview No. 66, CEO, 300 employees)

Our basic assumption is that we are clear in our statements and generally understood.

This is an illusion! We need to understand how far this illusion reaches, before we can really grasp our strengths and weaknesses. Let us look at one of the key tools that you as a leader have in order to influence others: giving feedback.

Ideally, feedback works in such a way, that performance, commitment and motivation is increased while at the same time the employee has understood where things have gone wrong.

What does it look like when we openly and honestly give feedback to someone? Not only to appreciate someone's strengths – which we will cover in the next chapter – but also to highlight problems and lack of performance.

A quick reflection on why this is important in a book about humility? Because humility does not mean that a manager does not share with others the truth where it is necessary. Appreciating someone for their strengths does not mean overlooking weaknesses. It also does not mean, promoting a person who is not aware of their weaknesses and not willing to do something about those that are harmful to their role.

When it comes to negative feedback, there is a dangerous automatism in our mind. We see clearly what it is that we want to convey. Therefore, we tend to assume that the other side will equally clearly grasp what we are saying. Are we usually right in this assumption? Fiddlesticks!

First of all, employees generally give their managers bad marks for their feedback. Employees from 53 countries found that only 5 percent, indeed, only a sad 5 percent of managers are good at giving open feedback.56 This failure to communicate clearly is expensive for companies. Either because employees do not notice where there is room for improvement. Or, they end up feeling unappreciated for skills and actions, the result of which will be stubbornness and a reduction of commitment.

Secondly, we are bad at giving negative feedback in a way that the employee really sees what we mean.57 One study made this crystal-clear: managers were given the task to give clear feedback to an employee. The goal was for the employee to understand that their performance scores were a very moderate 4.6 out of 7. What did the employees really hear? A solid 5.8 out of 7.

Furthermore, the managers thought it obvious that as a consequence of the bad feedback the likelihood of promotion was a miserly 30 percent, i.e., a promotion would be quite unlikely. What did the employee see? A rosy 60 percent likelihood.

Now as a manager you may think that the reason for this big gap lies in the employee simply not wanting to hear the negative. That the employee likes to live in their own world being in self-defense fiercely dismissive of any critique.

The researchers, of course, had thought about this and included an impartial listener for control. This listener came to the same rosy conclusions as the employee. In other words, the managers were clearly focusing way too much on themselves instead of what the employee would really need to hear. Simply and plainly, the managers were shirking their responsibility by remaining oblique instead of being clear and open.

How can this be avoided? Quite easily. As soon as the managers were told to reflect on there being a danger that negative feedback might not be entirely obvious, they communicated much more clearly. The gap between what the manager said and the employee heard shrunk even further when it was the employee who asked the manager for open and honest feedback. The power of such a question is enormous: Now I, as a manager, am being asked for something. It is then my duty to spell out things so they cannot be misunderstood.

What was the biggest impulse towards clear communication? Allowing the manager to also focus on themselves. How? Once the manager's bonus was made contingent on the employee's understanding matching the manager's assessment, miraculously, the feedback became very clear and easy to understand for each and every employee!

This means that anyone can easily break the illusion of transparency... as soon as they become aware of it.

There is another famous example of how people assume that their thoughts and statements will reach the addressee as intended. In 1990, a psychology doctoral student at Stanford University named Elizabeth Newton designed an experiment. Participants were asked to tap a song with their fingers. A second group was asked to identify the song. A total of 25 well-known songs, such as Happy Birthday, were available for selection. The interesting thing was not that the guessers only recognized three percent of the songs. That did not come as a surprise – if you try it out you will realize how hard it is to guess. What came as a surprise was that the people tapping assumed that a full 50 percent of their chosen melodies would be recognized.58 In other words, they were completely wrong about the transparency of their tapping for the other side. Why: Well, they heard the melody so very clearly in their head while they were tapping. That would surely be child’s play to recognize!

To sum up: we generally believe ourselves too obvious to others and need to invest mental energy into adapting our communication in such a way that we truly are understood.

As an addendum at this juncture, I would like to share another vital point about giving feedback. Research shows that women on the whole tend to receive less honest feedback, both from men and women.59 The important study in more detail: Objective observers were asked to rate a product. As soon as it was made clear that a bad product originated from a female worker, the evaluators adjusted their rating upwards by an average of ten percent compared to the weak male performers. While this may be pleasant for the woman in question, it means that she is not made aware of how she is really seen.

Here too, the manager must be very careful not to unconsciously adjust their feedback and thereby harm the entire organization in the long run. Because if a person does not know that they have done something badly – why should they bother to learn?

Take-Away: Become clear and unambiguous – Focusing on the self, obscures the view of what reaches or must reach the other person. If you truly see your counterpart, you avoid weak and ineffective communication.

In Interaction: Overestimating Your Own Significance

There are different ways in which we overestimate our own significance. These are important to understand for two reasons. Firstly, because only once we realize where and how we are seen in an interaction with others, can we be sure that we achieve what our role requires of us. Secondly, an understanding of these limits is vital for us in order to grasp the bigger picture. I need to understand, how much space of the bigger picture I can ever begin to take up. Once I realize how limited this is, I can – as we will do later on in this book – focus on the real bigger picture.

The Spotlight-Effect

People generally assume that they are seen more by others than is actually the case. This is the so-called spotlight effect.

Let us look at one study uncovering the effect: Students were asked to wear an embarrassing T-shirt. They were worried that at least half of their peers in the lecture hall would notice this miserable piece of clothing on them. In fact, only between 8 and 25 percent did.60

Other studies have looked at how much weight we believe our contributions carry in a discussion. The upside is that hardly anyone notices if contributions have gone wrong with a stutter, a slip of the tongue or with a faulty argument.

The considerable downside is that arguments we believe to be salient and decisive are rarely seen as such by others. This also explains why quite often we do not feel credited for our ideas. We believe that when we suggested something new, it would be obvious to others that it was our idea and that it was new. And that they acted consciously and with bad intent when later they brought up the idea as their own. What is, however, the case far more often is that the listeners did not really latch on to what was being said. When they end up returning to that idea, they have actually forgotten who voiced it first.

Conclusion: We are simply not as important in the lives of others as we think.

There are ways, in which this false perception can be corrected. For example, people are better able to judge their own impact on others if they have previously carried out a short breathing meditation.61 Why? The focus on the breath, plus becoming aware of what is going on in one’s mind as one tries to focus on the breath, allows the individual to take on an outside perspective. This is like the view from the balcony we spoke of before. As a result, they are no longer stuck in their own perception and can estimate quite accurately how they are perceived.

What does this mean for you as a manager? A number of things.

In general, you cannot expect anyone to realize what you want after only a few sentences. And even more so, you cannot expect that people perceive what it is that you do when you are not talking about it.

So, if you want something to stick, you have to be clear and repeat yourself, both in words and behavior. The CEO of Credit Lyonais summed it up: “One thing I really underestimated in my job as CEO: it's all about communication, communication, communication.”62

And if you want people to see what it is that you are actually doing, because this helps the role that you have taken on (and not your ego), then you must make it obvious. How you choose to make it clear is up to you. It may be lists with ticks, flowcharts with responsibilities or other ways to show others how you keep different stakeholders on board and what you do in order for your employees to be able to focus on their tasks.

It is vital in nearly all that you do, to take an outside perspective to understand what others are seeing. This holds true, when you are fearful of having exposed yourself or when you believe that you have done great deeds. Often people will not notice as much as you expect.

At the same time, we must make one important distinction here which has to do with peers, superiors and employees. Think back to the experiments? The students were not much noticed by their peers wearing the weird T-Shirt. The employees were not noticed by their peers or bosses when they slipped up in discussions. This is a whole different kettle of fish with your employees. They will actually notice every single one of your behaviors, your quirks, your slip-ups. And then you will be surprised at how seriously your employees take you.

As one of our former bosses once put it (when reminded of a previous declaration), “Why do you pay so much attention to the specific words I use? I certainly don’t.”63

Why is there a difference depending on the relationship you are in? Perception is related to status. Anyone with a higher status will receive more attention and be better remembered. Lower status people are only perceived more if they are threatening in any way.

The other way round, there is evidence that a high-status person will be less aware of what a lower status person has said and is doing. Even something as basic as face processing is influenced by the perceived social status.64 The brain of a higher status person is simply less concerned with or interested in someone of lower status.

This is, of course, a source of danger for the high-status person and may be one of the reasons that employees often do not feel seen. A manager’s brain needs more conscious focus to concern itself with someone of lower status. It is not automatically wired into the brain to be that attentive. The survival mechanism for which our brains have been optimized gears the attention towards higher status, not lower status.

There is also a danger the other way round: Just because you may not see your employees very clearly, you may end up not thinking enough about how you appear to them. This would be a mistake. Your employees (being typically in a lower-status situation) will notice every move you make, every inconsistency and every favoritism.

In total, to really be able to look at your strength and weaknesses, you must be aware of the fact that you will be seen far more by your employees and far less by your colleagues and people higher up in the company, than you might think.

Once you realize this, you can do something about it. If you are lucky, you may find some employees to openly tell how you are being seen. And someone to point out to you, how little you are being seen by higher echelons as in this example.

I had been selected for a high potential group at my company. At the summer party, the responsible HR person came up to me and said: “I do not see you. Therefore, you should not be surprised if other people leave you behind.” I replied: “Then give me a project, so I can prove myself.” I got one and that helped my general visibility in the company.

(Interview No. 96, Board Member, 60,000 employees)

More importantly, it is less about luck than about repeatedly asking and seeking a true reflection about how you are seen and then seeing if this is as you want it to be.

Take-Away: Do not think of yourself as too much or too little present – We fundamentally overestimate how much peers and superiors perceive us. Likewise, we fundamentally underestimate how much employees are always watching and analyzing us. A humble leader must have a good awareness of this, followed by an equally clear adaptation to the situation at hand.

The Role of the Team

Often people think – or are encouraged to think, that they are far more important as individuals than is actually the case. Remember the introduction to this book, and how the business press sees a very strong correlation between CEO and company performance? We have a similar effect with so-called star performers.

Who are these star performers? They are for example the top analyst, whom everyone trusts and who brings in incredibly high profits. Or the surgeon who saves every patient with his scalpel. Or the guru who is seen to manage every turn-around, or or.

These stars are often praised, told by their organization and admiring employees how great they are. No wonder that many begin to believe this. The test comes when these great ones change jobs – because they have been head-hunted for the enormous value they appear to generate. Off they go, striking out into a new world, on their own. Without their team.

That is where they perish. Bitterly. Research shows this for analysts65 and surgeons alike66. In fact, the “Star” analysts even succeed in significantly reducing the value of their new company.

Why does this happen? Because people often underestimate how important the existing team, the current processes and the whole working culture are for their success. They typically do not realize how much this contributes.

Again, this has something to do with the larger picture, which we will talk about later. But we already have to look at it at this juncture, since we can only understand our strength and weaknesses once we see how embedded we are in this bigger picture.

To recognize the dangerous trap of overvaluing your achievements and attributing them to yourself alone, you have to reflect deeply. Which of your strengths really lies within you and which are dependent on your environment? The honest answer to this question will be immensely helpful. If it is the team, the boss, the processes, then knowing this will help you to focus also on strengthening others so that you can continue to shine.

If, on the other hand, you receive the same feedback consistently across several employers, it is still worth thinking through whether these are your individual strengths or whether your key strength is in finding and developing the right people, superiors and processes. Both are great to have – and both give you the legitimacy to lead humbly with a solid self-confidence.

Take-Away: Do not forget others – It is fatal if we consider ourselves too important as individuals and overlook how much of our effectiveness depends on the team around us. Only those who are aware of how much they owe to others can be humble.

The Role of Chance and Luck

Humbition is one part humility and one part ambition. We notice that by far the lion’s share of world-changing luminaries are humble people. They focus on the work, not themselves. They seek success – they are ambitious – but they are humbled when it arrives. They know that much of that success was luck, timing, and a thousand factors out of their personal control. They feel lucky, not all-powerful. Oddly, the ones operating under a delusion that they are all-powerful are the ones who have yet to reach their potential. So be ambitious. Be a leader. But don’t belittle others in your pursuit of your ambitions. Raise them up instead. The biggest leader is the one washing the feet of the others.

Jane Harper, IBM67

I have been so lucky to be at the right place at the right time and being seen by the right people.

(Interview No. 80, CEO, 15,000 employees)

There is another very human flaw that we must make ourselves aware of. All humans, not merely the press or investors, love to find causalities. “Because I did A, B happened.” “Because we restructured the department, we gained three more clients.” Just like the journalists mentioned in an earlier chapter: “X is CEO, the company does Y, so this is because of X.”

The reason for this can be found in our brain, which especially uses its left hemisphere to find as well as invent causalities. These uncovered nuggets the brain happily tells to itself and others. This process is often summarized under the heading of “storytelling.”

Research has been fighting an uphill battle against our love of causality and regularly tries to prove the role of chance and luck to us.

Here are a few examples for our misconceptions.

It often seems to us that it is not the most talented people who are promoted, but the mediocre ones, those who only have little to show.68 Is this a conspiracy? Is it this a weakness of upper management, who does not want to let any one good rise up the corporate ladder? Is it egocentric narcissism in the leader, not wanting to be faced with someone better than themselves?

In most cases, it is neither but simply the pure power of randomness. The one mediocre employee meets a special mentor, the other is lucky that her boss moves to another company and space for promotion opens up. In the same way, the talented one may be unlucky: No position is available, there are three other equally talented people surrounding him, etc...

As there are always fewer of the best, it is pure statistics that average people will be more favored by chance, simply because there are more of them.

This effect of how chance played a role in success was demonstrated strikingly in an artificially created market for music. 14,000 participants were split into 7 separate markets and left to their devices to listen to songs, like them, share them, rate them etc.

The result: It was mere coincidence which songs became the most popular. Although the worst never ended up at the very top nor the best right at the bottom, all results in between were found. This means that the best does not necessarily prevail, and the worst not necessarily fail.69

At least in the film industry, this randomness is recognized by some seasoned executives. David Picker, a long-time manager of a major studio, said: “If I had made all the projects, I turned down, and turned down the projects I made, I probably would have had the same number of hits and flops.”70

Management thinker Jim Collins, whom I already mentioned, also found several CEOs who were aware of the role of chance and luck:

Joseph F. Cullman 3d, the Level 5 transition CEO of Philip Morris, flat-out refused to take credit for his company’s success, attributing his good fortune to having great colleagues, successors, and predecessors. Even the book he wrote – a book he undertook at the urging of his colleagues, which he never intended to distribute widely outside the company – had the unusual title “I’m a Lucky Guy”. The opening paragraph reads: “I was a very lucky guy from the very beginning of my life: marvelous parents, good genes, lucky in love, lucky in business, and lucky when a Yale classmate had my orders changed to report to Washington, D.C., in early 1941, instead of to a ship that was sunk with all hands lost in the North Atlantic, lucky to be in the Navy, and lucky to be alive at eighty-five.71

If we accept the role of chance, the question then remains how much impact a managers’ actions actually have.

This is where there is a big battle in the literature. First of all, when it comes to the importance of CEOs for the results of their company.

There are researchers who see a CEO’s effect on company performance of almost 40 percent.72 That is huge. Other researchers set the effect at a maximum of five percent. All the rest is luck, chance and circumstances.73

Both groups of researchers use large amounts of data and all are convinced of their reasoning. This shows another human aspect of our brain: in general, it loves to search for information that confirms its own opinions. This is called the confirmation bias and is of course closely related to storytelling. In this respect, every researcher after formulating hypotheses will tend to look for evidence to prove what he or she already believes. Of course, they are aware of this bias and also try to combat it consciously. Yet the key hope for us as readers is that due to the large number of people doing research in any given area, a clarity will eventually emerge that goes beyond individual biases.

How to reconcile these extreme positions: 5 versus 40 percent? Why not take the middle ground of 20 percent? Already Aristotle suggested this as a generally good way forward. On a less spurious note, numerous other studies actually point to this result as being a good one to place trust into.74 This means that, in the game of larger numbers, the actions of a CEO explain about 20 percent of a company's results. In fast-changing industries, the effect is greater than in regulated, more sluggish industries. Likewise, there are greater effects in smaller companies.75

Is the effect at lower levels of the hierarchy greater or smaller? Does a manager influence the results of his area of responsibility more or less than a CEO?

There is no concrete data on this. However, we already know from Chapter 4 that humble leaders have a measurable effect on their employees by bringing out the best in them through psychological safety and empowerment. As a consequence, it is up to you which of the following theses you see as more applicable in your company.

Theory No. 1: In a smaller area of responsibility, there are so many influential decisions, processes, cultural elements from other parts of the company that have a strong impact. The effect of a leader in such a situation can therefore only be less than that of a CEO. This is because the manager cannot work the large and important levers of the organization, but only small ones in his or her very own area. This also fits with the research finding that a humble manager at middle level cannot make up for a malfunctioning top management team.

Theory No. 2: Particularly where there is a clearly assigned arena to the manager, the effect should be much greater than that of a CEO. In this small world, the manager will have more direct influence on his or her employees, organization and processes simply because of the smaller number of employees, so that he or she will be able to move things considerably more. Again, there is research to support this, seeing as the effect of CEOs is stronger in small companies.

Many interview partners believe that there are generally stronger effects if previously a bad leader has been at the helm, regardless of the managerial level. In addition, the impact also depends on the type of leader the CEO is trying to be.

I suppose I can influence 50 to 100 out of our employees directly. A lot depends on what you find and what the economic environment is. Look at where I have landed. If it would be less than 30–40 percent influence, I would be disappointed. What I found was simply a mess with very unfortunate leadership. That makes my life much easier with much low-hanging fruit.

(Interview No. 123, CEO, 3,500 employees)

It depends on the role the CEO choses. If he or she is the sole decider – with nearly everything going across their desk, there will be a far lesser impact than if the CEO primarily sets direction and allows subordinates to act and decide within defined parameters.

(Interview No. 151, CEO, 3,700 employees)

One additional point is worthwhile mentioning. There will most likely be less of a difference in leadership talent on the top level, as compared to lower levels – making it easier to have a greater impact in management positions lower down the hierarchy. Why? When you look at CEOs, many have a similar educational background, followed by many formative years of work in different levels at the same or at different companies. Most will have gone through executive education; many will be working with a coach. This means that, statistically speaking, their skill set will not differ as much from each other as would, for example, differ the skill set of an IT specialist and a clerk.

It is very doubtful whether this same high average standard of leadership applies to lower-level managers. Remember that most have never had any leadership training. Also, there are so many different positions that it is likely there will be a far greater variance in the ability and fit of the manager. As a result, there should be a far greater differences in performance and in effect.

In general, however, your impact will always be stronger if an incompetent manager has been in charge before you.

By the way, while chance and luck play a large role, there are different ways to prepare an organization or your part of an organization for exactly these opportunities. You can run your area of responsibility in such a rigid manner, that even the most obviously great chances will bypass you. Or you can empower your people and organize your processes in such a way that even small chances can be taken up and transformed into successes. For this, the word “serendipity” springs to mind – the ability or skill for lucky and chance events happening to you. Chance and ability – they seem to be two contradictory concepts and are yet so very often linked. Think back to people or organizations or to yourself how on some days simply everything falls into place. Typically this is the case because you, your people and the organization were open to adjust and make room for the new.

There is always some part of luck. But it is the task of the CEO to ensure that the company can be lucky and grab the chances there are. You also need to have a basic optimism at all levels – then you will also be more open for the good coming your way.

(Interview No. 119, CEO, 200 employees)

Having looked at the role of luck and chance, the question arises: What does all this mean for your own strengths?

That you should be aware of them and at the same time not underestimate the effect of chance and luck. And not to overestimate your influence on the situation or the individual.

When it comes to your impact on the individual employee, please reflect. Can you recall times when you yourself worked under a boss? During how many hours of each day was your superior actually relevant for you? Surely that will have depended on the situation. There may have been times, when you were interacting with your manager all the time. And others, particularly later in your career, where you spent most of your time simply working on your tasks, talking to your internal and external customers, taking most decisions by yourself and only keeping your boss informed at weekly meetings. There may even have been times and positions in which you had no need for a boss at all.

There are many employees that focus primarily on the set task rather than on the person of their boss. In these cases, you as a boss will surely have a lesser impact.

But before you start becoming depressed and muttering to yourself what all this leading then is actually about, remember three things.

First, that there were certainly some very important inspiring interactions with your boss that had a large impact on your work life.

I was very proud when one of my employees said: ‘I loved it, it felt like I worked with rather than for you.

(Interview No. 130, Founder and former CEO, 300 employees)

Second, there are times when a manager really matters, for motivation, purpose, fairness and transparency. If done well this can lead to motivation and a willingness to work harder, happy to run the proverbial extra mile. And equally, another leader can then “ruin it all.”

I took over a business that was one of 14 companies in a Group of similar companies spread across the UK. This was an industry where there were league tables on all sorts of things – financial and non-financial. On almost all league tables, my new business was bottom of the table, or quite near. This was not a small business as it had around 1,000 employees. I wondered if they saw the situation in the same way. In a massive gamble I decided to ask every employee to attend one of two sessions in a large conference center where I showed them all of the league tables and explained the significance of each. I told them with great passion that I didn’t want to be the bottom anymore as this was my home and I felt it very personally. The reaction was incredible. Almost all of the 1,000 people decided that enough was enough and within an amazing year we were top, or near the top, on every league table. It was an incredible turnaround and the atmosphere was upbeat everywhere you went. As often happens with managers: when you are perceived to have fixed a big problem, you get given another one to solve. This involved me leaving the business and joining another Group company. Early on, the new leader managed to upset quite a lot of key employees, and at the end of the next year the business was back to mid-table. In the space of three years, the same people had been worst, best and average.76

Thirdly, the greatest impact of leadership will always be on the negative side when the manager acts or communicates badly, unfairly or stupidly. Bad leadership costs a lot of mental energy and motivation. And often makes people wish for another leader. Let us take a closer look at this.

A survey of 10,000 jobseekers showed that an enormous 42 percent had quit their jobs because they were dissatisfied with their manager.77 And as we have seen above, the loss of employees and the subsequent search for and training of new staff has high transactional and social costs.

In addition, more than 56 percent of employees are dissatisfied with their superiors and see them as bad leaders.78 (Which does not mean that they are always right. It may be good to know that there is line of research around the topic of ‘toxic’ employees).

What exactly constitutes bad management? There are several ways to look at this. What definitely counts as bad management is anything to do with the so-called “dark triad.” This contains narcissists (whose partly negative impact we have already discussed), Machiavellians and psychopaths.

Most academics take the concept of bad management one step further and conclude that between 35 and 60 percent of employees regularly experience this type of leadership.79 Allow me to take a closer look at these types of destructive and failing leadership.

First, tyrannical leaders. Those who humiliate, belittle and manipulate their subordinates to get the job done. Since they often achieve their goal for the company, they are usually viewed positively by their superiors. This is the most extreme form of bad management and is thankfully below five percent.

Secondly, the so-called “derailed” leaders. Around ten percent are leaders who act against their employees with harassment and manipulation on the one hand and against the organization by absenteeism, fraud or other theft of resources on the other.

Thirdly, there is a group with around 15 percent. These are managers who are supportive of employees but damaging to the organization, for example by giving excessive bonuses, being inefficient, not letting employees work for the organization’s goals or even making them work against them.

The fourth is the laissez-faire group with more than 20 percent. Here we need to make a distinction between the deliberate use of laissez-faire in good, independent teams and laissez-faire, where the manager withdraws from assigned responsibilities and duties. In this context, we are talking about the latter. If a manager avoids decisions, shows little interest in achieving goals and rarely connects with his or her subordinates, even when necessary – then he or she acts with destructive laissez-faire.

Of course, even this research does not mean that every action of these managers will be bad and have a negative impact. Yet we need to be aware that bad events are remembered more clearly than positive ones – so many managers that are in total seen as bad will have good sides to them as well.

This is the key point – even if destructive leadership should happen to you only from time to time, it has a significant impact not only on individual employees but also on the entire atmosphere in the organization.

This should then be THE motivation for you. Even if your upside impact may only be around 20 percent, you must be aware of a downside potential that is far greater.

Take-Away: Do not underestimate the role of luck as well as bad leadership – Humans are prone to overestimate their importance. Yet we are often favored by chance/luck and consequently limited in our planned impact, as far as the positive is concerned. In the negative, however, we quickly have a powerful impact. The focus must be to avoid leading poorly as a manager and causing wounds that (unfairly) outweigh anything positive.

Our own strengths and weaknesses must always be measured by whether our actions leave the company, employees and colleagues better off or worse off.

We have looked at different stumbling blocks that everyone of us encounters on the path to really seeing themselves. Now we will look in more detail at how to recognize one’s own strengths and weaknesses.

Recognizing Your Own Strengths and Weaknesses

CEO Finn was irritated. Once again, he was disappointed in his leadership team. Once again, it had failed to support him in generating a more open and cooperative atmosphere. He cringed when he thought back to the meeting. There he had been with his second in command opening the meeting by sharing the individual results of their 360-degree feedback. He had been sure that this would spark of a round of reciprocal sharing. Instead, the room had fallen silent and people had looked down to avoid eye contact with him. After a few minutes, he had proceeded to normal business. In the conversation with me, he was visibly annoyed and also disheartened. “It is amazing, that the team is simply not yet ready for a new style of leadership.” He seemed personally affronted and affected by the situation and referred to it more than once when mentioning how hard it was in this family business to get things changed.

What struck me in this conversation was that Finn did not for one second reflect, that he might have been at fault. That it was naïve to expect his managers to share their 360 results being put on the spot like this. None of them had been forewarned, Finn had not framed the sharing with a general conversation about how he would like to establish a different culture. One that was more open than the old one. One in which one could even share one’s 360. Then he could have asked, what each of the others would need in order to feel safe to share. He could also have talked to even only one member of his top team in advance, about what he was planning to get her or him on board. Had only one of “their own” started sharing, the rest of the team would have found it much easier to jump on the bandwagon.

This story made me realize how very hard it is to uncover your own contribution in any situation. How can you actually manage to see your own positive and negative sides more or less objectively? How can we learn with a second and third glance to understand what it is that we bring to the table and put ourselves into perspective? With our good and bad sides? With our unintentional weaknesses?

This is what this section is about.

Let us first look at some more examples, where people are unsuccessful or successful in really seeing themselves.

In an interview with the German news magazine “DER SPIEGEL,” the following interview was conducted with the conservative politician Friedrich Merz80:

DER SPIEGEL: “Nevertheless, certain qualities are always associated with you: Vanity, ambition, selfishness – there could be a reason for this.”

Merz: “My height is, of course, a conceivable projection screen for such prejudices. I look down on many people quite literally – the assessment of ‘looking down on someone’ then is an easy one to make.”

Merz is 1.98 meters tall, so he is really a tall man. Nevertheless, no one has ever heard anything similar of famous basketball player Dirk Nowitzki with his 2.13 meters – instead, he is considered a modest and down-to-earth basketball player. The friendly world star from next door.81

A second example comes from Carnegie and his famous book How to Win Friends, where he cites the president of a Wall Street bank:

For years I have kept an engagement book showing all the appointments I had during the day. My family never made any plans for me on Saturday night, for the family knew that I devoted a part of each Saturday evening to the illuminating process of self-examination and review and appraisal. After dinner I went off by myself, opened my engagement book, and thought over all the interviews, discussions and meetings that had taken place during the week. I asked myself:

“What mistakes did I make that time?”

“What did I do that was right and in what way could I have improved my performance?”

“What lessons can I learn from that experience?”

“I often found that this weekly review made me very unhappy. I was frequently astonished at my own blunders. Of course, as the years passed, these blunders became less frequent.”82

There is a personal humility debacle to add to this above story: I had found this book by Carnegie in a box with books to take. I had looked disparagingly at the title How to Win Friends and Influence People but still took it with me, since I had heard about it. The cover, however, did not seem like a business book which I would like to be seen with. It rotted away unread on my book shelf. Only years later did I realize a) my arrogance and, thanks to reading it b) what a brilliant book it is. So, I could immediately write down in my notebook: “Unreasonable rejection of a book as junk, just because it is well-known and has a funny cover,” but at the same time pat myself on the shoulder “Arrogance understood, put aside, book read and key concepts built into teaching and own life.”

Before we dive into your concrete strengths and weaknesses, let me remind you: All your strengths and weaknesses are relative – and they are always connected to the task you are currently engaged in or would like to take on.

One recommendation for truly uncovering yourself: Get a coach! The good ones amongst them can help you best in your self-reflection, better than all discussion with friends, colleagues or employees.

Eric Schmidt, CEO of Google until 2015, reports in an interview83:

Our CEO John Doerr said: “You need a coach”. And I said, “Well, I don't need a coach, I'm an established CEO. Why would I need a coach? Something wrong?” And he said “No, no. You need a coach. Everybody needs a coach”. And so, Bill Campbell became my coach, and that served all very well. Every famous athlete, every famous performer, has someone who is a coach, somebody who can watch what you are doing and say: “Is this really what you wanted? Did you really do that?” and give the perspective. The one thing people are never good at is seeing themselves as others see them. A coach really, really helps.

However, even for these coaching sessions, it is useful to have begun reflecting on your own strengths and weaknesses as a manager.

If you are interested in your personal strengths and weaknesses outside of your leadership role, personality questionnaires such as MBTI, Strength Finder, How to fascinate, Reiss Profile, DISC and others are an option.

One caveat: My experience is that the findings of these questionnaires are not always easy to integrate in everyday business life. This is even more so the case, if your company has no overall strategy of working with them. On the other hand, they may still be of use in helping to understand that other people actually are different. That sounds remarkably trite, but is not. All too often we assume that other people need the same kind of information, communication, interaction as we do. And following this assumption we are convinced that we are doing the right thing and end up communicating and deciding wrongly.

The most important first insight in all these profiles is that there is no right or wrong. There is only “different.” These tests show you along how many dimensions people can be different.

However, I must warn you against the “mental drawers” you or others might end up in. If you have ever done an MBTI, you may afterwards have said about a colleague: “Typical INTP: great analysis, but the involvement of colleagues and the proper communication of the results is lacking once again, isn't it?”

Or someone might turn to you and say: “Well, for an ENTJ that was a surprisingly modest statement.”

These categorizations tend to lead to the negative assumption that people cannot change because they have been identified as an ENTJ according to MBTI, as a harmony person following Clifton or as an implementer from Belbin.

Yet psychology and neuroscience show us that we can adapt strongly for different situations. For example, I have had two introverted bosses who have stubbornly trained themselves to speak well in front of an audience and to communicate often and openly with their staff. Why? Because, in order to achieve their goals, they knew that they had to.

Back to your strengths and weaknesses as a manager. On the following pages you can take action and begin recognizing them.

Step 1: What Does a Manager Need in My Job to Be Successful?

Make a list of what – in general – a successful manager needs in your area, your department, your company. How can you cluster this? Below I suggest a breakdown by knowledge, skills, tools, attitudes, connections, communication patterns, leadership styles and others. This may be the right structure for you, or you may create a different one for your job.

It is best not to stick merely to what you see at the moment, but to adopt an outside perspective. How does that work? You can consider, for example, what you would say to a head hunter in a briefing when it comes to filling your position. Or think about what an ideal candidate who takes over your job in a company (like yours) should bring with them. You can, of course, discuss this with colleagues so that you are not falling prey to some blind spots. It is possible that the colleague will see that you actually should be working much more closely with the legal department. Or that you seem to have fewer meetings with your team in comparison with other departments. This insight should then be included in the list.

There is a great story from an ex-Navy Seal officer John Havlik84 about the danger of missing out vital strengths.

In his book, John describes exactly how he managed to get into this special operations force. First “Hell Week,” which is a selection week, during which candidates get to sleep a mere four hours. In the whole week! Later he became Junior Officer, Assistant Platoon Commander, Officer of 14 SEALS. This was followed by the honor of commanding a special unit. He again succeeded and was allowed to lead a team of 40 of the best SEALs. Next step: The command of a special unit of 200 military personnel abroad. The upward direction of his career was clear. John played to his strengths – he was a great leader; he could deal with his team and the tasks at hand. He never really liked the political side of things, but that was not important alongside all the other leadership aspects.

And then it happened: his next promotion was rejected. Not just once. But twice. That was the end of his career. According to the up or out principle, John had to leave the Navy and the Seals.

And don't we all feel sorry for him? He was such a great leader and failed just because of some silly politics behind the scenes.

It quickly becomes clear that there is no need to feel sorry for him. It was his own fault: John had refused to see the subject of politics as relevant for him as a leader. He neither saw political power play as a requirement nor, consequently, as an area in which he was weak. If he did anything at all, it was to see his decision not to get involved in politics as a strength. A strength in the sense of “Politics is something unclean and unpleasant – it does not suit me.” Not getting into conversation with senators, not finding out from admirals what matters to them, not getting involved, not finding supporters etc. But the Navy was, is and will be political. Dozens of politicians like Kennedy, Nixon, Bush have served there and politicians have remained permanently interwoven with it. So, for John to say that politics is not something he wanted to focus on actually showed a disregard for the bigger picture, was stubborn and immature.

As so often the story has a happy ending. John realized in the end what had killed his chances, fought his way back, learned to be a politician and worked as a high-ranking SEAL for yet 17 more years.

What are the requirements for managers? Many, and different for each job, so as I suggested it may be useful to use the following list (see Table 5.1) to think through your job and what the different stakeholders need from a manager in your position.

Table 5.1 What Does a Successful Manager in Your Position Need?

Knowledge (e.g., about products, market, competitors, political developments, sanctions, languages etc.)

	

 




Skills (e.g., problem solving, creative thinking, customer orientation, collaborative working, etc.)

	

 




Tools (e.g., generating vision, exerting influence, making complex decisions, giving feedback etc.)

	

 




Mindset (e.g., fast is better than perfect; avoid risk, culture is the key, etc.)

	

 




Connections (e.g., to other departments, beyond the company, to customers, to societal groups etc.)

	

 




Communication patterns (e.g., humor, listening, storytelling, reserved, flexible etc.)

	

 




Leadership styles (e.g., charismatic, authentic, transactional, trans-formational, authoritarian)

	

 




Other (e.g., smart clothes, good manners, small talk, mentor, coach, club affiliations etc.)

	

 

Then take a deep breath and forget the whole thing for a few days. The next time you look, it is best to mentally go through a typical day in your job and put a “perfect” manager in place. That should give you some additional ideas. Also, in the second round you may find yet even more succinct terms to describe what it takes.

Step 2: What Are My Strengths and Weaknesses as a Manager in This and Previous Positions?

Again, take the following list, this time in two parts divided according to strengths (Table 5.2), followed by the weaknesses (Table 5.3). On the left you will again find the eight fields: Knowledge, skills, tools, attitudes, connections, communication patterns, leadership styles and others. And to the right are the strengths. Here it is best to think broadly and think of all the leadership positions you have ever held – because it may be that other elements were more important in earlier situations and that you were able to demonstrate these strengths at that time.

Try and find clear, concise phrases for strengths, such as “enthusiast for people and ideas,” “creative DNA,” “THE solver of conflicts” and for weaknesses: “conflict avoidant,” “lazy preparer,” “half-hearted delegator,” “control-freak.”

The motto here is, of course, to be open and honest with yourself. Therefore, I suggest a two-step approach: First, look at yourself and write down everything as it comes out on first thought.

Then a second look, but this time from the outside. Lovingly and as objective as possible. As if you stood on your balcony and were watching yourself at work. This should aid you in becoming aware of some of the stumbling blocks that we discussed above, such as unhealthy narcissism, overconfidence, negative self-perception, limits of biology, the danger of thinking yourself too transparent or too important.

Now you should complement your own picture with the picture that others have of you. Ideally, this is done in conversations with current and former superiors, with current and former colleagues, with current and former employees. Using the eight fields or any other structure you have given yourself above.

In addition, you should also ask for former and present clients. Where do clients suddenly come from? Well, they often notice when something is not going well in the team.

An anonymized story from a management consultancy

The associate had just finished his part of the presentation, sat down and happily reached for a biscuit. The partner hissed at him: “Don't eat! Listen!” In such an authoritarian relationship, it is unlikely that the associates would ever be able to give honest feedback to their partner. The valued client might have fewer problems with this – especially once the project is completed.

Perhaps obtaining personal feedback is not customary in your company. How can you still get some assessments?

One solution is to make the whole issue bigger: In other words, you start an open feedback process in the department, division, company. For everyone. Within the normal structure of anonymized feedback for everyone: One question about the strengths of the respective employee and the respective manager. Then one question: “What should this person continue doing?” That will give you your key strengths. Weaknesses will be recorded using the eight fields, followed by these three questions: “What should the person change?” “What should this person stop doing?” “What should this person start doing?” (see Table 5.4).

Table 5.4 Feedback

What should this person change?

	

 




What should this person stop doing?

	

 




What should this person start doing?

	

 

Now you have your results and your staff have theirs. Of course, you should offer at least three external coaching sessions per person to all those involved in order to enable everyone to learn from their results.

If this is still too much for you, you can at least gather clues by talking to friends and your partner. Of course, this will never be as meaningful as the alternatives above.

Step 3: Merging and Drawing Conclusions for the Current or a Future Job

Now, once you have both lists – what a leader in your position needs and what your own strengths and weaknesses are – make a third list, again divided by strengths (Table 5.5) and weaknesses (Table 5.6). Best to do this for your current job and for any future job you would like to have.
