For the strengths, you should note where and how you are already making good use of them and where you can yet make additional or better use. In other words, you should consider how much you can adapt your existing job according to your strengths. Most jobs have a surprising amount of room for maneuver when it comes to setting priorities, how you work and how you can involve your team or people from outside your team.

When it comes to weaknesses, you should consider which of them harm you, which ones you can show openly and which ones you should try to eliminate and how. This elimination can happen through practice, through coaching, through a different mindset etc.

You have now recognized your strengths and weaknesses. The next part will focus on how to show to show them properly.

Showing Strengths and Weaknesses Correctly

When I give feedback, people never write down anything about their strengths – only about their weaknesses.

(Interview No. 75, Group Head, 55,000 employees)

No one would define a brand only by its weaknesses!

(Interview No. 32, former Board member, 3,500 employees)

How exactly can you show your strengths and weaknesses? Without coming across as too weak? Or too strong? Can you admit to errors without it weakening your position? Can you be in charge and yet be humble?

How Do I Show My Weaknesses and Mistakes Correctly?

Brandon Black, CEO of the Encore Capital Group with 8,000 employees was experiencing a difficult phase in the company growth. Actually, he needed to close some departments and let people go. In his own words:

I needed to tell one of my direct reports, Fritz, that he was being let go. Instead of facing reality, however, I had begun to avoid him. Fritz had given me a call a few days prior to ask if there were any pending decisions about the Healthcare division. On impulse, I told him no. Upon reflection, though, I decided it was time for me to start being transparent with him. Fritz and I met for lunch at a local Chinese restaurant. I wanted to connect with him as a person instead of treating him as an issue to check off my list. After a few minutes of small talk, I told him, “When you called me the other day, I lied to you. I wasn’t prepared for the conversation.” I expected him to be angry. After a pause, however, he said, “I appreciate you being honest with me. I guess that means you’re shutting our business down?” “It does.” It was a relief to have it on the table. “I’m not surprised,” he said with a sigh. “OK, let’s talk about what needs to happen. I’ll do whatever it takes to help my folks transition safely.” I stared at him. He had just found out he was losing his job, yet he was professional enough to stay focused on our overall goals for the layoff. He expressed his desire to tell his team personally and to unwind the business in a way that lived up to the promises he had made to partners. At the end of the meeting, we shook hands. It was our first authentic conversation in a long time, if not ever.

When later Fritz was asked how he had experienced the whole situation he recalls. “In my two years at Encore, I felt closest to Brandon when he admitted he lied to me.”85

This story illustrates to me beautifully, how we as humans have the initial instinct to hide ourselves from difficult situations, not admit to others where we are fearful or off the mark and then how positively, genuine openness and vulnerability is received by the other side.

This section looks at how we can overcome this innate fear of appearing weak and how we best express ourselves.

A cool manager can always admit to weaknesses.

(Interview No. 86, CFO, 550 employees)

The old style was never to admit a mistake. Thankfully this has changed.

(Interview No. 44, Head HR, 15,000 employees)

I have never felt weak when admitting a mistake – you get rewarded by great trust and better relationships with employees.

(Interview No. 99 Board Member, 500 employees)

There are some interviews from the German Newspaper FAZ, in which famous people are asked what their weaknesses are. Here some answers:

Roland Koch, former Prime Minister of the State of Hessen: “Impatience.”
Rolf Peffekoven, Economist: “Impatience.”
Marcel Reich-Ranicki (†), literary critic: “Impatience.”
Donna Leon, author: “Impatience.”
Leander Haußmann, theatre director: “Impatience.”
Til Schweiger, Actor: “Impatience.”
Margot Käßmann, former bishop: “Impatience.”
Vural Öger, entrepreneur: “Everything here and now – impatience.”
Heiner Lauterbach, Actor: “Impatience.”
Nina Ruge, anchorwoman: “I always want everything. And I want it now.”
Roland Berger, management consultant: “Impatience; difficulty in saying no.”

Only Hans-Olaf Henkel, entrepreneur and former president of the BDI (Federation of German Industries), said with a considerable amount of anger: “Definitely not impatience, as many cowards say here – but self-righteousness, egocentricity and narcissism.”86

However brusque Hans-Olaf Henkel's answer may seem to you, research shows clearly that honest self-revelation of weaknesses (his self-righteousness, egocentricity and narcissism) is much better received than the fake answers before.

How do we know? A multi-level piece of research from Harvard delivers a solid indication.87

First step: Get people to write down what their weaknesses are. Approximately, 77 percent write an answer that is similar to the “humble-bragging” above. They write things like: “My inability not to be nice to co-workers” or “I'm too demanding when it comes to fairness.” Most write about these strengths dressed up as weaknesses for strategic reasons, believing they will, for example, get a job more easily that way. Only 23 percent own up to real weaknesses such as “I'm not always the best at staying organized” or “Sometimes I overreact to situations.”

Second step: The answers are coded either as humble-bragging or as real weaknesses.

Third step: An independent observer reads the answers and assesses how much they would like to hire the person.

The outcome is very clear – on a scale of 1–5, the humble-braggers are doing middling at 3.03 while the genuine answers have a nearly one-point higher chance of being hired – at 3.93. And we must not forget that the independent observers did not get a full profile of any of the candidates but merely this sentence about weaknesses!

Look at this example.

I have not invested enough time in team building, but this can be done step by step... My biggest obstacle is therefore time. I believe that my knowledge is sufficient, but not yet the time to use it.

(CEO 117)88

This CEO is not truly humble, when talking about his lack of time. Ultimately, he is saying that other things are far more important than team building – he is actually trying to turn a weakness into a deliberately chosen priority. Which means that he is trying not to show any weakness at all.

Yet, how can I show them correctly? From the animal kingdom we know all kinds of inferiority gestures that signal that you are weaker than the other party. Be it the presenting the throat, making oneself smaller or even slinking away.

All of these are not behaviors that make you feel good as a leader or that promise success for subsequent leadership.

What to do instead? Here is a good example.

Our new CEO recently presented the new strategy. The company had been extremely successful for over ten years, but the last two years had been more difficult ... The CEO ... looked to himself for the errors of the past. He said: “I myself was involved in the decisions of the past years as part of the management and made the mistake of not listening to my gut, but only looking at the returns”. That was the first time I had ever seen a CEO openly admitting his own errors to thousands of online participants. ... this was very well received, as we could see in our online discussion tool.

(Interview No. 4, Head Digital Marketing, 50,000 employees)

In this company, admitting errors gave credibility to the CEO and visibly brought employees on board for the coming change process.

Here is another example

In one training, I announced to my direct reports, “I am not a good listener. I just charge ahead. And so, that’s something that I need you to help me with because I’m just not good at it. When we’re in these meetings and we’re being collaborative, help me to remember to just be quiet and shut up for a bit and let people hammer out ideas.”89

That sounds open enough to get the buy-in from employees, and at the same time strong enough that employees do not lose respect.

For many, this is the golden rule: be open without losing respect.

Some hints on how you can achieve this:

By asking for support in areas of communication, as in the example above. In the case of interpersonal weaknesses which are obvious to everyone, it is probably quite easy to state: “I often speak too fast,” “I have to learn to listen more,” without losing respect. Why is this easy? Because everyone already notices it. If you do not address it, it will be the proverbial elephant in the room. And that elephant will cause eyes to roll anyway. So, it is far better to address the issue directly.

If you have the feeling that there is no right moment in your everyday leadership life for addressing such weaknesses – you can create one. What about a meeting in which you launch a general discussion on how to communicate within your team? And employees are asked to say where they think they do something well and where they find something difficult?

That sounds a bit like a silly game, but in most companies, it can be done quite easily. If you are afraid of running it yourself, there are excellent coaches out there who can moderate such a “day of culture”.

What do you do, if it is not about a weakness but about a concrete error? Research shows clearly that employees need you to admit your errors. In the 2016 Dale Carnegie Global Leadership Study, 84 percent of the 3,300 employees surveyed emphasized that it is important to them that their managers admits when they did something incorrectly. This was even more important than other topics such as “listening properly,” “appreciation,” “showing genuine interest” and “trying to put yourself in my shoes.”

I not only allow mistakes. I also admit them – “sorry, I did not intend this to be like this’. But I also know CEOs of larger companies that say: ‘Never admit to an error.”

(Interview No. 28, CEO, 14,000 employees)

How many of the managers dare to share their errors? A full 50 percent. Which at the same time is only a sad 50 percent.90 Yet, if the admission is not forthcoming, employees register negatively that the manager throws himself protectively in front of his ego instead of openly acknowledging and admitting what has gone wrong.

The conclusion for you: Simply learn to admit your errors. “Here I was wrong.” “I changed my mind based on the information you gave me.” “I'm sorry I didn't trust you.”

Look at this fine example.

A plant manager, we’ll call him George, was becoming frustrated at the lack of progress shown by his managers in terms of workstation organization. So, one day he simply went out and put little red “reject” stickers all through the factory floor on objects he felt were misplaced. That very afternoon, two of the managers came to his office to let him know just how furious everybody was. “Why?” he asked. “It was so demeaning,” they explained. “It made us feel like we were little kids being told to clean our rooms.” George asked them whether that was the general perception. “Yes,” they answered, at which point he realized he’d made a mistake: “Oops. It sounds like I blew it.” The next morning, without telling the managers, George went to every single operator on the floor and apologized personally. “Hey, I guess I offended everybody with these reject stickers yesterday,” he admitted. “I’m sorry. I screwed up.” The employees had never seen anything like it before—from George or any of his predecessors. In the words of one manager, “It blew everybody away,” not just because George was willing to face up to a mistake but also because he was willing to accept corrective feedback. Looking back, George realized, “I wasn’t trying to get any mileage out of it. I was just feeling like I screwed things up. But it was a real turning point. From that day on, the whole plant of over two hundred people and myself were truly one. … It was a truly cementing event for all of us.”91

Can you talk too much about an error? Figure it out for yourself: Suppose you are sitting across a competent person whom you experience as honest and open and they repeatedly tell you how shocked they are about their error and how nearly unforgivable it is, how unpleasant and how embarrassing. What would you start doing very quickly? Exactly: Say “It’s not so bad… everyone makes errors… I myself...” And you are already in a reciprocal exchange as equals and the error at stake is almost forgotten.

Emphasizing a fault should, of course, not mutate into a diversionary tactic. But if you have experienced first-hand how you almost wanted to comfort in the example above, you will see that an error on your part will probably have the same effect on others.

And if you still do not believe this, then I assume that you underestimate your competence and are probably hiding your light under a bushel. If that is the case, remember the “imposter syndrome,” take a few breaths, begin to look at yourself with affection and prepare yourself for sharing errors.

You should also look at the research of vulnerability. Brené Brown, who developed this field, defines vulnerability as uncertainty, risk and emotional exposure. If in the last sentence, you replace the word “vulnerability” with “leadership” you already get the sense that the two are closely connected. Vulnerability has to do with not knowing, with getting things wrong and of being in this together. Vulnerability is not about oversharing. It is not about hashing out all your weaknesses with the team, but it is about taking a step back from professional distance and cool and instead dare to be – actually – human. And from vulnerability stems a lot of courage. The courage to have open conversations with a mentor, the courage to ask for and receive feedback, the courage to not define your self-worth by status or productivity and the courage to admit to errors and weaknesses. The reward from teams and superiors alike is trust and a feeling of psychological safety.

Leaders need to role model vulnerability. Without shifting into the role of a victim, without being helpless or using it as a manipulation.

(Interview No. 65, Group HR Director, 70,000 employees)

We bring in vulnerability to help managers build connections with their teams, generate psychological safety and a sense of belonging to the culture – shifting from I to We.

(Interview No. 88, Head EMEA Learning, 100,000 employees)

The results of being vulnerable and admitting a mistake in many cases also work well when you share your errors with the level above. Like Bob Montgomery who had made a mistake that cost Southwest Airlines $400,000. CEO Herb Kelleher did not dismiss him, but said: “Bob, I am glad that you have finally learned from this.”92

Or like James Burke, who later became CEO of Johnson & Johnson, when he met the Chairman of the Board and expected to be fired for a failed chest rub for children.

“Are you the one who just cost us all that money?” asked Robert Wood Johnson. “Well, I just want to congratulate you. If you are making mistakes, that means you are taking risks, and we won't grow unless you take risks?”93

Another example from a medium-sized business: The production manager had overlooked that the customer magazine A still bore the claim of its sister brand B: “A new world every week.” Printing costs for this error: a whopping €200,000. He hurries to the managing director, offers his resignation and the latter says: “You messed up, but if you think I'm going to let you go, you're wrong. Just make sure it never happens again.” As a result, another correction loop was installed, respect for the managing director shot up and the story became known as a story of loyalty – not one of errors.

There are even some companies that go one step further and literally celebrate the failure of a project, i.e., the error of having embarked on an unsuccessful venture.

One of them is X The Moonshot Factory, an independent research laboratory of Alphabet. The CEO, Astro Teller, called Captain of Moonshots, summarizes their in-house error culture as follows:

Failure, seen properly, is just a recognition of fast learning. So, when someone raises their hand and says, “My project looks really good but I've discovered I don't think it's going to work out because of this subtle but important issue,” and those ten people now need to find a new jobs. If we say, “Sucks to be you,” no one at X will ever again raise their hand and say that. But if we say to them, “Hey, that was awesome. What incredible intellectual honesty you just displayed and good critical thinking to have discovered this. We're going to work super hard to find all of you new jobs somewhere at X or at Alphabet. We're going to give you all bonuses for having ended your project so thoughtfully. Take long vacations and then when you're done come back and join another project here and get going again with extra levels of passion because you're now working on something that you don't secretly know is going to fail at some point in the future, which is how you would have felt if you had stuck on your old project.”94

Life, of course, may be different, when you have a superior who never admits their own errors and slams down on those of others.

What do you do then? Or let me ask you differently? How well do you sleep during the time, when you have not reported a relevant error? Typically, not well. The anxiety will also make it more likely that you will not be your best at work, but instead will be prone to other mistakes. The only way out will be to bite the bullet and address the issue even with an unhumble boss. Will this damage you? Possibly. Can you make sure that is does not kill your standing with your boss? Well, you can decrease the likelihood by being aware of a concept called “Hedonic Editing.” This shows that we humans like to optimize our emotional state by having more positive emotions than negative ones. Or would you rather have 365 presents in one day instead of one a day? In the same way, we try and bundle negative pieces of news into one day. So, if you want to make sure that admitting an error or a weakness does not ruin your ability to continue fulfilling your role, the idea is to own up to all-in-one day and take the full brunt for it. Then a few days later, you come with a piece of positive news. A few days later with yet another one, and again… This way your boss will have ONE negative day – possibly consisting of a number of errors, and THREE positive interactions with you. And most likely he or she will be able to put you more into perspective – seeing you fully and not just as Jack or Jill who has messed up or is weak.

Is this manipulation? To my mind no! You will be doing most things well and only a few things will go wrong – focusing only on these is actually unhumble, giving a skewed picture of your work prowess.

Let us return to your employees. If I admit a mistake, will they not lose respect and my leadership subsequently become less effective? Will people not label me as a failure once I admit to one? One interviewee pointed to the following issue:

People are worried, that if you admit to a mistake or weakness, people will refer back to it. “You yourself said, that you could not do this well…”

(Interview No. 93, Head Talent Management, 36,000 employees)

Especially younger managers with older employees, fear that they will be seen as weak or as not meeting expectations. People also doubt that it is possible to admit errors in very hierarchical companies or in the military.95

Research, as we have seen above, clearly says otherwise. Just to remind you of Officer 2 who not only impressed his superiors but also made his Sergeant happy when he started listening to him. Or do you worry, that the Sergeant would now think worse of the officer? Well, from the description of the case, the Sergeant thought the worst possible of his Officer already during the exercise. Now not being given a chance to assist but also being ignored or chided would add injury to insult and alienate the Sergeant fully.

Take it as set in stone: employees can deal with errors. But what about the label you have been given, when you admit to a weakness or an error? Sure, it may happen in immature companies: “He’s the guy who messed up X,” “She lost that project by making that stupid mistake.” Yet if you as a leader model growth and change and more importantly openly appreciate growth and change in others your employees will not define you by the past but by the present.

What are the positive results of admitting mistakes?

Firstly, research shows that if a team knows from its leader that he/she admits mistakes, this strengthens the team's “psychological capital” and results not only in increased self-confidence of the team, but also in increased trust in the leader.96

Secondly, admitting mistakes leads employees to perceive the leader as more honest, warmer and more cooperative.97

Thirdly, admitting to errors lays to rest the “too-much-of-a-good-thing-syndrome.” If you as a manager are always perfect, your employees see you as too far removed from them, fail to be able to see you as a role model and subsequently struggle more with their own moral identity.98

Nevertheless, I must not conceal the fact that there are some downside effects when you reveal your errors. As a result of your honesty, the employees see you as somewhat less in control. They see you as slightly less effective, influential and autonomous.99 Now, as a professional and humble manager, you should not care how exactly you are perceived, as long as that perception does not in any way impact your effectiveness. After all, the goal of leadership is to create better employees, not to be mistaken for Superman or Wonder Girl. And if your error increases the psychological safety and thus the performance of the team, and only you are no longer seen as being most powerfully in control, so be it.

Again, you should be less afraid of what the employees think of you when you show a mistake than what they think of you when you hide a mistake! You should also ask yourself how willing employees are to show their faults when you hide your own!

So, it is not surprising that a manager told the following with great satisfaction.

This [humble leader’s] mantra was “Failure finds its grace in adjustment.” This phrase has impacted me and my work a lot. If I were to tattoo something on my body, that phrase would probably be it.100

For those of you who are still skeptical, let me put this issue of weakness even more into perspective. Admitting errors or weaknesses is not about presenting yourself as weak, but of addressing “one error” or “a weakness.” These are two different things.

All you are doing is to show in the overall context that errors can happen to anyone and that nobody is perfect.

Yet, I am fully aware of the fact that there may be times when it does not feel right to show a weakness or to address a fault. What can you do then?

Quite simply ask for advice. If you are not sure how best to solve something or how to make up for a mistake, you can go to a member of staff who is experienced in this field and just ask.

If you have not grasped a technical issue you can say: “Lars, you are our IT expert. Our proposed system has so many requirements – from your point of view, which of the many offers we have been sent is the best for us?”

If you have slipped up: “Layla, we have ended up in this unfortunate situation. How do you suggest we fix it?”

Including others and asking for advice firmly puts the focus on the action needed for the future, not on the past with errors or the present gaps in your knowledge.

People who are asked for advice actually feel honored.101 This is especially the case if the situation is tricky, which it will typically be in those areas where you as competent manager have weaknesses or commit errors.

The icing on the cake is that the person asked for advice ends up regarding the questioner as more competent and wiser thanks to the question. The inner working goes something like this: “If someone comes to ME with this complex topic, they must be clever.” This has positive payoffs both as regards how appreciated people feel and how you are evaluated.

My task as a leader is not to know things better. Instead, it is to give employees the feeling that they know more than me. This way, they feel more successful.

(Interview No. 131, CEO, 3,000 employees)

People are happy when they can help me – so sometimes I let them think that I am really struggling with technology. We laugh and joke together about it.

(Interview No. 104, CEO, 200 employees)

From my own experience: There is hardly a phrase that will be better at getting you a higher bonus than the following: “I have realized, there are some things I cannot not do on my own. During the next year, would you be willing to help me with X and Y?”

Asking for advice impresses the other party – with some amazing results beyond the bonus round. Research shows that executives who ask the CEO for advice are much more likely to be appointed to other bodies, such as supervisory boards where this CEO is a member. This shows that asking for advice works both upwards and downwards. Instead of triggering something negative, it results in a positive assessment of you as someone who appreciates the other and is willing to learn.102

Of course, in a book on humility, I am not going to suggest that asking for advice should be used as flattery – because that is not what it is for. But I put it to you that if the extreme version “I flatter the CEO and influence him/her with my many questions” works, then there is room for “normal” questions and advice seeking both to people above and below you.

Ergo: Getting opinions on a failure or a weakness is a powerful instrument.

Is the fear justified that your error or weakness will persecute you and never be allowed to go away? And I know, I am repeating myself here – yet the topic crops up again and again.

Well, on the one hand, as we already saw, you can simply point to the change and improvement in an area where you used to fail – and hence be a good role model for learning. On the other hand, you can agree that there may still be something in that area which you cannot do – but that is why you are glad to have Mrs. Y or Mr. X at your side. Easy-peasy!

As a final straw taken of your back: Research also shows that getting ideas and feedback, creating an open atmosphere for all levels, is perceived as a positive strength of the manager. Not only are people who ask questions seen as more competent – they also appear as more congenial and are liked more. One limitation: This effect is only valid in the dyad – an independent observer is not influenced.103 Asking questions seems to be about the specific attention that is given to the other person or your team. Then something relational happens which leads to mutual respect and greater liking.

Take-Away: Showing weaknesses makes you stronger – You can show your weaknesses or reveal errors in many more situations than you might originally think. It is actually not an option to hide your errors and weaknesses. Employees need you to show both. In some situations, it may be sufficient to resort to asking for advice. This is perceived positively and the person asking the question even gets a competence bonus.

Showing Strengths Correctly

Markus Sontheimer had just been nominated as CIO at DB Schenker, a company with 76,000 employees. The first Global Leadership meeting took place a few weeks later.

I went around the room, introducing myself. Then, when it was my turn to address the whole group, I said amongst other things: “I am not your slave. IT has to be on par with business. We are not there to simply implement – but we can bring something to your table, the same way as you with your business units can bring something to our table.” Some of my people were at the meeting and were shocked. No CIO had ever spoken as clearly as that. But I had to position myself strongly for the sake of the department and the greater whole.

Here was someone who had understood that he had taken on an important role and that he needed to make sure that both he as a person as well as his function in the company was seen in the correct light. Only once Markus was perceived as strong would his department be able to truly support the other teams and with it the whole company. (There is a clear indication that this clarity in behavior pays off – Markus is now CIDO (CIO and CDO) at ISS, a company with more than 250.000 employees!)

This is a good example of how you can show your strengths properly.

Let us look at another situation. A real, anonymized situation, one in which the relationship between a team leader and the divisional manager is at stake. So, two managers, but at different levels.

In order to be promoted from team leader to department head, Nikki had to complete two interviews. One purely personal and one professional. Both were to be conducted with the present supervisor, divisional director Sandeep, who was known to be reluctant to promote employees. In the second interview, a departmental manager for technical issues was also to be present. One hour before the first interview started, Nikki received a call that Sandeep had fallen ill and would conduct the interview from home via Skype.

In the Skype call, Sandeep was sitting in front of a bare cupboard door with a wilted bouquet of flowers in the background. After a short introduction to the meeting, he said: “Well, Nikki, I already know all about your strengths. Why don't you tell me about the learning fields that you see in the new position?”

Nikki responded over the next hour with clear and intelligent points regarding her learning fields.

In the second interview, for the technical discussion, department head Clare was also sitting at the table, hardly taking part in the conversation, but typing most of the time on her mobile phone.

Sandeep started the interview and said:

We know all about your strengths, yet, we need to talk about your development. You are still a very reserved person. You are not really prepared to talk about yourself and share things. You simply need to loosen up. I have already told you about my coach. He is great. You will be in a room with 300 other people. It's a great atmosphere and everyone is relaxed. I mentioned before that you can call my wife to get in touch with this coach. I like to see this as missionary work – it is very important to me.

Nikki was amazed by the course of the conversation and courageously answered where she could. She showed a willingness to open up in some future situations, but left open the question of whether she would ever go to the coach.

She did not get the job.

What went wrong here? Had she not “humbly” faced the process, answered questions, reacted wisely and sometimes wittily? Yes. But in both conversations, she had simply reacted, not acted; she had put her light under a bushel and allowed irrelevant topics to dominate the conversations.

What would a conversation with humility have been like – a conversation in which weaknesses AND strengths were properly acknowledged?

What if she had reacted differently from the very beginning?

Thank you, Sandeep, for mentioning my strengths. I had listed them in my letter of application. It would be important for me to understand which of these are particularly appreciated by you and which in your mind are the most important for the new position. Could you share with me the strongest three from your perspective, please?

That way, Sandeep would have had to consciously call the strengths to his mind and express them. Is this important? Yes, since research clearly tells us that something is more present and we remember something better when we say it out loud.104 Likewise, we feel more committed to what we assert publicly than to what we do not say.

This means that Nikki must consciously remind the boss of her strengths and get him to name them – only then will he duly appreciate these and have them present when he makes his decision. The way the conversation went, Nikki allowed her “learning fields” to dominate Sandeep’s brain. Who of us would be able to think a person strong who has just spent the last half hour talking about their learning fields? Remember the hedonic editing from above? Same thing here – Nikki is in charge of making sure that the brain of her boss is not only focusing on the negative.

Next step – what should she have said about this almost impudent term “learning fields”? How about something like this?

What you say about learning fields.... “Fields” are huge plains; I see something like gigantic yellow rape fields in front of me. I do not have anything resembling this. I have no learning FIELDS. I have individual learning POINTS, like the ones that are part of the course in every new job. And as you know from your personal experience and from watching me, we all grow by doing. As I have grown in my present tasks, I will grow with my new tasks.

Completely different! A clear positioning with a focus on strengths and an alert and calm approach to possible weaknesses. How on earth could Nikki know exactly which of her weaknesses would potentially be an issue for her in the new job? She can guess but really knowing requires the full situation to be at hand.

Humility also has something to do with the courage to get the attention you need. This is connected to bigger picture. If we assume that Nikki thinks that she will improve the company by being promoted, then it is humility to ensure that she is seen and perceived for what she can do. If the bigger picture shows that she would make the company worse of or only the same by being promoted, then she should not even apply for the new position.

Can people really do this and take a step back? Two examples:

The first actually comes from my own experience: At the management consultancy, the question arose as to whether I should be promoted to project leader. The situation was somewhat shaky, since at that time, I had not yet fully taken response for my working life – preferring to feel victim of the tremendously long working hours. I was simply not hundred percent engaged. I realized that being promoted would, of course, be pleasant for my ego – but that it would actually make the company not better by one iota. That realization helped me search for and find a new job in which I ended up finally taking full responsibility for my every day actions and behaviors. (Every time I read through this admission of mine; I feel a twinge. That the reader will see me as failure. But then I remember the sentences I wrote above about vulnerability and impostor syndrome and let it stand.)

The second example comes from one of my interviewees. He told the following story:

I was in charge of the company operations in one country. Now a global role was offered – increasing the number of employees for whom I would be responsible from 10,000 to 80,000. I was happy to accept, believing that I could indeed be effective in the new position. Later in the week, however, I encountered a colleague from another country, younger and more visionary than myself. So, I went to the CEO and suggested that this person should be promoted to the global position.

Impressive! Both the ability to see the bigger picture of what the company needed and being able to keep the personal ego reined in.

By the way, there is a lovely end to the second story: On the day, that the person who had been chosen, started the job he rang up my interview partner and asked him, whether he would be able to be his mentor. This also clearly shows that this new boss did not think the other weak for having stepped back, but instead felt that much could be learnt from him

Getting back to Nikki – in her case we assume that she thought she could make a positive impact. As a consequence, she has to make sure that the other side sees her in all her strengths as well as weaknesses.

In addition, Nikki has to be aware that the other side may actually not be interested in promoting her at all. This is neither evil nor unfair, but simply often the case. In other words, if you want something, you have to make clear why you can do it and take control of the conversation.

That would have been equally possible in the second interview. What would have been wrong with the following reaction?

Sandeep, I sense a feeling of surprise in me. What you are basically saying is that people in your department only get promoted if they work with your coach and behave in the same way as everyone else on your team. I find that strange and expect that the works council would as well. I would therefore prefer – as planned for this interview – to focus on technical issues. Let me describe a difficult case to you and Clare, on which I would love to receive your advice....

And now a technical discussion begins, which involves the boss and colleague as counsellors.

With this sort of example, it becomes clear that we may easily hide our light under the bushel without even noticing. The result? Our strengths, which we believe relevant for the bigger picture are not properly seen.

What does that mean for you? Do you have to think before every meeting with superiors and employees briefly about what the issues might be and which of your strengths fit best and how you can use them for the best of the company, the employees and yourself as part of the whole? Yes. Exactly. You must!

If there is a critical discussion with the board in a crisis situation and you want to make sure that you can support them with your strength of “calmness,” you must first consider how you can do this without appearing dispassionate or as if you are not taking the situation seriously.
If a staff meeting is about brainstorming and you yourself have a lot of ideas, you need to be aware beforehand that if your ideas are expressed too early, they will frame the others or prevent them from putting forward their own. You need to prepare yourself mentally to first listen quietly and openly, to secondly be prepared to throw your own ideas overboard and thirdly to have confidence in your own creativity so that you can play a good part in the subsequent discussion.

This sounds as if you always have to plan how you will perform at work and that you can never relax.

Exactly: that is the way it is! You have a special role as a manager. You are a permanent role model, so you must ALWAYS stay in your role. In the same way that you do not scratch your stomach or pick your nose in front of your children or on a train. As soon as you leave the bathroom in the morning, you take on one of your many daily roles, just like everyone else. Most of us realize this – the few exceptions shake us up severely.

Unfortunately, working life is sometimes not seen as a metaphorical train, where you have a public role, but as a place where you do not have to exert yourself, but can merely “be.” This is inappropriate. After all, you always have to be aware that all your actions have a powerful communicative effect on employees. An example for this.

Paul Anderson, then CEO of the Australian BHP Group, an Australian-British raw materials group with around 30,000 employees, was concerned. Ever since he had started as CEO, the company’s safety record had bombed. In comparison to the year before he took charge 2,000 more accidents had happened. And this despite the fact that Paul saw safety as a key topic. He had tried to position himself as a real proponent of safety.

He asked the head of safety to come to talk to him. He urged him repeatedly to tell him the reasons for this catastrophic result. The head of safety skirted around the issue; he prevaricated; he evaded. Pushed by Paul he finally burst out with the following:

You are to blame! Everyone knows you prefer to ride your Harley-Davidson motorbike without a helmet. The speed limit in the garage is 5 miles per hour. Everyone knows your car and everyone knows that you drive much faster. During factory visits, you don't follow safety regulations such as holding on to the handrail or wearing safety glasses at the right time.

When you visit a factory, safety is never the first thing you ask managers about – you ask about the cost, reliability and quality of production. Only then do you come to safety.

There were yet more examples and Paul sat back amazed and shocked. He realized that he was being closely watched in all his actions and that his behavior as well as his questions inadvertently had sent the signal that safety was after all not important to him. As a result, no one paid attention to it.

As for many stories in this book, there is a happy ending. Anderson took the subject so seriously that he started to behave in a conscious manner, and in a few months, he had turned the statistics around.105

There is no happy ending to the following story.

It was the company's summer party. There were benches in the garden, a barbecue and a volleyball court. On the way to the garden, two employees were surprised to see how one of their managers, who liked to think of himself as cool and not wanting to show his age of 50, stood by his car and was just changing from suit and shirt into T-shirt and jeans. In public!

Here we have an issue of a presumed strength. The managing director wanted to be cool, something which may at best be a strength in start-up – if at all. But in this company, he had misjudged – it was not a strength, but instead a weakness: not understanding what the company needs from its leader and not thinking through how his actions might be perceived by different employees. As we have seen, managers are by far not the only reason for good performance, yet they are one of the core shapers of company’s atmosphere. They quickly become the yardstick for both good and bad. Their actions cause employees to react positively or negatively.

As in this example. Suddenly employees start to reflect on how professionally their boss behaves – in general. At the next customer meeting he will be observed much more closely. Does he adhere to social standards in this case? Is it correct that he does not wear a tie to a meeting with VIP customers? Is it okay that he jokes with his Chinese customers about Chinese cooking? Any other unprofessional behavior is registered by the employees who have a sense for this kind of thing and discuss it with others. And organizations have a long memory.

That is why for showing your strengths you should instead take as a role model this senior manager. He told me that he thought through every action, every conversation and every meeting as if it was a chess game. In other words, he looked at his own position, the position of the other side, and how that other side would see themselves and him. And only once he had understood this picture, did he take action or go into the meeting. Not surprisingly he has now been made Executive Vice President of one of the largest conglomerates worldwide.

All in all, this means that you should show your strengths in such a way that they have the greatest possible benefit for your employees, the organization and for yourself.

Why “yourself.” Humility does not focus solely on the other person, but also takes into account your own life. That which is good and strong in you is also valuable and where it fits in the bigger picture it should be brought to the fore in such a way, that you can help making where you are a better place.

That means, no one is served if you are weak. To fulfill the bigger role that you have, you must be able to show your strengths just as much as your weaknesses. In fact, you also need to know what represents the currency of power and strength in your company.

What do I mean by this? In one company information might be key, in another realized profit. In yet others, the most important currency may be closeness to the CEO or the power to staff projects.

Whatever it is, it will have an influence on the bigger picture as well as on your position and your humility.

One anonymized example, that is hotly discussed when I bring it up in teaching is the following:

Let us call her Jo. She is head of HR for a large midsize company of 20,000 employees. During her tenure she has already introduced some fundamental cultural changes: She has moved from the plush Executive Office into a normal one where she sits with her assistant. That move raised many eye-brows. Yet she spends so much time on the road that for her it is vital to be with her assistant on the days that she is in the office. As a result, she is also seen as easily approachable compared to her predecessor who used his assistant to shield himself from too casual an interaction with his employees.

There is in her view, however a limit to what cultural topics she can address right here and now: One of the internal pieces of currency, one of the sources of power is the outward display of just that power via company cars. All the top managers drive large BMWs. She, personally, would rather not have a large company car. Why? What is your guess as to why? Because privately she drives a Jaguar. (I love this bit, because most people catch themselves having thought – “typical woman, does not value a cool car” – and now she drives an even cooler one….) Yet, in order to have her employees see their department on par with others, for the other top managers not to feel judged by her by choosing another car, as well as for them not thinking her weak, which in turn will weaken her clout in the company she has decided to go with the BMW.

Some managers see this negatively and regard it as opportunistic. Some say, this has nothing to do with humility but is a general power move.

My view differs: To my mind, she has truly understood the bigger picture: There are some rules she has to accept for the moment in order to be able to get through her more important changes. And it is humility to recognize that she cannot change everything at one go.

This argument does, of course, not hold, when the company is doing something against fundamental values. To go along, for example, with criminal behavior at Enron, so as to not endanger a planned culture change is not be an option. Yet here, where it is something that does not concern fundamental values, to my mind it is humble not to start a fight about the car, but instead concentrate on the other issues at hand. I see her taking the BMW as a strength and not a weakness.

Take-Away: A duty to show strength – It is up to every manager to show both – strengths as well as weaknesses. If the manager hides their weaknesses and errors, the workforce will notice and judge negatively. If they hide their strengths, they will rob themselves and others. No one benefits from this.

Managers must be aware of their role. They are role models every moment of a working day: they influence the culture of their team and must therefore neither let themselves go nor forget to check whether their behavior is in line with what they want to achieve.
