Chapter 6 Appreciating Others

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-6

Praise often. Admire rarely. Chide never.

(Walter Serner, Austrian writer, 1889–1942)

Summary

The consequences of appreciation are enormous in terms of performance and motivation in the appreciated. Appreciation of others is very much about perception. It is initially difficult to perceive others properly, although this can be trained. It will be discussed how appreciation can be demonstrated and which models can be followed in order to position not only the positive but also the negative or that which needs to be changed.

In his book The Listening Leader1, the former head of communications of the insurance giant Allianz, Emilio Galli-Zugaro (one of the most appreciative people I know), tells roughly the following story: If you were invited by the Chairman of the Board of Directors to a beautiful Allianz property in Bavaria together with up to nine other top-level employees, this could be your ticket for the way up. Several days were spent there, with plenty of time for relaxation, excellent food and good conversation. The last evening was a superb dinner with the CEO.

Oh, that must be the famous “fork test” of whether one can behave well at the dinner table. In other words, not merely using the right cutlery, but also being able to hold a good conversation.

Wrong! That was not the test at all. After the meal, it was not the CEO who evaluated the 10 potentials, but the gardener, the waitress, the chambermaid and the rest of the staff. Only those who had treated them well, not treated them arrogantly or derogatorily were considered for the really high posts.

With its recruitment test, Allianz ensures that it only recruits high-level managers who look beyond their own ego, who perceive and appreciate other people as human beings. Even if this sounds obvious, we often forget this in our everyday hassle.

Employees are human beings too. It is important, not to see them as different.

(Interview No. 18, VP Global Head, 110,000 employees)

You need to meet people symmetrically. I am neither their victim nor their judge.

(Interview No. 125, Group CEO, 1,200 employees)

When a new employee joins my team or we have a guest then I make the tea. I may have a different job but am not different from them in any real sense.

(Interview No. 130, Founder and former CEO, 300 MA

When working for Amazon, it was great to realize that I do not always have to talk in order to be noticed or appreciated.

(Interview No. 24, CDO, 6,000 employees)

I call myself the Chief Ignorance Officer. Others know their topics so much better, so I can only get things done with their support.

(Interview No. 26, CIO, 68,000 employees)

No one had expected much from us during Covid 19. We got so much appreciation for enabling everyone to work online that it was simply wonderful.

(Interview No. 58, CIO, 5,600 employees)

Appreciation is a vital skill in top managers, and one which Allianz sees as a prerequisite: do not think only of yourself but realize that you are in a permanent relationship with others. If you want to be effective you must be aware of this and learn to interact in an appreciative manner.

The amazing thing about appreciation is that we get it so easily wrong. Not because we are bad but simply because we are inconsiderate and often do not see what is needed by the other party.

It is therefore not as frequent as many wish that you see people feeling appreciated or being out there to appreciate others.

What do the numbers say about appreciation?

First of all, 14 percent of the interviewed managers saw this as an issue for themselves and others. And as we saw in Chapter 4, 78 percent actually believe that they often compliment others on their strengths, even more – a grand 91 percent say that they appreciate others for their contributions.

What do employees see? Those who have humble managers feel significantly more appreciated as you can see in Figure 6.1. On a scale of 1–10, they are at an okay 8.3 versus employees of non-humble leaders who stand at a miserly 6.2. If this was seen as an NPS score, all these employees would be detractors. And even the employees of the humble leaders cannot yet be counted as promoters (which only starts at 9). This shows that there is still room for more appreciation even by humble leaders. Possibly not in quantity but in quality? We will see later, how in everyday life we may fail in appreciation, even if we have the very best intentions!

Figure 6.1 More appreciation from and under humble leaders.

Interestingly, the appreciation by the leader also seems to have a positively infectious impact – those who feel appreciated, consciously also try to appreciate others more.

So, what happens when people feel appreciated?

The Consequences of Appreciation

We have a culture in which too little is appreciated. To my consternation I have discovered that I fare better, if I only do precisely what is asked of me instead of being proactive. Sadly, in my company at the moment, it is better not too be too good or too active.

(Interview No. 106, VP HR Strategy, 90,000 employees)

Appreciation is fundamentally important for the employee. Expressed in figures: if they lack appreciation, only 31 percent of employees manages to motivate themselves and work with commitment; if they are appreciated, the figure shoots up to 67 percent.2

So more than double the motivation! And since motivation in a well-run organization in turn results in better performance, this is a huge lever.

Yet, the 67 percent also shows one other key point: You as a manager cannot make up for everything. Even with the highest level of appreciation, you still fail to get the remaining 33 percent. Why? Perhaps the job is the wrong one for the employee. Possibly he or she does not fit well with the company or their colleagues. Or it may be that the employee simply does not like themselves and how they behave in their job. Whatever it is – from time to time you may allow yourself the calming thought: The whole world does not rest on your shoulders!

Other positive effects of appreciation?

The more the manager appreciates the strengths of their employees, the more the employees are convinced of their self-efficacy and once again the more motivated they are.3

In addition, the famous Pygmalion effect sets in. Named after the eponymous Greek sculptor who according to legend created a statue called Galatea whom he fell in love with. And it was this love that brought the statue to life.

The same happens with people you believe in.

Two examples: If teachers are convinced of the cleverness of some children – because they have been told that these are the smartest according to an IQ test – these children will objectively become better in the course of a year and end up the best in class.4 While, of course, the children were chosen purely by chance and there is no truth in the story that they had a high IQ.

The same applies to soldiers who also perform better on tests, think more positively and show stronger leadership skills, if their training officers fundamentally expect more of them.5 Remember the sea cadets who do not throw up because their officer told them that this will not happen to them – same principle.

Why does that happen? Research has identified four reasons. The one who is convinced that his/her teachings/thoughts/ideas fall on fertile ground due to the person being so smart, interested, good or great, firstly creates a “warmer” interpersonal climate, secondly entrust them with more and harder material, thirdly demands more feedback in the learning progress and fourthly gives more helpful feedback.

Dozens of studies show that this effect also works in organizations. One from 2017 documents that the appreciative behavior of the manager, who also communicates high expectations to the employee, increases employee's confidence in the manager and the company, as well as the motivation and trust in their own performance.6

An important point here is that the Pygmalion effect works at a subconscious level. In other words, teachers, officers and managers do not even notice that they treat people differently.

Interestingly therefore, this Pygmalion effect initially appears to be difficult to teach.7 Why? If I believe a staff member to be a total goof, I will have a hard time changing my perception of him/her and to treat them as differently as the Pygmalion effect requires. Since I will not be able to behave differently towards the employee, I will fail to pass on a new and positive view. Instead, I will then be stuck in my perception.

This is where the basic attitude of humility comes to the rescue. Since a humble person sees themselves with all their own weaknesses, and knows that all humans are like that, they will typically be able to develop a general kindness towards others. It then becomes easier to see what others already can or might be able to do. Where your own ego shrinks, there is room for goodwill and the willingness to see the positive in others. And when you communicate this belief, you act as with the Pygmalion effect. Consequently, in turn, the employees become just as they would if someone thought they were great.

In this respect, humility is the perfect attitude for making the Pygmalion effect work. And on the other hand, to banish the golem effect from the company. What is the golem effect? Pygmalion the other way round. Based on legends from the Middle Ages according to which rabbis created “stupid” creatures out of clay that acted at their will, managers create stupid employees if they do not expect anything from them and think they are no good. This happens especially when managers only throw themselves at the weaknesses of employees – then the employee soon loses sight of their own positive sides. As a consequence, the positive elements disappear not only from perception but also from the behavioral repertoire.

Research confirms this: If, for example, a working group does not consider itself to be good, its performance will be worse. And this is not because the group really consists of incompetent people, but because they do not believe in their own abilities as a group and therefore show less commitment and performance.8

Again, the attitude of a manager who leads with humility helps here. When briefing groups, they will point out the strengths and communicate high expectations, which will then be transmitted to the employees.

There is yet one other powerful effect that lies in appreciating the strengths of others. People feel committed to what they say. This means something positive happens when a manager says: “I'm sure you can do this because of your xxx” (it could say: creativity, discipline, perseverance) and additionally asks: “Do you see it that way too?” As soon as the employee replies: “Yes, I agree with that,” the inner doubts become quieter, the willingness to get involved increases and the probability that the employee will deliver exactly what he or she is aiming for increases.

It has also been proven that an increased sense of commitment has positive effects on interaction within the organization: More employee satisfaction, fewer redundancies and better performance.9 And if the humble leader verbalizes her own commitment from time to time, she will “infect” the employees so that they will do the same.

Take-Away: The more employees feel appreciated, the more they grow – Appreciation is a powerful motivation tool.

Stumbling Blocks in Appreciating Others

Most managers think that appreciation is a no-brainer appreciation, easily said and easily perceived. What do you think?

Well, in my programs we often discuss how actually to appreciate an employee. So, when I ask: Okay, so please say something appreciative out loud as if you were speaking to the employee, you just mentioned.

And then I get the following sentences:

“Generally, your work is seen.”
“On the whole, I think you are doing okay.”

None of you will have a problem in seeing what has gone wrong here. Qualifiers such as “generally” and “on the whole” immediately alert the listener to a point of criticism hiding behind the corner. Typically, the manager had not intended anything negative, and simply used the word in a non-thinking manner. But you can imagine that if an appreciative conversation starts like this, it typically does not go well…

The trick to avoid this stumbling block is indeed quite an easy yet tedious one: Walk yourself through the conversation in your head and initially – for some important ones - even in front of a mirror. Yet better, you write some sentences down and look at them before the meeting, so that you do not actually let the words lead you down the garden path.

I make no difference as to whether I am meeting the supervisory board or the toilet lady.

(Interview No. 86, CFO, 500 employees)

For me, it's all about being amongst equals. This means not talking down to anyone, whether working student, graduate student or board member. Anyone can talk to me; I have no receptionist to stop others. We're all on a first-name basis, from student to executive.

(Interview No. 11, Deputy Head IT, 1,000 employees)

At the beginning of the chapter, we saw that 91 percent of employees as well as leaders believe that they appreciate others. Yet, we also saw earlier on that between 25 and 51percent of employees do not feel appreciated. And in another study, employees said that more than 50 days have passed since they were last appreciated.10 That is too much even for the most well-meaning employees.

So why does this happen? Everyone seems to be well-meaning and still does not get it right. How come?

Well, in order to appreciate others, we must first notice them. Correct? So please go to https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wBoMjORwA-4 (or google “Daniel Simons Movie Perception Test 1997”)

Here you will find a very short video that I suggest you watch it with the following question in mind: What would I find to appreciate in this employee? Ideally stop at 0.23 seconds, and reflect if you saw something to appreciate. Be free to watch it again and again, until you find something.

(Spoiler Alert)

Most managers when they watch it comment first on the smile the person has and the untidy desk (hard to see how that can be turned into an appreciative comment). The second time round they notice a nice picture on the wall, something different with the shirt, possibly something different with the trousers. It is only at the third or fourth or fifth try that they notice that we actually have two different people here, wearing a different set of clothes and a different pair of glasses!

What an amazing lesson this is with a clear conclusion: we find it very hard to notice others, unless we really take time to look.

Instead, we are typically grounded only by that which we notice and that is only a small part of what there is. Remember the research about team members adding up their contribution to the team effort and coming to a total of more than 140 percent? We typically only see what it is that we personally are doing and find it hard to really appreciate what others do. Unless we ask. And listen.

Not surprising then, that if next to our human lack of awareness we throw in a pinch of narcissism, things even get worse. Researchers analyzed how different CEOs spoke about the company and about themselves. The more narcissistic they were, the more they talked about themselves. Indeed, they mentioned “I” or “Me” in four out of 10 sentences. They also did not mention other people – and following our findings from above, we can assume that they probably do not even notice that there are other people around let alone any relevant achievements!11

Look at this CEO of a company specializing in office furniture as a prime example. He said to the press: “I am the biggest farmer in the region with most pigs in the barn.” Meaning his employees! Who, we can expect, will not feel seen or appreciated by their boss.12

Okay, so we are not that good at noticing others and what they do. But surely, we notice what they feel? We saw above that the humble leader is good at being empathic, that must surely mean, that we as humans are generally good at recognizing emotions. Or? Well, we are definitely good when others clearly express emotions, such as sadness at being fired, anger at being shouted at, joy at being appreciated, we can read easily.

Yet, if we do not know the context, and the person does not really want their emotion to be noticed, we are rather helpless.

Take the great Micro Expressions Tool developed by Paul Ekman.13 Ekman had shown in his research that – all over the world – seven emotions are expressed facially in exactly the same way. This means that a North American uses exactly the same muscles to show fear, anger, disgust, sadness, contempt, joy and surprise as a Polynesian or a North Japanese. His research on this and on involuntary micro-expressions inspired the American television series “Lie to me.” He has also developed a tool for people to train recognizing emotions even when the other person is trying to suppress them. And these emotions only very briefly flash across the face. You have to imagine this suppressed emotion like a lightning smile of less than half a second, a frown, a look of disgust brought as quickly under control as the person can manage.

Within an hour, most managers in my programs become quite good at this. However, even the best ones have the highest error rate when it comes to sadness. In most cases, even after practicing in detail, most participants still confuse sadness with anger.

This effect is probably caused by a sort of biological self-protection – it is safer for me as an individual to think of someone as angry and hit them first in a defensive action, rather than mistakenly thinking they are sad and then being bopped on the head.

This highlights the point I am trying to make: Even after practice, we are good at perceiving some things, but bad at perceiving others. The danger is then that we will misjudge the other party and react on autopilot to what we believe their reaction to be.

What are the consequences if the humble leader knows about this innate human weakness of not being able to perceive others? Well, they realize that there is much that they do not perceive and that that which they perceive need not be taken at face value.

This means that they will try to really and consciously observe and afterwards carefully follow up by asking and listening, and asking and listening and asking and listening. And then react to what people really need and not with what you would want if you were in their place.

Managers often fail to appreciate as the other party needs it. Instead, they praise as they would like to be praised. But it is easy to find out what the other party really needs. Either you ask or you simply listen, then you know it.

(Interview No. 14, CEO, 1,000 employees)

Our next problem is that we often confuse the person with the situation he or she is in. This means that if someone is stern and brusque with us, we see this as his or her (unpleasant) character trait. If, on the other hand, we ourselves are stern and brusque, it is obvious to us that this is only because of the stressful situation we are in.

This is the famous fundamental attribution error – we see our negative patterns of behavior as owing to the situation, the positive ones as owing to our character. With other people it is the other way round.

This error goes into both directions. On the one hand, employees like to attribute actions of the manager to the manager as a person and not to the situation. Just think of cutbacks or restructuring. Here, employees often see the manager as being the decider and having freedom and choices. All of which may not exist at all. Instead, the manager may be trapped in a situation engineered from the powers above and may even be unable to point this out.

In the same way, problems arise in relation to the employees. Why? Because it is very human for the manager to attribute a lost pitch to the employee and not to the situation. Or to attribute a lack of politeness to a colleague not returning a friendly wave. While he may simply have forgotten his glasses or be – without me realizing – having a blue-tooth conversation with a bad piece of news.

Laszlo Bock, when he was Senior Vice President of People Operations at Google, recognized the problem:

Successful bright people rarely experience failure, and so they don’t learn how to learn from that failure They, instead, commit the fundamental attribution error, which is if something good happens, it’s because I’m a genius. If something bad happens, it’s because someone’s an idiot or I didn’t get the resources or the market moved.14

This means you also need to be humble enough to recognize what in your employees, your peers and your superiors is only due to the situation and what is really in and about the person.

A final problem arises in what research calls the “Set-Up-To-Fail-Syndrome.”15 It happens, when for whatever reason the manager believes their employee to be underperforming even ever so slightly. As a result, the manager actually slightly changes their behavior, becomes more directive, sets more precise targets, takes away decision-making autonomy and gives more routine projects. This has a hugely demotivating effect on the employee who soon finds himself or herself actually performing worse, thus confirming the bosses’ assessment of being a low performer.

Since our brain is so effective at providing us with reasons for our behavior, it is hard for a manager to realize their contribution to the bad performance of employees.

Those of you who are skeptical of this ever happening to you look at this piece of research to shudder and reflect.

166 new employees and their direct supervisors were asked to assess their expectations for their relationship during the first 5 days of working together. This very brief period with the expectations shaped during it was a predictor of how the relationship would fare six months later. That means, not having seen any piece of work, no project, no presentation, the expectation of the leader shapes how he or she would relate with the new employee after half a year.16 This is a self-fulfilling prophecy in the purest form.

You need to be aware that typecasting an employee as a strong or a weak performer will immediately have an objective impact on this person’s work. The way managers treat employees of whom they do not think highly projects underappreciation and low confidence, which in turn causes the employee to do exactly this: underperform.

A humble manager therefore will need to consciously analyse situations and learn to see the employee as he/she really is. The following questions may be useful to run through in your head:

Am I thinking less of this employee in comparison to others? What objective data do I have for this? What would the employee say to my assessment? Could it be that my behavior contributed to their weaker performance? What can I find to see positive in this employee?

It is your task as a humble manager to try all that you can, to really see your employees as they are and not as you think they are or as you have made them become due to your behavior.

Take-Away: Failure to perceive as a default human setting – People are not good at recognizing what it is that people do or feel. It is also easy to confuse the situation with the person and to change a person’s performance by the way we perceive this person to be. This means that you have to concentrate and work intensively on your own perception in order to be truly able to recognize the individual achievements of others.

How to Get Appreciation Right?

The managing director presented our business to the workers (blue-collar) on a flipchart. He was very calm and well prepared. It was really important to him that the employees understood the market and his strategy as well as why and how he acts. The whole thing took place on a very equal footing.

(Interview No. 8, VP People Development, 15,000 employees)

Our CEO actually manages to approach you with a personal touch, to listen attentively and not react with hollow phrases.

(Interview No. 7, VP Training, 22,000 employees)

I have a fairly new member of the team who is still quite young. She had a politically difficult task. Her job was to produce an information brochure and coordinate it with the works council. That is a tricky business in our company. She offered to write this brochure. Actually, I'm pretty good at formulating and writing these brochures myself. This is my home turf. I gulped, thought okay and then said: “Well, do it.” And it turned out exceptionally well. I was totally surprised; I couldn't have done it better. I learned something from this: even if I think I have humility on my radar, there's still something more I can do.

(Interview No. 2, Head of Talent Management, 10,000 employees)

Every time when I see something that merits feedback, I write it down in a structured way and give it to the person.

(Interview No. 67, Head of Learning, 49,000 employees)

How can appreciation be manifested? A good start is to simply take employees seriously, take the time to communicate with them or allow them to take on new tasks, as in the examples above.

In addition, you should definitely always be on the lookout for what employees do well. That is why one of the interview partners collects everything that merits feedback and a managing director regularly takes notes of good performance, whenever he is with his teams or when he sits in meetings. Then, come the time of performance review, he knows exactly who is sitting in front of him and can be rightfully appreciative.17

Want an example for a lack of appreciation? Then look at Huawei boss Ren Zhengfei. He asked an employee who had resigned for family reasons: “Why resign? You can get a divorce instead.”18

It follows almost directly from the Pygmalion effect that status is of course also an instrument of appreciation. In general – and up to a certain point – status has a positive effect on performance. This can be formal status, with a title and special bonuses, or informal status that is communicated verbally: “You are our expert for...,” “Without your expertise we would not be able to get anywhere.”

In other words, your status evaluation puts a sort of label, a stamp on the other person, which again becomes a kind of self-fulfilling prophecy. A number of studies have shown how powerful this label or stamp is and how it allows people to act accordingly.

One example outside the business world is the following: Participants are told that they are participating in a study about healthy eating. They are divided into two groups. The first is told: “If you are tempted, say to yourself ‘I can't do X.’” The second group is told: “If you are tempted, tell yourself ‘I don't do X.’” Different tests are taken and at the end the participants are allowed to choose between a chocolate and a muesli bar.

Lo and behold: 61 percent of those who said to themselves “I can't” grab the chocolate. Of those who categorized themselves as a type who simply does not give in to temptation, only 36 percent go for the unhealthy.19 In other words, through clever labeling of myself, I double the likelihood that I will do what I objectively would like myself to do.

Research also shows that you can just as effectively label others. If smokers are told that they were picked for their willpower to join a seminar on how to quit smoking, a whopping 67 percent managed to quit. While only 28 percent succeeded when they were told that they were picked by chance.20

There is another good story for successfully labeling others from Steve Jobs.21 For the first iPhone he decided that the screen should not be plastic but glass. For this he needed a very special type of glass. In search of a solution, he met a company called Corning and its CEO Wendell Weeks. Once Jobs was convinced of the product, he said he wanted as much gorilla glass as Corning could produce within six months.

Weeks replied: “We don't have the capacity, none of our plants are making the glass now.” “Don't be afraid,” Jobs replied. “Yes, you can do it,” he said. “Think about it. You can do it.”

When Weeks told this story, he shook his head in surprise. “We did it in less than six months,” he said. “We made a glass that had never been made.” Corning's factory in Harrisburg, Kentucky, which had made LCD screens, was converted almost overnight to make gorilla glass full time. “We put our best scientists and engineers on it, and we just made it work.”

And that is exactly what happens when I make employees feel that I believe in them. This belief generates the psychological safety which in turn leads to measurably better performance.22

How strong this effect is, depends on the company. If the hierarchy and the organization is stable and reliable, any status that is awarded leads to better performance, as shown above. But if the hierarchy is unstable and my status is endangered, performance will be measurably worse.23 This is related to pure biology in shape of the hormones: cortisone and testosterone. In uncertain times, more of these hormones are released, and that has an effect on performance. Amateurishly illustrated – when my body gears itself to flee from a lion in the savannah, my capacity for a well-structured excel sheet, customer-centric sales conversations or data analysis goes out the window!

Now the question is, how such an appreciation of strengths is best achieved?

Would you be pleased if your boss told you that “You make the best coffee” or “You always wipe the whiteboard so beautifully” or “As always, you've formatted the back-up slides superbly”?

In recognizing strengths in others, there should always be an element of a larger meaning. To the person above one could say the following: “You are our social glue! You simply see what needs to be done to make us work together as a team and to ensure that the customer always has the perfect product in his hands.”

Of course, for some people it may take time, until they manage to believe in the positive that you are saying.

The first time I was told that I was doing a great job, I thought my boss was being sarcastic – in my job before there simply had been no appreciation.

(Interview No. 48, Global Head Diversity, 16,000 employees)

This means the manager has to be aware of what the strength of the other person means for the work product, the colleagues, the customers, and give recognition for exactly that.

This is why it is not enough to merely say: “Great presentation” but instead “Thank you for this. I was especially impressed by slide 9 – you have really made the implementation gap so much clearer.” This sort of precision, of course, takes energy and focus.

You also have to be the one doing more of the appreciation if you see that there is too little going on around you – if the whole culture is not yet up to scratch.

The people on the shopfloor do not feel appreciated by the “pencil pushers” in home office and the other way round. Both groups feel not seen.

(Interview No. 108, CEO, 3,200 employees)

You need a lot of courage to say that someone has been doing something well – there is some softness involved in doing this. And in our industry, we feel that we have to be tough!

(Interview No. 62, Head of Learning, 27,000 employees)

If you share meaning and purpose, it incidentally has an enormous effect on work performance. A fine study on this was conducted in 2008 at an American university.24 The subjects were employees whose job it was to collect donations from alumni. These were then converted into scholarships for students.

For the study, the employees were divided into three groups. One group received … actually nothing. The second group was given four real stories to read about how the job of fundraising had been very helpful for former employees to set them on a good path for their future career. The third group read four stories from scholarship recipients who detailed how they had benefited from the scholarship, what they experienced and what they were able to achieve as a result.

The staff now went back to work. Nothing changed in groups 1 and 2. Group 3, on the other hand, shot up from an average of about $1,300 raised per week to more $3,100! Merely because the employees had understood more clearly what their endeavors were achieving. In other words, the appreciation came indirectly by allowing them to see the results of their work.

Transfer this to your company and it means that a trainee who sets up the conference room will feel appreciated if told that the atmosphere in the room was so good that a deal was struck. It means that an IT specialist will feel appreciated if the manager shares how clients were enthusiastic about the online registration process. And it means that an employee will feel appreciated if they are shown a bit of the larger picture. Some more positive examples in this vein.

I regularly remind our clients to thank their client officer if they have solved something – that is really motivating for them.

(Interview No. 37, CEO, 3,000 employees)

I write myself a reminder to thank for what has gone well – otherwise appreciation gets lost so quickly.

(Interview No. 49, HR-Director, 45,000 employees)

My boss started criticizing the candidate for not being able to make our preferred date and time for the interview. But to my mind appreciation already starts in accepting that a candidate also has a life outside of us.

(Interview No. 76, Head of Corporate HR, 7,500 employees)

I speak to every employee who has been with us for more than ten years and ask them to tell me about their time here, what was the worst and the best moment for them.

(Interview No. 87, CEO, 7,000 employees)

I have a nice example of appreciation. When we were setting up a meeting, a colleague said: Why does not Susan open the meeting, otherwise she does not have a part to play. And we want her to be seen.

(Interview No. 31, Head of HR Strategy, 300,000 employees)

When we talk about transformation, we think too little about the strengths of the past. We must remember to appreciate what has gone before.

(Interview No. 32, former Board Member, 3,500 employees)

It is clear then that appreciation demands time, focus and concentration. Something that we often fail to consider in our everyday hectic.

One important point, though. It must be clear that you as a leader appreciate all your employees and not just one employee in particular. Why? There is research that shows that if employees think that their manager acknowledges and praises them not because he or she appreciates everyone but because they themselves are unique individuals and unusually great, they end up feeling more entitled. The expensive consequence: They adhere less to internal rules and feel superior to others.25

That means, you need to make sure that you are generally seen as appreciative for all and not merely for the select few.

A question that often comes up at this point is what to do with the weaknesses of my employees. If I only see their strengths, this is surely not a complete picture. Not talking about weaknesses and not working on them can be dangerous for my company or my department.

To put it very clearly: Focusing on strengths does not mean that you do not see weaknesses and do not support your employee in mitigating them. We already looked at this before. Yet, the weaknesses are seen in the context of strengths and the focus remains on the strengths. If you only give negative feedback then research shows, employees lose their creative drive and focus mainly on what that negative feedback means for their relationship, their prospects, their self-image etc.26

Some researchers therefore stress that around 80 percent of the feedback should be positive.27 A similar ratio, namely 5:1, is known from the research on relationships. After decades of research, John Gottman was able to show that such marriages stay strong that typically have five positive interactions versus every negative one.28 Now if you see work as a form of marriage, that should also be your ratio…. Or at least follow a 3:1 ratio.

Focusing on the positive also gets rid of negative cultures in the company.

There was a toxic atmosphere in our company. So, I began every meeting by talking about the positive. What was achieved, who had done what. We congratulated each other. After a year all managers got it, we focused on the positive and where we may be hindered in achieving this – and stopped talking negatively about ourselves, the company and other people.

(Interview No. 16, General Manager, 160,000 employees)

Positive feedback helps improve performance. Especially for those employees who are only a little better than average. A database of more than 50,000 employees and managers shows that the majority of a group that was between 55 and 80 percent in terms of performance and received positive feedback improved on average by a whopping 24 percentage points29 (of course not beyond the 100).

But there remains the question of how to give negative feedback. In feedback discussions, it is easy to fall into the trap of starting with one positive thing and then piling on the negative ones. No wonder that people end up feeling unfairly treated and demotivated. In fact, statistically one third of feedback talks generate negative effects on subsequent performance and motivation.30 It is therefore no wonder that managers often avoid giving feedback at all.

Since it simply is not an option to shirk their duties, what should humble managers do?

Negative feedback must be seen as fair, otherwise the employee will immediately mentally shut down.31 For example, it is not considered fair when managers refer to something they have been told. “I heard from X that he quit because of you.” Even if a small element of this may be true, the employee will find it difficult to accept something like this if he or cannot be present at a meeting with X.

Hearsay simply has no place either in the courtroom or at work. Therefore, I am always surprised how frequently managers in role plays decide to share all the negative hearsay which they find in their confidential information. In the debriefing process, they say “you must not take everything you are told at face value” and on the other hand laughingly admit that the employee could have said almost nothing to fundamentally invalidate the hearsay.

A humble manager, therefore, would go through the information that they have about an employee and first look at what is actually known and what is hearsay. Then he or she would think about the bigger picture – what is to be achieved. Mentioning hearsay or even saying: “Max told me the following about you” will lead more to opposition and a feeling of having been betrayed by Max rather than to a change in behavior.

The managers who end up succeeding in their role play and motivate the employee actually pick mainly positive elements and limit their negative feedback to those issues that can be objectively measured. And then they focus on where the employee already successfully in the past managed to solve these sorts of problems. That is, switch the brain of the feedback receiver into active mode, remembering where things have gone right and how that can be repeated again and again.

What other tricks are there to give negative feedback? It is clearly not rocket science if the managers are aware of a few things.32

The feedback has to be given personally – this is the easiest part of the exercise because it makes no difference whether you do it sitting at a table or digitally.

Feedback must be based on objective standards instead of hearsay. For example: “In your position, you are expected to visit 15 customers a week. In the last quarter, you averaged only 5 clients.”

Where possible you should speak from your own perception: “At the last meeting I observed how you felt very offended by colleague Y. As a result, you raised your voice and started an argument.”

It is important that the feedback is not directed against the person, but against a precise behavior or performance.33

During the conversation, you should always listen to the employee how he/she sees the situation and be consciously prepared to change your mind. This is hard and takes a lot of focus.

And last but not least, it is helpful if you show your concern for the employee and spend time developing ideas together on how improvements can be achieved. The more the employee is involved in visualizing the steps that need to be taken, the better.

Of course, it is up to you to decide how exactly you want to run the feedback talks. Many find the following options very helpful.

Option 1: The BET model: It has the steps: Behavior, Effect and Thanks. It is suggested to use this model for positive feedback in the vast majority of cases.

Positive feedback: The BET model


Behavior




Effect




Thanks

You mention the behavior, you talk about the positive effect it had and you thank the employee for it. And just before you object to the word “Thanks,” and say that in your culture this is not really done, please note, actually every manager worldwide takes issue with this part. Thanking an employee for work that he or she is paid for is something that nearly everyone struggles with. Initially. Yet, it can be trained and leads to a deepened feeling of appreciation.

The model for difficult and often negative feedback is called BEAR: Behavior, Effect, Alternative, Result. This means that a concrete behavior is addressed (with measurable and objective standards) and the negative impact is explained. Now the desired alternative behavior is presented, which leads to the desired result. This result does not always have to be positive, but can also be made up of clear ramifications for the case that the behavior is not changed.

Critical feedback: The BEAR model


Behavior




Effect




Alternative




Result

As we have seen above, real appreciation demands that you perceive the employee as he or she is. But since you have grown aware of all your biases: the fundamental attribution bias, where you confuse character and situation and the set-up-to-fail-syndrome, where your very own skepticism worsens someone’s behavior – since you have become aware of how hard it is to perceive someone as they really are, it is far safer for you to focus on giving positive feedback and do this regularly. For those moments when you have to give negative feedback, you should also not forget that you can usually find more positive than negative feedback and that you should make the negative feedback not only as clear but also as warm and caring as possible. The aim should be to jointly develop a proposed solution.

For me a useful image is of a circle with space for more than 365 lines. Typically, most days of the year I would not find anything critical to say about the employee. More likely than not, if I could observe everything that my employee was doing, I would color in the greatest part of the circle with a shining green of things well done. Now say, I have three negative things – while they loom large in my mind, I can quite easily remind myself that most of the circle will still be green, even when I add three red lines.

One company that after numerous struggles has managed to institute a good culture for giving positive and negative feedback constructively is Netflix.

First, look at the experience of a new employee on his first day who had observed what to him seemed a surprising exchange.

A guy four levels below him hierarchically stopped (the speaker Ted) in the middle of his point: “Ted, I think you’ve missed something. You’re misunderstanding the licensing deal. That approach won’t work.” Ted stuck to his guns, but this guy didn’t back down. “It won’t work. You’re mixing up two separate reports, Ted. You’ve got it wrong.”

Later I ran into Ted in the men’s washroom. He asked how my first day was going so I told him, “Wow Ted, I couldn’t believe the way that guy was going at you in the meeting.” Ted looked totally mystified. He said,

Brian, the day you find yourself sitting on your feedback because you’re worried, you’ll be unpopular is the day you’ll need to leave Netflix. We hire you for your opinions. Every person in that room is responsible for telling me frankly what they think.

Secondly, see how feedback is organized:

Netflix managers invest significant time teaching their employees the right and wrong way to give feedback. They have documents explaining what effective feedback looks like. They have sections of training programs where people learn how and practice giving and receiving it.

These are the four key elements of what Netflix calls the 4A feedback:

Aim to assist.
The feedback must be actionable.
People should appreciate the feedback.
The feedback can either be accepted or discarded.

In general, feedback takes place in most one-on-one meetings and also in so-called Live 360 dinners every 6 to 12 months in the whole team.

At Netflix, it is tantamount to being disloyal to the company if you fail to speak up when you disagree with a colleague or have feedback that could be helpful. After all, you could help the business – but you are choosing not to.34

Finally, let's talk briefly about what many see as a problem in overpraising. Is it not silly, if every email, ever uttering, every presentation is praised? The appreciation could lose value and people may have no idea what it is they are actually doing well.

This is a valid point, but my fear is that too often we are simply too lazy to look for something constructive to praise. Sometimes we remember that we have appreciation on our To Do list, come out with a feeble “Well done” and are surprised that the engagement survey gives us bad scores.

Why do we find it hard to look for something positive? This is the negativity bias again – it is far easier to remember the times that someone barked at you or forgot to deliver their presentation in time compared to the many more times that there was a fine performance.

Yet knowing this, you, as a humble executive, need only take a few minutes per person to remember what it is that they do well. Even if you've known this for years, there's no harm in thanking them again for some details of a job well done. Just as you would like to hear a “thank you!” after every single meal which you have prepared. It does not have to be a eulogy; a few words of appreciation are enough.

It is also important to realize that appreciation not only lies in words. But in numerous other parts of our behavior and presence. A fine example comes from one of my seminars.

We had a whole day practicing presence and projecting yourself as a leader. The task now after lunch: simulate a real work situation in which the participants in the room represent the employees. The first participant on the stage was someone who up to then had always engaged fully, being very outspoken and active. He shuffled into the middle of the room with his eyes lowered, both hands in his trouser pockets and began to mumble something in our direction.

After a few minutes of this, the faculty and the audience gave some positive feedback plus some very clear negative points, regarding the presence, willingness to engage etc.

Five minutes later, we all found ourselves in a different universe. There walked into the middle of the room an upright person, making eye contact using his hands to get us all engaged. And very quickly we all were on board.

What does this have to do with appreciation? Is this example not simply about acting? No. In the first “performance,” the manager had only focused on himself, had allowed himself to develop an aversion to this exercise and had only wished to get off the stage as quickly as possible. In this situation, he did not care one whit about us being in the room and having to spend our time with him. In other words, in the first situation, he did not even notice us as relevant beings, which is the absolute opposite of appreciation.

In the second attempt, we became the center of his attention – he had remembered why he was up there: To bring us on board. The way he now structured his talk we were willing to sit up, listen and be enlisted in the cause.

To put it succinctly: Appreciation lies in perceiving that leading is always about the others. Not about yourself. As a consequence, all your actions must be tailored accordingly. If not, your employees will notice and judge accordingly.

This also entails not checking your phone while others are speaking, looking up when you are spoken to, taking time to listen to your employees, keeping your face under control, making an effort to be presentable to others.

Regarding dress code, there is, of course, quite a difference between companies. Said bluntly, in my view, a key point of appreciation lies in not making life harder for others. If I have a scowl on my face, so that people do not dare to ask me a question; if my dress code is prohibitively distancing or too familiar; if I mumble or clearly wish myself away from where I am, then I am not being appreciative.

Let us see some more examples of a lack of appreciation.

It is amazing how little managers appreciate both their colleagues from HR or their employees when once again they make their employees cancel their trainings for something they think is more important.

(Interview No. 53, VP HR, 800 employees)

He said, look this guy has been working in the same position for seven years – he needs to go. And I said, no way do you fire my people simply because you want a change.

(Interview No. 96, Board Member, 200,000 employees)

My boss held a meeting before Christmas. He spoke for 2 hours and never once acknowledged that people had been sick and dying from Covid 19. At the end, he said: “I have been told to reassure you and also ask you how you are. Since I cannot do this in this virtual setting, I will tell you how I feel. You know that I love to go out at night and party. Since I cannot do this now, I feel sad and tired.”

(Interview No. 139, Head Digital Learning, 100,000 employees)

There is also appreciation in being transparent. It is very easy for employees to feel that they are not being taken seriously, if too much information is kept from them. For many managers information is a source of power. Yet sharing is actually a sign of appreciation.

I got this team during a restructuring. Half of them were to be let go and this was not meant to be communicated. I decided to treat them like adults and share this information. It was a huge relief – they felt treated like adults and fairly at the same time.

(Interview No. 88, Head of EMEA Learning, 100,000 employees)

We have no personal office space. This is part of our value system. We also eat the same food as the employees and we all use the rest rooms on the shop floor. Our philosophy is truth, performance and love.

(Interview No. 97, Board Member, 2,500 employees)

The middle management is often frightened to pass up bad news – so we end up with little transparency throughout the company and people do not feel appreciated.

(Interview No. 54, Head of Global Training, 36,000 employees)

Being open is a powerful tool. If you cannot reveal something, then stating that already helps. And offering others a format in which to be open is another step towards generating an appreciative atmosphere.

One manager actually uses a team health status slide for this. Each employee gives a color code on dimensions such as Health, Stress, Workload, Manager Support, Colleague Support, Leadership, being valued. One week, all employees set the leadership dimension on red. Did the manager fume or rail? No, he admitted to having failed to deliver on what he had promised and allowed the feedback to stand just as it was. What a powerful tool of appreciating your employees by letting them vent in a safe environment!

Being really present, taking the other party seriously and giving feedback in a structured way ensures that the other side feels properly appreciated and recognized. It takes both time and focus to develop a consistent skill in appreciating others. Stepping out onto an internal balcony is the first step to take.

Finally, what about you yourself being appreciated? How can you ensure that you are not being left out of the equation?

Firstly, the research at the beginning of the chapter shows that the more appreciation you give, the more you also receive as a leader.

Secondly, being humble allows you to focus less on yourself. As a consequence, you will actually need less direct appreciation. If you do your job right as a leader, this already will give you the appreciation.

Thirdly, others will seek you out which many leaders also feel as great appreciation. Look at these two examples.

I get a sense of recognition from the greater responsibility and accountability that I have, as well as from you quoting me in a book.

(Interview No. 40, CEO, 24,000 employees)

I feel enriched and appreciated when employees from a former job call. When it is time for the last journey and the whole village is there, then have I achieved something!

(Interview No. 25, VP and Head Global Services, 31,000 employees)

Often, however managers themselves feel unappreciated. Have a look at this example.

I sometimes get angry – here we are very close to the employees, we spend a lot of time going round and talking to people. Then when I write my Thursday E-Mail about the bigger picture, there is for many this weird ritual of celebrating their distance to the CEO and claiming that their department is so very different. Then I get comments such as: ‘Do you even know what is going on here?’ How much more do I need to role model understanding and proximity?

(Interview No. 114, CEO, 14,000 employees)

While we do not know enough about his communication style to know if the CEO is actually doing as well as he is hoping for, he clearly feels unappreciated. Would it hurt the employees to first point out where the CEO had hit on their needs precisely? Before adding extra information on how their situation may be different? No, not at all.

Research shows that energy, vigor, well-being of a manager also depends on him or her being appreciated.35 Yet, they sadly receive it even less than employees do.

In one survey, 74 percent of employees say that they never or rarely express appreciation to their boss.36 This may have something to do with a fear of not wanting to be seen as ingratiating yourself or as flattering, in order to get something.

If you remember, how real appreciation works – concretely valuing an input, a presentation, a project, it becomes clear that appreciation upwards needs have neither of these negative connotations. “Thank you very much for your support in this.” “I appreciate, that you gave me the freedom to run this project independently within the parameters you set.” “Thank you for having collected all this feedback about me – this is an arduous task for which I am grateful.” “Great presentation – I finally understood, how this project fits in with the bigger picture!”

Nothing sycophantic in any of these utterances.

So, this is the final takeaway from this section – never forget to also appreciate upwards (and sideways, while we are at it). There can never be too much appreciation! And you will also reap the benefits of it as in this example.

I've prescribed myself a little personal program called SYP: Swallow your pride. This is how I endured when an older advisory board member tried to explain my business to me. Instead of being skeptical or irritated, I had everything recorded and every suggested change reviewed and partially implemented. Since then, he has been completely supportive of me.

(Interview No. 161, COO, 4,200 employees)

To round of this chapter, let us look at a final case to see how appreciation can be missed and what negative emotions it generates. It is a real case, though anonymized.

You, Alexander, are in charge of Business Development at Clarity, a supplier of special glass. You, your direct boss, Division Manager Diego, and CEO Kim have jointly visited an important potential client called Dias. After the interview, you prepare a proposal and send it by e-mail to Diego and Kim. This draft contains a placeholder for a recommendation (“______________”) from the CEO of one of your biggest clients, which the client wanted to see some comments from. CEO Kim has offered to obtain this reference. To be better able to imagine the situation, think of an enthusiastic quote about your company from someone as well-known and powerful as Satya Nadella, CEO of Microsoft, or Lawrence Culp, the CEO of GE.

No one thanks you for the draft, which you wrote late at night. Your boss Diego adapts the draft in numerous places and among other things takes out the placeholder for the recommendation. A misunderstanding, since he missed one of the last e-mails on the subject. CEO Kim sees that the space for the quote is gone and asks about it. Diego writes that it is not necessary.

You are very concerned that you are now sending exactly the kind of general gibberish that the customer hates and that you will not win the offer. You write an email to both of them and point out that there must have been a misunderstanding and that the quote is very important after all. CEO Kim leaves it up to Diego and you and is ready to approach the CEO for the quote after all.

You call Diego and explain once again the reasons for the quote – that everyone had agreed on it and that he had probably forgotten about it. Diego says curtly: “Thanks for the discussion, but no, we're not changing anything.” You angrily hang up and write a message to CEO Kim that in Diego's view the quote is no longer needed. Thus a “waffly,” weak offer goes to the customer.

Please reflect on where appreciation was lacking.

The vast majority of participants with whom I discuss this case are firmly on Alexander's side. There he was writing a draft late at night, and nobody thanks him for it. Then Diego messes it up with the quote and neither apologizes nor allows his mind to be changed. And CEO Kim just lets everything happen. No appreciation for Alexander – and actually no appreciation for the client.

Only after a bit of thinking does it become clear that Alexander is actually the main problem. Why exactly does he send his first draft not only to his direct boss, but also to the CEO? By doing so, in many companies he would be seen as passing over his direct boss, not appreciating his status. Why does he not send it directly to Diego? Okay, you may say, but if Kim was at the meeting, then surely addressing both in one email may be fine. Indeed, your company culture may allow this for the first joint mail. But what about the second one, in which Alexander actually exposes Diego and blames him for having made a mistake? Surely here you would agree that an email to Diego AND Kim is in bad taste.

Also, when Diego adapted the draft in numerous points, does Alexander thank him for this? Not one iota! He only focuses on the one single mistake of taking out the quote. And he goes at it in the most aggressive manner – by writing an e-mail to his boss as well as the CEO! With this he indirectly portrays Diego as a fool. Of course, Diego is also not acting in a particularly adult manner. Neither does he thank Alexander for the draft, nor is he able to admit his mistake in taking out the quote.

But! It is Alexander, with his focus on himself and his lack of appreciation of Diego's position as his boss who has brought this on himself. Why on earth did Alexander not call Diego directly after he had adapted the draft? Mere automatism, lack of awareness and mental laziness!

And during this call, he could actually have apologized for not making the placeholder more obvious. One way to ensure that bosses, many of whom have to work through hundreds of emails and dozens of documents every day, do not have to think too hard and do not make errors accidentally is to really make things obvious. Alexander might have added a big comment in his draft to (“______________”): “This is where we agreed the quote from X to be added where he tells how great we are etc.”

That would have been a much wiser thing to write, and quite an easy thing to apologize for missing….

Instead, Alexander looks at things only from his perspective: The need for the quote was obvious to him, so it should clear be to everyone else.

How long do you think it took Alexander (whom I know personally) to understand that the blame for the situation lay squarely with him? It was only when he talked to his coach at the end of the year after a bad feedback session with his boss Diego, that he realized that he had brought all this on himself.

Why? Because, he had not paid attention to what Diego needed in his role. Instead, he found it easier to feel victim of a thoughtless boss. Alexander was caught in his desire to win the customer. He found no time or room for humble behavior; he fully failed to perceive anyone outside himself.

And, of course, they lost the client!

Take-Away: Those who want to be master of appreciation practice – Being truly present, taking the other person seriously, and giving structured feedback where appropriate ensures that the other side feels properly recognized and appreciated.

Chapter 7 Ability to Learn and Openness to New Ideas and Feedback

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-7

Wise people don't judge – they seek to understand.

Wei Wu Wei (English philosopher 1895–1986)

Summary

While it appears a simple matter for all of us to be willing to learn, it does indeed require courage to expose yourself to many and partly uncomfortable learning experiences. It is important to understand that most things can be learned, that errors can be a great help in this process and that what you can learn depends on your own mindset. Exercises help open your own brain for learning as well as creating a culture of learning in your own team or company.

Professor Tom Hubbing, in his mid-60s, is a world-renowned professor of leadership. He teaches at numerous business schools and for large corporations. He spends whole days with board members or teaches in halls packed with executives. He regularly receives awards as a speaker, and dozens of executives send him thank-you emails for his inspiration.

I had the opportunity to work with him in a leadership development program. The group was a small one of only 15 participants. A day of lessons and a day of group coaching was planned. Then one of the coaches dropped out. I asked the professor if there was any chance that he could take on a group of five participants, as no one else was available at such short notice for the location we had chosen. Prof. Hubbing immediately said yes and stayed on for a second day. At the end of this day, the highly decorated professor turned to me and said: “I would like to thank you very much for the opportunity to work as a coach with your participants. I enjoyed this new format and have learned a lot from it”.

This struck me as the most open attitude I had ever seen in a person. While other professors kept away from anything that was not standard, Prof. Hubbing embraced all that was new and integrated it into his lessons. No focus on his own status, no arrogance – simply an enormous willingness to always be learning.

Yet there are many who act differently. Let us look at one negative example.

The three managing directors had made up their minds – they wanted to strengthen the whole team. Instrument of choice: A structured feedback process. Each of the 20 employees, as well as they, would give each other anonymous feedback answering four questions: What should this person continue doing? What should this person do differently? What should this person stop doing? Any other comments? A budget was allocated for everyone to have three sessions with an executive coach after the feedback.

Mark Peters, one of the employees, had just finished his three coaching sessions and was satisfied with the results. He had been able to put some of the comments into perspective, two had simply been spiteful, four had confirmed what he already knew and was working on, but six were new topics that he was able to discuss intensively with his coach. Now he would try out some new approaches.

On the way to the canteen, he met the three managing directors and they started chatting. Mark mentioned that he found the coaching useful and asked about their experience. The answers astounded him.

Managing Director 1: “Oh, at 50 I already know what I can and cannot do – I don't need any coaching.”

Managing Director 2: “I discussed the results with my best buddy in the pub.”

Managing Director 3: “I rang a coach, but we couldn't find a time slot, so I discussed the results with my wife.”

A few years later, the three managers were dismissed. The business results were still good, but the supervisory board had lost confidence in a future with the three at the helm.

The third core element of humility is the ability to learn, be open to new ideas and feedback.

While only six percent of interviewed managers saw this as a hard element of humility to embrace, this may be due to a faulty understanding of what true openness and willingness to learn actually entails.

Willingness to Learn

It is shocking that people in our times of change believe they can be successful with what they have learned years ago. This attitude transforms into a lack of openness as well as a foolish resistance to trends.

(Interview No. 52, CEO, 1,100 employees)

I offer learning in form of seminars etc. – and people simply do not take me up on this!

(Interview No. 100, CFO, 10,000 employees)

You should not force anyone into learning if they do not want to.

(Interview No. 74, 1,000 employees)

Some people do not want to really learn – they only want the certificates.

(Interview No. 78, Head of Talent Management, 200 employees)

Are there managers who live the first two elements of humility (i.e., who are good at assessing and appreciating their own abilities and those of others) but who are not capable of learning nor open to feedback and new ideas?

Yes: unfortunately, this is only too common. For example, I had a colleague who said smugly of himself: “I simply cannot remember birthdays. Not even those of my wife and children.”

The attitude “I just can't...” is widespread, is readily put forward and often masquerades as humility.

The following sentences sound both humble and appreciative:

Anne, why don't YOU call this difficult client – you're much better at this than I am. With me, it always ends in a fight... In return, I'll take the three customers in Paris off your hands.

Peter, you are such a great organizer – can I leave the team event to you? Sure, that is officially my job: but remember what happens when I work with Excel. We don't want that, do we?

And one more example from an interview:

An engineer came up to me and said how he admired me how I was connecting with people. He said, he wished he could also do what I had done. What had I done? Written a thank you email to an employee!

(Interview No. 96, Board Member, 300,000 employees)

Everyone knows: it is very comfortable not to have to change – and to say that there are some things that you simply cannot do. However much you would like to. After all, nobody blames an Arabian stallion for not being able to climb a tree.

What is really behind this attitude? Certainly, at times a solid self-knowledge. But often also defiance, laziness – and even arrogance: “It’s just the way I am….”

Like the managing director from the case study at the beginning of the chapter, who thought that at his age – a mere 50 – he didn’t need coaching.

Sounds like the CEO of a company who said to his CDO: “Sure you can take a coach. I don’t believe in it – it will be money thrown out of the window, but please go ahead” (Interview 24, CDO, 6,000 employees)

Well, any managing director should be aware of the research showing that outside perspective is invaluable – at any age. That outside perspective may come from colleagues, employees, bosses – or coaches.

The positive experience Google CEO Eric Schmidt reported on earlier is supported by a meta-analysis of almost 2,000 managers from 2016. It confirmed that coaching has a significant effect on the manager's performance and also a measurable effect on the company in which the manager works.1

What a sad time for any company when its leaders refuse to take up an offer of coaching.

To distinguish between correct self-assessment and lethargy or laziness, everyone has to honestly analyze themselves. After all, as we have seen, it is no mistake to concentrate on your strengths. Look at how in recent years recognizing and promoting strengths has become increasingly popular in management theory.

Why? It is seen as unhelpful to spend hours painstakingly identifying your own weaknesses and then trying to correct them. Better to focus on your strengths and play to them more effectively. Instruments like the Clifton StrengthsFinder help to uncover what the individual employee is particularly good at and what he/she likes to do. The goal is then to strive to use these competencies even more. The assumption is that someone who, for example, tries to compensate for his weaknesses in customer acquisition will never be as good as someone who loves nothing better than cultivating relationships, with all the ups and downs that it entails.

This approach clearly finds its limits when someone takes on a specific role in the company. To professionally fulfill any role, especially in these rapidly changing times, an individual will need to develop certain skills. In other words, if you get a job in customer service, you have to learn how to deal with difficult customers. If you are responsible for team events, then you should learn to master Excel to the extent necessary. By the same token, if you are a husband, you should remember the birthdays of your wife and children!

Last but not least: if you are a manager who has just received comprehensive feedback, you should try to learn something from it, no matter how old you are!

I have known very few who are truly open to learning. Many like to focus on the past. And if you want to learn, you have to admit that in the past there was something that you could not do or that you did wrong.

(Interview No. 82, CFO and CDO, 500 employees)

People do not ask for trainings. And when I order business books for me, they just pass them on to me and never ask to borrow them.

(Interview No. 83, CMO, 6,500 employees)

In professional roles, a willingness to learn is essential: new roles almost always require skills that go beyond those that employees or managers already have. Even if you have been in the same position for a number of years, you will need to acquire new skills as the role or the organization evolves. Remember the CEO of BMW, who just continued leading the same way, even though the situation had changed. He was actually unhumble in not wanting to adapt and learn a new way of leading.

Here are just a few of the competencies that a manager must have.

Different leadership styles for different employees.
An understanding of the company's strategy and how your own area fits in.
The ability to make good decisions under uncertainty.
The skill to communicate well with stakeholders, both internal and external.
For times of change, charismatic leadership and storytelling.
Have a positive impact as a role model (in concrete terms: team meeting without mobile phone, ability to keep facial expressions under control, concentration and posture).

As a result of having understood that their role requires more of them, some managers attend a management seminar once a year. They recognize that there is always something new to learn due to changes in the company, the employees or simply the situation. Some even book the same seminar repeatedly. Since they and their environment have changed, they find new lessons in the same cases and materials.

Others use the seminars as a disciplinary measure to really take time to reflect on their strategy, leadership or change challenges.

 

Take-Away: Learning not laziness – Despite the fact that people pay lip service to being open to learning – 99 percent saw themselves as willing to learn from others – many hide behind the fact that they simply cannot do some things.

Knowing That Something Can Be Learned

People become micro managers because they do not know what to do as managers. Instead of learning for their new role, they fear becoming redundant and cling to their old one. Yet the only thing I can work on is myself!

(Interview No. 21, Head of Sales Start-up, Former VP, 10,000 employees)

I think you can learn all – even how to appreciate others.

(Interview No. 115, CEO, 300 employees)

I will meet my coach this afternoon. I am nearly 62 but can always learn.

(Interview No. 45, 2,500 employees)

In my company, everyone can take a coach nowadays. It has lost the stigma of being a “repair”-measure.

(Interview No. 72, Head L&D, 14,600 employees)

The interesting question, of course, is whether everything you need for your professional role can be learned. Are there not elements in your own being that are fixed and cannot be changed? The most important personality traits, your core leadership style, the way you speak, how you make decisions?

Before we dive into this – I would like you to take a short questionnaire (Table 7.1). It takes about five minutes to fill in and to score afterwards (Table 7.2).
