We will look at the questionnaire and the implication of your result later on in this chapter.

Now I want to share some of the research with you as to what can actually be learned. Researchers have been working on this for decades, with results getting clearer by the day.

Let us take a fairly recent example and look at it more closely: Charisma. Charisma is the legendary ability to take up space, to inspire, to move and to motivate. Surely such an ability cannot be learned. To act like an Obama or a Jacinda Ardern, the Prime Minister of New Zealand, that is surely innate. Isn't it?

Three researchers from Lausanne, led by Professor John Antonakis, took the bait. They first investigated what exactly this famed charisma consists of. What patterns of behavior are displayed, what tactics are deployed by those who are perceived and celebrated as charismatic leaders?

The answer is surprisingly simple. The researchers identified twelve critical skills, nine verbal and three non-verbal. These are strongly reminiscent of what you learn in a course on leadership rhetoric or story-telling. Is that surprising? No! Leadership, after all, is a matter of communication.

The nine verbal charismatic leadership tactics:

Use of metaphors, parables and analogies (“We are worrying away at this project like pit-bulls. Can we become slyer like foxes?”)
Use of stories and anecdotes (“This reminds me of the time, when I went rafting and the current pushed me against the wall of the canyon….”)
Setting contrasts (“I don’t want you to think about what you can do for me; I want you to think what I can do for you.”)
Asking rhetorical questions (“Who wants to continue feeling like a victim of these budget cuts?”)
Three-part arguments and lists (“We have the budget for this. We have the people for this. And we have the will for this.”)
Demonstrating ethical and moral conviction (“Our goal: Never harm the client or give less than we could.”)
Recognizably sharing the feelings of the collective (“We are all tired and demotivated.”)
Having high expectations for themselves and their employees (“By 2030 our technology will be in every second household.”)
Communicating conviction that high expectations can be met (“Many others would be fearful whether their team was capable of achieving so much in such short time. I am not!”)

The three non-verbal charismatic leadership tactics:

Animated and modulated voice.
Active facial expressions: eye contact, showing emotions – smiling, frowning etc.
Use of gestures such as symbolic hugging, underlining, enumerating, conscious pointing, tapping

To test if and how this charismatic leadership can be learned, the researchers invited 34 managers from a large Swiss corporation (average age 42) to a five-hour-long training course.2

At the start, the researchers explained the 12 charismatic leadership tactics. They then asked participants to demonstrate them, right here and now. This was followed by feedback, tips and clips from films including “Dead Poets Society” and “’Any Given Sunday” so that the participants could observe and analyze how people put the tactics into practice. Finally, in groups of two, the participants drafted a speech for a situation that required charismatic leadership. After the training, the researchers had a short phone conversation with each participant to discuss how they wanted to develop over the next few months, especially with regard to charismatic leadership, and each manager drew up a plan accordingly.

After three months, ten employees per manager were asked a second time (the first time had been before the training) to assess the charisma of their managers. The result? Although it was only a short intervention of five hours, and although the employees were unaware of the intervention, the managers had moved up on the charisma scale from 2.45 to 3.00 (out of 5). The employees perceived 65 percent of the managers to be “better than the average,” while only 35 percent from the control group (that had not received the training) were thus rated.

Do people get even better with more training? The result will not surprise you. A second group of participants (41 MBA students, average age 35, with at least 3 years of leadership experience) received a significantly longer training – 20 hours over 7 weeks – with more film examples, role plays and feedback loops. In addition, the participants were filmed while speaking and received the films for discussion and feedback.

This photo example shows the fundamental difference the training made (Figure 7.1).

Figure 7.1 Example of improvement of non-verbal behavior: Before and after the intervention. (From Antonakis et al. 2011; with kind permission of © Academy of Management Learning & Education 2011. All Rights Reserved).

As with the first group, after the training the managers were subsequently perceived as considerably more charismatic.

The conclusion is clear. You do not have to be born with charisma; you can learn it. And if this is true for something as legendary and apparently intangible as charisma, how much more so for all the other skills that a manager needs?

Let us look at one other thing that we can learn – which may initially come as a surprise to you. This goes back to a great story that James Clawson, Professor Emeritus at Darden Business School at the University of Virginia, tells in class:

I played basketball regularly at noon.A fellow player turned out to be teaching sports psychology and invited me to his lecture in front of 500 undergraduates. In the middle of his lecture, he asked: “Does how you feel affect your performance?” Every hand shot up. Wait a minute! What? The logical and next question was, “How do you want to feel?” That question dumbfounded me. I couldn’t sleep that night.How do I WANT to feel? No one had ever asked me that. Not my mother, not my father, nor any of my teachers. It was always “What have you done?” Since then, I’ve learned that how one feels has a big impact on performance, not only for athletes, but for others as well.

Jim then goes on to reflect on how he discovered how he wanted to feel.

It took me 18 months and multiple drafts to figure that out. “Happy” isn’t good enough, it’s too general and non-differentiating. Reviewing my historical high-performance moments, I realized that when I felt “light, unhurried, engaged, and connected to others”, good things happened. I wrote those on a note on my bathroom mirror and on my check book; I wanted to overcome half a century of habitually focusing on results rather than the feel that produces results.

So let me put the question to you. How do you want to feel? One hint: look at how you feel when you are “in Flow. Or when you are “the best of you.”

Clearly then, one can not only master charisma but also learn to develop those feelings that lead to good things and good performance in your leadership role.

Is there a limit to what managers can learn? Well, for leadership I suggest not. Hundreds of academic papers show that communication, appreciation and other leadership skills can actually be learned. By the one wanting to. Even, of course, humility! Otherwise, this book would be a sad story.

Do people already know and believe that they can learn everything? Well, this varies. In my research, I do encounter some severe doubts. Look at this surprising result in Figure 7.2.

Figure 7.2 Limiting Beliefs: Half or more do not believe learning to be possible.

Nearly half of the respondents do not believe that everyone can learn to be a great leader. Only 41 percent believe you can learn personal charisma and only around half believe in personality change.

This shows limiting beliefs that will stop many people from embarking on learning for themselves. In addition, this will hugely influence what change they believe possible in their employees, regardless of the level.

Let me give you some additional arguments for yourself and others. First leadership: Research has shown clearly that (once again) coaching increases leadership effectiveness on average by 55 percent. And that was after only four coaching sessions!3

On the qualitative side, there are dozens of cases, where people decided to change their style and by being persistent, they achieved it. Look at the following examples.

Andrall E. Pearson4 had always been successful. First at McKinsey, then as president and COO of Pepsico and finally at what today has become Yum!, operating more than 40,000 restaurants worldwide. While he had been a tough nut, enjoying to prove that he was the smartest on the room, by picking on peoples’ mistakes, he later came to the realization that this no longer worked for him.

I remember bringing one of our market-research women to tears because I told her that the information, she was gathering wasn’t producing anything. I could just see the breath come out of her. I realized that in today’s world, you can’t treat people that way. If you’re not careful, you might discard a very good person. There are a lot of ways to ask tough questions without killing somebody.

He learnt how the human heart drives a company’s success — one person at a time — and how this kind of success cannot be imposed from the top but must be kindled through attention, awareness, recognition, and reward. As a result, Andy adapted his leadership style to become genuinely interested in people. “Ultimately,” Pearson says, “it’s all about having more genuine concern for the other person. There’s a big difference between being tough and being toughminded. There’s an important aspect that has to do with humility.”

His employees observed the change, believed it and were empowered by it.

LeRoy Pingho5, former vice president at Fannie Mae, took charge of his own personal growth. He wrote an assessment based on the 360 feedback he received. Then he distributed copies to 50 people: bosses, peers, direct reports, his wife. He sent everyone the same message: “You work with me, so you should know my strengths and weaknesses. Also, I’m going to ask four of you to help me work on the things I’m not good at.”

Pingho dubbed those four people his “spotters.” He chose two at his level, one above him and one below him. He met with each of the spotters to review the “flat spots” he’d identified. Then he told them that he wanted to focus on getting better at two of those weaknesses. (He didn’t think he could tackle all at once.) One was active listening: “When I’m in meetings, I’m already through the presentation before the presenter has gotten to the first page.” The second was empowerment: “I want to use the input I get from people instead of disregarding it.”

He asked his spotters to alert him when they saw behavior that related to those improvement goals:

I said, “You don’t have to do this in a formal way. But if you see something, tell me” It’s like being on the high bar. Just knowing that there’s somebody to make sure you don’t fall helps you become more self-confident.

If you put your mind to it, you can adapt your leadership behavior, consequently become a great leader, just as you can learn charisma, and story-telling, and active listening and, and, and… Sure, it takes training, sure it takes time, but then again what doesn’t?

Does the same hold true for our personality? One of the most reliable assessments for personality is the Big Five Personality Test that measures Extraversion/Introversion, Conscientiousness, Agreeableness, Openness and Neuroticism. What is the connection to leadership? Research shows, for example, that extraversion is the strongest and most consistent correlate of transformational leadership6 and that conscientiousness predicts ethical leadership7.

While in the past researchers have focused more on what has remained stable over people’s lifespan, newer studies have looked at whether measurable change in the Big Five can be effected, if so wished. This is an important distinction to make – the key question is whether people want to change their personality. Most people do not wish or aspire to – they tend to settle into it like a comfortable shoe and shift only if this shoe stumbles over uneven ground.

A study from 2021 measured how much change was possible when people actually desired it. They looked at more than 1,500 participants over the course of three months.8 What did people wish to change? About 25 percent wanted to decrease their neuroticism, 25 percent each increase their conscientiousness and become more extroverted. The other 25 percent were spread over the other elements both for increase and decrease.

The result? There was a significant change in the desired direction. Who could see this? The participants themselves both for the increases as well as the decreases in personality traits. For the case of increase also family, friends and acquaintances noticed the change. Not, however for any of those decreases that the participants were sure they had achieved. Why was this decrease in the personality element not observed? This may have to do a) with the study only taking three months, and b) with the fact that, as seen above, we humans are not particularly great at noticing others. How very hard, in our limited interactions to notice the decrease of something….

The message though is clear: If I really want to change my personality, I can. Of course, it is helpful to get support in the shape of education, a coach or even an app – in the study quoted above, the support came from a smartphone app.

This research would also be a support to all those who may be disgruntled at my research shown in Figure 7.3 showing clearly that employees judged their leaders to be significantly humbler when these were extroverted, agreeable, conscientious, open and low on neuroticism. Since we now know that you can achieve shifts in your personality – you can equally well achieve shifts in your humility.

Figure 7.3 Personality traits and humility.

Take-Away: Knowing that something can be learned makes you wise – Many people are not even aware of everything that can be learned - unconsciously, we often assume that some things are natural and thus limit ourselves and others. Research shows that everything that is necessary for leadership can be learned, even something as magical as charisma!

Learning from Errors and Not-Knowing

An interesting question now arises – what can you learn more from? From errors or from weaknesses?

We have a problem with our culture of dealing with errors. It is a mere buzzword. Instead, we are actually still perfectionists.

(Interview No. 68, CIO, 76,000 employees)

We had this bad investment – the biggest machine, we ever bought. People wanted the machine, so in the decision-making process all negative arguments were ignored. Now, no one wants to take responsibility and we find it hard to learn from the mistake. It is still part of the culture that lies are actually wanted and rewarded.

(Interview No. 87, CEO, 7,000 employees)

As we have already discussed, errors are eminently important for companies and sometimes this is understood by the highest echelons.

Peter Senge in his book “The Fifth Discipline” tells the story of Ed Land, inventor and founder of Polaroid who had a plaque in his office that read: “A mistake is an event, the full benefit of which has not yet been turned to your advantage.”9

Surprisingly, people find the use of mistakes not immediately easy to recognize.

Let us look at the research. A study from 2020 shows that people generally prefer to share successes rather than errors. Even in those cases where errors were not connected negatively to the individual. Despite there being no chance of an attack on a vulnerable ego, people still prefer to be associated with successes than with errors.10

Do the test: If you could share either a failure or a success which would you prefer?

Those of you who work in a competitive environment, where there is a greater chance of being humiliated instead of appreciated will probably think: “Of course I only share successes.” But what if you were working in a good atmosphere, with people talking openly about their shortcomings. The underlying question would then be: What do you learn more from? From errors or from successes?

If you think about it that way and look back on your own experience, you will probably have a considerable number of examples where your own failures or those of others have been very helpful to you.

Two examples of learning from one’s own mistake.

The easiest way for me to think something through is to write it down. When I wrote my doctoral thesis, I did exactly that. After I finished the first draft, I went to my thesis supervisor. And he was furious. Why? He had wanted to influence the basic structure, something I had now denied him. Since then, I have continued writing down everything, so I can be sure that I have really thought things through (just like this book). But when I go into discussion, I only take a very rough agenda, so that the person being asked for advice has the chance to comment freely and make suggestions. They do not have to know that one version is already finished. As to implementing their advice, that is not difficult, since this first version never looks as much like a final version to me as it might to other people.

Siemens, together with the French company Areva, is building a pressurized water reactor for Finland at a fixed price of €3 bn. Construction has been underway since 2005, commissioning is postponed annually – the new plan envisages 2022 for a start. The costs have risen gradually from the agreed on €3 bn to more than €8.5 bn. Siemens will probably have to swallow more than €2 bn of this. It seems that Siemens has learned from this concatenation of errors. There was a cancer therapy center in Kiel, which they had already almost completed building. When it became clear that €300 Mio. additional costs were threatening and that the project would never be generating a profit, Siemens pulled out and demolished the almost completed building. They clearly did not want another Finland!11

Yet, sadly, our human brains seem to be designed in such a way that it is hard for us to appreciate the informational value of errors, unless we humbly push ourselves.

This even leads to the following strange result. Imagine an experiment with numerous quiz questions. In the first part, participants are asked to answer different questions. Afterwards, they either receive feedback that their answer was right, wrong or no feedback at all.

In the second part of the study, they can decide what to share with others who are about to embark on the same quiz. The absurd result? A whopping 70 percent actually preferred to share those questions with others on which they had not received any feedback! Where they did not yet know the right answer! Rather than those on which they had chosen the wrong answer. The information that the answer 2 b is wrong is far more useful (because then I know that 2 a must be correct) than that the participant still has no idea, whether the right answer is 3 a or 3 b.12

What is the reason for this? It may have something to do with emotions and memory. Negative emotions caused by mistakes are often and successfully repressed. This may not come as a surprise if, for example, 70-year-olds are asked to remember their high school grades. The past is already long gone, why use neurons for depressing facts. Yet even college students who have just left school remember their grades miraculously (and wrongly) far more positively.13

In contrast, our brain is easily fascinated by the fact that something has remained unsolved, i.e., has not found an answer. This fascination overrides our ratio, which clearly tells us that it is better for others to know which mistakes to avoid rather than to share an interest in the unsolved question.

In real life, however, there is also much value in sharing the not-knowing. Why?

Only, if someone first admits to their self, that there is a lack of knowledge, can they begin to learn. Moreover, when asking openly, it is often surprising how many other people were also struggling for an answer. Once all profess to their lack of knowing – they are already on a common journey to knowledge.

Anne Mulcahy, former CEO of Xerox, was proud to be called “Master of 'I don't know.’” In a program at Harvard Business School, she said that this willingness to be vulnerable to others turned out to be a great asset: “Instead of people losing confidence, they actually gain confidence [in you] when you admit you don't know something.”

This then created space for others at Xerox to offer their expertise and participate in the process of transforming the company.14

So, be prepared to share not only your errors but also your ignorance. The learning effect is enormous.

Take-Away: Mistakes and not knowing are gifts – One can learn a great deal from one’s own mistakes and from those of others. It is worthwhile to learn to love mistakes - as well as to share one’s own ignorance with others.

Knowing How Something Can Be Learnt

Let us look now at how exactly we learn.

Every week I have a feedback round with my head of HR and my assistant. They say what they as well as the employees understood from me in the last week and what did not get through. My appeal to all managers: get one or two people who really tell you what got through.

(Interview No. 37, CEO, 3,300 employees)

If you really want change you need to stop measuring successes and start measuring learning speed.

(Interview No. 127, Managing Director, 85,000 employees)

The virtues we get by first exercising them…. For the things we have to learn before we can do them, we learn by doing them, e.g., men become builders by building and lyre-players by playing the lyre; so too we become just by doing just acts, temperate by doing temperate acts, brave by doing brave acts.

Aristotle15

What does research say about how we learn?

Firstly, we need openness to listen at all. That sounds so easy, but often we do not listen properly.

For one, we may feel threatened by the knowledge of others as seen in this interview.

People find it hard to say that someone is better than them and be truly prepared to learn. Even when they are talking to an expert – they tend to say “sure, sure, sure” – instead of “thank you for this opportunity to learn”. They often duplicate what the experts say and pretend that they already knew.

(Interview No. 27, Head of HR, 57,000 employees)

The problem lies in my pretending to know. If I do this, I will want to protect myself and not ask open questions or probe deeply.

And for the other, we often only hear what we want to hear.

Bob Pittman, the founder of MTV, described in an interview with the New York Times how, in his view, real listening works:

Often in meetings, I will ask people when we’re discussing an idea, “What did the dissenter say?” The first time you do that, somebody might say, “Well, everybody’s on board.” Then I’ll say, “Well, you guys aren’t listening very well, because there’s always another point of view somewhere and you need to go back and find out what the dissenting point of view is.” I don’t want to hear someone say after we do something, “Oh, we should have done this.”16

This type of very open listening is part of the intellectual humility which is vital for any leader. Why? Studies show that those people who had greater intellectual humility were more interested in the viewpoints of others. Which sounds like a rather obvious result. Until you learn that these people were not only more interested in the opinions of others, but also in the actual person holding these opinions. Which led to less condemnation of others and more willingness to be open for interaction.17 There is hardly a more vital prerequisite for good leadership to be found than this intellectual openness!

In addition, with more intellectual humility, people were prepared to read more arguments from the other side and as a consequence learned more about the situation as a whole.18

In general, the intellectually humble are more intellectually curious which sets them firmly on a path of learning.19

How to acquire intellectual humility? A first step may be to realize that many of the smartest people had it. Newton adopted for himself what scholar Bernard of Chartres from the 12th century had poignantly expressed: “We are like dwarfs standing upon the shoulders of giants, and so able to see more and see farther than the ancients.”

Einstein went one step further and took a critical view of humanity as a whole: “As a human being, one has been endowed with just enough intelligence to be able to see clearly how completely insufficient this intelligence is when confronted with the existing.”

Did this attitude make Einstein, Newton or Chartres dumber or less successful? No, the opposite. Precisely because they were able to put their own knowledge into perspective and admit when they were wrong, were they able to develop wisdom. Even Warren Buffet understands when he has thought something wrong and without a hint of pain points the finger at himself. The question of why he didn't buy Amazon shares prompted him to make a clear statement: "Stupidity".20

Where do you find this sort of humility today? In managers who decide to spend the little free time they have in educating themselves. One good example is Dirk Rossmann, former CEO of a large eponymous Europewide chain of drugstores with more than 55,000 employees. He had left after primary school, yet inculcated in himself a yearning for learning. In his autobiography, he tells how he loved to read Nietzsche, Schopenhauer, Balzac, Dostoevsky, Zola, Goethe etc., and it becomes clear that he believes this sort of openness to the world to be a prerequisite for modern leadership.21

As a parent, I believe something similar. Put concretely, I believe it is my duty to give the children an overview of what has been thought and experienced by those that came before us. Only then can they extend and expand into their own future. I have, therefore noted down nearly 400 films and 300 books that I proffer to the children before they leave home. My fear is that otherwise they will neither appreciate what has already been thought nor will they be inspired to build on it. Which focus on the past, of course, does not mean, that the new and modern is not also vital for them to learn. Both lists have many modern films and books on them. Missing out on the modern would be an equal fault. I remember a friend at Cambridge who pompously leaned on the mantlepiece in his room and announced: “All literature you ever need to read was written before the First World War.” He actually wanted to stop with Anthony Trollope (1815–1882) and thought it a great concession to extend the period till 1914!

You can also find the intellectual humility needed for learning in managers who keep on going to seminars. One interviewee, who had actually been nominated in one of my questionnaires by his employees as a humble leader that I must interview, told the following story:

I was working at a company, where everyone was meant to go to two management courses a year. A lot of people did not like this and surprisingly conveniently fell ill around the time of the seminar. When the paid for place was then advocated, I was the only one who typically took it.

(Interview No. 149, Head of EMEA, 7,000 employees).

How different compared to many others, who find themselves too busy and too irreplaceable to take time to learn. I interviewed one manager who was disappointed that his employees were not taking time to approach him at lunch with their topics. “Here I used to be sitting in the canteen and no one came to me with their issues.” To him, that showed a lack of openness, a lack of responsibility and bravery. When asked if now during Covid-19, he could not help his employees to become more open, by arranging online lunches – in a blind date format which is so successfully used in other companies, he said: “I no longer take lunch. I have too many meetings.” Which in the long run becomes too little learning.

Intellectual humility is also found in belief systems. Buddhism, for example, has the term “Shunyata,” which carries within it both the concept of emptiness and openness. The key is not to believe that you have grasped something, but to always be open to the new.22 This is also called the “beginner’s mind,” with which you can perceive everything afresh. How to train it? By asking yourself questions such as: “If I had no preformed opinion about this matter, what questions would I ask to better understand the issue?” Or you could even take your inquisitive stance one step further and say to yourself “I have just come from a different planet, and really need to understand. X or Y.”

One of my interview partners told of how the CEO of an acquired company shadowed him for a day and afterwards said that he had never heard anyone say “Please forgive me, I don’t understand” as frequently as on that very day. His answer was: “This is by far the best way to learn.”

Interestingly, intellectual humility is not correlated with any reduction in self-esteem. Instead, it is the other way round. The lower the self-esteem, the harder people battle with intellectual defeat.23 And the higher the humility, the higher the self-esteem.

Aristotle not only showed as in the quote above that humility can be learned but also points out that the word for virtue, “ethike,” is only one step away from the word habit, “ethos.”24 And habits can be learned through repetition. Ergo: Right action creates virtue.

What does this mean for your learning?

You must build on a solid basis. What do I mean by this? Look at your own strengths and weaknesses while liking yourself as a being. No self-mortification and no self-hate. Instead, the willingness to accept yourself in general. Of course, this sounds terribly esoteric and soft – but do you really think that a Warren Buffet, a Jeff Bezos or a Marillyn Hewson, CEO of Lockheed Martin, regularly have deep self-doubts? Only once you have accepted yourself at a fundamental level, will change or learning lose its feeling of danger.

If you like yourself, and also see yourself in total, any learning will be a sound investment into your personal growth. As we will see in the next chapter, one element of the greater picture is your own life. In total. Whenever we get mentally or emotionally stuck; whenever we focus only on being a victim or on how fearful we are; whenever we cease to learn and adapt; then we are actually not being humble towards our whole life.

To make sure that you find a fundamental acceptance that is neither too self-critical nor too lenient, once again coaching may be an option. If you are interested in seeing how much a coach can open and shake you up without damaging your self-esteem, check out the book by former Google CEO Eric Schmidt about THE Silicon Valley coach, Bill Campbell. He was able to nudge dozens of CEOs roughly and lovingly into the open – and into clarity.25

What else do we need?

We clearly must consider ourselves capable of learning. That sounds so easy. Unfortunately, we often have reservations at our innermost core and as a consequence do not even realize that we are doubtful about being able to change. Like the 50 percent who do not believe that everyone can become a great leader.

Some of these reservations happen automatically without us even noticing.

There is a wonderful study showing this.26 Participants were divided into two groups. Both groups had to judge whether a series of words could make a meaningful sentence. Some examples:

“pupil apt Todd an is pencil,” “the brightest nothing idea everything promoted”
“welcome not morons one are here,” “the room obtuse had white green”

The participants had to read and evaluate a number of these sentences. One group those containing words about wisdom like the first example (apt, brightest) and another group devoted to stupidity like in the second sentence (morons, obtuse).

Then the participants worked on a memory task with numbers. During this they were lying in an FMRI and their brains were being scanned.

What makes this experiment relevant for you? In those participants who had read the negative sentences, the brain was much less perceptive of the fact that it was making mistakes. The area of the anterior paracingulate cortex, which is needed for learning, was only activated in those brains that had anchored themselves as “smart.”

The consequence of this? If I think I am stupid, I actually absorb less information about what I am missing and therefore will be less willing to learn than if I think I am smart. “Stupid will remain stupid!”

The first step to learning is therefore to consider yourself and others capable of learning.

There are various techniques to achieve this. On the one hand, of course, to remind yourself of the many occasions when you have learned something new that you now master. Most of us simply have to think back to puberty to shudder at the huge amounts of things we didn't know back then – be it actual knowledge or how to interact with others or drive a car or assemble Ikea shelves. And observe what is now so easy to do. This can range from cold calling to understanding blockchain to speaking in public. Or look at us after the Covid epidemic, how quickly we all have learned operating, managing and leading remotely.

If you cannot recall any such great learnings, you can at least trick yourself into learning mode. How does that work?

Research shows a path. Let us look at one study to begin with: If I write down the characteristics of a professor, I end up answering more Trivial Pursuit questions correctly than if I either do not write anything down beforehand or think about football hooligans.27 Why? According to the stereotype, hooligans only have fists and no sparks of wisdom! Our brain follows the stereotype of what food for thought we give to our neurons.

This effect is called priming and has to do with the fact that our unconscious is strongly influenced by the environment.

To understand this effect in more detail, take a look at some study results from outside the business world:

If German music is played in supermarkets, considerably more German wine is sold. Hearing French chansons, buyers instead waltz to the door with a Bordeaux in hand.28 By the way, the same applies to the amount of wine purchased – the more classical the music, the more bottles.29 The motto of the brain seems to be: Rosso for Rossini; Belting it down for Beethoven.
It has long been known that women objectively perform worse on maths tasks when they are reminded of the fact that they are women. Why? Because the reminder awakens in most women the stereotype that they – as women – simply cannot do maths.30 And then they can't. Absurd! Yet it also works the other way round: A new piece of research from 2018 shows that women perform better than the control group if they are previously primed by reading a story about a successful man.31
And last but not least: If I prime someone towards young people, they take more risky decisions than if I prime them towards older people.32

In summary, this shows that the decisions and the performance of our brain depend on how we approach a task, with what attitude towards ourselves as well as our environment.

The same applies, of course, in a working context.

In a beautiful study from 2018, researchers showed that a CEO can have a resounding impact on the brain of his employees with one single e-mail. The only difference between this e-mail and his normal weekly e-mail was that his usual 100 words now contained 12 words that focused on performance. Words like: “achieve,” “strive,” “success,” “triumph” and “mastered.” The result: The following week, the employees were 15 percent more productive as well as 35 percent more efficient.33 Did the employees perhaps consciously allow themselves to be inspired by the clever manager? No! When the employees were asked whether anything in the boss's e-mail had been different as compared to the other weeks, the employees were unaware of anything illuminating. Only that the boss “is sentimental,” “he tries,” or more brutally “He is just getting old.”

Clearly then, learning and performing is closely linked to our individual mindset: How much do we honestly believe that we can really learn something new, that we can truly change? How much, on the other hand, do we see our talents and our abilities as set and fixed?

On one level, hardly any of us will have doubts regarding some elements of future learning. Do any of you doubt that you will succeed in understanding the next two or three new technologies. Since all of us have mastered mobile phones, memory sticks and Zoom, we will certainly master holograms or external brains. If, of course, they are presented in a customer-friendly way.

Now the time has come to look at your results from the questionnaire that you filled in at the beginning of the chapter. Over the years I have adapted it from the research of Stanford professor Carol Dweck, to make it more applicable to managers. What you will see is whether your own answers categorize you as someone with more of a growth mindset or more of a fixed mindset.

Before I go into one of the major points of contention in this questionnaire, which is actually the word “intelligence,” I would like to turn to the word “growth mindset.”

For years, Carol Dweck has been investigating whether it is possible to correct unconscious assumptions about one's own intelligence. A widespread assumption is that intelligence is present from birth and that some children simply do not stand a chance in certain school subjects. This explains the often-heard sentence: “I just can't do maths. I am simply too stupid for that.”

Take a closer look at one of her studies34: In a high school, 1,594 young people are divided into two groups. One group is given reading material about the brain, especially about neuroplasticity, i.e., the fact that the brain can be shaped and trained like a muscle. The second group is also given reading material on the functionality of the brain, without going into the subject of plasticity or the trainability of the brain.

At the end of the semester, school grades are compared and now something happens which does not surprise those who have previously understood the Pygmalion effect. With the Pygmalion Effect, somebody else thinks the person is smart or capable of learning. In this case, half of the young people (those that have read about the brain's plasticity) think of themselves as capable of learning. And the impact of this belief is large.

In the “Growth-Mindset” group (“My brain is just a muscle that needs to be trained – by myself”) 10 percent more pupils than in the reference group reached their class goal, in mathematics even 20 percent.

The information about the brain helped one group realize that there was no fixed, biological judgment made about them by their DNA or their neurons. No genetic damnation. Instead, it is all about practice, practice and practice. Any neuroscientist will confirm this: The more I use the same neurons, the more they form a kind of highway, so that the electrical impulses move faster and more effectively along them.

This may also serve to explain why countries that focus more on practicing show higher overall average intelligence quotients per population than those in which there is no structured education.

Look at the numbers: The average IQ in 2022 was highest in Japan and Hong Kong at 106 (where children practice and practice and practice) and lowest in Nepal at 43 (where many people have no access to formal education at all).35

Back to the students in the Carol Dweck experiment: Due to the knowledge about the brain, something like mathematics has become learnable. 20 percent more achieving the goal is already pretty good for a short intervention. That is probably the jump from a D grade to a C and for some from a C to a B.

Of course, I do not want to overshoot the mark – a Growth Mindset will not allow everyone to become an astrophysicist. However, it is important to recognize that a Growth Mindset means that a momentary failure is no absolute judgment of your being. It is only an indication that your muscle has not been sufficiently trained.

It is therefore not surprising that research shows what can be trained in many other areas.36 For example, emotional intelligence37, transformational leadership and, as we have already seen, charisma. Not surprisingly, tenacity also has a measurable positive effect on performance38, with the equally negative consequence that many people who are described as talented do not achieve anything special because they lack stamina.

Therefore, you should not praise anyone for being the smartest in the team or the one who is by nature good with customers. If people are praised this way, fear arises that at some point it may transpire that they are not actually naturally smart or customer-oriented after all. Fearing to be found out that an innate quality is not there typically leads to shirking challenges or avoiding learnings. And in the blink of an eye, the praised ones become objectively worse.

This does not happen if you praise practice, effort or perseverance: “Really impressive to see how dedicated you have been in preparing for the presentation. As a result, it was really mind opening for me!”

If things go wrong, it is not about a missing character trait or an immutable truth about yourself, but only about insufficient preparation.

Back to the questionnaire and your results: About a quarter of the participants get angry after this questionnaire and argue the following: “Newspapers say that intelligence is largely hereditary. I cannot agree that everyone can learn the same amount, or that their own intelligence can still change hugely.”

Unfortunately, researchers have made life hard for us with their phrase of “60 percent inheritability of intelligence”: This seems as if our brain is not so much ours to develop but simply is as it is. The 60 percent, however, does not mean that I can only have 40 percent influence on myself and my understanding of maths and physics. (By the way, even those 40 percent may already constitute a jump from a D to a B.)

The research does not say that the environment or the self only make up 40 percent of each individual – it only says that in the STATISTICAL MEAN 60 percent of intelligence can be explained by inheritance. And we all know that the statistical mean is just a game of large numbers and does not necessarily have anything to do with me or my co-worker.

Instead, research has shown often how much intelligence is influenced by the environment as well as education.

To give just two examples:

In a study of 1,237 identical twins, it was found that one quarter had a similar IQ, half of them had 10 points difference and a quarter showed a deviation of 20 points. This quite enormous difference – between being normal and highly intelligent, or between below average and a solid average – is seen as due to environment and individual adaptations.39 And here it should be emphasized that, due to the same genetic make-up, identical twins are seen as the gold standard for measuring effects.
Since the 1980s, the so-called Flynn effect has been known. This shows that if we were to believe fully in IQ tests, our ancestors in 1900 would have had a lousy IQ of 60. Why? Since 1900, IQ test results have improved by about 3 points every ten years. This means that there has been a global intelligence gain of almost 40 points.40 To truly believe this, at least I lack the everyday proof of greater wisdom in our age. What it shows, instead, is how we humans have learned to think in abstract concepts. Which is exactly what the IQ tests are asking for and loops back to Japan and Hong Kong having a higher average IQ. In these countries, schools and culture put a strong emphasis on the practice of concept application for hours every day, already at a young age.

The implication of this research is that it is possible for people's IQs to be altered by their environment, their mindset and their training. Which, as we have seen, is confirmed by research on humility. Those who are humble in terms of their own intelligence learn not only to be more open, but also learn to perform better.41

Is it actually important that managers are intelligent? We could argue that many of the soft skills a manager needs to learn are actually quite independent of intelligence.

How intelligent is the average manager? You get a first inkling when you look at the degrees managers have. Statistically speaking, physics students have the highest IQ at 130, economic students score at 113 and those studying administration around 109.42

22 percent of students with an IQ of 95–104 completed their studies, 33 percent of those having an IQ of 105–115, and 60 percent of those with a yet higher IQ. Quite some difference, though it also shows that grit can partly compensate for a lower IQ and a high IQ is no guarantee for anything!

Some people even argue that our IQ fluctuates quite considerably depending on how much you actually challenge your brain. Neuroscientists have found that at the end of a three-week holiday, the IQ had dropped by up to 20 points.43

It is also clear that if you keep on using your brain in one specific way, that will give you some remarkable expertise which is typically only related to a high IQ. To better understand this, let us look at something with the bizarre title of “carton Calculus.”44

Psychologists in Baltimore observed workers in a factory. Their IQ scores were rather low. Their job was to process milk orders, packing cartons of 12 bottles of milk each with exactly the right amount of whole milk, low-fat milk and buttermilk. Depending on the order. Experienced workers can do this at tremendous speed bundled with an enormous amount of higher mathematics.

If an order called for 6 pints of whole milk, 12 pints of two-percent milk and 3 pints each of skim milk and buttermilk, an experienced assembler might select a case for 24 pints that was already half-filled with two-percent milk and one-third filled with whole milk, rather than try to prepare the order from scratch with an empty case. Using the half-filled case would enable the assembler to fill the order by removing 2 pints of whole milk and adding 3 pints each of skim milk and buttermilk, for a total of only three [back] bends. Moreover, when the orders were not evenly divisible into cases, the assemblers were able to shift between different representations of the order, a feat equivalent to shifting between different-base systems of numbers.

In other words, the brains of “normal” workers are able to acquire mathematical skills, which outside of work they would never have expected of themselves.

The takeaway of all these investigations: You and your employees can learn most that you or they wish to learn.

Of course, it makes a difference when you learn something. Some talents even seem to be innate because we typically do not really understand the environment of the first few years. For example, Mozart, just as Beethoven and later Yo-Yo Ma, were surrounded by music from birth on. And yet they all worked like fiends at their compositions and/or their playing – and you can see how much better they got year by year.

Regardless of whether you follow the approach of 10,000 hours that measurably successful musicians and athletes tend to put into their performances – the effect of environment and practice on one's own performance is enormous.

The question now is, of course, what are the take-aways for your leadership? When you come to implement strategies, understand customers, communicate with employees – all of which appear less daunting than astrophysics.

Once again, meet Professor Antonakis from the HEC Lausanne. He has interesting results based on 379 executives from 7 multi-national companies.45

Firstly, as regards their IQ which stood at an average of 111. This means that participants had a lower IQ than your typical business graduates and still were successful managers. HEC Lausanne is one of the great business schools. It is also not cheap – which means that companies only send those executives that they value highly internally.

Secondly, as regards their effectiveness as managers. This was assessed by their employees. The result: employees perceived those managers as particularly capable whose IQ was somewhat higher than their own. The best grades were awarded to managers whose IQ was exactly 18 points higher than that of their employees.

If the IQ was below or – and this is of course very intriguing – above these 18 points of difference, the manager was perceived as less effective – both in terms of the emotional as well as the task component of leadership.

What emerges from this is the following: Intelligence and leadership are not linked absolutely, but rather relatively. This means that statistically you need a higher IQ to lead consultants at McKinsey than factory workers in the food industry. And too great a distance to the employees is damaging. Presumably because the role model of a leader no longer works, when the gap is too great.

As an aside, I wish there were research, as to whether this gap can be closed. My gut feeling tells me that a leader who is far smarter by IQ but has also trained their emotional intelligence and their humility may well be able to overcome any “official” gap in IQ between themselves and their employees.

What happens if the IQ of the leader is very high while neither EQ nor humility is particularly strong became very clear to me at one company. The Group-CFO was an ex-management consultant and could laugh heartily at his own jokes which dealt with stochastics and quirky details of US GAAP accounting. His colleagues and employees, most of whom were high-ranking managers themselves, chortled politely and very quickly returned to their own business. They did not get the jokes and had trouble perceiving their manager as particularly effective. As super smart, but not as relevant for their own behavior.

Now, finally, let us return to you and your questionnaire. First of all, this questionnaire is only an indication as to how you assess yourself. If, when talking about intelligence, you only thought about IQ and followed the unreflecting press about its heritability, your results may not show your true attitude.

Therefore, please reflect independently of the results: How much do you believe that people can learn something new?

Do you believe that someone can learn to implement strategy at age 20? Be a great coach at 30? Master excel at the age of 35? Deliver great public speeches at 40? At 43 learn how to do small talk? Become an innovator at 47? Decide to be more conscientious at 50? Change their leadership styles at 54? Be at the helm of a new technology at 57? Start a new business at 61? Become less of a control freak at 63? Develop into a story teller at 67?

You may find that you see some of these as possible, others not at all. By observing this, you can uncover in yourself what research calls belief systems or mental models.

James Clawson from the Darden Business School, whom you already met above, puts it yet more succinctly. He describes what he calls “VABEs,” short for Values, Assumptions, Beliefs and Expectations about the way the world is or should be.46 In discussions with leaders around the world, he repeatedly discovered that managers declare that 95 percent + of peoples’ VABEs are habitual, that is mindlessly repetitive. If Level One is “visible behavior” and Level Two is “conscious thought,” Clawson asserts that our semi-conscious Level Three VABEs are the basis for virtually all behavior and that the vast majority of people live their whole lives “simply” passing on not only their genes but also their VABEs to the next generation – like viruses.

We all have hundreds even thousands of VABEs and these have a big influence on what we believe you can learn and what your employees colleagues or supervisors can learn.

As soon as you draw a line somewhere, you need to be aware of that line and reflect on how substantiated it is. You need to reflect on how much your attitude to learning may limit you as a manager, or limit what you dare to expect from your others around you.

Of course, this is not a plea for having to be able to do everything perfectly. All the more so, since the best CEOs say of themselves that they have always hired people who were better than themselves. This is humility in action. Recognizing your own strengths as a leader while not being afraid when every single person can do something better than you can.

The key point is that it is vital not to believe in fundamental limits for learning. Neuroscientists confirm daily that the brain changes considerably and is able to incorporate new knowledge and skills at any age. What does it need? Enthusiasm and practice. Sometimes it even does not have to be active practice – imagination is enough. Mental piano practice is enough to strengthen the corresponding area of the motor cortex.47 That is why chess players, ballerinas or conductors constantly practice in their heads. Good communicators do the same. Spending time to mentally go through a speech to their employees, what sort of coaching conversation they could have, how to moderate a workshop as to get truly everyone’s thoughts and ideas. And as a good manager you should do the same. Both your perception of others as well as your social interaction with others can be strengthened by thinking, reflecting and practicing before going into action. Remember my colleague at the consulting firm – who thought through his day when on the bus to work? Now it becomes clear why he became so successful: he practiced his presence, his leadership, his communications skills in his head. Over and over again.

One tool for how you can become aware of your unconsciously set limits follows in the next section. At this juncture, the key conclusion is: Intelligence is not an issue for you as a manager or for your employees – it is far more the mindset that counts!

One final important point regarding the connection between your mindset and your goals. Research shows that we actually absorb more new knowledge when we act with a mindset, that the goal is learning per se and not the achievement of one particular goal.48 This does not mean that clear and high targets do not generally lead to better performance. Because they do. For example, in terms of grades49, or negotiation results.50 The higher my goal for a grade or a negotiation result, the more successful I will be. But learning in itself is a different kettle of fish: The key here is openness, not a focus on any specific achievement.

In other words, if you want to learn something new, just set yourself the goal of wanting to learn the new – and not to achieve X percent by day Y.

Take-Away: The mindset counts – A positive attitude towards learning, as well as knowledge about the neuroplasticity of the human brain, are prerequisites for learning. Your IQ does not play a decisive role in most of the competencies that a manager has to master. Instead, it is the mindset that counts. It informs what learning you believe possible for yourself and others. Yet once you have uncovered your areas of fixed mindset, you can see if you cannot find enough examples out there as to also allow this area to become one in which growth is possible.

Meditation as a Tool for Learning and Humility

I had connected to more than two thousand leaders via LinkedIn for writing this book. Once I had the German version of the book finished, I mailed the information about this to many of my contacts, adding a short abstract of my research results. Amongst the numerous reactions was one from a board member from a company with 36,000 employees. He congratulated me and said that he was too busy right now to delve into the matter more deeply. “Also, I have made the resolve to no longer read books. For five years I have been successful in keeping it.”

As a believer in books opening minds and enabling vision as well as empathy that came as a shock to me. My first reaction was negative and I wondered how on earth I would reply to such a message. I thought about some light-hearted joke about humility and openness or one gently prodding at what I saw as hybris. Then I stopped. And reminded myself consciously that I was actually a trained mindfulness teacher and as such was not called upon to make immediate judgments. I decided to let the topic sit with me. And lo behold, in the course of the next few days I realized a number of things: First, since I was an avid reader everyone who was not appeared to attack my choice. A truly narcissistic perspective. Secondly, was I actually saying that readers were in general wiser people and that one could not understand the world without reading? Was I thus calmy designating billions of people as incomplete and somewhat foolish? Had I not realized that not only the wise but also the criminal love books? The murderer of John Lennon was reading Catcher in the Rye, while waiting for the police; the Unabomber loved Joseph Conrad so much that he shaped his writing on him; Hitler and Stalin were both enthusiastic readers.

The consequence: After a few days, I wrote a message back, expressing my genuine thanks for having been given a new perspective on what had been a set tenet for my life. Reading could be good, but did not have to be – and people could be insightful and wise without it.

My gut feeling is that years ago, before I started to train my brain to perceive itself, my neurons would have happily fired me up into a quick and judgmental reply, keeping numerous doors shut to me and my thinking.

Most likely you already expected that somewhere in this book homework would appear. Homework that takes effort but which will enable you to plunge courageously into humility and get better at it day by day.

Nobody said that it is easy to be open, perceive others, keep on learning, see the bigger picture and be honest about yourself to yourself. And we have seen, while more than 80 percent of people believe they are already humble, this is not what employees see.

How do you want to deal with this gap? How do you want to become humble while handling all the other leadership tasks that come up every day?

One way to get there is via mindfulness, which is nothing more or less than the trainable skill of perceiving whatever there is right now in a non-judgmental manner.

Mindfulness has nothing to do with religion or esoterics. Instead, it is practiced by Formula 1 engineers and Navy Seals, by the boards of Google, Amazon, Apple and Beiersdorf. It is also taught and practiced in the English parliament (House of Commons and House of Lords), the US Senate and the German Bundestag.

As a bottom line, you might say that mindfulness is about training the brain to make it easier for it to take an outside perspective. It is as if you set up your balcony inside you from which you can observe yourself and others in a friendly manner. This non-judgmental observing encompasses your own thoughts, feelings and goals, as well as the situation and the people around you.

The outcomes of mindfulness training which have been reliably measured by research include: a greater ability to concentrate, more emotional control, more creativity, increased resilience and the ability to deal with difficult memories and fears.51 Moreover, mindfulness creates a secure ego that is self-aware and does not have to resort to aggressive defenses in order to feel safe.52 And finally, mindfulness strengthens the focus on our surroundings. Which is a vital perspective for a manager to have. One of the studies confirming this growth in awareness showed that people who meditated regularly for a mere three weeks had developed a better perception of others and were far more likely to intervene to support them.53

Responsible for these results are changes in the brain that lead to increased self-regulation.54 Our brain learns to focus less on our actions and thoughts and instead observes itself. As a result, it can perceive all of the following:

What is currently moving me? Where do I feel endangered? Why has my ego suddenly become defensive?
What are my feelings and what established behavioral patterns are being activated?
What is happening around me, in the room, in my team, in my company?

Likewise, the brain learns to perceive that thoughts and emotions come and go, come and go, come and go – and that it is our choice which of these thoughts and emotions we assign meaning to. This choice frees us from short-sighted reactivity, allows us to see the larger perspective, not lose sight of our goals and make conscious decisions.

Mindfulness can be practiced in numerous ways:

Focus on breath: Consciously notice how you breathe, where and how the breath is felt. Learning to register when your thoughts drift off. Not to get angry when they do, merely perceiving the drift and returning to the breath. Again. And again. Digressing happens even to the most experienced meditators. Like eyes see, ears listen, in just the same way, the mind thinks. The goal is to perceive this. The goal is not to have no thoughts at all or to relax.
Focus on being open to all there is: After having noticed where and how you are sitting/lying down, focus on the breath for a few minutes. Then shift to sounds that you can hear within and outside of you. For another few minutes you notice the body, then your thoughts and yet later your feelings. Last but not least, stretch your awareness and give room for all there is. Coming, going, being. It may be a sound, a thought, an itch in the left knee. Learn to perceive all of these without getting involved or judgmental.
Eat consciously: Notice the texture of what you consume, what it looks like on the fork, its scent, what the sight of it triggers in you, how you bite and swallow it, what taste and aftertaste it has.
Do other things consciously: Deliberately climb the stairs; consciously walk down the corridor; open the door with awareness; put your cup down with full intent; type each letter with full focus and alert fingers. Any everyday action can be done mindfully.

Of course, as with any training, more meditation also brings more results. This means that people who meditate for thousands of hours become ever more attentive, ever less reactive, ever clearer. A consolation though, for all of us who may not wish to spend thousands of hours: dozens of research papers show that even a little practice goes a long way.

Many of my participants mention that merely the realization that we do not have to take our emotions and thoughts seriously has given them enormous freedom – and this, they say, is quite easy to recall even when a short breath meditation has once again fallen victim to an emergency call.

Not only do you strengthen your learning with mindfulness, but also humility in general. With mindfulness it is far easier to perceive where I feel threatened. Where I may not be honest with myself regarding my weaknesses. Where I might be too lazy to fight for what I believe in. Where I might have judged an employee as incapable of change. Where I may not believe myself that I can learn something. Where I am focusing so much on the task at hand that I am losing sight of the bigger picture.

Take-Away: Mindfulness, the path to humility – Learning to be non-judgmental in the here and now makes it possible to be open and ready for learning. Practicing this regularly for short periods of time has a lasting effect.

Knowing How to Create a Culture of Learning

Narayana Murthy, former CEO of Infosys:

I have often said that Infosys is an intelligent and enlightened democracy. I collected lots of smart people around me. And, we would decide every strategic issue only after we have had intelligent, informed and passionate discussions. On any non-classified issue, I would invite, in addition to internal experts, outside people with experience in that area, listen to them, argue the pluses and minuses, and then come to an informed conclusion. The important ingredient for a good decision is to create an open, pluralistic and fearless environment where the best debates are possible.55

The Mercedes racing team56

In 2013, Torger Christian Wolff, known as Toto, became head of motorsport at Mercedes. Until then, according to world champion Nico Rosberg, the employees had “been afraid to bring up mistakes.” With Toto everything changed. Born in Vienna in 1972, son of a Pole and an Austrian, who died of cancer when Toto was 15 years old, Toto was already a successful investor before he joined Mercedes.

His key phrase is:

The days when we fail are the days when we learn the most. The pain of losing lasts for many days, usually until the next race. The joy of victory disappears on Monday morning after the Grand Prix. This keeps us moving.

What were his big changes? For one thing he actually introduced meditation. First with 20 hostile and critical engineers. Now more than 1,000 employees practice mindfulness every day. In this way they become aware of their own mistakes without in any way endangering their ego or feeling fundamentally shaken by them.

Toto built a culture in which any error is openly discussed. Those of the engineers. And those of the managers. Time is spent on this not only after a defeat but also after being victorious.

Result: Mercedes has been THE leading racing team for years: eight consecutive titles from 2014 to 2021. Only one other racing team managed to come close to this: Ferrari in the years 1999–2004, and unlike Ferrari, whose driver only managed to win the world championship five times, Mercedes has won eight times.

If you want to create a culture of learning, you have to consciously institute it in yourself as well as others. Research shows that those who have internalized humility often keep a learning diary.57 This reminds us of the story from Wall Street banker as told above who, at least once a week, “objectively” reflected on himself.

In addition, a culture must be created in which open feedback is the norm, and permission is given for everyone to ask hard questions.

An example for this can be found at Rockwell Automation. The company uses a technique called Fish Bowl.58 Managers sit in a circle, surrounded by dozens of other employees. Everyone can step into the inner circle, sit down and discuss a topic. Over the years, this has led to managers becoming humbler and more open. Through discussion they learn to respect and accept the opinion of others. At the same time, the employees feel appreciated in contributing to a culture of dialogue and sharing, which is essential for learning.

To generate a culture for learning, learning must be available to all as well as visibly fostered.

In most companies, people get promoted into middle management because there is a need and an external person is too expensive. Then they are not given any support in seminars or coaching because there are no funds.

(Interview No. 64, Head of Learning, 1,700 employees)

In my old position, I had started a reading circle. Every month we met for an hour and three of us presented a book in 15 minutes each. At the new company I gifted my executives this year with Kahnemann's book, Fast Thinking, Slow Thinking. We now talk about it regularly.

(Interview No. 161, COO, 4,200 employees)

In well-organized companies, of course there will be support at every level. And coaching will also play a key role.

I know I am tirelessly repeating myself as regards using coaches – a consequence of the research and having got a coach myself more than 10 years ago. And having seen the impact in companies where they shift from seeing coaching as a repair mechanism for something broken to a vital external viewpoint.

There are some, that do not yet get it – or find it too strenuous.

In my old company we instituted a coaching process in the management team. After a first very good meeting, my two colleagues said, that this was too unpleasant for them and they stopped it.

(Interview No. 153, CEO, 1,800 employees)

But many see the clear advantages. Like the former CEO of Infineon Peter Bauer, who sought a coach at the age of 43. His trigger was actually an article about conflicts in management teams and the narcissistic personality structures of many managers. This fitted his actual situation, and the writer of the article subsequently became his coach.

I became aware of how certain conflicts affect me and how I can deal with them - better and more easily. This was also a relief for those around me…. I realized that I can better moderate the situation by knowing or understanding what the function of my behavior is and how my contact person will react to me. In coaching we were able to practice this in advance.59

What is the percentage of humble Peter Bauers? There is no reliable data on this. But there is interesting evidence of CEOs who manage start-ups and who are scrutinized as to their strength and weaknesses, their willingness to learn and their ability to see the bigger picture by investors.

In the first phase of funding, 39 percent have a coach, and in the later growth phase over 60 percent!60 For many of these CEOs, learning does not stop with a coach. Consequently, the percentage of CEOs who have a therapist and deal with even more fundamental personal issues increases from 4 to 30 percent.

The important thing is to realize that a learning culture needs to start from the top.

Half of the board went to the same coach – looking at the internal triggers, what made and what hinders each of us.

(Interview No. 68, CIO, 76,000 employees).

Incidentally, the consequences of learning if allowed and fostered are consistently positive, as can be seen from the following examples.

A manager was responsible for profit and loss of a business unit and had reached her limitations. She was taken off the position. One or two years later she got a second chance. Now she was successful. Partly it was due to the fact that the new job was a smaller business unit, but I think it was also because she did not break down as a result of the failure, but used it to develop further. Humility also helps to deal with difficult situations and to use them. It has something to do with resilience.

(Interview No. 8, VP L & D, 22,000 employees)

Three years ago, we changed our learning organization and converted it all to online. There was a manager who was very attached to traditional classroom learning. He absolutely did not want to accept the new version. But then he jumped over his shadow and has been a proponent ever since pushing the topic enormously.

(Interview No. 11, Head L&D, 300,000 employees)

Only when she started to learn, did she understand that her strength did not lie in designing, but in listening and coordinating people who knew more than she did. She learned the basics about the subject, but also learned to let go and concentrate on bringing the team together, selling things internally and making their performance visible.

(Interview No. 12, VP Change, 100,000 employees)

Of course, the company needs to allow this personal learning to take place and not to label people as being one type of person forever. This means the culture of learning truly needs to be pervasive from top to bottom.

Let us look at three more examples where the whole company has tried to become a learning organization.

We shifted the company to a model somewhere between Laloux and holacracy. As the board we now only decide what legally needs to be decided. All else, we pass down to the right level. Initially we had 20 percent who thought this great, 20 percent willing to observe things and 60 percent sceptics. Now we are down to 25 percent sceptics and I am hopeful that we can get another 10 percent. All of us are learning every day.

(Interview No. 118, Board Member, 1,200 employees)

We must all have the same understanding of leadership. So, we have identified 4 styles, and each manager practices all four with an actor. Then we can help each other realize that in this situation the dictatorial style No. 1 is no good and that he or she could try style 3 or 4.

(Interview No. 128, CEO, 600 employees)

We have this small production facility with 500 employees. When I joined them, I expected them to be run by some local families, not really interested in learning. I was so wrong! First of all, the company took care that all managers had seen a lot. They sent them abroad and into education. Secondly, they were truly open to feedback. They said to me: As an economist, you may not really understand what we are doing here – but if you notice anything that we could do differently or better, please tell us. And they meant it! No wonder this is our most profitable factory.

(Interview No. 95, HR Director, 31,000 employees)

A true learning culture must encompass all levels in the company, easy access to learning from the outside, regular discussions of learnings inside and humble leaders and employees that role model learning.

While everyone is clear about wanting to learn and change – I mean, who would be proud if someone told them: “You really are exactly the same as you were in school!” – we sometimes see learning as drudgery and mere duty.

There is a better way to look at it, if you follow how this CEO had a burst-out at a conference in 2011 about electro mobility. “Stop thinking only about the good it does. Stop only thinking that we must do it. Just go and drive an electric car. It is fun, it is fast, it is sexy!”

The same is true of learning: “It is fun, it is fast, it is sexy!”

Take-Away: Consciously create a culture of learning – A positive learning culture doesn’t happen on its own. You have to deliberately work on your own and the company's. Different tools can be used. A learning culture needs genuine commitment at all levels.
