Chapter 8 Seeing the Bigger Picture

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-8

Consider yourself light. Think of the world as deep.

Miyamoto Musashi (Japanese Ronin 1584–1645)

Summary

It is easy to focus on your own momentary situation without putting yourself into perspective within the bigger picture. If you truly want to see the bigger picture, you have to see how finite we all are, that we are easily replaceable and that feeling like a victim is not an option. Tools for becoming aware of what is happening in and around you are provided.

My grandfather was a war criminal. Called “the Butcher of Poland,” he was trialed and justly hanged at the Nuremberg trials. My father wrote numerous books against him, so from my teens onwards I knew a lot about him. And soon formulated a burning question for myself: How could I and anyone learn to avoid becoming a war criminal? What steps does someone like my grandfather take that lead to the gallows?

My grandfather was a well-educated man, with some romantic notions about wanting to be important for Germany. Step by step he corrupted himself: with the people he served – he was Hitler’s private lawyer; with the money he yearned for – he loved being minister and General Governor of Poland, all that art and gold and food; with the sense of importance, he craved – he loved inviting famous conductors, composers and chess players to Cracow; and worst of all with the power he desired – he relished being the one able to order incarcerations, deportations or executions.

I think a lot of it has to do with a horrendous freely chosen blindness, an unwillingness to really see yourself and the bigger picture. What am I involved in here? Does this fit with my values? Do I think I'm too weak to get out? Do I feel like a victim of the situation instead of recognizing my own part and doing something about it?

Yet most people find it easier to blame circumstances and see themselves as victim, instead of taking control of that which they can control. Research with 55 war criminals confirms this.1 They all thought circumstances to blame and that someone else in their position would have been even worse. Also look at the balderdash my grandfather wrote on the night of his execution: “My 'guilt' is a purely political matter – but not a legal one. If men were wise, they would see the peace of my death and realize: 'No criminal dies this way!’”2

Pah, he had educated himself to be a criminal in the best possible way and had cowardly swept aside all opportunities to follow a still tenuously existing sense of justice.

Now, hopefully, these days we are not in danger of mutating into war criminals, but my grandfather's lesson remains: We need to understand what it is that we are doing, what we are responsible for, and at the same time to put ourselves in perspective. It is often the bigger picture and for that matter humanity that is important and not we ourselves in our small desires.

Before we go deeper into how this can be done, some proof that many of the managers I interviewed are aware of this issue and strongly active to keep this awareness alive in themselves.

It is important not to take yourself too seriously. If you think you are the smartest person in the room, better change the people around you or the room.

(Interview No. 24, CDO, 6,000 employees)

We are only a small part of the universe. There is much chance involved. We have an obligation to society simply by the fact that we have been born.

(Interview No. 25, VP and Head Global Services, 31,000 employees)

I am the smallest cog, since I produce nothing.

(Interview No. 47, CEO, 3,500 employees)

All of us are small ants only.

(Interview No. 70, CEO, 46,000 employees)

Humility is like golf. There is only so much that I can control.

(Interview No. 60, Director, 8,000 employees)

Many think of themselves as irreplaceable. I think this is hybris. Instead, we are finite, easily replaceable. Therefore, we should always ask ourselves, if we are actually doing the right thing.

(Interview No. 79, CEO, 1,300 employees)

I am happy when I can say that all works without me. I was ill for 7 weeks and all went perfectly.

(Interview No. 129, CEO, 600 employees)

I have been lucky in always meeting people who have supported me by giving me the right tasks. And I was lucky in being healthy. All of which are gifts that have nothing to do with my own skills or my own doing. Once you realize, how lucky you are you can only behave humbly.

(Interview No. 125, Group CEO, 1,200 employees)

There have been many blows of fate in my life, and yet I am blessed by people around me, my talents and my huge amount of luck.

(Interview No. 133, Board Member, 23,000 employees)

Learning humility is often linked to strokes of fate – death, illnesses etc. at least that was the case for me.

(Interview No. 117, Board Member 15,000 employees)

Whatever it is that you do – the question is always: Is it actually of use to the company that you are there?

(Interview No. 142, CEO, 3,000 employees)

What Is the Bigger Picture?

Seeing the bigger picture is a hard thing to do. More than 20 percent of my interviewees said that this was the linchpin of humility. Next to owning up to weaknesses the toughest nut to crack. This bigger picture was what seemed alien to many of the managers they observed and even more so to the employees.

What exactly is the bigger picture? It has numerous dimensions. It means, that we become aware of following:

We are finite: which sounds so simple and is so hard to remember as long as we are in full command and full sail.
We are easily replaceable: Who has not been surprised at how well things can run without others or even oneself if there is a crisis? Or if you have broken a leg, went on sabbatical or had a child – strangely things go on quite as well without us.
Much of our success is due to luck and circumstances: remember the research about the music markets? It was a mere chance which song was downloaded first and this first piece of luck hugely influenced the end result. Also remember the fact that it is not always the most talented that make it to the top – which is also attributed to luck. Of course, you may increase the chance of luck happening to you, by being prepared, by cultivating serendipity – but it is clear that much of what we tend to attribute to our talents is due to luck, circumstances and consequently other forces in terms of economics, politics, people etc. Take a look at the fact that you are employed right now, in an age where there are more options than hunting or farming, in a society not at war, with a roof over your head, an education under your belt and most likely enough technical support at hand that you and your close ones can look forward to a future even if the climate should change yet more strongly. All your doing? Mostly your doing? Some of your doing? 1 percent your doing? I leave it to you to assign the percentage.
Last but not least, the "bigger picture" is about the fact that there is actually a bigger perspective that you can take in relation to all actions and events. Like my grandfather should have taken, in terms of humanity!

Less powerful, yet still real, is the large amount of coincidences and circumstances on which depends what exactly you tell your employee today, how to write their presentation: From your education, your childhood, the state of your brain, your mood, the weather, the colleagues around you, the leader, the role you have taken on in the company, the pressures your company feels in world markets, world markets reacting to fundamental political changes or weather phenomena, the world as part of a galaxy where solar storms and gravity affect the entire system.

A lot to bear in mind, when all you want to achieve is that your team is motivated for today’s workshop.

And of course, we do not have to be aware at every moment that our planet is hurtling through the universe and that our decision to buy a pair of new shoes may be affecting the rainforest someplace. But we also need to avoid the opposite. The opposite is that in the daily hustle and bustle we lose sight of the big picture for ourselves and others.

So how do we fare with the big picture in today’s managers? We already saw in Chapter 3 that this got the worst scores. Only 48 percent of managers see themselves as a part of a bigger whole, only 51 percent understand that not everything can be under their control and only 46 percent want to make the world a better place.

These numbers get better when you look at those managers who are seen in total as humble. There we have 80 percent who see themselves as part of a bigger whole, 73 percent who understand that they do have not everything under control and 76 percent who are trying to make the world a better place – at least in the eyes of their employees.

Let us now look into more of the subcategories of the bigger picture. I asked participants to judge their managers on the statements as shown in Figure 8.1. How to read it? Look at the first column, the one saying 0.91: This means that on average those leaders that were generally seen as humble across all the different behaviors nearly scored – yet only nearly – an agree in the statement: “My manager subordinates his/her individual interests to the larger corporate goals.” In terms of percentage of managers scoring an “agree” or “agree strongly,” 74 percent achieved that, while the unhumble are not much worse at 69 percent.

Figure 8.1 Humble and non-humble managers and the bigger picture.

If you look in detail, there are some good and some shocking results. 97 percent of humble managers are conscious of their responsibility towards their employees, 76 percent seem to know that life is finite, 90 percent of humble managers adapt their behavior to fulfill the necessary tasks and 98 percent are aware that there is more in terms of culture, attitude and approaches out there. Shocking are the unhumble managers in that only 47 percent are willing to adapt their behavior to fulfill their tasks as a manager. That shows utter egocentricity and a total disregard for what the role of a manager entails!

All managers, however, even the humble ones, fail to be aware of how easily we all are replaceable and also of how much our successes are a function of luck and circumstances.

Some of you may mutter discontentedly: If humble managers are still rare, will they not, indeed, be harder to replace? And if they keep on working on themselves, will they not be more favored by their own talents and grit?

This is the moment, when our ego rears its head, wanting to be able to play a decisive role in our lives. Of course, as we discussed above, we have sayings like “luck favors the brave” or “luck is what happens when preparedness meets opportunity.” This means there is much that we can influence in our lives. Yet, I would still postulate that merely the fact that I can take the time and have the chance to write this book is 90 percent due to luck and circumstances. And only 10 percent to my being decisive, willing, stubborn, organized, mentally prepared and working at myself on least some of the time.

So even for those managers seen as humble, it would be worthwhile investing time to understand better, how much luck and circumstances are relevant for them.

What else must we consider in the bigger picture? To understand, where in it managers have a role to play. And because it is so salient, I repeat the quote from the beginning of Chapter 5:

What is my job? To bring people together so that they can successfully get tasks done. If one of my people is better or as good as I, they should do it. What is left for me? To go where it hurts, what no one else likes to do. I am being paid to go there and deal with people who are down or in pain.

(Interview No. 150, CEO, 6,600 employees)

There is often a refusal to really take responsibility for oneself.

(Interview No. 19, Head HR, 250 employees)

Firstly, we need to think about the concept of serving.

If you look at the history of management, one amazing fact stands out – when the first business schools were founded in the late 19th century, they saw the primary task of a manager in caring for their employees and serving society. Profit was a secondary objective.

This makes sense, since the word “administration” contains the Latin word “ministrare,” which means to serve and wait upon.

Owen Young, a lawyer and General Electric's CEO from 1922, even called management “the ministry of business.” He insisted that it was important for “the ministers of business, like the ministers of the church, to recognize their responsibility.”

This focus on serving was seen as broader than the company itself:

The autonomy and authority of professional managers is not only based on expertise, but also on their obligation not to represent the interests of the owners or the employees – and certainly not those of themselves – but to see that the company contributes to the general well-being.3

Today, many managers profess to stand in the same tradition of service. A few examples:

In 2016, Siemens CEO Joe Kaeser said: “A company must create meaning, it must serve society.”4
In 2019, the Business Roundtable, which includes 181 board members of major American companies, issued a statement turning away from shareholder value as the sole yardstick for companies. Instead, companies should be about investing in employees, creating value for customers, dealing ethically with suppliers and supporting the community.5
Satya Nadella, CEO of Microsoft posits: “If Microsoft is doing well, that means we’re creating productivity for that small business somewhere in the world. That alignment, where we do well if we can drive good outcomes for communities and societies and countries around us – that, to me, is at the center of it.”6

Nevertheless, in the everyday life of every manager, it is often the case that goals – the larger goals that go beyond the here and now – are often lost sight of. It is too easy to get caught up in the implementation of a project, in monthly or quarterly figures, in employee feedback meetings. This is where the larger goal or the perception of the interrelationships is lost in a flash.

Some of the interviews show the danger:

The Corporate Center has a tendency to explain the world to others. You can be self-confident, but the real added value lies elsewhere. This understanding is humility for me.

(Interview No. 11, Head L & D, 300,000 employees)

Many lack the reflection that we are only a small part within the company and should deal with the resources carefully. It is amazing how often business dinners are booked or financial packages decided on that you would never go for if it was your own company and your own money.

(Interview No. 92, Global Head, 150,000 employees)

What examples are there of a bigger picture seen and lived?

For the politician Lucius Quinctius Cincinnatus, the goal was to serve the Roman people. But only for as long as he was truly needed. Never for his own ego. Consequently, he twice allowed himself to be called from his cornfields to the Senate of Rome in order to rule as a dictator. As soon as the crisis was over, he returned to his plough. This quality of letting go is something that one would wish for in some company managers
Alex Okosi, media mogul, understood at a young age that his bigger picture was the team. As a teenager he had been proud to have scored 38 out of 50 points for his basketball team. When the coach chided him on what kind of teamwork that was, the penny dropped. Since then, the focus of his management philosophy became being a “player coach.” If this is successful, he believes success will follow: “If I stay focused on helping others be successful, I get better results and I enjoy myself more.”7
An example from another world: for director Luciano Visconti, the greater goal was to make the invisible visible. To achieve this, the meals in his great film “The Leopard” were actually real; the spaghetti cooked to perfection, the wine bottle genuine and the glasses brimming with real wine.8
Sylvia Metayer, CEO Corporate Services Worldwide Sodexo and in charge of 120,000 employees, focusses on the company: “I’m learning that to be a CEO is to be a servant. My main job is to support our employees, and be a support to our clients and to our consumers.”9

Before we get to how you can actually perceive your very own bigger picture, one vital point: it is important to remember that the situation you find yourself in has been chosen by you – in other words, that you are not a victim.

When you meet managers in seminars, there are often participants who are very angry with their situation. This may be the next higher level, the processes, the colleagues – whatever it is, you realize that people often agonize over and suffer from the situation.

Typical statements are: “I can't do the right thing, because board member Miller...”; “If the processes weren't so stupid, I could satisfy the customers much sooner”; “The personnel department hardly leaves me any leeway as regards to bonuses to motivate my employees – no wonder Ms. M quit her job for this reason.”

Do you notice what is happening here? The managers feel robbed of their ability to act and instead see themselves as victims of circumstances.

In one company this was particularly striking. Innovation was the topic of the seminar. Participants were the management boards and top managers of the subsidiaries. In addition to the professors from the business school, the group management board was present for one afternoon each time. In each of the nine rounds, the participants from the subsidiaries demanded a budget for innovation from the group management board. And each time, the group management board said that innovation naturally should take place within the companies. So please let the management boards of the subsidiaries try out the innovation on a small scale within their own budgets. If these pilots went well, they would be free to come to the group management board again.

The result: Nothing happened.

The management boards of the subsidiaries did not feel supported by the group management board and saw themselves as victims who were not empowered; the group management board saw itself as victim of the inactivity of the management boards of the subsidiaries.

A perfect stalemate. And one which reveals that both sides were not actually taking responsibility for what they believed to be vital, but rather preferred to be a victim of the other side.

See, how one can go about this differently. Look at this example.

An employee has an idea. He presents to his boss who fails to agree. Believing himself still to be right, he conceives of a way to test the new system in one geographic market. He comes back to his boss to ask for a go-ahead and support.

This time, though, he left his charts and data at home. He just told her why he believed in the idea and how its merits could be tested with low risk. To his surprise, the crusty distribution chief started to offer help in designing the experiment. “When you came to me last week,” she said, “you were trying to convince me. Now, you're willing to test your idea. I still think it's wrongheaded, but I can see you care a great deal. So, who knows, maybe we'll learn something.”10

From feeling like a victim to making a (successful) change possible.

It is actually quite a miracle how quickly even the most clever, active and alert people can perceive themselves as victims. Just ask yourself the following question: When during the course of last week did you feel like a victim either at work, on the way to work or at home? It may be something as trivial as that the doors of the lift just close as you walk towards it so you have to wait for 10 minutes and are late for a meeting. Or that your computer breaks down and IT does not return your emergency call for a whole hour. Or that your budget is reduced by 20 percent as a result of cuts in all supportive functions?

What is the result of you seeing yourself as a victim? Typically, anger and a feeling that there is little that you can do and influence.

How do you overcome this sense?

Realizing that you are where you are voluntarily! Realizing that you have chosen this very job, that you can change it at any time – even if that may be a hassle and not easy. And that in general nothing, the lift doors, the budget cuts, the ITs processes are directed personally against you.

Feeling as a victim is only the result of your thoughts and your reaction. If you change your reaction, you stop being a victim. In the example above: You can choose to be grateful to the lift doors because now you can gather your thoughts one more time before the upcoming meeting. You can also be happy about your broken computer because while you are waiting for IT you can finally pay a visit to team 3 that you had planned a long time ago. And what better way to get your team to shift into innovation modus than if you tell them about the 20 percent budget cut.

If you approach work with that attitude, it is no longer “I'm not allowed to do this” or “X prevents me from doing this.” Instead, it is “How can I do this?”

For example: “How can I ensure that my employees are more motivated?” As soon as I ask the question like this, a financial bonus is just one option and probably not even the best one.

“How can I test this innovation with my existing resources?” leads to different thoughts and actions. Research even has a term for this type of “make-do.” It snappily calls what happens when I have to create something with only few resources “frugal innovation.” And can prove that the more our brain is set limits, the more creative we become. Boundaries and difficulties foster our creativity and lead to better results. Would you like a few examples?

Objectively better company slogans ensue if participants are only allowed to use five words for them instead of an unlimited number.
Children's book author Dr. Seuss wrote his best book “Green Eggs and Ham” after he had accepted a bet to write a bestseller with only 50 words. Feel free to count.
If people are assigned certain words to include, the poems they write are objectively better than if they were completely free in their choice.11

This means that one must consciously accept the restrictions and be willing to work with them. Not like the above-mentioned top managers, who only wanted to start thinking once they had received an official budget. But instead, like in the following example.

In 1999 Renault manager Jean-Marie Hurtinger took up the challenge of building a Renault that could be sold profitably for $6,000. This was at a time when even the smaller Renaults cost around $12,000. Less than five years later he and his team had fulfilled the board's mission.12

In other words, by accepting the limited situation, you stop feeling a victim and regain control over your actions. This way you also take real responsibility for what you do. And as we have seen above, humble managers and humble employees are more willing to accept full responsibility for their actions.

The Bigger Picture in Everyday Life

Let us now look at what the bigger picture entails in everyday life.

Because the picture is big, I must take care not to drift off into lethargy or fatalism.

(Interview No. 109, MD, 1,800 employees)

I know a CEO who reminds himself before any investment decision: Assuming this was my money, what would I need to have a good feeling about the investment.

(Interview No. 122, Board Member, 2,000 employees)

I must never confuse my position and career with whom I am.

(Interview No. 113. Ex VP, 295,000 employees)

How do you find your own bigger picture and make yourself aware of it in the stress of everyday life?

First of all, you have to be aware of your role. What do I mean by this? Have a look at the following example.

Emilio Galli-Zugaro, the head of communications at Allianz, was walking towards his office at the headquarters when he heard a steady ringing tone.13 It was coming from the room of his employee who was in charge of the Allianz staff magazine. He rushed in, answered and solved the caller's problem in a quarter of an hour. Only then did he see that Nils, an employee from the communications department of an Allianz subsidiary, had been sitting in the room the whole time. He angrily shouted at him, why on earth he had not answered the phone. “Never heard of customer focus?” Nils justified himself stutteringly: “But that wasn't my phone.” Galli-Zugaro barked back: “Was it mine? But I answered it!” Nils kept trying. He didn't know what he should have said; he wasn't from the holding company; he didn't know if he was breaking rules by picking up and say something wrong.

As a communications expert, the story came at just the right time for Galli-Zugaro. At last, he had a vivid piece of evidence for a disastrous lack of customer focus that he could use in his mission to make Allianz more customer-oriented.

Cut. Please think for a moment about where the humility debacle lay. It took Galli-Zugaro a whole summer to realize it.

Ready?

In fact, the disaster was not so much the employee's fault as the fault of the company's culture. As head of communication, Galli-Zugaro had an instrumental role to play in how this culture was shaped. After reflection he realized that he had failed to strengthen employees in such a way that they could be courageous and feel able to take initiative, such as answering the phone. Neither had Emilio Galli-Zugaro listened to the employee – in other words, he had not been the Listening Leader that he wanted so much to be. Put concisely, zero empowerment, zero psychological safety. It was an internal communication disaster on not just one level, but on several.

Once Galli-Zugaro had recognized his role in this bigger picture, he set out to change the culture – and talk openly about his initial wake-up call.

Typically, there is a problem of perception. People tend to perceive primarily what they focus their attention on. Humans, therefore, easily fall prey to their own frames and those of others. Do you remember the frame that CEO Anderson set? He wanted safety but asked about finances. In doing so, he shifted the attention of employees towards finance and far, far away from safety.

The same holds true of other foci: If I focus on streamlining processes, my culture of innovation goes out of the window. If I drive cross-selling to every customer, ethical standards will suffer.

So, it is imperative to focus yourself and your employees on the bigger picture.

That is, the leader must invest time and brainpower to consciously perceive the entire situation and then take the right action.

Sometimes, for example, a right action may be to postpone a decision.

After a discussion, Alfred P. Sloan, former CEO of General Motors, said to his colleagues:

Gentlemen, I assume we are all in agreement on this decision. I therefore propose that we postpone further discussion on this matter until the next meeting to give us time to develop disagreements and perhaps some understanding of what this decision is really about.14

He obviously perceived that they had not yet understood the implications of the decision and forced a pause for reflection.

The Antarctic researcher Shackleton, on the other hand, had his bigger picture influenced by the danger of death. Once his ship Endurance was locked in by ice, his main goal was to survive. With every single one of his men. To achieve this, a positive mood of the whole team was vital. So, he conducted entertainments, had competitions, and more importantly, he arranged a sleeping bag lottery. There were two types of sleeping bags, the inferior wool bags and the far superior reindeer ones. The lottery was rigged in such a way that the crew got “lucky” and the officers had to bite the bullet.15 The realization: If the crew slept well, this increased the chances of survival for everyone.

It is also clear that these big pictures come in layers… there is a present one as for Shackleton to survive and a longer term one – explore the Antarctic. That means also for you there will be different and changing elements of the larger picture.

What can help you in the process of perceiving them?

Step one: Generate in yourself the feeling of awe.

Awe is an emotion that research has only recently begun grappling with. It is identified as the feeling of wonder and amazement at being in the presence of something vast that transcends one’s current understanding.16 For studies, the feeling is typically generated by watching videos of nature, often combined with virtual reality glasses or by actually being in nature. The effects of awe are numerous. Awe reduces the focus on self, increases pro-social behavior, lets money appear less attractive, increases creativity and allows people to see their weaknesses more clearly than their strengths. Therefore, a sense of awe actually measurably increases humility.17

Neuroscientists have been looking at what happens in the brain during feelings of awe and have seen that our default network, i.e., that which causes us to ruminate and focus on ourselves becomes less active.18 Interestingly this is the same effect that happens under the influence of psychedelic drugs. These are nice prospects for the next team event, although it should be noted that the combination of drugs and psychotherapy is considered even more effective.19 And that would make for a very peculiar event.

In a more practical vein, how can you inspire awe in yourself in order to generate the bigger picture?

First of all, you can allow yourself to see the vastness of what there is around you. In terms of astronomy, geology, human history and nature. For some, it is a screen saver of our galaxy that inspires this sense of awe. Or a picture of our earth viewed from space. For others, it may be a picture of the family on a mountain top, or a plaque on the table commemorating the 100th birthday of your company.

Whatever it is, it is worthwhile to find something to inspire awe in you, since then you will find it easier to put yourself into perspective. Into perspective as regards your ego, your joys and also your sufferings. It also helps to see that we only have this one life. (Hopefully, I am not alienating some readers here – even if we did have several lives, awe would help you even more in seeing that our actions today have consequences for our future lives…)

It also helps to strengthen our understanding of what the world contributes to us. One of my interviewees expressed this succinctly:

I run a CSR program at the local business school. I may believe this position is about me as a person. But how can I say this? If I did not carry the business card of my company, no one would care a whit for me. This logo which awards me the opportunity of being allowed to teach has been generated by thousands of people.

(Interview No. 29, Group Head Customer Experience and CSR, 27,000 employees)

Step 2: Now look into the different levels you live in. I suggest to start with yourself and then consider what and to whom you feel connected and what roles you have.

See the Bigger Picture
Yourself

It is immensely important to see the first bigger whole as yourself. You should never lose sight of your own worth and value.

I have humility in respect to my own life. As a consequence, I need to see that what I do actually makes sense in the grander perspective.

(Interview No. 79, CEO, 1,300 employees).

The task is to not waste yourself, not allow yourself to be trampled on; if something is taken away from you in terms of tasks, status or even people, after a reasonable period of mourning you are, in my view, obliged to your own self not to remain down but to get up again. If you do not do this, because you perceive yourself as beaten, or as too weak to take action, then you are not paying yourself the respect that is due to this unique life of yours.

This does not exclude that there may come times when you may need to “sacrifice.” This can be something small such as canceling your dinner in order to stay with the team or canceling your team meeting in order to stay with the family.

It may also be something bigger, such as giving up what you wished for, for your children, for your family or for your commitment to truly serve others. One impressive example for giving up your position as a service to the greater whole came from one of my interviews.

The new director was hired to lead the company into a digital future. Once on board, she discovered that it was less an issue of innovation but one of survival. The situation was bad, insolvency threatened and she had to look for outside support. Two offers came to the table. One that would save one third of jobs plus her own, the other which would save all jobs apart from her own. To her the choice was clear. She ensured a smooth hand-over to the buyers and found herself a new job.

(Interview No. 119, CEO, 200 employees)

The choice made above also had something to do with her idea of what she wanted from her whole life. Which of the two choices allowed her to have a calmer heart and happier head? To her it was clear. Others take the mirror test.

I need to be able to look at myself in the mirror. So, I have decided to never lie anymore – and life has become much better.

(Interview No. 150, CEO, 6,600 employees)

You need to look at what humility means in relation to your own life. What values, what duties do you have, how do you want to look out of the mirror? Now and at the imagined end of your life?

Your Role

Next is what you have taken on. This may be responsibility towards your family and friends, or – more apt for this book – your role in the company. This entails reflecting on what this role requires.

As a manager I need to take care of the soft topics – no longer spend day and night looking at numbers, data and facts.

(Interview No. 41, Group CEO, 1,800 employees)

There are too many jobs involved, so I need to make myself replaceable.

(Interview No. 51, CEO, 500 employees)

Our task it to build our brand. The brand is above all so we have to do everything to serve it. There are some managers for whom their own well-being is the most important – such people have no place here.

(Interview No. 63, Head of supervisory board, 2,600 employees)

It is my task to take the brunt for my people. There are some managers who wish to shine, take on way too much and dump it on their team. I need to know what my team can do and not overstrain it.

(Interview No. 111, CIO, 1,000 employees).

Our CEO has a very strong sense of responsibility for the legacy of the company. We have 170 years of history behind us and our goal is to celebrate our 200th anniversary as a fully independent company. He bases his decisions on this, i.e., he does not focus only on quarterly results, but looks at the bigger picture.

(Interview No. 8, VP People Development, 22,000 employees)

Whatever it is that you think your role requires of you becomes your bigger picture by which you must measure your everyday work. This may mean standing up for your department or your position, where that is needed. It may mean sacrificing some of your resources where needed to the good of the whole company and not act like in this example.

We had a restructuring and needed free positions to match a reduced head count. Not one of the 44 department heads offered to give up positions. And this despite some of these posts having been left empty for more than a year. So clearly, they were not needed for what the heads saw as their bigger picture. Even when speaking to each of the 44 in person, their ego prevailed. So, we ended up having to decide from the top.

(Interview No. 120, Head Personnel, 10,000 employees)

It may also mean, stepping on someone’s toes.

I had a let one of my best friends go, because he could not lead as we needed to in our new culture. We are still good friends outside, though.

(Interview No. 144, Director, 20,000 employees)

Or staging an event in such detail, that the perspectives of employees is successfully shifted:

We had just been spun-off. There was a severe danger of this spin-off going under. For the first meeting, we were asked to come to a huge hall. After a while the three board members entered. We all fell quiet and looked at them expectedly. They went to one of the walls and stood quietly with their faces away from us, looking towards the wall. This lasted for a number of minutes, which felt very long. Then they turned round and one said: ‘We can look at wherever we want to – in the end we are only looking at ourselves. Only we together determine the fate of this company – no one else.’ That had a huge impact on us – it felt it was not only the board but us who needed to work out how to get this company of the ground.

(Interview No. 107, CEO Subunit, 12,000 employees)

The World and Beyond

For many there is also something transcendent, something spiritual and something connecting for all of mankind. This may be influenced by religion, by personal experiences, by philosophy.

There were quite a number of interviews, in which managers pointed to this bigger spiritual element and this bigger connectedness which Einstein tried to put into words.

A human being is a spatially and temporally limited piece of the whole, what we call the “Universe”. He experiences himself and his feelings as separate from the rest, an optical illusion of his consciousness. The quest for liberation from this bondage is the only object of true religion. Not nurturing the illusion but only overcoming it gives us the attainable measure of inner peace.20

For numerous, therefore, there was a much bigger purpose to pursue.

The aim must be to elevate human consciousness. This is also the task of the company culture.

(Interview No. 55, Director Global Learning, 34,000 employees)

It is all about becoming human-centric and a sociocracy. Supporting others in their catharsis and their path to enlightenment.

(Interview No. 30, COO and Chief Transformation Officer, 53,000 employees)

Just to make it clear – these statements came from managers in profit-oriented organizations, not NGOs.

This type of perspective on the world supports what Frederic Laloux and other researchers seem to find – that there is a change of consciousness – towards a different way of leading.

One caveat: already the famous management thinker Peter Senge, who coined the term “Learning Organization” seemed to spot this type of elevated consciousness in the 1990s. So it may be that in every age there are and have been humble managers who manage to see a connection and that the number remains more or less stable.

On the other hand, it may equally well be that we are in a Gartner hype cycle. This is a useful tool to look at technological innovation working as follows: You have rising hopes for a new technology. Soon, it seems to be able to solve everything – that is the peak of inflated expectations. Then comes the drop into the trough of disillusionment. Nothing works as promised, nothing happens – let’s give up on the idea. And now, when hardly anyone is looking, there is a steady climb to what is called the plateau of productivity (Figure 8.2). Want an example? Look at voice recognition. Hailed in the 1990s as one of THE greatest things, it initially failed to deliver, was discounted, nearly forgotten and now has quietly and powerfully emerged as Siri, Alexa and dozens of applications.

Figure 8.2 Gartner Hype Cycle adapted for humility.

Possibly all these years of neuroscientists researching, leadership coaches coaching, mindfulness teachers training for the here and now, organizational psychologists bolstering actions with research – possibly we are finally climbing out of the trough of disillusionment towards the plateau of productivity. Which in this case would mean indeed an elevated consciousness of managers, expressed in greater humility.

Once you have determined what your bigger picture is, for your own life, your role and the world, there are numerous tricks to keep it alive.

I know an executive who keeps a miniature skull on his desk. This is a daily reminder that her own lifetime is limited. The result: she devotes herself – as far as possible – only to those projects that make her proud at the end of the year.
Another manager sets himself goals for three years, which he also carries written on a laminated card in his wallet. He typically achieves the goals after 2.5 years, rests for a month and then sets himself new goals.
One manager wanted to contribute to making the world a measurably better place, and moved to a company that is involved in renewable energy.
Another manager has pasted the words of coach Bill Treasurer on his screen. “When you’re a leader your primary responsibility is to leave people and the organization better off than when you found them.”

In addition, there are several questions that you can ask yourself again and again to reduce the risk of forgetting your bigger picture.

If I imagine in this very moment of time that I am 80 years old, what does this eighty-year-old want me to do? What should I focus on from her/his perspective?

This is a kind of ex ante ex post approach which helps you to get up to speed on tasks or changes that you actually know are right but which are hard to imagine at this very moment. In addition, the projected octogenarian has a very good insight into what really matters in life and this may often have more to do with other people, with being part of something, than one is consciously aware of at the moment.

It is the year 2030 and I am dissatisfied with myself – why?

This is a so-called pre-mortem, which is often used in organizations to understand where there are weak points in project or a strategy design and implementation. It can also be applied easily to you as a person. This question can help you see what it is that you actually want deep down and what you should therefore get working on today.

When I leave my job, what would I like my employees and colleagues to say about me afterwards? How I was, what I achieved?

The check if a) I am perceived the way I would like to be and b) if I am even thinking about my effect on others.

What am I thankful for – and what do I have to do to keep that which I am thankful alive?

See all the good things around you. In everyday life we often do not spend much time seeing what there is and tend to notice only when whatever it was has disappeared. If you notice now, you can take action. That is, defend the company culture that appears to be crumbling. Put some weight behind values that are weakening. Or spend more time with your team or your family in order to ensure that both remain strong and connected to you.

Whom do I feel I belong to? Myself, family, friends, my team, the company, customers, stakeholders, my country, the world, humanity, all beings? What should I do for those to whom I feel I belong so that I can live well with myself?

This helps to put yourself in the wider context and to become aware of possible obligations towards yourself and others.

Where do I let myself be carried away towards thoughts and deeds that are only functional and not significant and meaningful?

This is against inner laziness and our tendency to take the easy way out.

Take-Away: From awareness comes the bigger picture – from awareness comes the bigger picture. It is difficult to see in everyday life where the bigger picture lies. It is useful to regularly foster awe in yourself, spend time on identifying your big picture and learning to keep it alive. For this you need to ask yourself numerous questions to ensure that you are still moving into the right direction and have not lapsed into a blind automatism.
