Chapter 9 The Limits and Disadvantages of Humility

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-9

At home I am a nice guy: but I don't want the world to know. Humble people, I've found, don't get very far.

(Muhammad Ali, Boxer 1942–2016)

Summary

There are limits and disadvantages even to humility. Many of these can either be circumvented or are actually par of course. Nevertheless, it is important to know that in some situations the positive effects of humility can be counteracted by oneself, by others or the prevailing culture. Where humility is not genuine, where employees feel too far removed from the manager, where competence is lacking or a different image is important, there it may be difficult to express some elements of humility.

It was a sunny afternoon in May 2020. By then we had all got used to having our meetings online. The interview was with the head of HR, let us call her Clarissa, of a company with more than 300,000 employees. When it came to the question, of whether she had seen limits of humility, she sighed and said:

I have a case, that seems to fit perfectly. A colleague of mine is running a very critical and risky project. He has a strong conceptual background and that has driven him to involve all the relevant stakeholders and groups in his discussions. He has also been very careful to run pilot phases in various business units. At the same time, he has coordinated with the Executive Board and co-opted all stakeholders from the beginning.

If you look at it from the outside, he has done absolutely everything right. Nevertheless, the project is currently only partially successful. It may be that this is the usual valley in the change process. But perhaps it is something else. When we talk - only the two of us – there is quite some emotion in him. A strong annoyance at all of the objections voiced in the process by the people involved comes through. It makes me think, that his humility is actually not quite authentic.

Hearing that story made me realize that next to all the benefits to humility, I should also focus on whether humility has boundaries, downsides or only works under certain conditions. For example, the question arose, if humility has to be genuine or if you cannot simply act it? The above story would indicate that this is not possible, And, are there some people with whom humility works more easily than with others?

This chapter is dedicated to what research so far has found on a dark side of humility. Some in the interviews doubted it, while others saw clear problems in being humble in front of some employees.

After 22 years at C-Level, I can actually see nothing negative in humility.

(Interview No. 110, CEO, 500 employees)

For political reasons one of my employees was nominated to be part of the worker’s council. That makes admitting errors in front of him much harder.

(Interview No. 141, Director, 140,000 employees)

One remark before we dive into the topic: Almost every new field of research first claims vast amounts of positive effects, followed by research that tests the limits. This is a healthy process, yet sometimes leads to absurd results.

Want an example? Let us take the subject of mindfulness, which as we have seen is important for the process of humble perception.

After years of neuroscientists explaining the helpful restructuring of the brain by means of meditation; after years in which psychologists found a calming effect on mind and soul; after years during which business psychologists attributed more creativity and a better working atmosphere to mindfulness it had to happen: the first articles about the dark side of meditation began to appear. Their arguments: meditation can lead to negative experiences, depressions can arise, people can become dangerously dissatisfied with their present lives.

This strikes me like carrying owls to Athens. Any qualified mindfulness teacher spends at least 20 minutes speaking with each participant individually to discuss the dangers involved. Why? Meditation, i.e., learning to be in the present moment, brings up many repressed feelings and issues. Even in the short period of an eight-week-long course, some people realize that they do not want to live the way they are living now. This understanding can lead to depression, but also to job changes or divorces. More often, however, it leads to an adjustment in perception as well as in behavior.

So yes, there are dangers in meditation, but primarily in uncovering existing wounds. A good mindfulness teacher can help staunch these wounds while not missing the moment when there is a demand for the stronger arsenal of a psychotherapist.

Equally, there is research that considers sport to be just as useful as meditation for reducing stress. Point taken! And great that people have options to choose from. On the other hand, the fundamental truth behind mindfulness remains untouched. It remains a simple training of the brain that allows us to perceive non-judgmentally, react non-automatically, while not forgetting our larger goals and values.

The same is true of humility. Other virtues might also be powerful. Great – you have options. And while there is no danger of depressions arising from humility, there are some issues you should know about, which researchers see as critical. And then decide for yourself, as to whether some of these are not actually intended results of humility, whether they belong to a slightly wrong interpretation of humility or whether they are genuine dangers and limits.

Humility Must Be Real and Not Fake

If someone has always lived the opposite of humility, it takes time for the staff to accept it.

(Interview No. 7, VP Training, 22,000 employees)

Humility has to be authentic. It must not be faked.

(Interview No. 132, CEO, 900 employees)

My colleagues pretend to be open – but are not!

(Interview No. 81, CHRO, 13,000 employees)

Having read the story above, it does not come as a surprise that research shows employees to have less confidence in managers whose humility they do not see as genuine.1 If employees believe that the humble behavior of the manager is merely for show or put on to make a good impression, they refuse to award their trust. If employees believe the manager to be mainly into humility for impression management, they will of course see him or her as hypocritical.2 Additional research confirms the result – if employees think that their manager does not really feel on the inside what he or she professes on the outside, humble behavior is of no use.3

This is not surprising: if I feel I am only being praised because it is on the to-do list of the manager and she does not really think and feel that way, why on earth should I trust or value her?

If my manager pretends to be interested in my opinion and yet does exactly what he has been planning to do from the beginning, I will not believe in him.

If she plays bottom-up but decides top-down, I won't stay on board.

If someone claims to learn from me and my experience but continues as always, I will not be motivated.

And finally, if someone pretends to do things for the bigger picture and only ends up serving themselves, why should I be willing to follow?

In all these situations, the maxim of the writer La Rochefoucauld applies: “Humility is often only feigned submission which people use to render others submissive. It is a subterfuge of pride which lowers itself in order to rise.”4

This pretending is closely connected to what we have already seen termed as “humble-bragging.” On LinkedIn this can often be found in sentences such as: “I was humbled to accept my award as manager of the year in front of 10,000 employees.” Or: “Even though it has been going on for 10 years now, I still feel uncomfortable being recognized by everyone on the streets.”

This is fake humility and carries none of the benefits of real humility. Research confirms that people sense it immediately and consequently think far less of the persons having posted this than of others who either merely communicate that they are happy about an award or write about something that hasn't turned out so well.5

Humility is about an inner attitude and it is this attitude that the environment tries to perceive and test as being real.

What does this mean for you? If you have no real interest in cultivating humility, then don't bother. As we have already seen, it is quite a complex process of looking at yourself in a truly objective and loving way, appreciating others, being open for learning and seeing the bigger picture.

Many, however, would say that becoming humble is a worthwhile journey to embark on. Especially in times of rapid change. If I do not assess myself properly, I will not be able to perceive the situation correctly and will not be able to manage my staff and my department or my company well. If I cannot praise others, I will soon have no employees left. If I cannot learn fundamentals afresh, I might as well return to analog photography.

Frankly, I assume that just about every manager – who is not caught up in their narcissism – has an interest in really living humility. In seminars and in my interviews, most managers seem to agree: I have never yet heard anyone say, that this humility stuff is pointless. Most aspire to it; many believe wrongly that they already have it and some are aware that there are large stumbling blocks in everyday life on the path to humility.

If what I am seeing is correct, then in numerous cases the impression employees get that the manager is only putting on an act may actually be a wrong one. Why? It may be more often than not that the manager is not yet able to properly communicate their humility.

This failure in communication may be due to a simple lack of practice. When someone pays a compliment for the first time, it often seems stilted and not real. Does this mean that the compliment is not genuinely intended? No. The compliment simply may have been expressed with insufficient words and gestures.

On the other hand, if employees have this impression of the manager not really being humble, they may after all be right. Why? The manager may still be missing one of the key elements of humility, namely genuine “active listening.”

That this is often the case is indicated by a 2017 study by the Hay Group. Now that you have learned to recognize overconfidence, the results will only bring a weak smile to your face. The board members in the study? They proudly state that they listen actively in 51 percent of cases. The human resources department puts a slight dampener on this optimistic assessment with 41 percent, while the employees appear to be seething. Only 27 percent of them perceive any active listening in their company at all.6 What a meager result!

This pessimistic assessment is unfortunately backed up by further research: Most managers admit that they usually live a 10:1 ratio: speaking 10 times more than asking. Listening is set at a maximum of 20 percent.7

The first level rarely speaks with employees. And sometimes the CEO takes 50 out of 60 minutes to talk. Both behaviors show a huge lack of appreciation.

(Interview No. 33, CTO, 300,000 employees)

In my company, the higher the level, the more Alpha, the harder they find to listen and to limit their own share of speaking. We have a huge gap there. And sadly, no willingness to listen to coaches either.

(Interview No. 126, Head of Corporate Talent Management, 15,000 employees)

This has an immediate effect – if I do not listen, this is a) a failure of seeing what the other may know and be able to contribute and b) the less I hear, the harder I will actually find something to appreciate. Accordingly, in the cited piece of research, only 39 percent of the employees felt that they were appreciated, while the management rated appreciation between managers and employees at a pleasant 76 percent.

Is it not simply enough for managers to undertake to listen more? Where a will, there an ear?

This is where we get back to the topic of status and look at one additional interesting neuroscientific finding. We have already seen that the brain of a high-status person is less focused on others. In addition, it also expends less energy on mirroring other people than the brains of people in a lower position.8

In other words, status entails, purely biologically, that I devote more attention to myself and upwards and less to others around and below me. That may be helpful in times when it is important to focus on content – because then the manager is not distracted by additional social information. In situations where it is exactly about better understanding the situation or my employees, this is of course a hindrance. In other words, as a manager you first have to be aware of this automatic default setting in order to be able to correct it.

Being caught in my power role, I automatically perceive fewer communication signals than when I step out of my role.9 As a rule of thumb, therefore you can say that you should actively give yourself twice as much time as you had planned in order to consciously and actively listen and to pick up social signals.

In other words, your humility may actually seem feigned because your employees do not really feel seen. Make sure that your employees clearly get the signal from you in terms of attention as well as of time “here I sit and I want to listen.” Then, in the long run, humility if already there will be seen as real and not fake.

By the way, if humility is only put on, something negative actually happens for the manager. He or she ends up suffering more from emotional exhaustion.10 This is no wonder when we recall the findings on surface acting and deep acting. Putting on a humble face while feeling no interest in others, in open feedback or in something new, costs a huge amount of mental and cognitive strength.

Take-Away: Fake humility is useless – Play-acting what you do not believe in does not benefit yourself or others. This does not mean that one has to be perfect from moment one and is not allowed to practice getting better at humble behaviors. Yet, if someone does not really want to be humble, he or she should not bother trying.

Humility Allows Employees to Object

As we have seen, humility makes employees believe in their own strengths, feel psychologically safe and gives them the courage to contribute by raising their voices. This may go so far that they end up taking a firm stand against plans that the manager would like to implement.11 The greater the psychological empowerment, the stronger the employees, their opinions and possibly their objections.

Is this a real limit? Or a genuine problem? I suggest not. First of all, a humble leader will be happy to have employees take responsibility and use all their brain power for discussing and rejecting ideas. Secondly, humility does not mean that the authority of the manager dwindles – unless he or she wishes to fully delegate his or her decision-making authority. Instead, a humble leader is equally free to exercise their right to decide, after having collected and listened to input with appreciation.

The key in this process is fairness. Research is crystal-clear: fairness motivates employees even if the outcome is not to their liking.12 This means that the only thing a humble leader must ensure is a fair process of decision-making. What would be unfair? If a manager already had made up their mind and merely pretended to take ideas on board. Or if a manager actually had no options and was still asking for input. In either situation, this would not only be unfair but also unhumble.

If, however, in a process with options, employees are heard and then not all their wishes granted, a humble leader will have no trouble communicating both the process and result clearly and then employ charisma or story-telling to bring all employees on board.

It is, therefore, still advantageous that humble leaders cause their employees to become more involved.

Take-Away: Welcome dissent – Even if a humble leader faces a lot of dissent from the “awakened” employees, he or she will not feel attacked or offended, but will remain firm in his or her role, shaping the process and taking the decision for the best of the company. In an atmosphere of appreciation and willingness to learn, employees will also be able to accept and endorse the final results.

Success of Humility Depends on the Employee and the Situation
Power Distance

There are organizations in which hierarchical structures are very strong. This typically stems from the current management style. There are those managers who love to project their status, attach great importance to being blindly followed while not being in any way prepared for open discussions or criticism.

These will be companies like the following.

The new CEO arrived. His first phone call was to his secretary. “Parking spot Nr. 1 is mine from now on – get that car moved!” Then he let himself be driven to the car dealer, bought an X5 and said: “No need for a bow, I’ll take it right now.”

(Interview No. 112, CHRO, 33,000 employees)

The Group CEO called people losers and clowns. One guy was shouted at so madly by him and took it so badly that he ended up in hospital with a paralyzed face. The CEO made jokes about it in the next meeting.

(Interview No. 67, Head of Talent Management, 49,000 employees)

250 of 800 people were let go. Some of the top team at the same time decided to fly in to the factory by helicopter – demonstrating that there would always be enough money for them.

(Interview No. 20, CEO, 250 employees)

This high-power distance may exist in the department, in the entire company or even in the entire work culture. To this day, purely statistically there is a greater power distance in German, Russian and French companies as compared to Swedish, Dutch or US companies.

Humble managers will try to reduce this power distance for two reasons. Firstly, they are aware of their own fallibility, and secondly, they appreciate the opportunity to work with and through committed employees.

In the long run, humility is contagious – yet in the short run there may be employees who find it harder to communicate openly and who are not actually interested in having an approachable manager, but rather want someone to tell them exactly what to do.

Consequently, research shows that employees with a greater distance of power to the leadership do not have as strong a relationship with their humble manager as those with a lower distance of power.13 This means that a lengthier process is needed for these employees to feel secure with their humble leader and to accept the offer of being open and honest.

For the humble manager, this should not pose a serious problem: Either they accept that not everyone thinks it right how they do things – which is the normal occupational hazard – or they trust that the experience of a lower power distance will be attractive enough in the long run.

Likewise, they could develop an intermediate version and approach these types of employees in a customized fashion. As we have seen above, employees are more accepting of a humble manager acting differently with different employees. That means, there will be acceptance of a manager leading some employees with a more authoritative and directive style.

On the other hand, it is important that the humble manager does not take it personally, when people are not yet ready for a low power distance. Remember the story at beginning of Chapter 6? The CEO who shared his 360° feedback and was disappointed and irritated when others did not? He had thought them at fault, while he had simply not prepared the situation and people well enough.

Look at one other situation:

I was allowed to pick my own people. That was a complete flop. I expected that without hierarchy there would naturally be knowledge sharing and joint learning. Poppycock.

(Interview No. 34, CDO, 35,000 employees)

The manager was trying to instill a low power distance, was not taken up on it and felt cheated. This feeling is understandable, while at the same time lacking in humility. It focuses on the ego of the manager instead of what the employees would need to really feel empowered. It could even be considered naïve to expect a desired culture to emerge by itself. Any form of culture must be instilled, shaped and kept alive by a common understanding and desire. There needs to be regular discussions in the team about what must be done in order to build trust or what is seen as model behavior.

What would a truly humble approach have been for the CDO? Read up on cultures first, see where there are good examples, begin developing ideas for the ideal culture for his team and in all of these steps consciously involve the team.

Seeing this example, it is then not surprising that changing a high-power distance to a low power distance will take time and effort!

There is another interesting research result regarding power: Humble managers tend to be more effective in situations where employees think that they truly have the power to implement their feedback and comments.14 In other words, if the leader has no power at all, humility loses its effectiveness.

Of course, it is possible that a managerial position is not endowed with comprehensive influence. If the manager correctly recognizes this as a weakness, it is up to him or her to try to eliminate this weakness. Whether this is through wise political maneuvers or by applying clever rules of influencing – whatever it is, it is part of the role of a manager to acquire the power he or she needs to carry out those tasks that have been assigned and taken on.

The humble leader must therefore ensure that the relationship with the superiors is strong. Literature calls this the LLX – the leader-leader exchange. In the rare cases that humble managers have not seen this bigger picture and fail to have a strong relationship with their bosses, this has a negative effect on their teams, especially as regards creativity.15 This is no wonder, since as we know, we human animals are great at sniffing out status. Once we realize that the status of our leaders is not high, then our own engagement and motivation to go the extra mile and think creatively suffers.

A humble leader must therefore find and assume the power to achieve what he or she wants. Then it also becomes easier to change a high-power distance culture to one of low-power distance, with all the sharing and open feedback it entails.

Demography

There is some evidence that humility is more effective when there is similarity in age and a match as regards gender.

The explanation for this has two steps and goes as follows. People are more likely to assess behaviors as individual characteristics if they themselves are similar to the person being assessed. If a person is very different from us, our empathy tends to fail and we attribute their behavior primarily to the situation and not to the character. This is again the fundamental attribution error, which we saw earlier. In the case of humble behavior, it means that employees may perceive it as less genuine in a manager when there is a significant age difference as well as if they are of a different gender. People generally open up more to someone who is similar to them – with all the positive consequences for trust and open atmosphere if similar and the negative consequences if dissimilar.

In the case of a humble leader who is demographically similar to us, something called “self-expansion” happens. What does this mean: Humans wish to develop their efficacy in life and therefore seek relationships that have the potential to further them. These relationships have to be desirable and it has to seem probable that such a relationship can be formed.

How is this self-expansion measured? Participants in studies are asked to assess their relationship with a leader by picking one of seven pairs of circles. These range from circles that are fully separate via circles with a small overlap to circles that overlap completely.

What happens with age and gender? In a piece of research with 57 managers and their 256 employees, there was more self-expansion if the humble manager was similar to their employee.16 The employees found it more desirable and probable to build a close relationship with someone of their own age or own gender.

This human leaning towards one’s own also finds some support in my data regarding gender, while not finding its expression in age. If you look at Figure 9.1, you will see firstly that men give a significantly lower score to their female leaders when it comes to the average of all 12 leadership behaviors. Only 28 percent of female leaders of male employees are seen at a 4 or better on all dimensions, versus 38 percent for male leaders. Female employees see no statistically relevant difference between their male and female leaders – both stand around 35 percent.

Figure 9.1 In-group bias by gender.

Figure 9.2 shows a few further elements where employees seem to see more humility within their own gender. Women experience male leaders as significantly less concerned about feedback, as well as less open to suggestions. They see far more praise from their female leaders. Men, in turn, see more praise from male managers.

Figure 9.2 More gender bias.

What to make of this? Firstly, put it into perspective. Yes, there are some differences, but only in a few out of the 12 dimensions as well as for the average. And the average of the male leaders is also yet nothing to be proud of.

Secondly, if you look at where the differences are – they are on those dimensions of leadership behavior that either get personal very quickly (seeking feedback and being open to suggestions) or have something to do with the type of communication (praising). For both, you need to have generated a feeling of trust as well as a common language of communication. It may be therefore that it is less a matter of self-expansion and more one that too few managers invest enough energy to ensure that they include all employees equally. Instead, they may prefer to communicate more within their own gender group.

The consequence? Managers must be aware of the fact that their humility might initially have a stronger impact on some employees in comparison to others.

Is this a real issue for a humble manager? I suggest not. For the following reason: we have seen above that humility does not mean treating everyone the same. It also does not mean that my ego as a manager is at the center of my focus. This means that I will be able to adapt my behavior to the needs of the situation.

Let us look at an example. If, as a young female manager, I have a seasoned IT specialist in my team who is twenty years older, I would and should not expect him to immediately approach me openly, see me as a humble role model or actually be at all that much interested in my appreciation.

The humble manager would arm herself for such a situation. She would acknowledge that she may not be liked or appreciated by everyone yet then focus on the task she has taken on. If that means one employee needing more freedom, another more guidance, yet another a clear reminder of joint goals, then it signifies humility if she attunes her leadership to ensure just that.

Humility is exactly this: not driving on autopilot and acting the same way regardless of the situation, but instead, consciously responding to what there is without the ego leading to actions that only serve oneself.

Humility also remains an inner attitude. What of it you carry to the outside strongly depends on the situation. In order to do your job well, it may be necessary to allow there to be distance or to rebuke someone strongly (without forgetting the merits of the person). It may also be that you have to be hierarchical or very self-confident towards someone in order to get that person on board.

I find the image of a good parent to be useful. Good parents know when to be firmer or softer, depending on what the child needs at this precise moment of time. The trick to getting your own ego out of the way is not to say “What does my daughter or son need now,” but instead: “What does this child need from me in this situation?” This helps silence one's own longings, one's own vanity as well as one's own hopes and disappointments. Instead, it focuses on the needs of the other without completely forgetting one's own. (By the way, one manager summed up parenthood with the following great sentence: “Having children is one of the strongest expressions of ego, raising them the biggest exercise of letting go” (Interview No. 15, Head of L&D Europe, 1,500 employees))

The same applies to the company. The question “What does THIS (not my) employee need now?” helps to change one's perspective, as does the question: “What does THIS company need now?”

That is why in the following example, in my view, the manager has only partially behaved humbly.

A manager had to take responsibility for a very fundamental change process. It had everything in it: customers, structures, role perception and cooperation. There were many situations where her original leadership role was in conflict with her role in the overall change process. She consistently put her own perspective last in favor of advancing the overall objectives.

In general, she was able to take a step back where others would have been offended. She was able to detach herself. This was very beneficial to the process. For her the humble behavior was actually both beneficial and harmful. Beneficial because she was perceived as strong, as someone who cares about the cause. But there were slight doubts as to whether she could really be tough when it comes to her own issues which are also those of her own team. This means that you have to be careful to keep the balance, that you are taken seriously by people who work with other styles and that you meet the expectation of your own employees to fight for their issues as well.

(Interview No. 6, Head HR, 300,000 employees)

The manager here did not really see the full picture. While she was fighting strongly for the sake of the project, she overlooked the needs of her own position and own team. There may be times when a sacrifice may be useful for the greater good, yet here the interview partner suggested that the project leader acted more along a default mechanism than in a consciously chosen manner. It would have been better had she openly gone into discussion or even productive conflict with her team to communicate her appreciation of them and explain the need in this case for putting the company before the team. And at the same time showing where she was fighting for them.

Coming back to the impact of demographics on a leader’s humility. It is worthwhile being aware of the tendency that you will get employees more easily on board if you are similar to them. But firstly, it is only a tendency and not a rigid one or zero situation, and secondly, there are many of us who have learnt to view a person as an individual and less as someone representing an age or a gender. In most cases one thing will still be true: constant dripping of humility wears away a stone, regardless of its shape or age.

Take-Away: With some employees, humility is easier to implement – Some employees will be more likely to relate well to you and open up more easily to humble behavior. For others, this will be more difficult. However, a humble, flexible, open manager with a whole toolbox of instruments can cope easily with this.

Without a Good Top Team, Humility Is Useless

As we have already seen above, humble top management results in a middle management that quits less, works more committedly as well as more effectively.

There is the justified hope of each and every manager of being able to create a good atmosphere in their own area of responsibility – even if these are only small “pockets of sanity.”

I try to develop a pocket of sanity for my employees. The mother company only talks about what is great, leaving out all the problems and never bothering to engage in self-reflection.

(Interview No. 154, Country CEO, 4,000 employees)

This, according to research, has its limits when a top team does not function well. Then, for all their humility, an individual manager further down the hierarchy will have less positive influence than would otherwise be possible.

When does a top team work badly? Research has been trying to measure as well as predict this for decades.17

At the core of this discussion are the so-called fault lines in the top team. Fault lines are present when the top team is split along different categories and as a consequence does not act uniformly. This can happen, for example, if the top team is made up of four men, two of whom are 30-year-old economists and two of whom are 50-year-old engineers. In such a situation, there is a great danger that a common understanding will form only along the educational and age lines and not for the whole team. If the team, however, is made up of two men (30 and 50) and two women (40 and 60) in such a way that one from each gender is an engineer and the other an economist, the risk of malfunctioning is much lower.

Why is this the case? On a cognitive level, we all know that diversity makes sense. Yet it is very human to still love best what we know well. This is why top teams are successful if they are either very homogeneous or so heterogeneous that there is no clear dividing line.

These fault lines in the top team have an impact on the humble manager lower down the line. If the top team radiates strongly into the whole company, the effectiveness of the individual manager is considerably limited. This will be even more the case in smaller companies or where the top management involves itself strongly in the day-to-day running of the company.

You should therefore think through for yourself and your position as to whether there may be effects of top management on your people which you cannot compensate for. If so, your options are probably limited.

One option is that together with your peers you pass the perception of the split up to the top team. Whether that step is possible and will lead to any positive consequences depends very much on the humility of the top team.

The other option may be to insulate your team from the top team where possible. This, however, may contradict that which you see as the bigger picture.

So, you either look at the big picture for the whole company, and perhaps scale down your own and your staff's expectations. Or you could look at your own bigger picture and choose another employer where you can achieve more in your chosen role.

Take-Away: See limits – If the company does not model humility from above, it will be difficult to make up for all the negative effects of a lack of humility. The solution: Create a small “pocket of sanity” - or in the long run also draw personal consequences.

Underestimating Your Own Knowledge

Remember the study with the person, tapping a melody on a table and assuming that the other person easily guesses the tune. This is called the “curse of knowledge” and sometimes has painful consequences:

Experts easily underestimate how much time it takes to acquire expertise. For example, people who are familiar with a device think that a newcomer would need about 18 minutes to solve a task on it. In reality it takes almost twice as long.18
Another piece of research for once exculpates second-hand car salesmen. Since they know their cars well and therefore know all those faults and weaknesses that a normal buyer cannot see, they find it difficult to ignore this knowledge and therefore actually tend to sell at a lesser value than what the buyer would have been willing to pay.19

A similar danger exists with humble managers to the extent that they look at their own knowledge with a slightly more critical and detached eye. In other words, they tend to slightly underestimate their own cognitive ability.20 They also do not regard their knowledge as in any way special. Since they value others for their strengths, they tend to assume that they either have the same knowledge or can acquire it quickly.21 (Thus, I also thought that all my children could study at Cambridge and earn a PhD if they wanted to – now I know that this is probably not the case. And find that – said in a non-judgmental way – just exciting and no problem!)

This means that humble managers are in danger of communicating less clearly and, despite all their humility, of having too high expectations of their employees.

This is a genuine potential trap for the humble leader. But it can be solved. As with communication, it is a matter of consciously putting yourself in the other person's shoes, which works because it is a matter of comprehension. It is also about asking questions.

A humble leader can easily cultivate both of these qualities in himself. That is, before going into a meeting, he or she needs to make themselves aware of what the others might not know. And prepare for this. In the same way that you prepare yourself for outsiders and reduce the use of company argot. Or in the same way that polite acquaintances always mention their children’s names or their own birthdays and never expect others to have to remember either.

Take-Away: Problem recognized, danger averted – A humble leader may expect his own knowledge to be replicable and absorbable more quickly than is actually the case. This may lead to overestimating others. The leader can guard against this by being open and aware of others.

Too Little Exchange of Knowledge

As we know, there are different approaches to leadership – sometimes it may be more about the person, more about the concrete task or more about a process. Since humility leads to employees appreciating and trusting the manager more, this can actually have a detrimental effect in some rare situations.

While psychological safety, as we have seen, drives employees towards voicing their opinions and ideas, research also shows that there may exist situations in which there is less knowledge sharing with a humble manager.22 Specifically, the study at hand examined what effect the humble behavior of a manager has on the exchange of information within the team.

Interestingly, when the manager behaved humbly, employees ended up sharing less information. At the same time, they felt more confident and affectionate towards their leader.

It is probably this feeling of confidence and affection, this feeling of safety, that leads employees to assume that the manager has everything under control. Therefore, they no longer see the necessity for making a big effort to communicate.

In addition, another very human reaction ensues: just as one is reluctant to discuss political differences in a relaxed atmosphere, it may also be the case that conflicts are avoided and fewer opinions are exchanged because employees do not want to damage the good atmosphere.

How can this danger be countered? Especially in virtual situations where social signals such as challenging or confirming nods do not work?

There are a number of possibilities. For example, the manager can point to the larger goal – solving the task for purpose X; spur on a playful competition by deliberately challenging the group; and last but not least, ask clever questions that make the employees get more involved. Example questions would be: “What additional information would you give to someone who is new to the team?” “What information do you have, but do not think is important right now?” Or merely say: “Everyone should share three more pieces of information that only they have.” Some questions are useful in order to garner and create healthy dissent and conflict: “I want Z to be our advocatus diaboli today” or “Three arguments from every one of you, as to why this idea/project etc. may fail.”

Take-Away: Fight coziness – Managers must consciously encourage knowledge exchange as well as conflicts. Conflicts do not have to be fought out aggressively but can be approached in a factual and appreciative manner. Just as the brain becomes more creative in difficult situations, it becomes more alert when challenged. The humble manager must never forget this.

Humility in the Absence of Competence Does Not Work

The question is whether humble behavior works in the absence of competence, or whether humble behavior can actually result in managers being seen as incompetent.

Only truly confident people can be truly humble.

(Interview No. 27, Head of HR, 57,000 employees))

It is my belief that humility is only valuable when you have a reason to be arrogant but you are not.

(Interview No. 29, Group Head, 20,000 employees)

In many interviews, managers say they can only show humility if they are actually competent. Since research shows that humble people learn better and get better grades, the case can be made that most humble people will very quickly become competent at what they do.

So, is there nothing to worry about? Well, research does show that admitting to not knowing something always entails a (small) loss of perceived competence if the knowledge would have been in the executive's area of expertise.23

Would this be a cause for worry to a humble manager? I suggest not. Firstly, their ego will be under control, not suffering if seen as someone with a weakness. Secondly, once he or she realizes that something is missing, it is always possibly learn it. If it cannot be learned or the effort is too great, then employees can be openly empowered to take on more of the role. A number of interviewees pointed out how great it was to be higher up the ladder when you can get employees who are expert at those things you are not!

Thirdly, as we have seen, there is power in asking. If the manager asks questions before admitting to not knowing, this will reduce any impact of not knowing. Moreover, employees are happy to be noticed and praised and are more willing to get involved.24

Nor do you need to constantly point out your own errors and weaknesses – unless it is for the bigger picture. Imagine a process that requires a lot of creativity or decisions – would you like to work on it with someone who constantly focuses on the negative and the past?25 How much better to focus on opportunities for change and improvement.

Take-Away: No humility bonus without competence – Put bluntly, if you are not competent or do not appear to be competent, you will not be able to reap the positive effects of humility. The probability of being incompetent for longer as a humble leader, however, is low. After all, it is not for nothing that they are willing to learn. In addition, they also like to use the team in order to generate competence in the entire team through appreciation and motivation.

Humility Can Be Stressful for Employees

You may know the situation in which you are entrusted with a large project. The manager says she is sure you can handle it and that you will show good results in a fortnight. Just like Steve Jobs said to Weeks: “You can do it!”

What happens? The joy of getting the project is quickly followed by stress. You want to prove yourself worthy, but may not yet have everything under control. After all, under a humble manager, you want to achieve more and show more initiative than under a dictatorial one, who very precisely defines your tasks and their solutions.

Research has recognized this problem: People who are given a lot of credit by humble leaders see themselves as having more work to do, worry about their reputation and suffer more from emotional exhaustion.26

This is not very surprising but is worthwhile reflecting on, since it requires action by the humble leader. He or she must notice the stress and then support with good assistance and helpful regular feedback. Should you stop giving stretching tasks? Not an option. Instead give them repeatedly since once the task – with more initial help from you – has been completed well, this will strengthen the feeling of self-efficacy. The next project will then automatically be less stressful.

At the same time, it is important to remember that trust can be a burden. And some employees will not desire this trust because they are not willing to learn, have no interest in developing themselves further or in seeing the big picture.

Here, the constellation of not humble employees and humble manager may lead to employees having a negative attitude towards the manager and even deliberately acting destructively.27

The manager may have to lead these employees with a stronger hand while allowing them to take on less personal responsibility. If this turns out to be destructive for the team, the humble manager de facto has no choice but to part with those employees who prove to be unwilling to learn.

Take-Away: Free trust from burden – It is the task of the humble manager to ensure that employees are not burdened by the trust extended to them. If the manager keeps this in mind, he or she can strengthen self-efficacy through support as well as appreciation in such a way that an assignment of tasks does not degenerate into negative stress.

A Different Image

Someone who emphasizes the “WE” and talks about their own errors naturally creates a different image as compared to someone who presents themselves as the key agent who is never at fault. We have already seen that investors initially lower their expectations for humble CEO. The results are then “surprisingly” always better than expected, which in turn leads to a better performance on the stock markets.

This type of analysis is also carried out by employees. Someone who leads with humility is perceived as someone who is more capable on the relationship level. While employees like this leader more, trust him/her more, feel more secure and empowered, they also perceive him or her as less agentic and less decisive.28

Why? Well, when I put others in the limelight, when I enable others to work independently, when I always speak of the “we,” then I am not only perceived as less instrumental, but to be honest I really am.

Therein lies the solution: As a humble manager, you should be able to live with this image. Right? Since you would never want to be seen as the sole agent.

Will this lesser agency immediately be accepted by all? Well, no. Sometimes you may have to fight for this.

I was told in one interview for a top position, that I was framing achievements too much as being the result of teamwork. They wanted to hear more “I have done this” and “this is my contribution”. When I gave more details about my leadership style and was brutally honest that I would never claim to be doing things that I can only do through others, it was okay and I got the job.

(Interview No. 123, Group CEO, 3,500 employees)

So yes, a humble leader is perceived as less agentic because he or she has indeed empowered others to become more active. Yet, this typically fits in wonderfully with the manager’s self-image and is therefore harmless.

Take-Away: The We before the I – Humble leaders are perceived as less agentic, because they have indeed empowered others to become more active. This fits the self-image of the leader and should therefore not be a problem.

If we now look at all the limits and downsides that research has found for humility, I personally remain undaunted. There seems not to be a single study that indicates that humility, if lived correctly, could ever be in any way seriously damaging.

At this point, I would like to once again share the results from my research in Figure 9.3. If you are ever worried about the downsides of humility – look at this slide and see how much better humble leaders are perceived for competence, intelligence and strengths. The graph shows how many of those managers whose employees identified as humble received a 4 or better out of 5 on all the dimensions. As you can see for example, a whopping 90 percent say that their humble leaders should be successful in the company, compared to only 68 percent for the unhumble manager.

Figure 9.3 Humble managers are not seen as weak.

The conclusion is clear: despite any hiccups humility will contribute to your success!

Chapter 10 Different Nationalities, Ages and the Topic of Gender Revisited

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-10

Reason respects the differences, and imagination the similitudes of things

Percy Bysshe Shelley (1792–1822)

Summary

While humility as a concept works all across the world and results in the same positive effects, there is some difference as to how strongly it is being lived in different countries. In addition, the manager who wishes to express humility must adapt to the different needs of different cultures in order to be effective. While there are some small differences in how managers are seen across gender and age, in general, there is no large statistical difference.

Once I had the luxury of having four one-week-long modules with Russian managers from an energy conglomerate that had been bought by a German company. Most of the participants were males, many from Siberia and their hobbies were – simply put – hunting and fishing and fishing and hunting. One participant, Maxim, a large man lovingly described how he relished his weekly fishing expedition: once he and his friends had caught some fish, one person would scale, gut and cut the fish into small pieces (Maxim’s huge hands came into theatrical play here). At the same time, someone else would pour the vodka. In installments of hundred grams, of course. And then – he smiled widely – “You would bite into your raw fish, down the vodka and that was heaven!” Hearing this I was sure that when we were together in Düsseldorf, I knew just the right place for them. So, we went to the Uerige, a famous old restaurant in the center of town with local beer and the beloved specialty of steak tartar. Raw meat for tough Russians – that surely would be another heaven! What happened? No smile from Maxim, no smile from Sergej, Oleg or Artem. None of them touched the tartar. Why? Not knowing Düsseldorf, the way restaurants worked in Germany, the legal requirements for hygiene, they had no trust in how the meat had been procured. They had no trust in how the meat was cooled and served. They had no trust – full stop. As a consequence, they stuck to all the other food and I returned to Berlin, delighted to have three kilograms of the finest meat in my luggage!

That brought home poignantly that habits, traditions and behaviors do not translate easily from one country to the other. Too different are the experiences, the assumptions and the traditions. Yet at the same time, anyone who has read books like War and Peace, Les Misérables or the Buddenbrocks, has seen films like Slumdog Millionaire, The Red Lantern or The Leopard or has made friends in Singapore, Kenia or Denmark knows that there is ever more that connects us as humans than separates us.

So where does humility stand? Is it like steak tartar to be eaten in some countries and not in others or is it a common fiber that comes in many colors and textures but which connects us all not only across nations but also across gender and ages?

Nationalities

In nearly every seminar, the question is raised whether there are national differences when it comes to the understanding and practice of humility.

Let us start by understanding how the definition of humility is received around the world. The good news? There is no difference! Regardless of whom I interviewed, managers from Asia, Africa, the Americas and Europe, they all understood and approved the definition of humility as developed by research.

This makes sense, when you see that it was also academics from all over the world that had a part in generating the definition as well as measuring the effects of humility. The researchers come from Australia, China, France, Germany, India, Italy, the Netherlands, Pakistan, Portugal, Spain, UK, the US to name but a few. And in all these countries, the effects of humility as we saw them in this book were observed and measured.

So why are we instinctively looking for differences? Because many feel that there simply should be a difference between the Asian countries and the West, or between the US and say Germany or Italy.

At the same time, we also know that there are core values and virtues that are universal. Research confirms that you can count humility amongst these – once you focus on the “right” elements of the concept. What are these?

As one researcher pointed out:

In cultures that hold independence and self-esteem in high regard, dissent about the value of humility comes to a great extent from a misunderstanding of its meaning. In fact, individuals in ANY cultural context will tend to reject humility if it is associated with self-denial and abasement. On the other hand, when humility is defined as a down-to-earth perspective of oneself in relation to all other beings and accurate and realistic self-knowledge, it not only becomes desirable, but also necessary for survival and success regardless of tradition or culture.1

While the concept of humility fares well worldwide, the same is not necessarily true of the word used to describe it. Let us look how much the word used for humility actually conveys that it is a virtue of strength and of a down-to-earth perspective. Or does the word nudge us into thinking about weakness and therefore making the average manager unwilling to be associated with it?

Despite its positive roots in “humus,” i.e., “earth,” for many the word “humility” is historically charged and way too close to abasement and humiliation. This holds true not only for English but also for French (humilité), Italian (umiltà) or Spanish (humildad). Despite this, some interviewees really liked the word and emphasized that to their mind it was closely and beautifully connected to humanity.

Outside the Latin sphere of influence some languages have an even greater struggle. The Dutch call humility “Nederigheid,” which is close to lowliness, the Finns use Nöyryys which to them has associations of prostrating yourself before kings and queens. The Slavic languages go more for modesty (Скромность/skromnost – Russian), submitting oneself to the will of God (Смирение/smirenie – Russian again) or putting yourself below someone (pokora – Polish). As we have seen, the word “Demut” causes some trouble in Germany since the courage element (“Mut”) is often overshadowed by the serving bit (“Dienen”). In Europe, only the Norwegians seem to be quite happy with their “ydmykhet”. (“It includes for me not only weaknesses but also strengths!”, Interview No. 89, Head of organizational development, 10,000 employees) even though the word actually comes from the old Danish containing the elements of “easy” and “soft.”

Some countries on the other hand are in peace with their words. In Chinese, either “qian xun” (谦逊) or “qian bei” (谦卑) is used. I love the way Chinese words are made up – the first part of “qian” is the basis, the so-called radical for “word” – which indicates that “qian” has something to do with speaking. The second element of the first picture contains a hand grasping a sheaf of wheat 兼 giving hints to the pronunciation as being “qian”. The second picture is either 逊 (xun) which is someone walking with their grandchild – giving it the feel of there being something greater – the sheaf of wheat as well as the grandchild. Or the second element is 卑 (bei) which is either seen as a servant holding a fan or someone having a high rank in the Imperial examinations. This nudges us again towards being part of a bigger picture – one possibly weak (the servant), the other quite strong (part of the empire of the son of heaven). Possibly as a consequence of this focus on the bigger picture, none of my Chinese interview partners struggled with the word. And loved the concept!

In Turkish the word is “alçakgönüllülük”, consisting of “alçak” and “gönül”. “Alçak” means low – also as in a plane flying low, “gönul” means soul or character. Together, for the Turks I talked to, they make a positive mix and lead to sayings such as “Those who want to rise must be humble, there is no ladder but humility to rise to the roof of greatness” (Sadi Şirazi).

In India, three interviewees pointed me to a Sanskrit saying “Vidya vinayen shobhate,” which means “humility adorns knowledge.” The connection between competence and humility was seen as particularly strong – each strengthening the other. The more you know, the humbler you become. And the humbler you are, the more you learn.

In Hindi the word for humility is “िवनम्रता vinamrata”, which has to do with bending down. Interestingly, despite a potentially difficult root, all Indians I spoke to were very positive about the word and the concept behind it.

Which shows that any semantics can be topped by the way a concept is being lived and talked about.

This to me is the key lesson regarding the word used for “humility” – it is important to be aware of its connotations in every language and then it may actually be a good idea initially not to use it. Why? In many conversations with HR managers or CEOs, I have discovered that I lose them if the word “humility” is brought up too early. How can something as weak-sounding as that have something to do with the bottom line (which we know it does); how can something as modest inspire employees (which we know it does) and how can something as gentle be a strength (which we know it is) and not a weakness?

The solution for the time being? I talk about “modern and mature leadership” or “timely feedback” or “becoming accountable” and then I use the topic at hand as a Trojan horse. Once – to continue the metaphor – the humble soldiers come out of their wooden horse, they master, convince, lure, do whatever is required and quickly the concept is taken on board. During this process, the word is infused with more positive connotations, associations and stories.

Is this not a somewhat feeble and defeatist approach? In my mind the opposite: it is strong and humble to realize that some people may need an easier route to humility than by ramming the word down their throats! The worry should not be about what letters are used to describe the concept but instead about whether the concept can be strengthened and lived worldwide.

So having bought that the concept of humility engenders positive results all over the world and that the word used for it does not represent a hindrance, the next question is if there is a difference in how humility is being lived.

For this, I conducted a piece of research in 10 countries with 100 employees in each. More than half of these employees were also in a leadership position themselves. The countries I analyzed were Britain, China, France, Germany, India, Italy, the Netherlands, Singapore, Switzerland and the US.

The results show at a first step that indeed there is a statistically relevant difference between the different countries!

Remember how to calculate the average score of humility? We look at the 12 leadership behaviors and take an average across these. The resulting range is quite large from 3.89 in India to 3.51 in Germany down to 3.22 in China. This span even gets bigger when (Figure 10.1) you look at the percentage of managers that are seen as humble across all 12 elements. These scores range from a mere 16 percent in China to a still uninspiring 53 percent in India.

Figure 10.1 Average score and percentage of humility per country.

The scores get somewhat better, if we look at what employees say once they have read the definition of humility and are then asked how humble their direct manager is according to this definition. Then, as you can see in Figure 10.2, we have far more positive scores between 48 and 80 percent.

Figure 10.2 Percentage of humble managers according to definition.

I personally find the results across the 12 leadership elements more indicative. Why? Because this way we measure concrete behaviors and not simply wishful thinking or a warmish feeling one may have towards one’s manager.

Before I look in some detail at each country, you may ask yourself if some of these positive or negative measurements do not also depend on how generous the culture of scoring is in each country. Anyone who has ever taught Germans and Americans in the same class knows that the written scores for any faculty differ wildly, while the communicated satisfaction with the program is typically the same.

It is therefore not surprising that you can see a similar difference across the countries when it comes to participants of the survey awarding themselves scores for their personal humility. The British and the Americans are exhilarated at around 90 percent, while many mainland Europeans go down to the 70s and China stands with the lowest self-score at 64 percent. (Figure 10.3).

Figure 10.3 Percentage of participants that see themselves as humble.

In general, employees (and please remember that more than 50 percent of these also are managers) see themselves on average across all countries as 46 percent humbler than their direct leaders. In Figure 10.4, you can observe this hybris.

Figure 10.4 Gap between own and manager’s humility.

This means that indeed, we should be careful and not overinterpret the results across the countries but mainly take them as a starting point for a discussion about each country as a stand-alone.

This becomes yet more obvious when you look deeper into the data. Figure 10.5 shows what I mean. If you formulate an average for each country and see which strengths are above and below this average, you see something similar all across the globe. Every single country assesses its own managers as being on average weaker in seeing the bigger picture. Apart from China, all countries also seem to struggle with seeing and showing their strengths and weaknesses.

Figure 10.5 Elements of humility and country average.

While there are these similarities on the higher level, we will also encounter some differences, when we look at each country. And if we take this not as something set in stone but as a beginning for a conversation, this analysis will be valuable. What sort of conversation am I looking for? A conversation about what is possible in each country, where some hurdles may lie and how best to deal with them.

Experience shows that in any country, in any company there is much potential once you put your mind to it. CEO Wilf Blackburn expresses this succinctly.

I think, in any country, you can build a company culture that is different from the local one. The recipe: Take the best from the country culture and add what you need to make the best company culture.

Finally, I also believe that any cross-cultural research must avoid resulting in labels or drawers that belie the multi-faceted world we live in. There are narcissistic Indian leaders just as there are humble Chinese or German ones. And if you look at the result of another piece of research, it becomes clear that even “officially” humble countries still have their own battles to fight.

I asked the participants of my study the somewhat polemic question: “How high do you think the percentage of narcissistic managers is in your company as compared to humble or other managers?” And look at the result in Figure 10.6. Even in “humbler” countries such as India and Switzerland, we still get a frightfully high 26 percent of narcissistic managers.

Figure 10.6 Average percentage of narcissistic managers in company.

So, bearing all this in mind, are you ready for a quick dive into the ten countries? And then for a look at how you can best express humble behaviors across the world?

Britain

Britain (Figure 10.7) together with the US, India and Switzerland ranks most highly amongst the ten countries analyzed. In particular, the understanding that not all is under control seems to allow British managers to be empowering with their employees and untroubled by mistakes or sudden changes.

Figure 10.7 Britain.

This may have something to do with the stiff upper lip that used to be much cultivated. While research shows that today there may be some lessening of that stoicism with emotions becoming more acceptable and prevalent,2 this development does not seem turned British managers into control-freaks.

One of the underlying reasons for Britain doing well on many of the scores may come from the fact that many managers still start working at the very young age of 20/21 directly after finishing university. The assumption is that anyone entering the workplace so early on in life knows that he or she still has much to learn. Which is one of the prerequisites of humility.

In addition, Britain seems to have a leadership culture that appears more people-oriented than in many other parts of Europe. Coupled with the willingness to attend leadership seminars, this results in high scores when it comes to learning, seeing the bigger picture as well as appreciating others. While this appreciation typically is not done with extensive emotional exuberance, it seems to work nonetheless. The CEO I cited who makes tea for his new employees comes from Britain. So does Wilf Blackburn, who as we have seen leads both in a clear and appreciative manner.

Yet, still there are areas for improvement – only 40 percent of managers are seen as trying to make the world a better place and only 44 percent seek feedback, which is the second worst score amongst all the countries looked at.

In addition, we have a whooping score of 92 percent of participants who believe themselves to be humble. That seems to point to a serious issue of self-awareness, which should surely be addressed in each and every company striving for humility. How genuine is what we seem to see? And how much is it simply wishful thinking?

China

China (Figure 10.8) received or rather gave itself the lowest scores from all the ten countries analyzed. Initially, this came as a surprise. Be honest – did we not all expect Asian countries to show more humility than Western ones? Yet if you look more closely, the conundrum is resolved: The great respect that Confucianism as well as the Communist Party demands for elders and people of higher rank has as a consequence a very traditional leadership style. One that is based on authority, paternalism and unquestioning obedience.

Figure 10.8 China.

Humble leadership behaviors on the other hand require a sense of equality and a willingness to deal with the other human being as an individual person. And not as a cog in the wheel, not as someone to merely fill a role. In addition, humility needs the inclination to be truly seen by others in all one’s own weaknesses and strengths. How likely am I to have that inclination when I am placed miles above others? And someone else is placed miles above me?

Many employees therefore report a lack of this necessary sense of equality. Remember Huawei’s boss Ren Zhengfei who suggested that an employee rather divorce than leave his job. That was far from being a humane, appreciative, humble statement.

On the other hand, despite the sad scores, there are also numerous Chinese companies and leaders that showcase humble behaviors. Just hear what the reclusive founder of one of China’s largest companies has to say about the value of individual employees.

I would rather give up a business with a profit of one million, than give up a person who brings the right talent to our enterprise.”

He Xiangjian, founder of Midea Group (150,000 employees)

And if you look at the white-goods company Haier with its 100,000 employees, you will find an organization where CEO Zhang Ruimin has consistently tried to empower employees to act more freely and independently. Already in 2005 Haier made the bold move towards a management concept called Rendanheyi. This entailed getting rid of a whole layer of middle management and shifting the decision-making powers right down to the smallest team units. Add to this a strong culture of job rotation for any of the managers remaining and you get a Chinese company that in my assessment ticks many more boxes for humility than others worldwide.

In addition, younger Chinese leaders who have worked abroad tend to bring back a more open leadership style. This fits well with the research, which shows that in all Asian countries there is a growing interest in participative leadership.3 Therefore, I expect that humble behavior will be more desired as well as become more feasible over the next years.

And finally, we must take these bad scores with a pinch of salt. Yes, only 16 percent of managers are assessed as humble across all 12 leadership behaviors, yes, at best 50 percent of managers engage in each single behavior – yet, the Chinese employees also only saw their own humility at 64 percent. So, we may actually have the case of at least some humble employees giving overmodest scores not only to themselves but also to their managers.

France

France (Figure 10.9) scored itself quite low on many of the different leadership facets. While 78 percent of employees saw themselves as overall humble, they saw their managers only closing in on that acceptable score when it comes to noticing the strengths of others. Shifting from noticing to complimenting is already seriously lacking with a mere 57 percent.

Figure 10.9 France.

Together with Italian managers, French bosses are given the lowest scores within Europe when it comes to the whole segment of being open and willing to learn.

This may have to do with the prevalence of an autocratic style of leadership, with managers finding it difficult to take on ideas and suggestions from others along the lines of: “If I am the boss and need to show to everyone that I am the boss, then surely I cannot publicly be seen to need to learn anything.” The fear is that openness may be seen to harm the power of the position.

Interview partners confirmed this. They saw many managers as fearful of being seen as weak. Instead, their focus was on how to exert individual power. In addition, a tendency towards power games reduces the space for humility.

People are always in competition mode and fighting to keep their power. That makes them unwilling to cooperate and learn.

(Interview No. 55, Director Global Learning, 34,000 employees)

On the other hand, executives from some smaller companies shared how over the years they had managed to shape leadership behavior towards the humble by giving regular feedback. Not only once. But again. And again. And again. And over the years the desired behaviors have been fostered.

We have invested hugely in helping people gain better self-awareness. We measure perceived behaviors every half a year, provide coaching and just keep at it. After the third or fourth feedback people slowly start to change.

(Interview No. 160, SVP Human Resources, 15,000 employees)

Nevertheless, it will take time for the hierarchical leadership to disappear. In particular, since it is still strongly modeled in the political world, where many perceived how President Macron lead during Covid times as far more autocratical than in most other European countries.

Germany

Germany (Figure 10.10) has the worst scores worldwide when it comes to seeking critical feedback! In other areas, scores are middling to good, until we get to the bigger picture, which also sports some of the lowest scores.

Figure 10.10 Germany.

How to explain this? I suggest two routes for interpretation: Firstly, Germans start work quite late in their lives. Many managers are of the generation that used to study beyond their mid- or end-twenties. The consequence: if you are already age 27 when you begin working, there is a huge danger that you will think of yourself as more or less finished. Not perfect, but having learned most there is to be learned. Why then should you benefit from feedback? Or share your weaknesses with others?

This also matches the impressions I have gained from seminars that I have run over the years. US and English managers from large multinationals were typically far more willing to talk about their own weaknesses than their German counterparts.

There is a second element to look at. In research, there exists a concept called humane orientation. This is a value at work that encompasses caring, kindness and a focus on employees as humans. Assessed by the Globe Study, an international long-term research project, Germans actually score lowest on this element out of all the 62 societies studied.4 This has little to do with Germans not being able to be kind or caring, but more with them seeing professional and private life as two very different spheres. There is no tradition of Friday drinks as in the UK or the concept of being vulnerable to your staff as in the US. This may explain why Germans seem to be less willing to bring their whole personality to work and share their weaknesses.

Regarding appreciation there is a growing upside: over the past decades a culture of appreciation and valuing others has slowly made its way into many German companies. The result being that within Europe Germany scores quite highly on this. While a few managers still follow the adage “Not being scolded is praise enough” (“Nicht geschimpft, ist Lob genug”), many others have realized that appreciation is neither a sign of weakness nor some overemotional verbal hugging but an essential part of effective communication.

Having said this, people who have worked abroad still tend to see better leadership culture elsewhere.

When I worked in the US, I was impressed by a much humbler management culture. There was a willingness to listen and really work on things together.

(Interview, No. 153, German CEO, 1,800 employees)

So, it will be interesting to observe whether the younger generations of German managers will be able to let go of some of the negative behaviors described in the following quote.

In Germany people play boss – when they do not know something they simply become assertive. This is very different in Sweden or Holland – there the manager realizes that it is far healthier to work together on something.

(Interview No. 148, Head Customer Innovation, 4,000 employees)

One last point. Why do Germans get amongst the lowest score on the bigger picture – especially when it comes to trying to make the world a better place?

Partly this is an issue of wording: Germans do not like what they see as exaggerated exuberance. Most Germans would cringe at the thought of even daring to think that they could make the world “a better place.” This is the kind explanation. The ruder and – to my mind – more truthful one is that Germans tend to not go for discussions about purpose or their role in the bigger picture out of fear and for historical reasons. “As long as we don’t start another war, we are doing fine – thinking about bettering the world is too big and dangerous for us” is the motto. For other nations, this is hard to understand, in particular since Germany sports considerable resources which it could choose to put at the service of the greater good. Including the brains and hearts of its managers.

India

India (Figure 10.11) has the highest scores worldwide on numerous dimensions: actively seeking feedback, complimenting others and being open to ideas. The participants also see only 26 percent of managers as narcissistic – a result that otherwise only the Swiss achieve.

Figure 10.11 India.

Every Indian interviewee emphasized how humility was a core value both for society as a whole as well as for companies. One manager told me how as a child he used to read comics about Hinduism and how they were all about the need to understand yourself. And to “know that you can become better every day if you try.” A very humble mindset!

Not surprisingly, there is also popular awareness of business leaders not only professing to be humble but actually behaving thus.

One anecdote from personal experience. During a large conference in Berlin in 2010, the founder and CEO of Infosys Narayana Murthy related how during the financial crisis the whole management took a pay cut in order to be able to keep the lower-level employees in full pay. He was deeply appalled at other companies worldwide that had resorted instead to downsizing while preserving their own management jobs.

More material comes from two widely shared social media posts that show Indian business billionaire, Sir Ratan Tata, visiting an ailing ex-employee and former Indian president A. P. J. Abdul Kalam giving a humble speech at the European parliament.

While I have seen humble behavior in many managers, I have never ever been forwarded from any other culture a link celebrating that humility. It could be that it is this pervasiveness of the concept of humility that makes it easier for managers to behave humbly.

Most interviewees agree.

The focus in India is not on you as an individual.

(Interview No. 25, VP and Head of Global Service and Customer Care, 31,000 employees)

Humility is part of Indian culture.

(Interview No. 29, Group Head Customer Experience and CRS, 27,000 employees)

Not wanting to sound too rosy though, there are many who see the leadership culture as still too hierarchical as to be able to truly reap all the benefits of humility.

We should also remember that the average score in the country was still below a 4 out of 5 – namely a 3.89. And that there may also be some overconfidence in place if 85 percent of participants see themselves as humble. Possibly they are also overconfident about their managers and tend to overlook what may happen to some of them.

I have seen people lose their way the higher up they get. They no longer get enough feedback to be able to remain humble.

(Interview No. 158, Indian Head – L&OD and Corporate HR, 70,000 employees

But still not all Indians are humble, if they can afford to exhibit arrogance. This happens most easily on the way up in a company.

(Interview No. 29, Indian Group Head Customer Experience and CRS, 27,000 employees)

However good scores remain good scores and humility is seen as a core value for managers. Which is more than can be said of many other countries.

Italy

You saw above that 46 percent of managers in Italian companies are assessed as narcissistic. As a result, it does not come as a surprise that Italian employees see a deficit of managers that appreciate and are willing to learn. Indeed, within Europe, Italy fares worst on those two dimensions (Figure 10.12).

Figure 10.12 Italy.

Some interview partners try to give a reason for this.

Our top leaders find it very hard to say that there is a problem. And ideally, they solve it on their own.

(Interview No. 139, Head Digital Learning, 100,000 employees)

There is too little appreciation – possibly because you only appreciate those whom you like.

(Interview No. 83, CMO, 6,500 employees)

At the same time, there still exists a large power distance between the different levels.5 And what manager within such a culture will be willing to admit mistakes, ask for feedback and “lower” him- or herself to appreciate others?

While there is a strong paternalistic element in the leadership culture – that inhibits creativity and engagement – much is also changing in Italy. The duty to innovate has pushed numerous companies further along the path of humility.

The CIO whom I have cited and who calls himself “Chief Ignorance Officer” comes from Italy. He is convinced that humility is the way forward. Together with his CEO they are attempting to foster at least elements of this virtue.
