When I first came, the company felt more or less invincible. But once they saw that there were failures everywhere around, they began to become humbler. We then implemented “my best fail-program”. I shared my mistakes; the CEO shared his mistakes. That was needed for others to open up.

(Interview No. 26, CIO, 68,000 employees)

So, while Italy may be quite low in its own assessment, times may be changing more quickly than expected. Who would have thought that a nation that loved Berlusconi and Matteo Salvini could (if only briefly) unite behind an Italian Prime Minister Mario Draghi, a man as far removed from a narcissistic leader as is possible.

The Netherlands

The Netherlands (Figure 10.13) is a very egalitarian society with a low power distance. I remember one seminar in which I had three layers of management from a Dutch company. Observing their relationships with each other, I could not have guessed who was higher up the corporate ladder. This equal footing is a prerequisite for nearly all of the humble leadership behaviors, so that the managers I interviewed had no problem relating to the concept of humility within their work experience.

Figure 10.13 The Netherlands.

On the other hand, the Dutch also pride themselves as being very open and direct with little need for a culture of praise or acknowledging others. “Being professional surely is good enough.” They do not mince their words; they voice their criticism loudly and clearly.

In particular, this last point may explain why the Netherlands do not score very highly on any of the dimensions of humility. There is only a single one of the 12 behaviors where they score amongst the top 3. This is “seeing oneself as a small part of a larger whole.” Here only the more self-certain British and Americans score more highly. Possibly being so close to an ever-rising sea, together with a strong protestant legacy of duty, has instilled a sense of being not too important after all.

To some, this subjugation to the bigger picture does not only represent an upside.

We are a warm and friendly culture so we are not good at discussing the inconvenient. As a result, many of our problems stay under the water line.

(Interview No. 138, CEO, 1,200 employees)

So there seems to be room for looking at the bigger picture not as a friendly culture but also at times as one of excellence, which needs conflicts to be delighted in and not shirked.

By the way, this very same CEO quoted above is in a shared leadership role with a female CEO. That demands considerable humility from all the involved! So, we seem to have a mixed bag in the Netherlands: A large amount of openness, the right culture for humility to thrive and yet it has not taken off fully.

Singapore

Even though by self-assessment the Singaporeans see themselves as quite humble at 77 percent, they do not extend this assessment to their managers. On two fronts Singapore leaders garnered the lowest scores worldwide: acknowledging when the manager does not know how to do something and being willing to learn from others (Figure 10.14).

Figure 10.14 Singapore.

If you compare it to China – which also has numerous low scores in the leadership behaviors – there is a far higher rate of narcissists in Singapore. Employees in China seem to think of their managers more as mindless than narcissistic, counting only 28 percent as being the latter (remember Figure 10.6). Singapore managers, however, are assessed with the second highest narcissism score (after Italy) with 41 percent.

How to explain this? A very strong performance orientation might have something to do with it. According to data from the Globe Study, Singapore has the second highest score on this dimension of all societies studied. This focus on individual performance may result in managers focusing less on the individual employee and more on the desired outcome – for themselves. In consequence, an employee is more a cog in a wheel that simply has to function and not someone to enter into a personal relationship with.

Another explanation could be that many of the Singaporean managers have worked abroad and have adopted behaviors that are seen as non-compatible with humility, as in this example.

In general, I see more humility in Asian countries. But some of my countrymen would no longer see me as humble, since in my years abroad I have learned assertiveness.

(Interview No. 152, Singaporean CFO who worked at many MNCs, 2,200 employees)

If there is a shift within society towards other behaviors that did not use to be socially acceptable, then it may be that managers receive lower scores or are seen as more narcissistic even though their behavior – in this case assertiveness – may actually represent a humble behavior. If assertiveness is not used egocentrically but for the role that one has taken on,then it is actually an essential part of humility.

This reminds of when humility is perceived as fake or play-acted, while it is simply the case that managers need time to practice how to verbalize appreciation or talk about their weaknesses. In Singapore it may be that the concept of humility still needs to expand in order to encompass more assertive behaviors.

There is however also one other argument to be found for the generally low scores. As in China, there exists a high reverence for age, experience and rank, making humble behaviors that need more of an equal footing harder to role model or emulate.

But remember, managers like Wilf Blackburn have been successful in Singapore which gets us once again to the realization: it is “simply” a matter of intent and grit that gets cultures shifted.

Switzerland

The Swiss garner the best scores on five dimensions: Acknowledging others, taking notice of the strength of others, showing appreciation, being willing to learn from others and being open to the advice of others (Figure 10.15). They also only see 26 percent of managers in their company as being narcissists.

Figure 10.15 Switzerland.

For Swiss interviewees, this positive result does not come as a surprise. The CEO who told me that he was chosen for his humility was Swiss; the CEO who asserted “When you have a good culture, numbers will be good” was Swiss. He was the same CEO who told the story of how his Swiss supervisory board members before interviewing for the job gave him the Jim Collins’ book “From good to great” to read in order to convey their expectations for a humble CEO.

Many Germans who work in Switzerland also mentioned to me how much less prevalent the ego is.

While other nations are proud of their (sometimes tempered) narcissists such as Steve Jobs or Richard Branson or Andrea Agnelli or Jürgen Schrempp, the Swiss are proud of Adele and Gottlieb Duttweiler. These were the founders of the cooperative Migros, a grocery chain that saw it as its duty to make life as easy as possible for the consumer. Both are well-known not only for having been full of ideas but also for an intense sense of serving others.

This cultural precedent may help in understanding the relatively high scores. Yet, there are also some foreign managers who have had different experiences.

I sometimes feel that the Swiss are merely toying with the idea of being humble and not being interested in ego or competition. After a while I realized that there is a lot of comparing and measuring going on after all.

(Interview No. 95, German HR Director, 31,000 employees)

To add to this, many see the former CEO of Deutsche Bank, the Swiss banker Josef Ackermann as anything but humble.

Yet, as we have seen repeatedly – research is the game of large numbers. On average the Swiss fare really well in the assessment of the employees, and while there is always room for improvement it would be churlish to continue looking for the fly in the ointment!

US

The US scores highest on three dimensions (admitting when not knowing, making the world a better place, seeing themselves as a small part of a larger whole) (Figure 10.16). They are also amongst the top three for another five dimensions. These are great results. Which must be partly put into perspective, when you see that 88 percent of participants (62 percent of whom were also managers themselves) assessed themselves as humble.

Figure 10.16 US.

So, there may be some bias in the US that drives people towards the positive side of things and results in overscoring both for the self and others. I remember the US part of a management consultancy being flabbergasted by what they saw as very negative feedback from the German employees. While the Germans saw their comments and scores as a loving support for one of the best companies they knew. They saw the US grades and comments as (mindless) overpraise not at all helpful for anyone anywhere.

On the other hand, the US management culture appears to allow managers to show some of their weaknesses and failures without losing respect. In some cases, it may even be regarded as strange for a manager not to have errors or weaknesses to talk about.

On top of that, there is a greater sense in society that you can learn whatever you need to learn, whenever you want to. When business leaders like Eric Schmitt from Google openly speak about having a coach, this demonstrates to others that leadership can be learned and that you need support from others for this.

While amongst fifteen-year-olds, the US only scores number 10 worldwide on the growth mindset that we discussed above (behind Germany and the UK, yet ahead of the other seven countries analyzed)6; I am very sure that this data would be different if you asked older people. The advantage the US used to have as regards believing in possibilities and personal growth may be eroding, yet amongst the managers there still seems to be a great can-do atmosphere. Which is a vital prerequisite for learning.

In addition, the culture of appreciation is strong. This culture starts early on: one US mother told me how their primary school teacher asked the children in the class to say what they thought great about Lucy. Just a few weeks later, a German mother told me that her teacher had said: “Now please each of say what you don’t like about Noah.”

Add to this, a general discussion about purpose and the bigger picture, possibly influenced by a high level of openly professed religious belief if compared to Europe, and you have an explanation for the high scores, which even with a slight discounting stand valiantly next to the results from Britain, India and Switzerland.

I found incredibly humble people at GE. Neither pushy, nor do they need to talk all the time.

(Interview, No. 116, French Head of New Work, 147,000 employees)

On the other hand, even in a country that seems to do so many things right, there are still considerable elements to work at. Where up to a third of managers do not know to ask for feedback when it is critical, there is still room for improvement! And across all 12 leadership behaviors, only 52 percent of managers were seen as humble.

What to Take From All of This?

By now we have seen differences and similarities in different countries. We have tried to identify some reasons for why the numbers are what they are in this moment of time. And this is useful for anyone who works in different countries.

At the same time, it is also often simply interesting to look at one’s own country and agree or disagree with the findings. There might even be a comfort in being assessed and labeled.

Yet this to my mind is not an option. It is no option to say: We have few managers in my country who are humble, so I will not be able to introduce humble behaviors in my company. Why? Because there is no reason to believe that humility will not work in every company worldwide if the leadership wants it to.

Humility would also work in Russia, but the managers do not do it for fear of losing face.

(Interview No. 143, CEO, Russian Nationality, working in US for an American company, 100 employees)

In particular, I would agree with interviewees who point out the following:

In any international company, there is more similarity between the different functions than there is a difference between the country cultures.
A company culture can exist across borders.
Across all nations as well as across age and gender, there is always more that people have in common than what differentiates them.

So even if you come from a country with low humility – that is no excuse. And if you come from a country with high humility, this is no excuse either. In each category at least a third of manager are not seen as humble. And as we have seen across all 12 dimensions, 53 percent of humble managers is the maximum for any country.

So, the goal of implementing humility should be there for any of you regardless of what country you come from!

So how do you go about this? Which leads me to the next question. Can you express your humility the same the world over?

Of course not. There is a considerable difference in exactly how you have to go about being “successfully” humble.

First a look at admitting weaknesses. A typical story comes from a Dutch manager who went to a meeting in Italy.

Just when I was made board member, I had a meeting in Italy and apologized for something. I could see in the face of one of the directors, that this had not gone down well. I went for a one on one with him. He told me that apologizing in his view was a weakness. We talked about Italian culture, about Dutch culture and now we understand each other better.

(Interview No. 138, CEO, 1,200 employees)

The lesson from this story? You need to adapt what you do to each country you work in. You need to reflect: what do I need to have done beforehand, how do I need to have established myself before I can share what I want to share.

Is this in any way new or rocket science? Not at all. This is common fare for any manager working abroad: It is vital to first understand the culture and then see in what ways and when you can project your desired behaviors, including your humility. This goes back to being humble yourself and seeing the bigger picture of which country, company, department and individual culture is all a part.

One example: giving feedback. Reed Hastings and Erin Meyer’s book No Rules dedicates pages and pages on how the Netflix culture of giving feedback had to be adapted to the different countries.

Why? Well, in Japan, for example, it is not done to criticize directly or give open feedback to your boss. Is it therefore impossible for Japanese employees to learn to give feedback? Is it impossible for a leader to ask for feedback? No, of course not. You just need to shift. If you find yourself in a more formal country like Japan, you need to move away from the ad hoc immediate feedback to more formal feedback which employees have had time to prepare beforehand. Netflix had thought about this and gave employees some guidelines on how to prepare for an open feedback session.

The results were remarkable. During the 360 process the Japanese on my team provided higher-quality feedback than my teams in the US had in previous years. The comments were frank and well-constructed. Their recommendations were actionable, and they didn’t pull punches. They received the feedback with grace and appreciation.7

For each country, Netflix tries to ensure that the feedback is practiced and ritualized in such a way that the desired effect appears. And if this means more prepared feedback and less live feedback, so be it.

In a less formal country like the Netherlands, it actually needed the mental preparation for the American managers not to be stunned by feedback that is far more direct and personal than in the US.

Erin Meyer in her book The Culture Map8 has a great story about how the Americans were shocked at what they thought too blunt a feedback between two Dutch colleagues. They even expressed their worry about it having damaged the relationship, only to hear from the manager who had been on the receiving end:

Of course, I didn’t enjoy hearing those things about myself. It doesn’t feel good to hear what I have done poorly. But I so much appreciated that Maarten would be transparent enough to give me that feedback honestly. Feedback like that is a gift. Thanks for that.

So, showing one’s weaknesses, receiving feedback, showing one’s strengths – all needs to be adapted to the situation you find yourself in.

Let us take a look at appreciation: Again, it works worldwide but needs to be expressed differently.

Appreciation in Asia is generally weak. But I believe they need it as much as in Europe. The important thing is, it needs to be seen as sincere. If it smacks of flattery then it is discounted.

(Interview No. 40, English CEO in Asia, 24,000 employees)

This is why many Europeans take issue with what they see as a culture of over-praising in the US. That, however, does not mean that they do not want to be appreciated, nor that there is not some improvement possible also in the US.

Imagine the following situation. After a presentation, the boss nods to you and says: “Great presentation.” This will be pleasant for some, fake for others. What if she had said: “Thank you very much for this presentation. I was particularly impressed by what you showed on slide 9. That made this process so much clearer for me.” A totally different kettle of fish and an appreciation that will actually work in all countries. At least if it is directed at the person in a one-on-one situation. Other rules apply in groups. So, before you blurt out individual praise, it is vital to understand how much society allows or expects individual contributions to be singled out.

In summary, if you look at all humble behaviors, they may appear much harder to express in some countries than in others. Yet, if you speak to more people from any one country, even those where things are seen as tough, they all know managers who are humbler and more successful with their humility than others. Compare, for example, an old Russian state company with a modern Russian internet company such as Yandex. Well, you actually cannot compare it. Like you cannot compare the Siemens of 20 years ago with the Siemens of today. One interviewee remembered how people were shouted at on stage until they cried, something that would be impossible in the Siemens of today. But I am sure somewhere in Germany, there still exists a company where people end up crying…

So humble and non-humble managers and companies can exist side by side in any culture. The important thing therefore is to find out how to role model humility in your very own company and your very own situation!

Take-Away: Humility works everywhere – Humility works everywhere! There may be certain differences between countries in the extent to which humility is already practiced there today. However, if humility is communicated in a way that is appropriate for the country, the effects are equally positive in every country.

Gender

I have many female employees. They don’t want to lead since they want harmony and not be responsible for negative decisions.

(Interview No. 104, female board member, 200 employees)

If I had the choice, I would always employ a woman over a man.

(Interview No. 99, male Board Member, 500 employees)

I think it is hardest for elder white males to admit errors.

(Interview No. 134, young male managing director of a Start-Up)

Women tend to underestimate themselves.

(Interview No. 23, female former VP HR, 1,000 employees)

My managers stopped me when I began to apologize for not speaking German well enough yet: “Don’t undersell yourself, there is a good reason why you have joined the team”. That sort of thing happens more to women – but is not humility!

(Interview No. 77, female HR Partner, 80,000 employees)

Many women when they get to be manager take on wrong behaviors – as do men.

(Interview No. 71, male COO, 2,100 employees)

There is one member of the Executive Committee. She was very direct to us all. I thought it great, but my team was frightened of her in a way they would not have been with a man.

(Interview No. 84, male CLO, 29,000 employees)

I think men are humbler – just because women in a more male environment tend to be quieter, does not mean they are humble.

(Interview No. 152, male CFO, 2,200 employees)

My male colleagues undermined me – they just had to be so much greater than me. So, I quit.

(Interview No. 85, female CEO, 1,200 employees)

As we have seen in Chapter 4, there seem to be some interesting points for discussion when it comes to gender. If you remember, it was the case that between 17 and 44 percent of top managers saw their female employees as humbler than their male employees. When the employees were asked the picture changed. Only those led by leaders below the age of 40 saw some elements of greater humility in their female managers. Below 50 there was parity with male managers and above 50 female leaders performed worse on a number of counts. At the same time, younger male leaders fared better when it came to asking for feedback even if it is critical, while elder male leaders seemed to gain some benefit of becoming older.

While it needs yet much more research to dissect this topic and to come to firmer conclusion on this “hot” topic, I would like to use this chapter to share some observations the interviewees made.

First of all, from my interview partners, 60 percent saw a difference between men and women leaders in the sense that in their view women exhibit more humility. They spoke about less ego, more self-reflection, finer perceptions, being more relaxed, more honest, better at assessing themselves. Some said if there was an option, they would always prefer to employ a woman. At the same time, they saw women as underestimating themselves as well as being more reluctant to take on responsibility. They perceived men to be more narcissistic and also equipped with more confidence, as engaged more in fights and less willing to self-reflect.

The other 40 percent were partly appalled at the question, thought it outdated and clinging to old role models, saying that they saw no difference. Or they saw the opposite, that in their view women get too tough as they climb up the ladder. Some said that men took some time to catch up, or that they expressed their humility differently as compared to women. Some saw women not as humble but as frightened of alpha males. Yet others said that the humility associated with females is often not real humility but simply a failure to stand up for oneself – hiding one’s light under the bushel and taking the easy road in avoiding conflicts for the bigger picture. And finally, there was a group that told stories about former female colleagues who once promoted had become cool and distant believing they could not show vulnerability the higher they get.

All in all, it seems that there are all viewpoints held on this matter. Let me share some more quotes from the interviews with you on this.

When I was a young woman, mid 30 to 40, I tried to be very male and power oriented. I ended up very unhappy, took a coach and decided to stop pretending.

(Interview No. 133, female Board Member, 23,000 employees)

I am tall and sporty. When there are only men in a meeting, I go into power modus. I am much more willing to compromise with women.

(Interview No. 46, male Head L&D, 2,000 employees)

Unlike others, I don`t expect a woman to be nice to me as a leader. My boss is caring but she can get tough.

(Interview No. 94, male Head of Learning, 3,000 employees)

There are 10 women, all candidates for bigger roles – none of them want to. They do not see it as a game that could be fun to play, as many men do.

(Interview: No. 103, female Global Head 36,000 employees)

Men are keener to lift others up, because they are more secure.

(Interview No. 48, female Global Head Diversity, 16,000 employees)

So, what to take from this all.

To my mind there are three key points.

Firstly, all different results are possible. There are humble men and unhumble women, there are humble women and unhumble men.

Secondly, our stereotypes may contribute to us expecting women to be humbler and then be disappointed when they are not, causing us to judge them more harshly. These stereotypes also may contribute to us expecting men to be unhumble and then either not notice, because it does not fit the picture or be overly enthusiastic about it.

Thirdly, there are numerous indications that some women may be underselling themselves – i.e., they may be seen as humble but what they are actually doing is hiding the light under a bushel. This entails them being too conciliatory instead of fighting those fights that they should for their and the company’s bigger picture.

All in all, I would say that one should humbly perceive each man and woman and see what there really is. And that when you lead humbly you will set the tone for your whole team, your colleagues and even partly your superiors – and then gender will not count any more.

In addition, one of the key points of humility has to do with not looking into the past, but into the present and the future. That means, even if there is in one female or male leader at a moment in time a lack of humility, this need not continue. See this wonderful example:

When I became CEO, I promoted the head of accounting to CFO. She had obviously felt, she had to be very tough and bite her way to the top. She was very distant, very curt and reluctant to invest time for example to bond with our supervisory board. We had a good relationship with each other, so I was able to suggest for her to go and work with a coach. She was willing to learn, worked with the coach, changed quite a lot and is now a successful CFO of a stock-listed company.

(Interview No. 151, male CEO, 3,700 employees)

The more humility reigns, neither woman nor man will need fear to appear weak when they are humble. There may come a time when it is clear to all that it is actually the narcissists who are weak.

If you look at another piece of research, there is already an indication for this downside of narcissism.

My aim was to see if the gender of a humble or narcissistic leader affected how they were being perceived. 239 participants from all over the worldd read short vignettes of two candidates. Without them realizing, there were two questionnaires in distribution: one with a narcissistic female and a humble male and one with a narcissistic male and a humble female.

The text ran as follows:

Candidate A: Nina/Nick

Nina/Nick regularly informs the board about her/his departmental successes. She/he sets a tight framework for her/his staff to work within and expects regular feedback on the steps taken. In meetings she/he compliments staff for special achievements and usually takes a speaking share of 50–70 percent. She/he does not discuss her/his own errors or weaknesses with anyone – her/his focus is on strengths. She/he has a private office with an assistant in the anteroom. She/he attends career-relevant training seminars and is a mentor to younger employees in order to share her/his experiences. She/he sees herself as difficult to replace and favored by her/his talents. "Without my grit and my skills, I wouldn't be in this position," she/he says frequently.

Candidate B: Hester/Henry

Hester/Henry regularly coordinates her/his goals with the board to ensure contributing to the larger corporate goals. She/he gives her/his staff broad guidelines. Within these they are free how to do their work. In meetings, she/he praises much and usually takes a speaking share of about 10–20 percent. She/he discusses her/his errors with her/his nine direct reports and asks them to support her/him in her/his weaknesses. She/he has moved her/his office and her/his assistant into open space. She/he attends training seminars and has a coach at her/his own expense. She/he is mentor to younger employees in order to share and learn from them. She/he sees herself/himself as easy to replace and favored by fate. "Without luck and circumstances, I wouldn't be in this position," she/he says frequently.

Approximately 90 percent of all participants saw Hester and Henry as humble leaders. Now, of course you will be interested to know if one of them reaped more benefit from being humble.

Well, the good news first. There was no statistical difference for participants wanting Hester or Henry as their leader or as a board member. Regardless of which one they read about, 88 percent wanted the humble leaders, 70 percent also as a board member in their company.

The sad news is that 12 percent did not want them as their direct manager and 30 percent not as their board member. Why not, we ask ourselves? Yet, whatever answer you may have, you need to put this result into perspective by looking at the narcissistic leader. Here only 25 percent wanted them as their leader and only 22 percent as a board member.

So, narcissism comes at a cost to your leadership attractiveness. In addition, only 18 percent saw Nick and Nina as likeable, compared to more than 80 percent for Hester and Henry.

What about being seen as weak versus being seen as competent. Here are some interesting statistical differences: As you can see in Figure 10.17, 13 percent actually see Hester as weak, while Henry is only negatively evaluated in 6 percent of cases. That may fuel fears that humility may indeed be seen as weakness. Yet if you compare it to the weakness seen in Nick and Nina, this pales into insignificance.

Figure 10.17 Narcissists are seen as weak.

While the difference for Nick and Nina is actually not statistically relevant, it nevertheless serves as an indicator that narcissism seems to be more costly to male leaders than to female leaders. Why do I say this? Look at Figure 10.18. While more than 80 percent saw the humble leader as competent, regardless of gender, there is a difference in the narcissists. Nina is seen as competent by 67 percent, Nick only by 53 percent.

Figure 10.18 Humble leaders seen as competent.

This is an interesting aspect – it looks as if there is still something in the way we perceive leaders, that it seems to harm females less when they are pushy and self-focused. They are not liked for it, as are the male leaders of that ilk, but it seems to award them with a halo of competence.

There is one additional gender effect to consider when you look at the gender of the rater. Women seem to be more accepting of male narcissists than men, while at the same time in general agreeing more than men with the competence narcissism transfers to women.

So, you can see in Figure 10.19 that 43 percent, i.e., nearly half, would be happy to have Nick as a board member, while men have no desire for such a leader. And 81 percent of women believe that Nina is competent, with men seeing this not quite as rosy.

Figure 10.19 Gender differences when it comes to narcissists.

Yet, overall, I want to emphasize a key point: Apart from these cited differences on all other dimensions, there was no relevant statistical difference. None. That looks as if there is primarily parity of the sexes here, with women being no better nor worse than men, and female employees and male employees being at most times in agreement on the level of humility of their leader.

Take-Away: A praise of equality – Since there seem to be only a few differences between the sexes in terms of humility, it can be stated that men and women show the same amount or the same amount of humility on average. Finally, a field where equality reigns?

Age

In Chapter 4, we also saw that there seems to be only a small age difference amongst the male managers, and a slightly larger one amongst female managers. So, it is interesting to look more deeply, into whether age plays a role for humility.

Amongst my interviewees there were four types of answers. About a third said that there was no difference either way, a third said that it gets better the older you are, a third asserted that it depends on the person and a final smattering of about 5 percent said that young ones were humbler.

There is a danger, the older you get: You start losing people who are senior to you – and have limited sources to learn from – unless you learn from the young. But in the past people only learned from older people – therefore the older have less reason to be humble.

(Interview No. 29, Group Head Customer Experience and CSR, 27,000 employees)

Bad leadership often gets passed on like cancer, therefore I do not think that the young generation will be better – they will be conditioned.

(Interview No. 65, Group HR Director, 70,000 employees)

Someone once said: You do not become wiser with age, but it becomes clear that others don't either.

(Interview No. 39, Board Member, 3,000 employees)

I have seen many older managers turning down their volume and becoming humbler.

(Interview No. 136, Director, 11,000 employees)

If you look at the data, there is no clear-cut answer.

On two dimensions, managers seem to become worse with age. Firstly, the older they get the less they seem to be willing to ask for feedback. And secondly, the over 50-year-olds are seen as considerably less humble by their employees once these have read the definition of humility (Figure 10.20).

Figure 10.20 Differences regarding the age of the manager.

There is once again a small bias for one’s peer group. Yet only for the over 50-year-old managers. Here, as you can see in Figure 10.21, the employees that are of the same age are much milder with them than those that are younger.

Figure 10.21 Some ingroup bias.

On all other dimensions, however there is no statistically relevant in-group bias.

That leads me to the conclusion that age indeed is hardly a relevant factor in how humility is lived.

If you look at the impact of other factors, this becomes even clearer: As we saw above, managers with a coach are seen as far humbler than those without a coach and this holds true regardless of age. If someone suffers from a stroke of fate when he or she is young, humility may come early on, if people use age to reflect, humility will come then.

My conclusion would be to see humility as a virtue that anyone can choose at any age.

That may sound like the same cop-out as above with nationalities and gender. But if all is possible, why should we try to find the right data set to press people into.

There is no safety in data for any future assessment – every person can decide to change, to act differently. It is the task for you as leaders and for your organizations as systems to create conditions in which humility becomes the desirable virtue to have. And then the demographic or national differences are only like a tune being played with a slightly different interpretation.

But it remains the same tune!

Take-Away: The agelessness of humility – Although there are small statistical differences, they are not relevant in the grand scheme of things. One can be humble at any age. Unfortunately, you can also lack humility at any age!

Notes
Vera, D., & Rodriguez-Lopez, A. (2004). Strategic virtues: Humility as a source of competitive advantage. Organizational Dynamics, 33(4), 393–408.
   https://www.britishfuture.org/english-identity-british-future-poll-findings/. Retrieved 12.11.2022
Park, S., Han, S. J., Hwang, S. J., & Park, C. K. (2019). Comparison of leadership styles in Confucian Asian countries. Human Resource Development International, 22(1), 91–100.
Schlösser, O. (2006). Humane Orientation: A Cross-Cultural Study in 26 Countries [Doctoral Dissertation, Universitätsbibliothek].
Tavanti, M. (2012). The cultural dimensions of Italian leadership: Power distance, uncertainty avoidance and masculinity from an American perspective. Leadership, 8(3), 287–301.
   https://www.oecd-ilibrary.org/sites/bd69f805-en/index.html?itemId=/content/component/bd69f805-en. Retrieved 12.11.2022
Hastings, R., & Meyer, E. (2020). No Rules Rules: Netflix and the Culture of Reinvention. Penguin Press, New York, NY.
Meyer, E. (2014). The Culture Map: Breaking Through the Invisible Boundaries of Global Business. Public Affairs.

===== FILE 19/23: xhtml%2fc11.xhtml =====

Chapter 11 Humility in Politics

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-11

A politician thinks of the next election. A statesman, of the next generation

James Freeman Clarke (US author 1810–1888)

Summary

Initially it looks as if humility in politics is harder to achieve. In particular, the voters, while “officially” claiming to desire humble politicians, tend to be drawn to more narcissistic versions. Yet, there are considerable upsides to humility and both research and case studies show that it can be cultivated in such a way as to be treasured by voters and the press alike.

The politician had once run a larger German federal state. When asked about humility in politics, he sighed:

In politics you are stuck with your party. You cannot simply quit and join another one… So that is why people fight harder and tend to be less open about their weaknesses and less willing to show appreciation of others.

He continued:

Yet I have seen numerous politicians, who do behave humbly. One has really stuck in my mind. As a young politician I was part of a group visiting England. There we were hosted by a member of parliament, who had been made lord a few years back. He was very religious and yet had been given the task to introduce the first bill on abortion. And he said in front of us: “From the very moment on that I received such an order, I forgot everything I had ever thought before. It had simply become a task that will be performed through me for the good of the people.”

Here was a politician willing to look at the bigger picture and see himself in his role as a servant to the people who had elected him. A rarity? A common occurrence? What do you think?

In this chapter, we will have a look at the empirical evidence to determine whether humility can actually play a relevant role in political life. Or whether due to the many pressures from the party, the constituency, the press it is well-nigh impossible to be humble and successful at the same time? Can you be humble on the way up or only once you are at the top? What else is unique when it comes to humility in politics?

One thing is clear upfront: where politicians have employees, they reap all the benefits of humility and all the negative effects of narcissism just as if they were in charge of any other group of subordinates. Numerous interviews confirm this.

I loved working for him. He was the most appreciative person I have ever met. Seeing him on the public stage made you cringe, because he put on this strange persona, but in every day work life, he was a delight.

Employee of minister of state)

She took time to regularly check how we were doing. She seemed genuinely interested and remembered not only what we told her but also often thanked us explicitly for our work.

(Employee of high-ranking party politician)

I have never worked for someone who was less humble. Outwardly he tried to portray himself as modest and interested in the welfare of others. But behind closed doors he was simply an arrogant narcissist!

(Former employee of a former secretary of state)

Do Humble Politicians Manage to Get Elected?

Things become more complex when it comes to the relationship with voters. Do voters actually value humble politicians, people who know their weaknesses as well as their strengths? People who acknowledge others in their own party, in other parties? People who are obviously open to learning and actually see a bigger picture that goes beyond them?

Well, voters definitely claim to want humble politicians! My research in Figure 11.1 shows that once voters have read the definition of humility, 86 percent of voters see humility as a desirable quality in politicians. This is a huge number, yet at the same time, less than for business leaders, where 97 percent are desirous of this virtue.

Figure 11.1 Percentage that sees humility as a desirable virtue.

This gives a first indication that people see a difference between the two types of jobs. From the interviews it becomes clearer where the two diverge.

To live this brutal life as a politician, where everything you do is scrutinized, I believe you need a dose of narcissism to not only want to become a politician but also to survive as one.

(Activist in party politics)

Politicians are constrained by rather hierarchical party cultures, because so much of their rise is determined by their standing within the party (and increasingly the press). Most don’t develop individual opinions, except perhaps a crafted eccentricity. They need a strong ego in order to advertise themselves. So, though they would undoubtedly benefit from the breadth that true humility would give them, it’s usually not a priority for them.

(Professor of history)

86 percent of voters who want humility is still a large group. Do these voters actually believe that they can get that desired virtue in their politicians? Or is there an equally huge gap as there was with the managers? Remember: 97 percent of employees wanted a humble leader and only 35 percent on average worldwide get it.

Remember also how employees judged a third of managers in their company to be narcissistic?

When asked about politicians, the results in Figure 11.2 are even more depressing. Only 23 percent are seen as humble, 61 percent as narcissists and 16 percent are designated as others. What are these others? The comments range from: “10 percent are psychopaths” to “either idealistic or corrupt.”

Figure 11.2 Humble and narcissistic leaders.

That shows that public perception of politicians is far more critical than for managers. Not surprising then that politicians rank very low when it comes to trust:

Members of the US Congress are at the bottom of the list when it comes to trust: they are distrusted by 58 percent of the population versus for example nurses, who garner 84 percent of trust.2
In the UK 78 percent said that politicians were the least trustworthy, followed a distant second by journalists (37 percent) and car salesmen (27 percent).3
Even in India, where humility scores are as we have seen generally very high, only 59 percent of the population trusts politicians.4

On a second level, it also is doubtful if the voters truly want humble politicians. They may claim to want them and then prefer a narcissistic leader after all.

Take a look at my research. In 2021 more than 200 voters answered questions about political candidates. The candidates were described as either a male narcissist and a female humble candidate or as a female narcissist and a male humble candidate.

The focus was on what voters observe and judge in a one-time communication with this politician. This is, of course, of a different magnitude compared to what I as an employee can see from my manager in everyday work life. However, since we can only know politicians by how they chose to communicate with us, this must become the litmus test of their humility. As a result, it is even harder with politicians to judge what of this is real or play-acted.

This was the text describing the candidates:

The two candidates are the same age, are running for an important leadership position in the same party and have similar positions in terms of content.

The Narcissistic Politician

Excerpts from candidate Nina's/Nick's speech, described by a journalist afterwards as follows: "With grand gestures and verve, here is someone who sees herself/himself as the right person to lead the party into the future."

Each person has their destiny – and I see mine as doing the best for you and the party. To that end, I have assembled and briefed the most capable team so that we can tackle whatever is at hand from the moment "Go."

I am grateful that my fighting spirit has brought me to this moment in time and being able to take on this job for the party. Precisely because our times need strength and spirit my goal is to implement our political agenda in a way that benefits all of you who are sitting here.

As for my competitor Hester/Henry, we have known each other for a long time – I appreciate her/him for her/his openness and for the fact that she/he also knows her/his own weaknesses.

Last but not least: All of you here from the party base know what the future demands from the leadership of our party. I am ready to fight for it, teeth, claws and all.

The Humble Politician

Excerpts from candidate Hester's/Henry's speech, described by a journalist afterwards as follows: "With great team spirit and full of gratitude, someone wants to achieve the best for everyone."

Everyone has weaknesses and makes mistakes – that's where I'm grateful to my team for standing by me with their expertise and help, and also for being relentlessly open with me.

The fact that I am standing here is due to an enormous amount of luck. My goal is to formulate and implement our political agenda in a way that benefits everyone. Even those who are not as fortunate as I am. Even those who will not vote for us.

As for my competitor Nina/Nick, I have respect for her/his skills and abilities. We have accomplished a lot together in recent years, and no matter in which constellation we find ourselves after this election, I hope for an equally successful future collaboration.

Last but not least: You know best what exactly is needed for this party and our future. I'm happy to stand up to your scrutiny.

Participants rightly identified humble Hester and humble Henry as being humble (89 percent) while narcissistic Nina/Nick clearly did not fit the bill (less than 10 percent).

Yet! Yet only 58 percent wanted to vote for Hester and only 50 percent for Henry (Figure 11.3). This was better than the 23 percent that wanted to vote for Nick and the 32 percent that wanted to vote for Nina, but compared to 86 percent who want humble politicians this is somewhat depressing.

Figure 11.3 Percentage who would vote for…

It demonstrates once again that we say we want something and when the option arises, we go for something else. Like wanting to live a healthy life and stuffing down cake or swigging back beer instead.

Research confirms this dilemma on other fronts: If you ask voters how they vote, they of course point to their own rationality and that the politician has to be competent. And then there are dozens of study results from all over the world that show that voters prefer the more attractive candidate5, and that attractiveness alone predicts more than 70 percent of the election outcomes6.

So, it is not surprising that on the humility/narcissism front the result gets even more irrational, when you dive more deeply into the data.

For one, giving exactly the same speech, female humble politicians are seen as more competent than their male counterparts! 62 percent see Hester as competent, while only 50 percent agree for Henry (Figure 11.4).

Figure 11.4 Percentage of voters who see as competent.

Male voters are least impressed by Henry, only 42 percent think him a good politician versus 65 percent women (Figure 11.5).

Figure 11.5 Female voters more impressed by male narcissist.

The first result therefore: humility in politicians seems to serve women better than men. As you saw in the chapter on gender, this problem does not arise for managers – here the humble female manager and the humble male managers are both seen as competent by 88 percent of employees.

There are yet some other fascinating results from the data that show how much more complex humility is for politicians.

Firstly, the effect of age. Interestingly, young people are more open towards narcissists than older people. More than 70 percent of voters below the age of 35 think of Nick or Nina as competent, while only around 30 percent of the older voters agree with that (Figure 11.6).

Figure 11.6 Enthusiastic young and disillusioned older voters.

Yet this effect is neutralized when it becomes obvious that the young are in general more enthusiastic, while people older than 35 have become cynical or lukewarm and appear to be disillusioned with all types of politicians.

There are other elements in the make-up of the voters that seem to condemn humble politicians to fail.

First the issue of self-esteem. We have already seen above that humility and self-esteem are correlated. How? Well, the greater the humility, the higher the self-esteem; the lower the humility, the lower the self-esteem – and the higher the narcissism and arrogance.

If these are correlated, is it then not to be expected that people with low self-esteem find humble politicians a waste of space?

Indeed, a piece of research from 2020 shows exactly this7: Assessing the video of a politician speaking humbly, those with greater self-esteem assessed the person far more positively than did those with low self-esteem.

My research, as you can see in Figure 11.7, bolsters this finding. Those voters who had lower self-esteem were far more positive about the narcissistic politician. A full 56 percent of these see Nick or Nina as competent versus only 38 percent of those with higher self-esteem. Those with higher self-esteem were also more skeptical as regards the performance of the narcissist, even though still somewhat impressed with 64 versus 77 percent.

Figure 11.7 Low self-esteem correlates with acceptance of narcissism.

The inner argument of voters lacking self-esteem seems to run along the following lines: “If I am a sad little sparrow, I want a glorious peacock to fight for me.”

This means that at least in part humble communication in a politician will depend on the audience for its effectiveness.

Two other groups also make life hard for humble politicians. One group consists of those people that believe it to be quite right that (their) in-group is in charge of things, that equality in humans is overrated and that those who know better or have more resources should actually be in charge. Research calls this snappily “Social Dominance Orientation.”

How to assess this Social Dominance Orientation? By getting people to rate statements such as “It’s probably a good thing that certain groups are at the top and other groups are at the bottom” or “Superior groups should dominate inferior groups” or “Group equality should be our ideal.” 16 questions later you have your personal score.

Now, what do you guess? I expect you have predicted the outcome that you can see in Figure 11.8. Those who believe it desirable that there are different groups, i.e., those with a high score on Social Dominance Orientation have a big issue with humble politicians. Fewer vote for the humble politician, fewer think him or her competent, fewer think the humble one to be a good politician and even fewer think that humility is a desirable virtue for a politician.

Figure 11.8 Correlation between social dominance orientation and humility.

This reminds of the research that humble managers have a limited effect on those employees who believe in high-power distance. These employees believe it right for there to be an above and a below and as a consequence think someone who treats them as an equal as slightly strange and not to be taken too seriously.

The final group of “difficult” voters has to do with what attachment type they are. Which one are you? No idea? Well, read the following and then pick one of the answers.

These questions are concerned with your experiences in romantic love relationships. Take a moment to think about these experiences and answer the following questions with them in mind. Read each of the three self-descriptions below (A, B and C) and then choose the single alternative that best describes how you feel in romantic relationships or is nearest to the way you feel. (Note: The terms “close” and “intimate” refer to psychological or emotional closeness, not necessarily to sexual intimacy.)

Response options:

(A) I am somewhat uncomfortable being close to others; I find it difficult to trust them completely, difficult to allow myself to depend on them. I am nervous when anyone gets too close, and often, others want me to be more intimate than I feel comfortable being.
(B) I find that others are reluctant to get as close as I would like. I often worry that my partner doesn’t really love me or won’t want to stay with me. I want to get very close to my partner, and this sometimes scares people away.
(C) I find it relatively easy to get close to others and am comfortable depending on them and having them depend on me. I don’t worry about being abandoned or about someone getting too close to me.

So, which one did you pick? A is the avoidant style, B is called the anxious style and C the secure attachment style.

Research has shown that the avoidant style in general is negatively correlated with humility.8 Why? Humility is grounded in a sense of security that allows you to consider your strengths and limitations in a non-defensive manner. Avoidant attachment on the other hand causes people to be dismissive and to try to extricate themselves from the judgment of others by – yes you guessed correctly – becoming defensive and arrogant.

My research confirmed this also for the political sphere. Voters who were of the avoidant type (36 percent of the participants) were much more impressed with the narcissist. A full 41 percent thought him or her a great politician vs. only 26 percent for the anxious or the healthy attachment style.

As a result, we have a quandary for any politician. There they may be wishing to be humble and due to the fact that voters come in all types and shapes many of them will then be assessed negatively.

There is however a positive piece of news that reflects back to what I have brought up many times in this book: We are not stuck with our styles. Research shows that we can easily be shifted.

What do I mean by this? Look at this piece of research regarding priming and its effects: Participants were asked to recollect a scene from their childhood where with a care-giver they had either felt safe, anxious or avoidant. Those who were primed into feeling safe ended up being much more open to arguments that had a different view point than people primed into anxiety or avoidance.9

The message is clear: If I feel safe and do not feel threatened, then I am willing to deal with people who are different from me and am even willing to listen to them.

This means that a humble communication which manages to convey a sense of personal safety will be far more willingly received. Is this sort of communication difficult? Well, it comes down to how you are talking to others. How much are you focusing on them and how much on yourself.

If you look at some of the politicians that are regarded as humble such as former US-President Obama or the former President from Uruguay José Mujica, you can see that in their speeches they show concern for their audience, they manage to convey that they are taking care of them and that people can feel safe with them. In this way, they will partly manage to shift people of avoidant type to trusting them.

Similar ways of adapting the communication will also help appeal to people with lower self-esteem and those with a greater social dominance orientation.

Of course, I am not postulating that a politician will be able to communicate well with absolutely all types of voters – yet as we know a few percentage points here and there can make the difference between being able to implement policies versus not being elected.

My research in Germany gives an indication that this might be possible. Just before the 2021 national elections, I asked 200 voters about the humility and the narcissism of each of the candidates. You can see the result in Figure 11.9: Those politicians fared best with the voters who were either seen as mainly humble and hardly narcissistic (Annalena Baerbock, Green Party) or those who were middling on both fronts (Olaf Scholz, Social Democrats). They gained more than five percent for their party as compared to the last election. Even Christian Lindner (Liberal Democrats) with still a good score on humility seems to have been able to counteract his high narcissism score. Yet if you look at Armin Laschet (Christian Democrats), it gives an indication that someone who is hardly seen as humble and mainly as narcissistic was not able to succeed. Instead, the ruling Christian Democrats ended up not only losing nine percent but the whole election and now there is a (humble?) coalition of Greens, Liberals and Social Democrats in place.

Figure 11.9 The impact of humility on the German election of 2021.

While this is only a first piece of evidence, this goes in the direction of the research seen above, which showed narcissism tempered by humility to be a successful combination also in managers.

Can Politicians Stay Humble after Having Been Elected?

This brings us to the next question. Even if I am humble when I get elected, is not holding office then typically the death of humility? Even politicians see many hurdles here.

As a consequence of these structural pressures, people see a different reality for politicians and seem to have some doubt as to whether humility in politics is truly possible.

If you ask politicians, they agree in parts, since they feel so strongly tied to their own party and their own voters that it limits their ability to act freely.

The party and the voters get you into office with a clear agenda. But in order to get things done you actually have to compromise. And of course, not all on the other side are bad. Often, they are reasonable people, with whom you can establish good working relationships and actually get things done. But when you come home, you have to pretend that you were forced to give in and that the other side is hateful or unreasonable. A real quandary!

(Former member of parliament)

To solidify their base, politicians have to be partisans. Red meat must be thrown to the hounds. At the same time, the politician has to reach beyond the partisan corral to those floating voters who want to be spoken to, not spoken for. A good politician has to be simultaneously in the battle and above the fray. A great old Scottish politician once put it this way: “A man who can’t ride two bloody horses at once has no right to a job in the bloody circus.”

(Michael Ignatieff, former Canadian politician)1

And riding two horses may make it impossible to be humble, open to learning and serving a picture bigger than one’s own party.

This is true for the “ordinary” member of parliament. It is even more dangerous for the government, especially its head. Because that’s where hubris looms. Studies show that political leaders are even more likely to fall victim to this kind of hubris. How is hubris different from normal narcissism? Firstly, in that it is developed during the time of holding office, while narcissism typically stems from childhood and then is only strengthened or weakened by the surroundings.

Secondly, it has some additional elements that go beyond narcissism. Former politician Sir David Owen, who served in an UK labor government and who as a qualified doctor took to studying politicians once he had quit active service, has developed the following assessment to uncover who has developed hubris during their time in office:10

I suggest you check this with some politicians from your country in mind.

Hubris syndrome is formulated as a pattern of behavior in a person who:

Sees the world as a place for self-glorification through the use of power.
Has a tendency to take action primarily to enhance personal image.
Shows disproportionate concern for image and presentation.
Exhibits messianic zeal and exaltation in speech.
Conflates self with nation or organization.
Uses the royal “we” in conversation.
Shows excessive self-confidence.
Manifestly has contempt for others.
Shows accountability only to a higher court (history or God).
Displays unshakeable belief that they will be vindicated in that court.
Loses contact with reality.
Resorts to restlessness, recklessness and impulsive actions.
Allows moral rectitude to obviate consideration of practicality, cost or outcome.
Displays incompetence with disregard for nuts and bolts of policy making.

Some of these behaviors are also found in a narcissistic personality or in what scientists call Anti-Social Personality Disorder or Histrionic Personality Disorder, yet five of the assessments are unique to hubris (5, 6, 10, 12, 13)

Who do researchers see as people who have developed this syndrome?

Former British Prime Minister Neville Chamberlain, who in 1938 had come back from Munich negotiating with Hitler and believed that only he had seen the light and that everyone else was badly mistaken thinking of Hitler as a vile and evil man.
Former Chairman of the communist party Mao Zedong who in 1958 flabbergasted the Russian foreign minister Andrei Gromyko by telling him that he was willing to sacrifice the lives of "300 million people" in a nuclear war to further his ambition to reunite with Taiwan.11
Former US president George W. Bush, when in 2003 he believed his mission in Iraq accomplished despite copious proof to the opposite.
Former British Prime Minister Margret Thatcher, once she stopped heeding the advice of her counsel Willie Whitelaw, started referring to herself in the royal we, e.g., “We have become a grandmother,” and began waging war against her peers.
Former British Prime Minister Tony Blair, when he gave hyperbolic speeches after 9/11, citing God as being the only one to judge his actions.
Former US president Donald Trump during most of his presidency, in particular once the pandemic struck.
President of Brazil Jair Bolsonaro, when he started flaunting and mocking recommendations from health experts regarding mask wearing and social distancing.

What can politicians do to prevent them from succumbing to hubris? Ensuring that there are strong people around them who give them open and blunt feedback. Remind oneself continuously that power is finite and not tied to oneself but only to the role. Not confuse the role with oneself.

Are there some politicians who have succeeded in this?

Some say that former German chancellor Angela Merkel did well. While there is some skepticism about her ability to communicate effectively with the electorate or see the bigger picture in how Germany should act within Europe, she seemed to be willing to continuously learn as well as admit publicly to mistakes. Not only did she announce that her government initially misjudged the pandemic but also personally took all the blame for a hasty decision to declare an extra bank-holiday to break the second wave. “This mistake is solely mine, because in the end I bear the ultimate responsibility for everything.”

In addition, she seemed only little occupied with her ego or status, going to the supermarket like any other person without jumping queues or demanding special service.

An anecdote that underscores this low self-focus comes from a 2021 John Hopkins University ceremony for an honorary doctorate. She was asked about her upcoming retirement and plans for the future. Her answer was that she needed a break to think about “what really interests me. And then I’ll try to read, then I’ll rest a bit, because I’m very tired, and then I’ll sleep, and then we’ll see what happens.”

Another example often cited by interviewees is the acting prime minister of New Zealand Jacinda Ardern. She received much praise for the respectful and heartful way in which she responded to the Christchurch Mosque terror attacks in 2019 as well as how she acted during the Pandemic. She regularly and informally connected via television and engendered a consensus on how best to deal with the situation.

In interviews she also takes a humble stand:

We need our leaders to be able to empathize with the circumstances of others; to empathize with the next generation that we’re making decisions on behalf of. And if we focus only on being seen to be the strongest, most powerful person in the room, then I think we lose what we’re meant to be here for. So I’m proudly focused on empathy, because you can be both empathetic and strong.12

Her ability to focus on the bigger picture also helped her rise above any self-doubts when she became prime minister:

I actually don’t think that there was some big moment where I suddenly found confidence, I found responsibility instead. And that actually has always been a bigger motivation for me, more than anything else; that sense of duty. I had opportunities where people would come to me and say: “We think you should do this.” And I think, “Ooh, I don’t know that I’m up for that”, but then I’d feel like I had to, that I owed it to the people, I owed it to others who are asking me, that I had a duty, a responsibility. That in the end was what overcame my confidence gap; feeling like I needed to for other people, and look, whatever it takes.13

Another leader who is seen as humble is the above-mentioned José Mojica, who was Prime Minister of Uruguay from 2010 to 2015. A former guerrilla who spent more than ten years in prison, he became known as the “poorest president” donating all but $800 of his $12,000 salary to charities. In addition, he continued to live in the ramshackle farm he and his wife owned before he became president. It is said that his default setting was love: “I do not hate. Do you know what a luxury it is not to hate?”14

While there is some dispute as to whether Mujica ended up being an effective president, an article published in 201515 posited that even though the tangible effects of his presidency were not very clearly measurable, all the poor people the author had spoken to had felt that their life had become better. They were enthusiastic that for the first time they “had been seen.”

A similar story is told of former President Obama. While of course there is also much he is seen to have failed at, many see him as one of the greatest communicators – genuinely appreciating others:

He has such great power and yet talked with such humility. He has a unique ability to connect earthly, daily activities to aspirational dreams for a greater future, and has told countless stories to help us emotionally connect with others who are separated from us by distance and time.16

A more scientific data to look at is the Gallup Presidential Approval Ratings, where Obama reached an average of more than 50 percent not only in his first year as president but also in his last year. This last data point puts him above 10 other presidents in final-year approvals. Only G. W. Bush, Ronald Reagan, Eisenhower and Roosevelt were equally good or better at generating cohesion.

Obama also admitted to having made mistakes. When in his first year he had been too hasty in appointing someone to health secretary he openly admitted: “I screwed up,” setting a new tone for the administration, which the electorate appreciated.

Gerald Ford, who acted as president between 1974 and 1977, is another politician perceived as humble. He took over from President Nixon after the Watergate affair and focused on what he called “the healing process.” Serving this bigger picture, he declared a full and unconditional pardon for Nixon, which act actually cost him re-election. Yet he believed it to be essential for the bigger picture. He also had a fine sense of what should remain private and what should be out in the open. He was very devout and had daily meetings with three other Congressmen for late-morning prayer sessions that never were publicized – on purpose: The sessions, which began in 1967 and continued off and on through 1975, were "very quiet," totally off the record, Ford said. “Talk about going to Bible study and people will get the idea that you think you're somehow better than they are.”17 He also refused to use his religion in the election campaign against Carter who won most of the vote from the evangelical community. Something that Ford might have changed but following his own values did not.

Nelson Mandela is another example of someone who became humble and managed to serve and steer South Africa through difficult times. Three anecdotes to exemplify this:

He was obviously very much aware of his weaknesses18: "It is a tragedy to go to jail, especially a white man's jail," Mandela said. But while there, "you can sit down when everyone else is asleep and distance yourself from yourself and discover how you have behaved. Some of the things I discovered made me ashamed. I was convinced I did not belong to this human race."
He could show appreciation:19 “On his first day in office he came upon an Afrikaner by the name of John Reinders, chief of presidential protocol during the tenure both of the last white president, FW de Klerk, and his predecessor, PW Botha. He was packing his boxes, when Mandela came in. ‘Good morning, how are you?’ said Mandela, with a cheery grin. ‘Very well, Mr. President, and you?’ ‘Very well, very well …’ Mandela replied. ‘But, ah … may I ask, what are you doing?’ Reinders, who was packing away his belongings into cardboard boxes, replied: ‘I am taking away my things, Mr. President. I am moving to another job.’ ‘Ah, very good. Where is it you are going?’ ‘Back to the prisons department. I served there as a major before coming to work here in the presidency.’ ‘Ah, no,’ Mandela grinned. ‘No, no, no. I know that department very well. I would not recommend doing that.’ Turning serious, Mandela proceeded to persuade Reinders to stay. ‘You see, we people, we are from the bush. We do not know how to administer a body as complex as the presidency of South Africa. We need the help of experienced people such as yourself. I would ask you, please, to stay at your post. I intend only to serve for one presidential term and then, of course, you would be free to do as you wish.’ Reinders, as astonished as he was charmed, needed no further explanations. Slowly, shaking his head in wonder, he began to empty his boxes. Reinders, whose eyes filled with tears as he recalled that story sometime later, told me that during the five years he had served at Mandela's side, travelling far and wide with him, he had received nothing but courtesy and kindness.”
And thirdly, he managed to convince his party colleagues not to strike out the old South African hymn “Die Stem” but to agree to keeping it and for the time being (1994–1997) simply adding the unofficial hymn of Black South Africa “Nkosi Sikelel' iAfrika.” Why? Because he had understood the bigger picture and that he must not threaten and loose the white people.

There are yet more examples of humble behavior observed in politicians:

Charles de Gaulle is still extolled for paying his own electricity bills when living as president at the Elysée.
Helmut Schmidt, former chancellor of Germany, in 1977 took five hours to talk to three highly valued authors (Heinrich Böll, Max Frisch, Siegfried Lenz) and the publisher Siegfried Unseld in order to understand what drives young people to terrorism.
Austrian chancellor Bruno Kreisky remained listed in the public phone directory of Vienna and publicly claimed that he wished to serve “this country as did Sonnenfels serve Maria Theresia.” Sonnenfels was a proponent of enlightenment, ensured the abolition of torture and other legal reforms.
We already saw how former Indian President APJ Abdul Kalam was mentioned as an example for a humble person. The press points out how he strongly focused on appreciating others, regardless of religion or age. For example, he made it a priority to meet one-on-one with young people setting a target of 500,000 such exchanges for his five-year term! He donated his salary to a trust, living a spartan life while communicating a clear bigger picture for a strong India, saying: “We must think and act like a nation of a billion people and not like that of a million people.”

With all these examples, it is clear that humble politicians can at times succeed.

Is it still harder than in a company? I think that political power coupled with party politics and the press creates even more challenges than is the case in normal business. The feeling of being involved in something valuable, of being dedicated to something greater - namely governing - can easily lead to taking oneself for too important. Being part of a party whose interest it is to scarcely see anything good in the other party builds up the pressure not only to see others as enemies, but also to portray them as such.

Add to this the slightly perverse pleasure that most readers take in crises, conflicts and corruption, and the result is that many politicians think little of those they are supposed to represent, which voters of course immediately sense and attribute to narcissism and arrogance.

Thus humility in politics remains a difficult business, which John F. Kennedy wisely summed up thus:

Mothers all want their sons to grow up to be president, but they don’t want them to become politicians in the process.

Take Away: Possible if hard – It is possible to succeed in politics with humility. Yet it is hard to escape external pressures toward narcissism, hubris, and partisanship.
