Chapter 12 Examples of Humility as an Inspiration

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-12

I speak to everyone in the same way, whether he is the garbage man or the president of the university.

Albert Einstein (Swiss physicist 1879–1955)

Summary

There are a number of companies and people who successfully embody humility, be it in the search for openness, the willingness to acknowledge others for what they do, to analyze one's own weaknesses or to perceive the big picture.

Pixar1

Pixar is an animation studio in Hollywood. Founded in 1979 as a subsidiary of the Lucasfilm computer department, it became independent in 1986 with investment from Steve Jobs. In 2006 it was bought by Disney but is allowed considerable freedom and independence. Pixar has produced 26 films, all of which have been hits.

One of the reasons why Pixar has become so successful has to do with something called Braintrust. This is an open feedback process which was instituted in 1999. It entails a group of colleagues meeting regularly and intensively discussing the film that is currently being developed. According to Ed Catmull, one of the core figures at Pixar, the goal is to “push toward excellence and to root out mediocrity.” Behind this phrase lies the clear recognition of own weaknesses. “Early on, all of our movies suck. Pixar films are not good at first, and our job is to make them so – to go, as I say, ‘from suck to not-suck’.”

What happens in this process is openness, openness and openness. With a few rules: The feedback to the filmmaker must be constructive. The filmmaker must not take the criticism personally and be prepared to hear the truth. The comments made are only suggestions, not rules. Very important: The feedback must be empathic – strongly peppered with praise and appreciation.

The results? A different and better ending, in which WALL-E is saved by EVE. A better storyline to explain what happens to our memories in “Inside Out.”

Ed Catmull sums it up like this:

Candor isn’t cruel. It does not destroy. On the contrary, any successful feedback system is built on empathy, on the idea that we are all in this together, that we understand your pain because we’ve experienced it ourselves. The need to stroke one’s own ego, to get the credit we feel we deserve – we strive to check those impulses at the door. The Braintrust is fueled by the idea that every note we give is in the service of a common goal: Supporting and helping each other as we try to make better movies.

And the best thing: “Sometimes, magic happens. And by magic, I mean the ego has left the room.”

Catmull himself had a strong role as a catalyst in the process, while repeatedly pointing out that he was probably the stupidest in the whole company. Yet they say about him:

The thing about Ed is, he’s one of the most brilliant guys you’ll ever meet. In the world of mathematics, he’s a demigod. The Catmull-Rom spline (a function in mathematics) is named after him. He built this place – yet his philosophy was always to hire people smarter than he is. He’s this really rare combination of incredible intelligence and zero ego.

The key realization that underlies the culture at Pixar is that everyone, absolutely everyone can contribute to an idea. Employees are thrown into different environments and processes – all so that everyone can learn to express and represent their ideas artistically. Why? Because “fear is the greatest killer of creativity. To cultivate a strong creative environment, you have to make it comfortable for people to express their ideas.”2

Zappos

Zappos was founded 1999 in San Francisco as an online shoe store. Following a first big growth spurt in an aim to reduce its high churn rate in 2004, the company moved to Las Vegas, Nevada. Nearly 80 percent of employees were willing to make the 600-mile move – all for the sake of an even better corporate culture. In order to bolster this very culture, Zappos introduced a boot camp for new employees. With a twist. After its four weeks, everyone is offered a monthly salary if they decide not to start at Zappos! You have to savor this: I apply, get accepted, get to know the company and now they offer me a month’s salary so I don't start! Why? A safety measure, so that only those people who truly fit in with the culture and who want to stay are brought on board. And not those who feel they have already invested too much. This is a clever way to prevent sunk costs from arising in the first place.

Since 2009 Zappos has been part of Amazon as an independent unit. Today Zappos has about 1,500 employees and a turnover of $2 billion.

One of its ten core values is humility. Founder Tony Hsieh (1973–2020) summed up how Zappos tries to test for humility:

The hardest to test is core value no. 10: humility. You can't just say, “How humble are you?” And you say, “I am the humblest person in the whole wide world”. One of the ways we test for this... many of our candidates are from out of town, so we pick them up from the airport with a Zappos shuttle, offer them a tour, and then they spend the whole day doing interviews. At the end of the day of interviews, the recruiter will return with the shuttle driver. The recruiter will then ask exactly how the shuttle driver was treated by the interviewees. It does not matter how well the day of the interviews went, if they did not treat the shuttle driver well, we will not hire them. It is not even a question.3

Benjamin Franklin4

Franklin, one of the American founding fathers, wrote in his autobiography:

A Quaker friend having kindly informed me that I was generally thought proud, that my pride showed itself frequently in conversation, that I was not content with being in the right when discussing any point, but was overbearing and rather insolent, of which he convinced me by mentioning several instances, I determined endeavoring to cure myself, if I could, of this vice or folly among the rest, and I added Humility to my list, giving an extensive meaning to the word.

I cannot boast of much success in acquiring the reality of this virtue, but I had a good deal with regard to the appearance of it. I made it a rule to forbear all direct contradiction to the sentiments of others, and all positive assertion of my own. I even forbid myself, agreeably to the old laws of our Junto, the use of every word or expression in the language that imported a fixed opinion, such as certainly, undoubtedly, etc., and I adopted, instead of them, I conceive, I apprehend, or I imagine a thing to be so or so, or it so appears to me at present. When another asserted something that I thought an error, I denied myself the pleasure of contradicting him abruptly and of showing immediately some absurdity in his proposition; and in answering, I began by observing that in certain cases or circumstances his opinion would be right, but in the present case there appeared or seemed to me some difference, etc.

I soon found the advantage of this change in my manner; the conversations I engaged in went on more pleasantly.

The modest way in which I proposed my opinions procured them a readier reception and less contradiction; I had less mortification when I was found to be in the wrong, and I more easily prevailed with others to give up their mistakes and join with me when I happened to be in the right.

And this mode, which I at first put on with some violence to natural inclination, became at length so easy, and so habitual to me, that perhaps for these fifty years past no one has ever heard a dogmatical expression escape me. And to this habit (after my character of integrity) I think it principally owing that I had early so much weight with my fellow-citizens when I proposed new institutions, or alterations in the old, and so much influence in public councils when I became a member; for I was but a bad speaker, never eloquent, subject to much hesitation in my choice of words, hardly correct in language, and yet I generally carried my points.

In reality, there is, perhaps, no one of our natural passions so hard to subdue as pride. Disguise it, struggle with it, beat it down, stifle it, mortify it as much as one pleases, it is still alive, and will every now and then peep out and show itself; you will see it, perhaps, often in this history; for, even if I could conceive that I had completely overcome it, I should probably be proud of my humility.

Humility in Action: Interacting with Employees5

Dan Cable, professor at London Business School, tells of an English company that supplies food, especially dairy products and bread. It was struggling with its culture: a focus on cost reduction and better delivery accompanied by dozens of meetings full of criticism had irritated the drivers.

The management noticed and decided on a change. All managers partook in training and then started “the new.” What was this “new”? Instead of getting bogged down in nitty-gritty discussion of problems, each manager simply and repeatedly asks his drivers: “How can I help you deliver excellent service?” “How exactly can I help you deliver excellent service?” What a simple idea!

In the beginning, there was great skepticism. Affection and trust in the managers had hit rock bottom. As the questions were repeated over and over again, some drivers began to make some small suggestions. For example, one driver suggested new products such as “Gogurts” (yoghurts to go) and playful string cheese that could be delivered to parents early in the day for their children's school boxes. Another driver thought about how to report stock shortages more quickly so that customers would not be left without the food they ordered.

As the drivers received appreciation for their ideas and saw how they were implemented, their willingness to engage grew, which in turn impressed the managers and increased their respect, which in turn reciprocally increased the creativity and engagement of the delivery staff and so on.

The managers also learned in this process that much of what they had previously labeled “mistakes” were in fact innovations by the drivers which they had created to streamline processes and still deliver everything on time.

As one manager summed up, the whole cultural change process:

We really thought that we knew our delivery people inside out, but we’ve realized that there was a lot we were missing. Our weekly customer conversation meetings are now more interactive and the conversations are more honest and adult in their approach.

Humility in Action: Communication

Professor and former executive: Edward D. Hess, (1947), Managing Director Arthur Anderson, Professor Darden Business School6:

I woke up one morning with the idea that I would sign all my emails as “ed” not “Ed”. A small step, but it had meaning for me. To me, it was my way to accept the mediocrity principle. I’m just one of the billions of humans on this planet, and I’m not worthy of being a legend in my own mind. It was my way to remind myself many times a day that “it’s not all about me” and that I’m just a little player in a big world. I am little “ed”, not big “Ed”. I expect that some of you may think that this is weird. I’m not advocating that you copy me. You may find your own Quieting Ego reminders.

In the beginning, signing my name “ed” had another, unexpected impact. It caused me to reread my emails to check for tone, whether I had been a positive force and whether I tried to connect and relate to the person I was writing to before I took positions or got into a “telling” mode right off the bat. Did I use a greeting and address the person by name? Did I try to leave people in a good place? I continue those practices today. That small step slows me down to focus on the messages I’m sending, not just the words.

Humility as Seen by Benedictine Monk

Albert Altenähr, Order of the Benedictines7:

Even after 40 years in the monastery and even as abbot, it is not easy for me to make the subject of humility accessible and palatable to confreres, newcomers and people in general. The word is loaded. It is not seldom associated with a “broken back’ and “kowtowing.” It does not seem to fit today's aspirations of fostering a true self or individual self-realization. I say of myself now and then: “I have been told a lot of things – but never that I am humble.” If “humility” is associated with a weakness in walking upright and with feelings of inferiority ... then I do not strive for it. Yet, humility is in my opinion, a positive value that simply belongs to a successful life. Every so often I have seen a little bit of it ... and have possibly even held it in my hand.

Benedict's 12 stages of humility have motivated me to formulate 12 sentences on humility, which inspire me – and perhaps the reader too – to think about humility anew:

Humility grows from strength.
Humility can take the head under its arm without losing face.
You cannot humiliate a humble person.
Humility looks in its own mirror.
Humility does not explain away failure.
Humility apologizes to others.
Humility is able and willing to learn.
Humility is saying yes to earth and longing for heaven.
Humility dares to step out from limitations into vastness.
Humility is the imposition of courage.
Humility practices heaven.
Humility is never possession, but always attempt.
Humility in Selected Well-Known CEOs
Admitting Weaknesses and Errors
a. Tim Cook, CEO of Apple, 147,000 employees: Apple had been criticized in China for only repairing and not replacing iPhone 4 within the one-year guarantee. Tim Cook gave out a public apology: “We express our sincere apologies for any concerns or misunderstandings this gave consumers.” Apple also changed its warranty policy in China so that customers with a faulty iPhone would receive a new handset, rather than a repair, and Cook pledged to educate local resellers of the change to avoid any repair disputes.8
b. Satya Nadella, CEO of Microsoft, 163,000 employees: In a conference, he gave an unsatisfactory answer when he was asked about the pay gap for women. There was negative reaction in social media and the press. “My chief of staff smugly read me a tweet capturing the moment, ‘I hope Satya’s comms person is a woman and is asking for a raise right now.’ I was frustrated, but I also was determined to use the incident to demonstrate what a growth mindset looks like under pressure. … A few hours later I shot off an email to everyone in the company. I encouraged them to watch the video, and I was quick to point out that I had answered the question completely wrong. Any advice that advocates passivity in the face of bias is wrong. In some ways, I’m glad I messed up in such a public forum because it helped me confront an unconscious bias, I didn’t know I had, and it helped me find a new sense of empathy for the great women in my life and at my company.”9
c. Warren Buffet, investor and CEO of Berkshire Hathaway, 391,500 employees wrote in a letter to his shareholder in 198910: “Last summer we sold the corporate jet that we purchased for $850,000 three years ago and bought another used jet for $6.7 million. Those of you who recall the mathematics of the multiplying bacteria on page 5 will understandably panic: If our net worth continues to increase at current rates, and the cost of replacing planes also continues to rise at the now-established rate of 100% compounded annually, it will not be long before Berkshire's entire net worth is consumed by its jet. Charlie doesn't like it when I equate the jet with bacteria; he feels it's degrading to the bacteria. His idea of traveling in style is an air-conditioned bus, a luxury he steps up to only when bargain fares are in effect. My own attitude toward the jet can be summarized by the prayer attributed, apocryphally I'm sure, to St. Augustine as he contemplated leaving a life of secular pleasures to become a priest. Battling the conflict between intellect and glands, he pled: ‘Help me, Oh Lord, to become chaste – but not yet.’ Naming the plane has not been easy. I initially suggested ‘The Charles T. Munger.’ Charlie countered with ‘The Aberration’. We finally settled on ‘The Indefensible’.”
d. Reed Hasting, CEO of Netflix, 12,000 employees: “Earlier in my career, in the early days of Pure Software, I was too insecure to talk openly about mistakes with my staff, and I learned an important lesson. I was making a lot of leadership mistakes and it weighed on me heavily. Beyond my general incompetence at people management, I had indeed hired and fired five sales directors in five years. The first two times, I could blame the person I’d hired, but by the fourth and fifth failures, it was clear the problem was me. One thing I have always done is put the company before myself. Certain my own incompetence was bad for the organization, I went to the board and, as if in the confessional, detailed my inadequacies and offered my resignation. But the Pure board didn’t accept it. Financially, the company was doing well. They agreed that I’d made mistakes with people management but claimed that, if they hired someone new, that person would make mistakes also. Two fascinating things happened during that meeting. One was that, as expected, I felt immense relief because I had told the truth and come clean about my errors. The other was more interesting: the board seemed to believe in my leadership more after I had opened up and made myself vulnerable to them. I went back to the office and, at our next all-employee meeting, did the same thing I’d done in the boardroom. I outlined my mistakes in detail and expressed my regret for having hurt the company. This time, not only did I feel more relief and build trust with my staff, but also people began telling me about all sorts of mistakes they made, mistakes they’d been previously sweeping under the rug. That offered them relief, improved our relationships, and gave me more information so I could do a better job managing the business.”11
Showing Strengths
a. Tim Cook, CEO of Apple, 147,000 employees: In an interview Tim Cook gave in 2014, he explained that Jobs never expected him to lead Apple in the same way that he had. “He knew, when he chose me, that I wasn’t like him, that I’m not a carbon copy of him. And so, he obviously thought through that deeply, about who he wanted to lead Apple. I have always felt the responsibility of that.” Cook says he desperately wanted to continue Jobs’ legacy and “pour every ounce that I had in myself into the company,” but he never had the objective of being the same as Jobs. “I knew, the only person I can be is the person I am,” he continued. “I’ve tried to be the best Tim Cook I can be.”12
b. Satya Nadella, CEO of Microsoft, 163,000 employees: “I seized on an upcoming meeting with about 150 of my top leaders to tell a few stories and to share my expectations with them. First off, I told them about an anonymous Microsoft manager who had come to me recently to share how much he loved the new growth mindset and how much he wanted to see more of it pointing out, ‘Hey, Satya, I know these five people who don’t have a growth mindset.’ The guy was just using growth mindset to find a new way to complain about others. That is not what we had in mind. I told these high-potential leaders that once you become a vice president, a partner in this endeavor, the whining is over. You can’t say the coffee around here is bad, or there aren’t enough good people, or I didn’t get the bonus. ‘To be a leader in this company, your job is to find the rose petals in a field of shit.’”13
c. Götz Werner, founder and former CEO of DM, 63,000 employees: “I resolved to break the habit of asking people questions. Whenever someone asks me a question, I ask a counter-question, not just one, but three – and if possible, I add one that requires a bit of work. So instead of reacting reflexively to the question ‘Mr. Werner, how should such and such be done?’ as I have done up to now, and telling them how it's done, I now answer: ‘Have you already looked into this? And this might also be relevant. Besides, I've heard there's this company somewhere else. Why don't you go there? Find out how they do it.’ My new management logic: from director to evocator. The idea behind it was compellingly simple: whenever I, as a superior, give an answer, then the employee says: ‘All right. That's what Mr. Werner said. That's how I have to do it.’ In this way I stupefy people. The new method was incredibly successful. Whoever asked me a question got three new questions. People immediately understood: ‘If I go to Mr. Werner even though I already know the solution, he will dig deep.’ This kind of instinctive redelegation worked wonders. Suddenly no one asked without having a real problem. Now people took ownership of the answer they actually already knew and also acted on it responsibly.”14
d. Dirk Rossmann former Founder and CEO of Rossmann, 56,000 employees: “The basis for trust is honesty. People, who have been working with me for a long time, know they can tell me their opinion. When I say to an employee: ‘Now tell me what I probably think of you’, most are confused, but their answers are all the more honest. My biggest quality as entrepreneur, as boss: Bringing together people. I am an integrator, feel disagreements and address them openly. In the most cases, I am successful with this.”15
Appreciating Others
a. Tim Cook, CEO of Apple, 147,000 employees: “Cook trusted and empowered his staff to make decisions for themselves. He inspired a mentality where, according to his employees, ‘everything is possible, let’s try harder, let’s be creative, let’s try to solve it. You know we can do it. There’s this can-do attitude. People at the leadership level constantly reminded us to be creative. How do you solve the problem?’”16
b. Reed Hastings, CEO of Netflix, 12,000 employees: “We are perhaps the only public company that shares financial results internally in the weeks before the quarter is closed. We announce these numbers at a quarterly business review meeting with our top seven hundred or so managers. The financial world sees this as reckless. But the information has never been leaked. When it does one day leak (I imagine it will), we won’t overreact. We’ll just deal with that one case and continue with transparency.”

“A while back, Sheryl Sandberg of Facebook spent a day shadowing me at work. She attended all of my meetings and one-on-ones. It’s something I do occasionally with other Silicon Valley executives, so we can learn from watching one another in action. Afterward, when Sheryl and I debriefed, she said, “The amazing thing was to sit with you all day long and see that you didn’t make one decision!” I felt great - because that’s exactly what we are going for. Our dispersed decision-making model has become a foundation of our culture and one of the main reasons we have grown and innovated so quickly.”17

Willingness to Learn
a. Satya Nadella, CEO of Microsoft, 163,000 employees: “He did a bunch of things that were totally un-Microsoft-like. He went to talk to start-ups to find out why they weren’t using Microsoft.”18

When Nadella began as CEO he decided: “For starters, I decided to listen. Straightaway I heard from hundreds of employees at every level and in every part of the company. We held focus groups to allow people to share their opinions anonymously as well. Listening was the most important thing I accomplished each day because it would build the foundation of my leadership for years to come. To my first question, why does Microsoft exist, the message was loud and clear. We exist to build products that empower others.”

In his book, he also describes how he had to learn empathy: “Empathy grounds and centers me. Ironically, it was a lack of empathy that nearly cost me the chance to join Microsoft as a young man some twenty years before.” In his final interview, there was one simple question.

“Imagine you see a baby laying in the street, and the baby is crying. What do you do?” he asked.

“You call 911,” I replied without much forethought.

Richard walked me out of his office, put his arm around me, and said, “You need some empathy, man. If a baby is laying on a street crying, pick up the baby.”

Somehow, I got the job anyway, but Richard’s words have remained with me to this day.19

b. Carly Fiorina, former CEO of HP, 320,000 employees: “I needed a team who would cut me some slack while I figured out what being a sales manager was all about. This was long before the days of 360- degree feedback. Nevertheless, every three months I would give Pattie, Bill and Don a little questionnaire I’d devised and ask them to tell me what I was doing right and what I was doing wrong. They took the request seriously, were thoughtful in their responses and helped me tremendously. We also had fun with it. Occasionally Bill would stray from the assignment and give me wardrobe advice. He was usually right.”20
The Bigger Picture
a. Tim Cook, CEO of Apple, 147,000 employees: In May 2014, a member of a conservative think tank, the National Center for Public Policy Research, pushed him to consider the impact sustainability programs were having on Apple’s bottom line. But he refused. “When we work on making our devices accessible by the blind, I don’t consider the bloody ROI [return on investment],” he said.

Shortly after Cook was inducted into the Alabama Academy of Honor in 2014, he personally told Representative Todd that Apple had no intention of investing in Alabama until the state passes antidiscrimination laws. “When I think of ethics, I think of leaving things better than you found them. And to me, that goes from everything from environmentally, to how you work with suppliers with labor questions, to the carbon footprint of your products, to the things you choose to support, to the way you treat your employees…. Your whole persona fits under that umbrella.”21

b. Satya Nadella, CEO Microsoft, 163,000 employees: “I like to think that the C in CEO stands for culture. The CEO is the curator of an organization’s culture.”22
c. Götz Werner, founder and former CEO of DM, 63,000 employees: “Occasionally, in the first years of my ‘retirement’, I was met with pitying looks, especially in business circles, and someone asked the question: ‘Do you really want to give up your life's work?’ I nodded in a friendly way and answered: ‘A life's work only becomes a life's work when it is set up in such a way that it can be continued by others.’”23

Chapter 13 How to Implement Humility in Your Company and a Glance into the Future

DOI: 10.4324/9781003358015-13

I believe in getting into hot water; it keeps you clean.

G. K. Chesterton (English writer 1874–1936)

Summary

Anyone who wants to implement humility in a company has several levers. The most important one is the example set by the manager. It is also possible to use interventions that make the topic more acceptable. A glance to the future shows what may be possible

I just recently read a book with case studies. They were concise, well-written and detailed. Yet I found in myself the same reaction that I so often observe in my participants: “Sounds all good and well, but would not work at my company. I do not quite believe the story - I bet there is lots more behind it, that the authors are not telling us.”

Is this healthy or unhealthy skepticism? To be frank, I have no answer to this. All I know is that we humans find it very hard to imagine recreating someone’s result for ourselves. Yes, we can see that Netflix has a powerful feedback culture. But how to get there? Yes, one employee at Prudential Singapore decided to stay after having observed the humble way in which the company dealt with the crisis of having deducted too many premiums. But who knows what was the real reason for her staying?

How do I get to a culture in which I can expect my people to act humbly more or less all the time?

This leads me to the question, of how can the topic of humility be promoted in your company? How can it happen, that you think of what is possible, not of what is probable?

Interviews show considerable concern and also pose serious questions.

How can the strength of humility be recognized and used without it being – and I say this now quite deliberately – without it being too womanish.

(Interview No. 2, Female Head Talent Management, 10,000 employees)

How can we raise the humility not only of managers but also of employees. Humility also means courageously going forward and speaking out and bravely revealing ourselves. How can we encourage this - with employees, with managers and also among managers themselves?

(Interview No. 3, Branch Manager, 22,000 employees)

From my point of view, there are three steps you need to take.

First, take an honest inventory to see how you are doing with humility. Using the questionnaires in the book and others to see what barriers to humility exist in your organization. Is there no real culture for dealing with mistakes? Is there a lack of psychological safety and empowerment? Do employees feel unappreciated? Is there a pervasive mentality that change is impossible? Do employees and colleagues, as well as yourself, truly take responsibility for their roles? How is open learning practiced? What is being done to ensure that everyone sees the big picture? Do people believe they are already humble, but no one recognizes it? On all of these points, you should know where you and your team stand.

The second step is to launch some interventions to create awareness about the benefits of humility, but also the stumbling blocks along the way. This is simple cognitive work where employees and leaders can learn about transparency illusions, hubris, lack of appreciation or any of the dozens of other obstacles described in this book. More behavioral work follows in other workshops: what exactly are my strengths and weaknesses and how do I show them, how do I acknowledge others, how can we continuously learn without getting stuck in a fixed mindset, what is the bigger picture for everyone, the team and the company.

In the third step, you then promote the everyday humble culture by clearly acting humbly yourself and consistently making humility an issue. Be a conscious role model for personal growth in your everyday life. This will usually have an impact on your colleagues and superiors. If this is not possible, focus on your own team first and try to win over the peer or higher levels step by step – with all the wisdom and perseverance that your higher goals award you with. And if that fails, it may be a good idea to look for the next challenge in your work life without feeling like a victim.

What to do when you encounter resistance? Well, one option is to respond as this CEO of a media company with more than 16,000 employees did. He had suggested to his top team that they take not only strategy courses but also leadership courses at a prestigious business school. The CFO informed them that he neither needed nor wanted these courses. And the response? The CEO said, "I've been there – so you go, too." That was the end of the resistance.

And keep at it. In the long run, any culture can be changed. The important thing is that whenever you doubt that humility is possible in your part of the company or in the company as a whole, check your assumptions, look at your attitude and keep trying. Recall the examples of disgruntled leaders who felt their efforts were undermined by unwilling employees. Most of the time, it has not much to do with unwillingness, but more to do with employees lacking a sense of security and motivation. Once the manager focuses on this, life usually gets easier.

Use your communication skills and persistence to push the issue. And avoid Paul Anderson's mistake of demanding humility but only asking for numbers. Why? Because it's clear to many that financial results and humility go hand in hand.

I am convinced that a company with a healthy culture will also be financially healthy.

(Interview No. 17, CSO, 400 employees)

When you have a good culture, numbers will be good. And if we compromise somewhat that gives us pay off in people’s wellbeing.

(Interview No. 125, Group CEO, 1,200 employees).

And finally, it is reassuring to know that once the definition of humility has been established, employees and managers find this to be a highly desirable virtue. Both in my seminars and in the interviews, I have noticed a real hunger for humility. It is as if a weight falls off the shoulders of both employees and managers when they are allowed to embrace and live this virtue.

An internationally active trainer further confirmed this impression when she told me that during an exercise, participants most frequently paired the term "humility" with the word: "liberation." The second most frequent term was "purity", i.e., a kind of self-purification that is taking place via humility.

And this is exactly how humility is to be seen in the company: As liberation and purification from an obstructive ego. From an ego that blocks our view of the situation, others and ourselves and drives us towards egocentric automatisms that harm us all.

A Glance into the Future

Next to me there are other optimists among researchers and management writers. One of them is Frederic Laloux, who has written a highly acclaimed book on how companies reinvent themselves.1 For this book, he interviewed dozens of managers who, in his view, have developed a new awareness. He calls these executives by the color “Teal.”

And he describes them like this:

Today, in small but increasing numbers, leaders are growing into the next stage of consciousness…. They are mindful, taming the needs and impulses of their ego. They are suspicious of their own desires – to control their environment, to be successful, to look good, or even to accomplish good works. They develop an ethic of mutual trust and assumed abundance.

(This ethic) accepts reality and can observe facts and gather data. But is also open to other sources of knowing than just rational-analytical. Intuition honors the complex, ambiguous, paradoxical, non-linear nature of reality – and connects or discerns patterns on another level.

The new mindset transcends the either-or thinking and embraces both-and thinking.2

That sounds like a perfect description of humility. It is about recognizing yourself and trusting others. About penetrating cognitively while listening to what there is beyond cognition. About living a healthy narcissism. About recognizing when you are the best and then taking full responsibility. Like Cincinnatus within a framework that is sanely limiting – circumstances, the ability of others, your own weaknesses. With the ease of relinquishing the scepter and still remaining a person at peace with yourself.

Acknowledge duality: It is about a strong self, just as it is about one's own weaknesses. It is about the ability of others and how to support them in their weaknesses. It is about what you already know and what is to be learned. It is about the little things and how to take good care of them, while the big things are not lost and are always the central guiding principles.

In the end, it is not about an “either or” but about an “AND.” And we humans can do that.

Notes
Laloux, F. (2014). Reinventing Organizations: A Guide to Creating Organizations Inspired by the Next Stage in Human Consciousness. Nelson Parker.
   https://www.leadershipandchangemagazine.com/teal-organizations-and-cultures/; https://www.thegeniusworks.com/2018/04/reinventing-organisations-creating-a-more-agile-human-and-soulful-organisation-for-the-21st-century/. Retrieved 12.11.2022.

===== FILE 22/23: xhtml%2fappendixA.xhtml =====

Appendix A: FAQs
Is humble leadership a new leadership style?

I am often asked how humble leadership fits in with existing leadership styles of which there are many. Let us take a closer look.

Research tends to sort leadership styles into three larger categories: Relational, task-related and change-related. A distinction is made between whether I as a leader focus more on people, things or the larger process.

Amongst relational leadership styles are:

Charismatic or visionary leadership that uses charm and persuasion to convince employees of ideas.
Laissez-Faire leadership which trusts employees and lets them more or less walk alone.
Authentic leadership which requires a very personal contact between employee and manager that generates trust.
Ethical leadership which is based on personal values and respect for the values of others.
Servant leadership which sees the manager first and foremost as a tool and guide for his employees to reach their potential.

Amongst task-related leadership styles are:

Transactional management which acts through clearly defined tasks and intensive control paired with rewards and sanctions.
Authoritarian leadership which is an extreme version of transactional leadership, in which the manager accepts almost no suggestions or ideas from the workforce, but primarily decides everything by himself/herself.

The key change-related leadership style is:

Transformational leadership which attempts to inspire and lead employees to new shores by means of a vision.

There are numerous overlaps between the individual styles. Transformational leadership contains a lot of charisma and I would like to see the leadership style that completely ignores the issue of ethics and authenticity.

At the same time, it is clear to everyone that all three elements typically have to be addressed in a normal leadership role: Building relationships, completing tasks, creating change. Ergo, most managers will tend to use a number of styles at different times. This is also the focus of experts such as Daniel Goleman who proposes a leadership typology, which requires emotional intelligence for using the right style at the right time for the right person. Six distinct styles should be at hand: coercive, authoritative/visionary, democratic, affiliative, pacesetting and coaching.

In general, a differentiation between and a classification of leadership styles makes sense for research purposes (and consequently for you), because the research helps measure the effectiveness of the individual styles in different situations. Think of it, as the right clothing to wear. Some situations require a suit, others a pair of linen trousers, yet others a hiking outfit. In the same vein, the servant leadership style may be helpful for insecure employees, the transformational style in times of communicating a new strategy and – depending on the maturity of your team – either the laissez-faire or transactional style for a nitty gritty SAP implementation. The same holds true for the Goleman styles: Coercive for situations of crisis, coaching or authoritative for onboarding and pace-setting for situations, where it is important to level differences within the team.

As a summary, the recommendation is clear: it is worth your while having your wardrobe stocked in such a way, that in whatever leadership situation you find yourself, you will never be stark naked in front of your employees.

What now about humility? I do not want to claim that humility is a style of leadership that could be described as an egg-laying wool-milk sow (a useful German expression for something that is ideal) – that would be truly presumptuous in a book about humility.

Instead, I follow a group of researchers who see humility not as a leadership style at all, but as a virtue that forms the basis of every action and thus of every leadership style.

This view has a lot to offer because the four core points of humility are about a fundamental approach to life. To recognize yourself, appreciate others, be willing to learn and see the bigger picture is less a style than a general view of the world and oneself with a liberating effect. After all, humility rejects any label that one so readily gives oneself: “I am more of a relationship person” or “I am a transactional type.” Humility does not permit a manager to use the excuse of internalized or inherited behavioral patterns. Instead, a humble leader who is willing to learn will use any style of leadership that is known to him/her wherever it is justified.

Ergo: Humility is not a leadership style, but a basic attitude, a virtue with which the leader enters the world.

The additional bonus of this view is that humility is therefore also a virtue for employees. A common language can be generated around this concept – something that you cannot do with a leadership style – since those are confined to … leaders.

What is the difference between the concept of humility and servant leadership?

When Robert K. Greenleaf published his seminal book on Servant Leadership, he defined it as follows1: “The servant-leader is servant first… It begins with the natural feeling that one wants to serve, to serve first.” The CEO of the Greenleaf Center for Servant Leadership Kent Keith elaborates further: “A servant-leader loves people and wants to help them. The mission of a servant-leader is, therefore, to identify the needs of others and try to satisfy those needs.”2

Since 1977 hundreds of academic articles have been published on the topic citing numerous positive effects of servant leadership for employees, such as more psychological safety, more commitment, more innovation, less exhaustion and cynicism.3

You can quickly spot the similarities with humility in that both concepts are other-focused. Both see less room for the leader’s ego, for arrogance or narcissism. On the other hand, the differences also emerge quickly.

Firstly, only managers can be servant leaders, while also employees can be humble.

Secondly, for many managers the challenge in servant leadership is that the focus is ONLY on employees and their growth. This neglects the company and also the person of the leader him- or herself. In humility, one of the key elements is to perceive the bigger picture and lead with that in mind. The bigger picture includes the needs of the department, the company, the society as well as the self. This does not square with the definition of servant leadership that both Greenleaf and research propose.

Yet, in many conversations it seems that managers have for themselves extended the definition of servant leadership to also cover these two points. Which actually turns it into humility, which as you now know not only focuses on the person of the follower but also on the bigger picture including one’s own person.

Thirdly, servant leadership is seen to be effective by actions that are taken to foster the growth of employees, while humility works indirectly by the manager modeling growth.

So, while the concepts have some similarities, I humbly propose that the differences make humility the more attractive option for all.

Are there sub-categories of humility?

Research singles out two sub-categories of humility, namely intellectual and cultural humility.

Intellectual humility is about people accepting the fact that they are not always right and provides them with the willingness to listen to the opinions of others while possibly even changing their own. It focuses the topic of humility on individual convictions and views of the world and is thus somewhat narrower than the general concept of humility.

Cultural humility, on the other hand, has something to do with how much we succeed in not considering our own culture, our own background, our own experiences as superior to others. This is of course relevant for every manager who has contact with other cultures. Do you catch yourself thinking that the English, Chinese, German or Italian way of doing things is the best of all? Then this form of humility is for you.

It does, of course, not stop you from openly agreeing with your business partners on, for example, an “English” understanding of responsibility, a “Chinese” understanding of respect, a “German” understanding of time or an “Italian” way of relationships. But it also means that you accept in principle that excellence and valuable cooperation can take place in other cultures and that these cultures are equal to your own. Neither is yours better nor theirs – each has its advantages and disadvantages and if you interact humbly with each other, then success will ensue.

Notes
Greenleaf, R. K. (1977). Servant Leadership: A Journey into the Nature of Legitimate Power and Greatness. Paulist Press, New York.
Jacobs, G. A. (2010). Book review: Kent M. Keith’s (2008) The case for servant leadership. International Journal of Leadership Studies, 6(2), 297–300.
Eva, N., Robin, M., Sendjaya, S., van Dierendonck, D., & Liden, R. C. (2019). Servant leadership: A systematic review and call for future research. The Leadership Quarterly, 30(1), 111–132.
